A Comprehensive Treatment. This book first sets the stage by present ing the Period of Exploration. Following this come the story of each of the thirteen colonies; a comparative study of life in the English, Dutch, French, Spanish, and Portuguese colonies; the story of the Intercolonial Wars; the struggle for independence; the formation and adoption of the Constitution; and the expansion of the country to its present boundaries. This comprehensive treatment lays a splendid foundation for the later study of United States History. 

Style Simple and Interesting. The authors have told the story in a fastflowing narrative style that the pupils will both understand and enjoy.

The seventeen, clear, uncrowded maps show all places mentioned in the text. Both the pictures and the maps are accompanied by full, explanatory legends.

PREFACE 

In the year 1490 a vast unknown continent, inhabited by savage Indians, lay between the world's two largest oceans. Three hundred and seventy years later this continent was the homeland of a strong young nation, its people drawn from many European countries. Who discovered the new continent? Who explored it? How did it come to be named America? What tempted white men to give up the known life in Europe to test existence on the Atlantic coast of the New World? And how did these white men come to form a new nation? 

These are the questions answered in EARLY DAYS IN 

AMERICA. 

In a simple story style the pages of this book put the reader aboard Columbus' little flagship; land him on the shore of Virginia with the earliest English colonists; make him, in turn, a member of each of the thirteen original colonies; fire his heart with the desire for independence, and at length arouse his lasting pride in the new nation, the United States of America. 

EARLY DAYS IN AMERICA unrolls the frontier from the first landings in the unbroken forests along the Atlantic coast to the acquisition of the shores of the Pacific. It carries the story of our nation from the very beginning to the possession of the lands now occupied by all the forty-eight states. 

iii

iv 

PREFACE 

The development of the subject matter in this book is in accord with the outstanding courses of study in history and with the Report of the Committee on American History in Schools and Colleges of The American 

Historical Association, The Mississippi Valley His torical Association and The National Council for the Social Studies, published in book form in January, 1944, and entitled American History in Schools and Colleges. In speaking of the "middle grades" (grades 

IV, V, VI) this report says, "Here the emphasis is to be on the periods of exploration and colonial history and on the simpler patterns of life in the pre-industrial era." Then follows a list of subjects, of representative dates, and of representative persons which are to be the "recommended minimum content" for American history courses for the middle grades. This new edition. of EARLY DAYS IN AMERICA has been built to include every detail of this latest history course. 

Included in the text are well over a hundred illustrations. On the seventeen maps are shown all places mentioned in the text. The teacher can make very effective' use of these illustrations and maps, together with their legends, to supplement the study of the text. 

Many teaching helps are included. Each chapter in the book is preceded by a brief section in bold-faced type to arouse interest and to give an insight into the significance of the chapter content. Following each chapter is a summary of its high lights, and one or more new-type exercises on the subject matter. Although the directions accompanying these exercises

PREFACE 

V call for their use as oral reviews, the teacher who so desires can readily use them as written exercises by having the pupils write the key words (those to be supplied, selected, etc.) on a separate sheet of paper.

It is not necessary to copy an entire exercise. 

The pronouncing index gives the pronunciations of all difficult proper names and historical terms. 

For the convenience of those who wish to teach by the unit method, the various chapters in the book have been grouped in the Table of Contents into major units of work. It is suggested that the teacher wishing to use the unit method give her class a preview of the text material of each unit before undertaking the study of the chapters themselves. This may be done by reading aloud the introductory paragraphs of the various chapters included in the unit, and then conducting a class discussion based upon them. The use of the exercises at the ends of the individual chapters may be withheld until an entire unit has been studied, and thus provide a splendid checkup on the unit. 

The tables at the back of the book will prove helpful both in graphically presenting and in reviewing important text material. They present essential facts about the thirteen colonies and the Intercolonial Wars in compact form for ready reference.

The history of America's early days is a fascinating story, and the pupil who knows it well has a splendid background for the intelligent interpretation and appreciation of United States History.

PILGRIM COLONISTS, JOHN ALDEN AND PRISCILLA 

The ship which brought the Pilgrims to America has sailed for home, leaving the colonists to face the hardships and dangers of life in the wilderness of the New World.

CHAPTER ONE  

"Is the world round-or flat?" "Are there other lands besides the ones we know? If so, what sort of people live there?" Five hundred years ago, the people of Europe were asking these questions. In this chapter we shall learn how daring explorers crossed land and sea, risking their lives to find the answers. 

Trade between Europe and Asia. Several hundred years ago, the people of Europe began to be interested in the huge neighboring continent of Asia. Explorers traveled far to the east and came back with strange tales of the wonders they had seen. With them, these explorers brought back silks, spices, jewels, and other products of the countries they had visited. 

In time, a flourishing trade grew up between the countries of Europe and those of southern and eastern Asia. European merchants and traders traveled to the east by sea and by land, taking with them goods from their own home lands to be exchanged for the rich products of Asia. Though the way was long and the dangers were many, the trade continued to grow and prosper. 

Trade with Asia Is Interrupted. In the year 1453 the profitable trade suddenly came to an end. The 

Turks, a savage, warlike race from the central part of 1

Asia, conquered the lands lying around the eastern end of the Mediterranean sea. No longer could traders safely carry their goods through these lands, for the Turks attacked them and took their wares and sometimes even their lives. As the demand for goods from 

Asia increased, the supply grew steadily smaller, for merchants no longer dared to risk their lives in order to transport merchandise through the Turkish lands. 

The people of Europe came to realize that a new route to the countries of southern and eastern Asia must be found, or the rich trade upon which they had come to depend would be at an end. 

Christopher Columbus. About twenty years before the Turks blocked the eastern trade routes, a boy named Christopher Columbus was born in Italy. While he was still a young boy, he helped his father, who was a weaver of woolen cloth. Later he attended school, where he learned to make maps and charts. At the age of fourteen he became a sailor and went to sea.

When the trade routes to Asia were closed, Columbus began to think about some new route to the countries of southern and eastern Asia. Like many of the wisest people of his day, he believed that the world was round instead of flat. If this were so, a person should certainly be able to sail around it. Columbus did not know how big the world was, nor did he know that west of Europe two huge continents stretched north and south to bar the way from Europe to Asia. It seemed to him that if a person should sail directly west from Europe he might easily travel around the world to Asia.

For years Columbus traveled throughout Europe trying to find someone who would supply him with ships and men. Wherever he went, people laughed at him. It seemed ridiculous to them that one could reach the countries of the East by sailing west. Many times he appealed for help to the king and queen of Spain, then the leading European nation. Though they refused the aid he asked, he spent much time in their country, studying maps and talking with experienced navigators. At last, when he was about to give up in despair, Queen Isabella of Spain offered him the help he wanted. 

In 1492 Columbus set sail from Spain with a fleet of three little ships, the Pinta, the Nina, and the Santa Maria. Though his boats were small and many members of his crew were inexperienced and afraid, he was confident of success.

Columbus and his men made one brief stop at the Canary islands, then sailed on to the west for two long months. As day followed day and no land was sighted, the sailors grew more and more frightened. First they pleaded with Columbus to turn back, then they threatened to kill him if he would not. Only Columbus remained calm. Always he refused to turn back. At last, when it seemed that the crew was about to carry out its threat, land was sighted. 

The land which Columbus first discovered was a small island north of Cuba. Joyfully he and his men. went ashore and claimed the land for Spain, giving it the name San Salvador. Then on he went, looking for the rich cities of Asia. Though he did not find them,

ROUTES OF THE EARLY EXPLORERS TO THE NEW WORLD 

Leif Ericsson, Christopher Columbus, Americus Vespucius, and the Cabots made the earliest voyages of discovery to the New World. As you read about each voyage, trace it on this map.

William Thompson, Saugerties, N. Y.

COLUMBUS DISCOVERS THE NEW WORLD 

On October 12, 1492, Christopher Columbus landed on the island of San Salvador, in the West Indies. Here we see him planting a cross on the shore of the island and claiming the land for Spain. Although Columbus made four voyages to the New 

World, he never knew that he had discovered unknown lands, but died believing that he had reached Asia. he found other islands. Columbus believed that these belonged to the great island group known as the East Indies, so he called the natives Indians. 

The Indians whom Columbus saw were timid, friendly people who believed that the white men were gods. At first they ran away and hid, but later they approached the Spaniards and made signs to show that they were friendly. When Columbus returned to Spain,

he took some of the Indians with him to show to Queen 

Isabella and King Ferdinand. 

Though Columbus never knew it, he had not reached Asia. The islands he had found were part of the West 

Indies group which lay off the coast of North America. 

He was not the first white man to reach the lands of the New World, for nearly five hundred years before, about the year 1000, a Northman named Leif Ericsson had crossed the ocean and discovered the North American continent. Leif's discoveries, however, had been forgotten, and Columbus, in rediscovering the New World, was making just as important a discovery as though he were the first white man to visit the lands of the Western hemisphere.

In later years Columbus made three other voyages to the west, always searching in vain for the rich cities of Asia. On these later voyages he discovered several other islands of the West Indies and also sailed along the coasts of South and Central America. Because he did not discover rich lands with which a profitable trade could be established, the Spaniards at last became tired of him, and he died a poor and neglected man. 

It is a strange fact that Christopher Columbus never knew the great importance of the discoveries he had made. Someone once said that when Columbus started he did not know where he was going, when he arrived he did not know where he was, and when he returned he did not know where he had been. This is true. But the results of his voyages, as we shall see, were most important. Not only did they lead to many more voy-

ages of exploration by European seamen, but they also gave Spain a claim to the lands which Columbus had discovered in the New World.

Americus Vespucius. Some years after the discovery of America by Columbus, another Italian, Americus Vespucius, made four voyages to the New World. 

Two of these were in the service of Spain, and two for Portugal. On these voyages he explored much of the eastern coast of South America. Later he wrote an account of his travels which was read by many Europeans. Among the readers of the account was a man who was preparing a geography book. This man wished to have a name by which he might label the newly-discovered western lands, and he called them America after the writer of the article, Americus Vespucius. Ever since that time the two great continents of the Western hemisphere have been known as the 

Americas-North America and South America. 

Spanish Colonies in the West Indies. On Columbus' second voyage he stopped at the island of Haiti in the West Indies and left there a number of men to establish a colony. Not long afterwards, other groups of Spaniards came to the New World and other colonies were established. Spanish settlements were made on 

Cuba, Haiti, Puerto Rico, and a number of other West Indian islands. Towns were built up and trade was begun with the mother country. In a very few years the West Indies became a valuable Spanish possession. 

Ponce de Leon. In 1513 the governor of the island of Puerto Rico was a man named Ponce de Leon. He

Following Columbus' discoveries, many Spaniards came to the New World to explore and to add new lands to the Spanish Empire. This map shows the routes of six Spanish explorers.

loved the rough life of the New World, and it grieved him to think that he was growing old and soon would have to give up many of the things which he enjoyed doing.

From the Indians, Ponce de Leon heard a strange story about a magic fountain. The Indians said that anyone who drank the waters of this fountain would remain young forever. The Spanish governor believed the story of the Fountain of Youth, and hoped that its waters would make him young again. So he determined to go and find the wonderful fountain, which was said to be on an island not far from Puerto Rico. 

Leaving Puerto Rico, Ponce de Leon and his men sailed northwest. They found a number of islands never before seen by white men, but they did not find the magic fountain which they sought. At last, on 

Easter Sunday, they came to the shores of a warm and beautiful land, which they named Florida, from the 

Spanish word meaning "flowery." The Spaniards did not know that Florida was part of the North American continent but thought it was a large island. After claiming the land for Spain and exploring much of the 

Florida coast, they returned to Puerto Rico.

Some years later Ponce de Leon once more visited the shores of Florida, this time for the purpose of establishing a colony. The native Indians, however, attacked the Spaniards so fiercely that they were forced to leave. 

Ponce de Leon himself was wounded by an Indian arrow, and not long afterward he died as a result of this wound.

Balboa. Among the Spanish settlements in the New 

World was a town named Darien on the coast of the isthmus of Panama. From Darien a Spanish explorer 

BALBOA DISCOVERING THE PACIFIC OCEAN 

In 1513, a Spanish explorer named Balboa crossed the isthmus of Panama and discovered the Pacific ocean, which he claimed for Spain, together with all the lands which bordered it. named Vasco Balboa set out in 1513 to see if he could find a great sea whose shores were rich with gold. The 

Indians had told him that there was such a sea not far away across the mountain range which runs through the isthmus.

For many days Balboa and his men struggled through the hot jungles of Panama and up the mountain slopes. At last they reached the summit of a mountain, and there, stretching away to the south, they saw the blue waters of a mighty ocean.

Down the other side of the mountain slope the Spaniards made their way, and at last they came to the shore. 

The gold which the Indians had spoken of was nowhere to be seen, but this did not discourage Balboa. Drawing his sword, he waded out into the water and claimed for Spain "the South sea" and all the lands which bordered it. Today we call this ocean the Pacific.

Cortez. From Cuba went another Spanish expedition, headed by a daring soldier named Hernando Cortez. Cortez landed in Mexico in 1519, and set out to conquer the country, which was ruled by the powerful Aztec Indians. Straight to the Aztecs' capital city marched Cortez, where he was kindly received by Montezuma, the Aztec ruler. In spite of this friendliness Cortez seized Montezuma and held him as a prisoner. This led to fighting. After a number of fierce battles, the Spaniards defeated the Indians, and Mexico became a Spanish possession.

As a reward for his conquest of Mexico, the king of Spain made Cortez governor of the land which he had conquered. At once the new governor began to develop the country. Many Spaniards from Spain and from the West Indies flocked to Mexico to share in the country's wealth. Priests came, too, to teach the Catholic religion to the Indians and to make them give up sacri-

Ferdinand Magellan, exploring for Spain, led the first expedition which sailed around the world, 1519-1522. About fifty years later, Francis Drake, an Englishman, made the second voyage around the world. Follow the routes of these two explorers.

ficing human beings, as they had been doing. Under their new rulers the Indians were made to work very hard in the fields and in the mines. Though their condition under the Spaniards was little better than slavery, most of them were no worse off than they had been under the harsh Aztec rulers.

In many ways the Spaniards improved the country of Mexico, but they did one unfortunate thing-they destroyed the Aztec library in Mexico City. This rash deed wiped out practically all of the valuable Aztec records, which would have told us much more than we now know about this interesting Indian nation. 

It was not long before Mexico proved to be Spain's most valuable possession. The country was very rich in gold, silver, and precious stones, and soon Spanish ships began making regular voyages to the mother country loaded with these treasures. This, more than anything else, served to arouse Spanish interest in the 

New World. 

Pizarro. High in the Andes mountains in western

South America lay the country of Peru, where the Inca Indians lived. Balboa heard about the Incas and determined to conquer them, but he died before he had an opportunity to do so. It remained for another Spaniard, named Francisco Pizarro, to add Peru to Spain's growing empire. 

Sailing from Panama in 1531, Pizarro and his men landed on the western coast of South America and marched inland. The Incas wanted to be friendly, but the Spaniards attacked them and seized their ruler.

For a long time the unfortunate Inca ruler was held in captivity, and at last he was cruelly put to death. Without their leader, the Incas could not resist the Spanish soldiers, and soon their country became another colony of Spain.

In time Peru came to be almost as valuable a Spanish possession as Mexico. The gold and silver and jewels of the Incas were carried to the coast and shipped to the isthmus of Panama by boat. There they were unloaded and carried across the isthmus to be reshipped to Spain.

Magellan. One of the most interesting of the Spanish explorers was not a Spaniard at all but a man from 

Portugal. His name was Ferdinand Magellan. Like

Columbus, Magellan believed that the rich lands of Asia could be reached by sailing to the west. True, the continents of North and South America lay in the way, but Magellan felt sure that somewhere there was a channel across the New World through which he could sail and reach China, India, and the East Indies. It was in the hope of finding such a passage that he sailed in 1519 in command of a Spanish expedition of five vessels.

South along the eastern coast of South America sailed Magellan, hopefully searching for a channel through the land. At last he reached the narrow strait which is now called the Strait of Magellan in his honor. 

Through it he passed and out on the quiet waters of a great sea which he named the Pacific ocean because it was so calm.

MAGELLAN CLAIMING THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS 

Ferdinand Magellan commanded the first expedition to sail around the world, 1519-1522. He discovered the Ladrone and 

Philippine islands, which he claimed for Spain. Here we see 

Magellan's men planting a cross on one of the Philippine islands.

Magellan now had but three ships, for one had been sunk and another had turned back to Spain. On he pushed with his little fleet, sailing northwest across the Pacific. Weeks went by. The food gave out, and the water became foul. At last a group of islands was reached, the Ladrones, and was claimed in the name of Spain. Taking on new provisions, Magellan sailed on farther to the west, and discovered and claimed the 

Philippines. Here the brave leader and a number of his men were killed in a battle with some natives. 

Leaving the Philippines, the members of Magellan's expedition who had escaped the natives sailed to one of the islands of the East Indies. There they loaded one of their vessels with a cargo of spices, and then sailed for Spain, crossing the Indian ocean and passing around the south of Africa. Only thirty-one of Magellan's crew lived to see their homes again, but these men had completed the first voyage around the world. No longer could there be any doubt that the world was round. 

Magellan's expedition also gave the people of Europe their first idea of how large the earth really was. 

De Soto. One of the men who had gone with 

Pizarro to Peru was named Hernando de Soto. Some years later De Soto was made governor of Cuba and Florida. Believing that there were rich cities on the mainland, he laid plans for an expedition to find and conquer them.

In the year 1539, De Soto set out from Cuba with a large party of men and a number of horses. They sailed to the western coast of Florida, where they landed and

started inland. Slowly and painfully De Soto and his men traveled through the thick forests of what is now southeastern United States. Along the way they captured many Indians and put them in chains to be used as slaves. Other Indians, angered by the Spaniards' cruelty, made sudden attacks upon the party, and many a Spaniard was killed. Finally, in 1541, De Soto came to the Mississippi river and claimed it for Spain. 

Crossing the Mississippi by means of boats and rafts, the Spaniards continued their explorations west of the river. All along they had been hoping to find rich cities to conquer, but they had been disappointed. 

At last they gave up hope, and turned back towards the mighty river which they had discovered. On its banks 

De Soto died, and in its waters he was secretly buried under cover of night. The Spaniards were afraid that if the Indians learned of the leader's death they would make an attack and massacre the entire party. 

Without their leader, the Spaniards' only thought was to escape from the wilderness and return to civilization. Freeing their captive Indians and turning loose their horses, they set to work to build some boats. In these they traveled down the river and at last reached a Spanish settlement in Mexico. 

Coronado. At the same time that De Soto was making his explorations, another brave Spaniard, Francisco Coronado, was exploring the country farther to the west. Coronado was governor of a part of Mexico. From the Indians he had heard tales of seven rich cities to the north, and he was determined to find them.

The seven rich cities turned out to be seven little Indian pueblos, without wealth, much to Coronado's disappointment. Traveling farther, he and his party explored a large section of the western plains, and a group of his men discovered the Grand Canyon of the Colorado river. It was mainly due to his explorations that the Spaniards later laid claim to a great area now included in the southwestern part of the United States. 

Even today one may see early Spanish churches, missions, and other buildings in this part of our country.

The Portuguese in America. The Portuguese, too, laid claim to lands in the New World. Portuguese explorers discovered lands in eastern South America, and later the colony of Brazil was founded there. 

Several hundred years ago, a rich trade grew up between the countries of Europe and Asia. When the Turks captured the lands about the eastern Mediterranean, this trade was interrupted and a new route to the countries of Asia had to be found. 

An Italian sailor named Christopher Columbus, sailing under the flag of Spain, crossed the Atlantic ocean in 1492 and discovered the New World. In later years Columbus made three other voyages to America.

America was named after Americus Vespucius, an Italian who explored the eastern coast of South America and wrote an account of his journey.

Not long after Columbus discovered America, several Spanish colonies were founded in the West Indies. 

While searching for a magic fountain of youth, Ponce de Leon, the Spanish governor of Porto Rico, discovered Florida and claimed it for Spain. 

A Spanish explorer named Balboa crossed the isthmus of Panama in 1513 and discovered the Pacific ocean. He claimed for Spain the ocean and all the lands which bordered it. 

In 1519 a Spanish army commanded by Cortez landed in Mexico and overcame the Aztec Indians. 

Cortez was made governor of Mexico. Soon gold, silver, and jewels were being shipped to Spain. 

Peru was conquered by a Spanish soldier named Pizarro. The new colony supplied Spain with much valuable treasure.

Ferdinand Magellan, a Portuguese seaman, commanded the Spanish expedition which made the first voyage around the world. On the way he discovered the Strait of Magellan and also the Ladrone and Philippine islands, which he claimed for Spain. 

In 1539 a Spaniard named De Soto landed in Florida with some followers and started through what is now southeastern United States in search of rich cities to conquer. In 1541 De Soto discovered the Mississippi. 

Coronado, the governor of part of Mexico, explored the western plains of North America. Some of his men discovered the Grand Canyon of the Colorado river.

The Portuguese discovered and settled the land that is now Brazil.

EXERCISES 

1. Whom do you consider the most important explorer in this chapter? Tell the class your reasons. 

2. Imagine that you are one of these explorers. Give a talk on your experiences.

CHAPTER TWO THE ENGLISH, FRENCH, AND DUTCH EXPLORERS 

The Spaniards and Portuguese were not the only ones who sent explorers to the New World. Across the broad Atlantic sailed ships flying the flags of France, England, and Holland. Let us see where these daring French, English, and Dutch explorers went, and what they found that aroused their countries' interest in developing the New World. 

The Cabots. Columbus' discovery that land could be reached by sailing west aroused much interest in Europe. Among those most interested was an Italian seaman named John Cabot who lived in the English city of Bristol. In 1497 Cabot fitted out a ship in which to go exploring. The king of England encouraged Cabot to make a voyage to the west, and gave him authority to claim for England any lands which he might discover. 

Taking his son, Sebastian, with him, John Cabot sailed to the west, following a route far north of that over which Columbus had gone. After many weary weeks he at last sighted land, the cold wind-swept shore of Labrador. This country he claimed for England. 

John Cabot was the first white man to set foot on the mainland of the North American continent since Leif Ericsson's forgotten voyage nearly five hundred years before. 21

FRENCH EXPLORATIONS IN THE NEW WORLD 

While the Spaniards were exploring southern North America, the French were exploring the northeastern and central parts of the continent. Find the two great French colonies, Louisiana and 

New France, also called Canada. Trace the route of each explorer.

The following year the Cabots made another voyage to the west. Reaching Labrador, they turned south and sailed along the coast, probably as far as the present state of North Carolina. Then they turned eastward and sailed back to England.

Both the Cabots and the king of England were disappointed in the two voyages. Like Columbus, the 

Cabots had hoped they would reach Asia by sailing to the west, but they had not succeeded in doing this. 

Nevertheless the voyages had important results, for in later years England laid claim to almost the whole eastern coast of North America because of the Cabots' explorations.

Verrazano. For more than twenty-five years after the voyages of the Cabots no more explorers visited the northern part of North America. Then, in 1524, an

Italian seaman named Verrazano sailed in command of a French ship, searching for a westward passage. 

The first land which he sighted was probably somewhere on the coast of what is now North Carolina. 

First sailing a short distance to the south, Verrazano then turned his ship about and sailed northward along the shore, landing occasionally, and exploring several rivers and bays. At last he reached the coast of Labrador and turned east toward Europe, as his supplies were getting low. Many years later, France used the voyage of Verrazano as an excuse for claiming much land in North America.

Cartier. Ten years went by, and then, in 1534, another French explorer set sail for the New World. He

was Jacques Cartier. Like many another explorer, Cartier was searching for a passage through the lands of the New World by which he might reach Asia. 

JACQUES CARTIER 

This daring French sailor discovered the Gulf of St. Lawrence and the St. Lawrence river, and claimed the nearby lands for 

France. 

Cartier's supplies were already beginning to run low when he reached the New World, so he dared spend very little time in exploring. Into the Gulf of St. Lawrence he sailed, and reached a bay not far from the mouth of the St. Lawrence river. Going ashore he made friends with the Indians and set up a large cross bearing the coat-ofarms of the French king to show that the new land he had discovered belonged to France. Then, as winter was coming on, he sailed for home. In France, Cartier reported that the waters he had discovered were rich in cod and many other kinds of fish. As a result, in the years that followed, many French fishermen came to fish in the shallow waters of the Gulf of St. Lawrence. 

The next year after his first voyage to the New 

World, Cartier came once more to North America.

This time he sailed up the St. Lawrence river, hoping to find that it was the passage he sought through the New World. At several places he landed and made friends with the Indians.

Far up the St. Lawrence river, Cartier found an 

Indian village built on an island. Behind the village was a high hill, to which he gave the name Montreal, meaning Royal Mountain. That is the place where the great Canadian city of Montreal is now located. 

Not far from Montreal, Cartier reached a point where the water was so shallow that his boat could continue no farther. Here the river rushed swiftly among the rocks in a series of rapids. Bitterly disappointed, and believing that the rapids blocked his way to Asia, Cartier named them La Chine rapids. "La Chine" is 

French for "China."

Returning to the Indian village, Cartier and his men went ashore. The explorer was surprised at the richness of the furs which the Indians had taken from the animals they had trapped, and he decided to spend a winter in the New World gathering furs to take back to France with him.

Close to the spot where Quebec now stands, Cartier and his men made a settlement. The winter was cold, and most of the men were attacked by scurvy, a disease caused by a lack of fresh fruits and vegetables. 

Many died, and, had the Indians not shown the Frenchmen how to make a drink which helped to cure scurvy, perhaps none of the men would have lived. As it was, those who were alive in the spring sailed back to France

with the furs that they had gathered. As a result of 

Cartier's explorations, France laid claim to all the lands bordering the Gulf of St. Lawrence and the St. 

Lawrence river.

Five years went by, and then Cartier once more returned to North America. Where the city of Quebec now stands he tried to found a colony. The attempt was not successful. When winter came, the weather grew bitter cold, and the supplies of food ran short. 

Many of the colonists died, and in the spring the rest returned to France.

The Huguenots in Florida. For almost twenty-five years after the failure of Cartier's colony, the French took very little interest in the New World. Then, in 1564, a Frenchman named Jean Ribaut brought a group of Huguenots, or French Protestants, to America. Since Ribaut intended to found a lasting settlement, his colonists included men, women and children. 

Remembering Cartier's description of the cold winters along the St. Lawrence, Ribaut and his Huguenots sailed farther south and established the settlement of 

Fort Charles in northern Florida.

At this time there were no Spanish settlements in Florida, though Spain regarded the country as hers because of the discoveries of Ponce de Leon and De Soto. It would never do to have the French seize and colonize the beautiful land of Florida. So in 1565, only a year after the landing of the Huguenots, a group of Spaniards under a leader named Menendez started a Spanish settlement on the east Florida coast. This

Spanish settlement still exists as the city of St. Augustine. It was the first permanent, or lasting, European settlement within the borders of what is now the United 

States. 

Naturally, the French were alarmed to hear that the Spaniards had settled so close to them, and they decided to destroy the Spanish settlement before the Spaniards could attack them. South along the coast they sent a fleet of warships, but a storm arose, and the fleet was wrecked on the hard Florida beaches. 

In the meantime the Spaniards were sending an expedition against the French by land. Out of the forest rushed the Spanish soldiers, and soon every man left in the French Huguenot settlement had been killed, and all of the women and children had been taken as prisoners back to St. Augustine. So ended the French 

Huguenot settlement in Florida. 

Champlain. Forty years went by, and then another

French expedition sailed to America. The leader was a man named Samuel de Champlain. Like Ribaut, he wished to found a lasting settlement in the New World. 

Profiting by the experience of the Huguenot colonists, he avoided the Spanish lands of the south and landed on the western shore of Acadia, now called Nova Scotia. 

There he founded the settlement of Port Royal. 

Four years later, in 1608, Champlain once more came to America. This time he made his way up the 

St. Lawrence river to the point where Cartier had spent the winter long before. Here he established a settlement called Quebec.

Champlain has often been called "the father of New France," for he, more than any other man, was responsible for the development of New France, as the French lands along the St. Lawrence river and north of the 

Great Lakes were called.

Though the Quebec settlers suffered greatly during their first years in America, Champlain encouraged them and kept them from returning home. He also explored the lands about the St. Lawrence river and the Great Lakes, and made maps and records of the sections which he explored. Not many years after the founding of Quebec, other settlements were made, and little by little the French gained a firm foothold in the lands lying about the St. Lawrence river. Most of the people in this region today are descended from the early French settlers, and the French language is widely spoken there.

The Indians who lived in the region where Champlain had made his settlement were Algonquins. At this time they were engaged in a fierce war with the Iroquois Indians who lived farther to the south. Champlain wished to make friends with the Algonquins, and offered to help them in their war against the Iroquois. 

With two other Frenchmen, he led a war party south along Lake Champlain into the Iroquois country. 

On the shore of Lake Champlain the Iroquois attacked them. The Iroquois were brave, but they could not stand against the guns of the Frenchmen. Never before had they seen such terrible weapons. They fled in terror, leaving Champlain and his Indian allies victorious.

William Thompson, Saugerties, N. Y.

CHAMPLAIN'S ATTACK ON AN IROQUOIS VILLAGE 

With a party of Frenchmen and Algonquin Indians from New France, the French explorer Champlain boldly entered the Iroquois lands to the south and made an unsuccessful attack on an Iroquois village. Notice the high platform which the Frenchmen built so that they could fire into the village. This very old picture was drawn by Champlain himself. 

Several years later, Champlain once more fought against the Iroquois. At the head of a party of Frenchmen and Algonquins he entered the Iroquois lands and attacked a village, which was protected by a palisade, a wall of pointed logs. Champlain and his men built a high platform near the village, and from it they were able to fire over the wall into the village. Nevertheless,

the Iroquois fought bravely and would not surrender. 

At last Champlain was wounded by an Iroquois arrow, and was forced to give up the attack and return to 

Quebec. 

Though Champlain did not realize it, his battles with the Iroquois were a sad mistake. From that time on, the Iroquois hated the French. In later years, when France was struggling with England for control of disputed lands in North America, the hatred of the Iroquois proved to be very costly to the French. 

In 1629 France and England were at war in Europe. 

Quebec was still a small town, defended only by a wooden palisade and a few small cannons. When an 

English fleet sailed up the St. Lawrence river, Champlain knew that there was no hope of holding his settlement against the guns of the English vessels. He therefore surrendered, and was taken to England as a prisoner of war. Three years later, the settlement of Quebec was restored to the French by the terms of a treaty of peace. Champlain was released from prison, and he at once returned to his colony in the 

New World. 

The French Missionaries. Not long after the founding of Quebec, many French missionaries began to go out to teach the Catholic religion to the Indians. 

The most important of these missionaries were members of two religious societies, the Jesuits and the Franciscans. Bravely they made their way deep into the forests, traveling far from the French settlements to establish their mission posts. In spite of hard-

CHAMPLAIN SURRENDERS QUEBEC TO THE ENGLISH 

In 1629, a strong English fleet sailed up the St. Lawrence river, and Champlain was forced to surrender the French settlement of Quebec. Here he is handing his sword to the English commander. Quebec was later returned to the French.

ships and dangers, the missionaries worked to help the Indians and to teach them to be Christians. They even went into the lands of the unfriendly Iroquois and risked their lives to do missionary work among these Indians.

The French missionaries were helpful not only to the Indians but also to the French colonists. Their kindness to many tribes of Indians won the red men's friendship for the French. When unfriendly Indians were planning to attack the settlers, the missionaries often heard about it and sent word back to the French settlements. Then, too, the missionaries on their journeys into the wilderness discovered many new lakes and rivers and added greatly to the French knowledge of the lands of North America. Father 

Hennepin, one of the missionaries, did much exploring around the Great Lakes, and he is thought to have been the first white man to see Niagara falls.

Marquette and Joliet. Among the most famous of the French missionaries was Father Marquette. With a fur trader named Louis Joliet and a few companions and guides, Father Marquette was sent by the governor of New France to explore the unknown lands which lay west of the French settlements and to discover whether or not the Mississippi river flowed into the Pacific ocean, as the governor believed it might. 

Father Marquette also hoped to teach the western Indians to be Christians. 

Leaving Green Bay, on Lake Michigan, Marquette and Joliet slowly made their way toward the Missis-

sippi river, traveling part of the distance on foot and part by canoe. As they went along, Father Marquette preached the Catholic religion to the Indians. At last 

MARQUETTE AND JOLIET EXPLORING THE MISSISSIPPI 

With a few companions and some Indian guides, a French missionary named Father Marquette and a fur trader named Louis Joliet explored the Mississippi river as far south as the mouth of the Arkansas. the explorers came to the broad waters of the mighty Mississippi, and down the river they traveled in their canoes.

By the time they reached the mouth of the Arkansas river, Marquette and Joliet were sure that the Mississippi did not flow to the Pacific, so they turned about and started north again. Father Marquette was so

exhausted by his travels that he stopped at the French missionary settlement of Green Bay. Joliet went on and brought to the governor at Quebec a report of the land which he and Father Marquette had explored. 

La Salle. Perhaps the greatest of all French explorers was La Salle. He planned to make his way west along the Great Lakes and then south along the Mississippi river, exploring the country and building French forts as he went. In this way he believed he could gain much valuable new land for France. 

Leaving Quebec, La Salle and his party traveled by canoe up the St. Lawrence river and along the southern shore of Lake Ontario. When they reached the great falls of the Niagara river, the travelers had to carry their canoes overland through the wilds of what is now western New York state. Beside the Niagara river, a little distance above the falls, they built a fort and trading post, and then set to work to build a ship. This ship, called the Griffin, was the first sailing vessel on the Great Lakes.

Leaving the fort, La Salle and his companions sailed west in the Griffin to Green Bay, on Lake Michigan, where some men whom he had sent ahead were waiting. These men had a large store of valuable furs which La Salle planned to sell in order to pay for the cost of the expedition. The Griffin was loaded with furs and sent back to the fort near Niagara falls, where it was to take on provisions for La Salle's party. 

For some time La Salle and his companions waited at Green Bay for the return of the Griffin, but they

waited in vain. The Griffin had been wrecked along the shores of Lake Huron, and La Salle never heard from his ship, his cargo, or his crew again. At last, 

LA SALLE CLAIMS LOUISIANA 

La Salle, greatest of the French explorers, traveled down the Mississippi and reached its mouth in 1682. In the name of King 

Louis XIV of France, he took possession of the Mississippi and all of the lands it drained, calling this vast territory Louisiana. with a few men and some canoes, he went on toward the Mississippi. Near the Illinois river he built a fort which he named Fort Crevecoeur, meaning "heartbreak." Shortly afterward he returned to New France.

Before long, La Salle once more started out to explore the Mississippi. After weeks of travel, he and

his men reached the river's mouth, and there the explorer set up a column upon which were these words in French: "Louis the Great, King of France, rules here, April 9th, 1682." He then took possession of the Mississippi river and all of the lands drained by it, giving this vast area the name Louisiana in honor of King Louis XIV of France. 

La Salle now planned to build a fort and establish a colony at the mouth of the Mississippi. With this in mind he returned to Quebec and then sailed to France to get the equipment he needed. Two years later he returned with four vessels and a number of colonists. Sailing through the Gulf of Mexico, the 

Frenchmen searched in vain for the entrance to the Mississippi. In some way they missed the river's mouth and sailed beyond it to a place called Matagorda bay, where they went ashore. Sickness and hunger killed many of the French colonists, and at last La Salle and some of his companions were forced to start overland towards the French settlements in the north to get supplies. On the way quarrels occurred, and the 

French leader was murdered by his own men. The settlers at Matagorda bay fared no better, for not long afterward they were massacred by the Indians. 

England Again Becomes Interested in the New World. The voyages of the Cabots had been made during the reign of King Henry VII of England. The rulers who followed King Henry had little interest in the new lands to the west, and for nearly eighty years no more English explorers sailed across the Atlantic.

In 1558 Queen Elizabeth came to the English throne, She was a wise ruler and she saw clearly the advantages to England of having rich possessions in the New World. During her reign, English vessels once more sailed toward the American shores far to the west. 

Drake. Among the bravest English seamen of Queen 

Elizabeth's time was a young man named Francis Drake. He hated the Spaniards, and in 1572 he set out to see how much trouble he could make for them. 

Across the ocean sailed Drake, capturing a number of Spanish treasure ships and taking their cargoes. He also raided some Spanish towns in the West Indies and on the isthmus of Panama. Then he returned in triumph to England.

A few years later Drake set sail on another expedition. Once more he crossed the Atlantic ocean, and this time he sailed south along the eastern shore of 

South America. Through the Strait of Magellan he went and north along the coast, seizing Spanish ships which were carrying the wealth of Peru to Panama. 

He even traveled as far north as the present state of Oregon, claiming for England the lands along which he sailed. At last, realizing that a strong Spanish fleet must be waiting for him to return through the Strait of Magellan, he sailed due west across the broad Pacific ocean. Only Magellan had ever sailed across that sea before.

About three years after he had set out on his expedition, Drake returned to England. He had taken much wealth from the Spaniards, had claimed new

lands for England, and had made the second journey ever made around the world. As a reward for all these deeds Queen Elizabeth herself came on board his ship and made him a knight, with the title Sir Francis 

Drake. 

The Spanish Armada. In 1588, a few years after 

Drake's voyage around the world, this daring English seaman once more had a chance to serve his country. 

The Armada, a mighty Spanish fleet, set out from Spain to conquer England. As the Spanish vessels neared the English coast they were met by an English fleet commanded by Drake and Howard. In the battle that followed, the Spaniards were defeated. They sailed to the harbor of Calais, in France, where Spanish troops were waiting to help them. 

At Calais the English renewed their attack and the Spaniards were forced to put to sea, where the English again defeated them. In trying to sail north around 

Scotland and return to Spain, the Armada ran into a storm and many ships were wrecked. Less than half of the vessels managed to return to Spain. The defeat of the Armada meant the end of Spain's great naval power and the rise of England as ruler of the seas. 

Gilbert. Among Queen Elizabeth's subjects was a nobleman named Sir Humphrey Gilbert. He believed that England should have colonies in the New World, and he planned to establish some settlements on the island of Newfoundland. 

Gilbert's first expedition, in 1578, was a failure, for ocean storms and attacks by the Spaniards forced

SIR HUMPHREY GILBERT IN NEWFOUNDLAND 

In 1583, an English nobleman, Sir Humphrey Gilbert, tried to found a colony on the island of Newfoundland. Lack of supplies soon forced him to give up his colony and start for home. On the way, his ship was wrecked and he was drowned.

him to turn back to England without ever reaching the shores of the New World. 

Five years later Gilbert commanded a second expedition of colonists. This time he reached Newfoundland, claimed it for England, and started out to explore the coasts farther south. His supplies soon ran short, however, and he set sail for home. On the way, his ship was wrecked, and he was drowned.

Raleigh. With Sir Humphrey Gilbert on his first colonizing expedition went a relative of his, Sir Walter 

Raleigh. Raleigh was a favorite of Queen Elizabeth's, and after Gilbert's death he had little difficulty in obtaining permission to continue Gilbert's work of trying to found colonies in the New World.

In 1584 Sir Walter Raleigh sent an expedition to America to select a good site for his colony. The expedition sailed along the eastern coast of North America and at last found a good location for a settlement. 

This was on Roanoke island, off the coast of the present state of North Carolina. The expedition returned to 

England with glowing accounts of Roanoke island and the nearby lands, and Queen Elizabeth was so pleased that she named the country Virginia after her title, "the Virgin Queen."

The next year Sir Walter Raleigh sent out a large group of colonists. They landed on Roanoke island, built a few cabins, and started to search for gold. After a time, food became scarce, and the Indians grew unfriendly and dangerous. When a fleet commanded by 

Sir Francis Drake appeared off the coast, the colonists

Because of the discoveries made by their explorers, the Spaniards, French, English, and Dutch all claimed lands in North America. Find the lands claimed by each. Follow on the map the routes of Henry Hudson's voyages of 1609 and 1610.

England. 

Raleigh's colonists brought back to England a plant which had not been known in Europe before. This was the tobacco plant, which proved popular in England and soon was being widely used. 

Later, in 1587, Sir Walter Raleigh once more sent a group of colonists to the New World. These, too, landed on Roanoke island, and for a while it seemed that they would succeed in establishing a permanent settlement. Then food ran short, and Governor White, whom Raleigh had appointed head of the group, returned to England for more supplies. 

To Governor White's distress he found that England was at war. Every English ship was needed, and it was three years before he could again set sail for the New World. When at last he returned to Roanoke island he found that the settlers had disappeared. The only clue they had left was the word "Croatoan" carved on a tree. Croatoan was a nearby island, but the settlers were not there, and no one has ever learned what happened to them. 

Hudson. Henry Hudson was another Englishman who went exploring. He believed that by sailing north of Europe he might succeed in reaching Asia. His first two attempts to make such a voyage were in the service of an English company. Both of these attempts resulted in failure because his way was blocked by ice. 

The people of the little country of Holland were much interested in these voyages of Hudson's, and

they soon asked him to command one of their ships and try to find a western route. So, in 1609, Henry 

Hudson sailed across the Atlantic in command of a little Dutch vessel known as the Half Moon. Sailing along the eastern coast of North America, he came to the body of water now called New York bay, and entered it. To the north stretched the waters of a broad, deep river, which Hudson believed might be the channel for which he was searching. For about one hundred and fifty miles he journeyed up the river, which we now call the Hudson in his honor. Then the waters grew so shallow that he could go no farther, so he turned around and went back to Europe after claiming the river, the bay, and all the lands about them for 

Holland. 

The next year Henry Hudson again sailed to the New World, this time in command of an English ship. 

He now planned to search for a water passage north of North America. Forcing his way through icy channels, he came to the broad body of water now called Hudson bay, and claimed the surrounding lands for 

England. Along the shores he sailed, ever hoping to find a passage through which he could reach Asia. 

While he was still exploring, winter closed the bay with ice, and Hudson and his crew had to spend several months in their helpless ship. When spring came and the ice melted, the tireless explorer wanted to continue his search for a western passage, but the crew were determined to go home. They rebelled, and Hudson, his small son, and eight sick companions were

HENRY HUDSON IS CAST ADRIFT 

In 1610, Henry Hudson discovered and explored Hudson bay and claimed the surrounding lands for England. Because he refused to return home, the crew of his ship placed him, his small son, and eight sick companions in an open boat and cast them adrift to perish in the icy waters of Hudson bay.

set afloat in an open boat to perish in the icy wastes of Hudson bay while the crew returned to Europe. 

European Claims in America. Because of the discoveries and conquests their explorers had made, several European countries laid claim to land in America. In eastern South America the Portuguese claimed and settled the hot lands of Brazil. The 

Spaniards, for their part, laid claim to Peru, the West Indies, the Florida peninsula, and the entire southwestern part of the North American continent. The 

French claimed as theirs a broad belt of land running through the heart of North America from the mouth of the Mississippi river to the icy coasts of Labrador. 

This area included the main waterways leading to the interior of the continent-the Mississippi river, and the Great Lakes and the St. Lawrence river. The 

Dutch, because of Hudson's discovery, regarded as theirs the land around the Hudson river and New York bay. The English also claimed this land, as well as the eastern coast of North America from Florida to the Gulf of St. Lawrence. In addition, the English had a claim to the northern lands around Hudson bay, and to the western lands which Drake had sighted on his voyage up the Pacific coast. These western lands were also claimed by Spain. 

John and Sebastian Cabot, two Italian seamen who sailed under the flag of England, discovered and

claimed Labrador in 1497 and explored the eastern coast of North America in the following year.

In 1524 an Italian named Verrazano, in command. of a French ship, explored the eastern coast of North America, thus giving France a claim to this land. 

Jacques Cartier, a French explorer, discovered the 

Gulf of St. Lawrence in 1534 and the St. Lawrence river in 1535. He claimed for France the lands lying about these two bodies of water. 

A group of Huguenots led by Jean Ribaut founded the French settlement of Fort Charles in Florida in 1564.

In 1565 Menendez and some Spanish followers founded St. Augustine, in Florida. Menendez and his men attacked the French settlement of Fort Charles and destroyed it. 

Samuel de Champlain, a French explorer, founded the French settlements of Port Royal and Quebec. 

Champlain made the Iroquois Indians hate the French because he helped the Algonquins in a war against the 

Iroquois. 

Soon after the founding of Quebec, many French missionaries began to go out and teach the Catholic religion to the Indians. Some of these missionaries also made valuable discoveries and explorations. 

A French missionary named Father Marquette and a fur trader named Louis Joliet explored the Mississippi river as far south as the Arkansas. 

La Salle, a French explorer, explored the Great Lakes and traveled down the Mississippi to its mouth.

In 1682 he claimed for France the Mississippi river and all the lands it drained, giving this vast territory the name Louisiana.

Sailing from France, La Salle attempted to establish a colony at the mouth of the Mississippi, but could not find the river. The colonists landed at Matagorda bay, where many of them died. The colony failed and 

La Salle was murdered by some of his own men. 

When Queen Elizabeth came to the throne of England, English explorers once more began to cross the ocean to the New World.

Sir Francis Drake, a bold English seaman, led two expeditions to capture Spanish ships and rob Spanish towns. On the second of these expeditions he sailed around the world. A few years later Drake helped to command the English fleet that defeated the Spanish 

Armada. 

Sir Humphrey Gilbert twice made unsuccessful attempts to plant English colonies in Newfoundland.

Sir Walter Raleigh made two attempts to establish colonies on Roanoke island. Both colonies were failures. 

In 1609 Henry Hudson discovered the Hudson river and New York bay and claimed the lands about them for Holland. The next year he discovered Hudson bay and claimed the lands about it for England. 

Several European countries claimed lands in the 

New World. The Portuguese claimed and settled 

Brazil, in eastern South America. The Spaniards laid claim to Peru, the West Indies, the Florida peninsula, and the entire southwestern part of North America.

The French claimed as theirs a broad strip of land running through the heart of North America from the mouth of the Mississippi river to the coast of Labrador. 

The Dutch claimed the land around the Hudson river and New York bay. The English claimed the eastern coast of North America from Florida to the Gulf of St. Lawrence, the land around Hudson bay, and some land on the Pacific coast also claimed by Spain.
