Here we see the English colonies and settlements which were established along the southern part of the Atlantic coast. As you read about the different colonies, turn to this map and find the places mentioned in the text.

CHAPTER THREE VIRGINIA 

Courage to face unknown dangers! Patience to endure countless hardships! These were needed to plant a colony in a vast wilderness. The wilderness we are going to read about in this chapter lay in North America, and the colony was Virginia-England's first foothold in the New World.

The London and Plymouth Companies. Sir Walter 

Raleigh spent a great deal of money in attempting to establish colonies in America. When his colonies failed, he lost all of this money. After this happened, no other Englishman was willing to risk his fortune in trying to colonize the New World. 

There were a number of Englishmen, however, who felt that a colony in America might pay for itself if it were properly managed. Though no one of these men wanted to risk his entire fortune, several were willing to invest some money in a new colony. So in 

1606 they formed the London Company for the purpose of establishing settlements in America and building up a profitable trade with England. King James I gave them a charter which permitted them to make settlements along the Atlantic coast anywhere between the parallels of 34° and 41° north latitude. 

A few years later another colonizing company was formed. This was the Plymouth Company, whose 53

charter allowed it to establish settlements along the Atlantic coast between the parallels of 38° and 45° 

ATLANTIC OCEAN KEY 

45° 41° 38° 34° 

-LANDS WHERE THE PLYMOUTH COMPANY COULD ESTABLISH SETTLEMENTS =LANDS WHERE BOTH THE LONDON AND PLYMOUTH COMPANIES COULD ESTABLISH SETTLEMENTS =LANDS WHERE THE LONDON COMPANY COULD ESTABLISH SETTLEMENTS LANDS GRANTED TO THE LONDON AND PLYMOUTH COMPANIES The English government granted lands in North America to both the London and the 

Plymouth colonizing companies.

Notice which lands were granted to each company and which lands could be occupied by both. north latitude. Though the lands granted to the two companies overlapped, neither company was permitted to establish a settlement within a hundred miles of a settlement already established by the other company.

The London and the 

Plymouth companies' lands extended one hundred miles inland, and included all islands to the east within a hundred miles. By a later charter, both companies were permitted to make settlements as far west of the coast as they pleased. The Founding of 

Jamestown. Early in 

1607, three shiploads of colonists, sent by the London Company, came to the New World. All of these colonists were men. Most of them were "gentlemen" who were not used to hard work of any kind and knew nothing of life in the

VIRGINIA 

55 wilderness. There were only twelve real workmen in the entire group. The ablest man of all was Captain 

John Smith. 

It was the intention of the colonists to land on 

Roanoke island, but a storm blew them out of their course, and they sailed into Chesapeake bay. After a two-weeks search, they chose a place on the banks of a river and made their settlement. They called the river the James, in honor of King James I, and the settlement they named Jamestown. Jamestown lay within the lands which Queen Elizabeth had called Virginia. 

The Troubles of the Colonists. The colonists soon found life in the New World very different from what they had expected. Many of them had heard strange tales of the gold and jewels which were everywhere in America, and they expected to return to England very wealthy. Of course there were no gold or jewels to be found. Instead there were forest trees to be cut down, houses to be built, fields to be cleared, and crops to be planted. Some of the men set to work willingly, but most of them sat down and watched. 

Very little was accomplished on the first day ashore. 

And on this very first day, misfortune overtook the colonists. Out of the forest crept a band of Indians armed with bows and arrows. Before the colonists succeeded in driving them away, two of the Englishmen had been wounded.

Attacks by the Indians were only one of the many troubles which the colonists had to face. The land about Jamestown was low and marshy, and there were

many mosquitoes. Soon most of the colonists were ill with fever. Sometimes only a few of the men were well enough to stand guard at night and watch for the dreaded Indians. 

Captain John Smith. One colonist after another died, and it was not long before less than half of their number were left. Then Captain John Smith took charge of the colony. It was very plain to him that the laziness of the colonists was one of the chief causes of trouble. All of the crops which anybody raised went into a common supply to be divided equally among the colonists. Thus, a lazy man received just as much to eat as a man who worked hard from morning till night. 

Captain Smith declared that any man who would not work should not eat, and after a few had gone hungry for a while, everybody became willing to do his share. 

Smith also declared that any man who swore should have a can of cold water poured down his sleeve, and soon the language of the colonists became much better. 

One day Captain Smith and two companions went on an exploring trip. As they traveled through the woods, suddenly they were attacked by a band of Indians. The three men fought bravely, but both of 

Smith's companions were killed and Smith himself was taken prisoner and brought before Powhatan, the Indian chief.

Captain Smith well knew that if he could not find some way of making friends with the Indians he would soon be put to death. From his pocket he took a compass and showed it to Powhatan. The chief was much

VIRGINIA 

57 interested in the little needle which always pointed to the north no matter how the compass was turned. He tried to touch it but could not because of the glass 

POCAHONTAS SAVES CAPTAIN JOHN SMITH 

Captain John Smith was the first leader of the Jamestown colonists. Once, when he was captured by the Indians, Chief 

Powhatan ordered him killed. But at the last minute the chief's daughter, Pocahontas, saved his life. which covered it. It must be magic that made the compass work! For a while he spared Captain Smith's life.

At length, however, Powhatan tired of the compass and decided to have his prisoner killed. A stone was set upon the ground and Captain Smith was made to lay his head upon it. Over him stood a powerful Indian brave with his war club raised. Just as the blow was about to be struck, Pocahontas, the chief's daughter, rushed to Captain Smith's side and begged her father to spare the noble white man's life. Powhatan agreed, and Captain Smith was allowed to return to Jamestown. Before he left, Powhatan promised to help the 

Englishmen by sending them corn to use during the winter months.

With the help of the corn which the Indians gave them, the Jamestown settlers managed to live through the winter. Early in the spring another shipload of colonists arrived. Captain Smith had written to the 

London Company asking to have skilful workmen sent to the colony, but the men who came were no more used to working than the first group had been. They proved to be of very little help. 

When the second shipload of colonists reached Jamestown, Chief Powhatan began to fear that more and more men might come and that he and his Indians would be driven from their hunting grounds. So he declared that the Indians would supply the white men with no more corn, and that they must get along as best they could without help.

Captain Smith well knew that without the help of the Indians the colonists would soon starve. With forty of his bravest men he marched to Powhatan's

VIRGINIA 

59 village. His little force was greatly outnumbered, but he was not afraid. Straight to the chief's brother marched the brave English captain. Seizing the Indian by the hair, Smith thrust a pistol against his breast and cried out, "Corn or your life!" Powhatan was afraid that his brother would be killed, so he promised to supply more corn to the Englishmen. 

During the summers of 1608 and 1609 Captain 

Smith went on many exploring trips around Chesapeake bay. He kept very careful records of everything he saw and made clear maps of the whole region explored. These maps proved of great help to the later settlers in the Virginia colony. 

One night in the summer of 1609, while Captain 

Smith was on one of his exploring trips, he was injured by the explosion of a bag of gunpowder. The unfortunate leader was so terribly burned that he had to return to England to have his wounds treated. The loss of Captain John Smith was a sad blow to the 

Jamestown settlers. 

The Starving Time. With Captain Smith gone, affairs in the colony went very badly. Many of the colonists would not work and food became scarce. Once more the Indians refused to help the settlers. To make matters worse, seven shiploads of colonists arrived at this time from England. Their voyage had been long and their supplies were gone. There were now too many mouths to feed from the colony's storehouse. 

Very few of the colonists lived through the winter of 1609-"the starving time," as it was called. Some

LORD DELAWARE ARRIVES IN VIRGINIA 

Lord Delaware was the first governor sent from England to the Virginia colony. He reached Jamestown with supplies and new colonists just as the settlers were leaving to return to England. 

Encouraged by his arrival, the settlers decided to remain. Our present state of Delaware is named after this English nobleman. died of disease, some of the cold, and a number from the arrows of the Indians. In the spring, the sixty who remained alive decided to return to England. They abandoned Jamestown and crowded into two small ships. It seemed that the Virginia colony had failed. 

Lord Delaware. At the mouth of the James river the departing colonists met some sailing ships from

VIRGINIA 

61 England. On one of them was a new governor, Lord 

Delaware. With him the governor brought more colonists and plenty of supplies for all. The two little boats followed the others back up the James river, and the Jamestown settlement was saved. 

The new governor soon proved himself to be a fine leader. The work to be done was divided up, and every one was given his own duties to perform. Two forts were built. The spirits of the colonists rose high, and it looked as though their troubles were over. 

Governor Dale. Unfortunately, Lord Delaware was taken sick a few months after he reached Virginia, and he was forced to return to England. In his place the London Company sent out Sir Thomas Dale. Dale was harsh and headstrong, but he knew how to get things done. He had the laws of the colony written down so that all people might see just what they could and could not do. He did away with the plan of having a common store of food, and gave each man three acres of land to cultivate for himself. He planned many new buildings and set the colonists to work building them. Though the colony was prosperous while he was governor, the people grew tired of Dale's strict rule, and they were very glad when he was recalled to England.

Governor Argall. Sir Samuel Argall was next sent over from England to govern the colony. He was much more interested in building up a fortune for himself than in making the colony a success, and the colonists discovered that he was cheating them and the London

From "Jamestown," one of the Chronicles of America Photo plays. Copyright. By permission of Yale University Press. 

A SCENE IN EARLY JAMESTOWN 

Jamestown, founded in 1607, was the first really important English settlement in America. From this picture of early 

Jamestown, what can you tell about the life of the settlers? 

Company, too. Soon word of Argall's wretched rule reached the officers of the London Company in England. 

At once they went to the king and asked to have a new charter for their colony. They wanted the governor to have less power and the people to have more. 

The king was glad to grant the new charter. It was not long before Governor Argall was on his way home in disgrace, and a new governor, Sir George

VIRGINIA 

63 Yeardley, was in Virginia ready to work with the people in running the affairs of their colony. 

Tobacco. Sir Walter Raleigh's colonists, you remember, took some tobacco plants from the New World to England. From England the use of tobacco spread rapidly throughout Europe. A few tobacco plantations were started in European countries, but so greatly did the demand for tobacco increase that these plantations were unable to furnish enough leaves, and the price rose very high.

In the Virginia colony there was a young man named John Rolfe. He well knew how valuable tobacco leaves were in England, and he wondered if the raising of tobacco plants could not be made a paying business in the Virginia colony. At that time the only crop raised by the colonists was corn, and the only tobacco shipped abroad was that which the Indians traded to the colonists. In 1612 John Rolfe planted a field of tobacco and soon had a fine crop. 

It was not long before tobacco became the most important product of the Virginia colony. Most of the settlers gave up their corn crops and planted their fields with tobacco. As the tobacco trade with England increased, the colony began to grow wealthy. So valuable were the tobacco leaves that they came to be used in place of money, and men often received tobacco for their work instead of money.

As time went on, the tobacco trade grew more and more important. The demand for tobacco spread and soon shiploads were going to many countries of Europe.

FIRST MEETING OF THE HOUSE OF BURGESSES 

In 1619, Governor Yeardley called a meeting of representatives from all of the Virginia settlements. The House of Burgesses, as this body of men was called, was the first representative assembly ever to meet in America.

VIRGINIA 

65 

The Great Year, 1619. While Sir George Yeardley was governor, three very important things happened in the Virginia colony. These three events were the granting to the colonists of a voice in their government, the arrival of the first shipload of women colonists, and the introduction of slavery. Since all three took place in 1619, that year became known as "the great year" in the history of the colony. 

The House of Burgesses. When Governor Argall was recalled to England, you remember, the officers of the London Company asked the king if they might allow the people of Virginia to take part in the government of their colony. The king said this might be done, and Governor Yeardley was instructed to see that the people were given a voice in affairs. 

In July, 1619, Governor Yeardley called the first meeting of the Virginia legislature, or House of Burgesses. This was a group of colonists chosen by the people to make the laws. Two men were chosen from each of the settlements which were growing up in the colony, and it was provided that in the future there should be two additional men from each new settlement which was established. From that time on, the people of Virginia helped the governor to rule the colony, and through their representatives, or men chosen to speak for them, they made their own laws. The House of 

Burgesses was the first representative assembly in

America. 

The Colonists Obtain Wives. There had been no women in the first group of colonists who came to

Virginia, and very few came in the years which followed. For the first twelve years of its existence the 

Virginia colony consisted almost entirely of men. 

By the year 1619 it was felt that the colony had become well enough established for women to live there. 

In that year the London Company sent ninety young women to Jamestown. The lonesome men there were glad to see them, and soon each of the women had a husband whose first duty was to pay the company for the expense of his wife's journey across the ocean. 

After 1619 many married men came to Virginia, bringing their wives and children with them. The success of the Virginia colony was now certain. 

The First Slaves in Virginia. By 1619, the raising of tobacco had become so important in the Virginia colony that many of the settlers had planted this profitable crop on all the land which they could buy. 

It was becoming harder and harder to get people who would work in the fields. Not many of those who came from England would do work of that kind, and the Indians were quite unwilling to do anything to help the white men.

One day the problem was settled. A Dutch ship sailed into the harbor of Jamestown and the captain brought ashore a large number of Negro slaves. Twenty of these were bought by the colonists, and in later years, when it was seen what good workers the Negroes were, many hundreds more were bought. In time slavery spread throughout the English colonies which were later established in America.

VIRGINIA 

67 

From "Jamestown," one of the Chronicles of America Photo plays. Copyright. By permission of Yale University Press. 

A SCENE ON JOHN ROLFE'S PLANTATION 

John Rolfe, a young Virginia planter, was the first of the settlers to raise tobacco on a large scale. Here we see him at the right, near a shed which he built for drying tobacco leaves.

Servants from Europe. People of many classes who were not well off in England came to Virginia to start life anew. Some were prisoners who would rather work in the New World than remain in prison in the Old. As the English government wanted to build up the Virginia colony, it was glad to release these prisoners from jail and send them across the ocean. In Virginia they were sold to colonists, and continued to work for them without wages for as long a time as they would have remained in prison in England. 

Sometimes these prisoners had to work for many years before they became free.

Others who came from England to act as servants for the colonists were poor people who did not have enough money to pay for their passage across the ocean.

Their fares were paid by the colonists. Each servant then repaid the passage money by working without wages until the amount due him for his services equalled the fare paid by his employer. People who worked in this way were called indentured servants, or redemptioners.

Plantation Life. As the years went by, the tobacco fields in the colony spread farther and farther until at last much of eastern Virginia was covered with green tobacco plants. Though some wheat and corn were also raised to supply the colonists with food, tobacco was by far the most important product of Virginia. 

Many of the colonists grew very wealthy from the tobacco trade. On their broad estates, called plantations, these colonists built beautiful homes for themselves-

VIRGINIA 

69 stately houses with wide porches and tall pillars. 

Usually such a plantation house was located near a river, so that ships could sail right up to the owner's docks and warehouses and take the barrels of tobacco leaves aboard.

These great Virginia plantations were so far apart that sometimes neighbors did not see each other for months at a time. Each plantation was really a little village in itself. Besides the large house for the master and his family, there were rows of little houses for the slaves. There were also barns and stables, a carpenter's shop, a blacksmith's shop, and often shops where clothes, shoes, and other necessary articles were made.

The owner of a plantation led a pleasant but busy life. Each day he rode about his lands overseeing the work of the slaves and other servants. When a ship sailed up to his private dock, he had to bargain with the captain about the price to be paid for his tobacco. 

Hunting was a favorite sport, and often the master of the plantation and his sons would ride for hours, following a pack of hounds on the trail of a fox. In the evenings there were games of cards or chess, and sometimes dances, which were attended by people for miles around.

The great distances between the plantations made it impossible to establish schools which the children of the plantation owners could attend. The sons of a planter's family usually received their early education from tutors, men especially hired to teach them.

Later they were sent to England or Scotland to receive college training. The girls received no schooling. 

The regular attending of church on Sundays was also impossible, because most of the plantation houses were so many miles away from churches. This did not mean, however, that the planters were not good Christians. Every Sunday the master of the plantation would gather his family and his servants together and hold a service. He would read to them a chapter from the Bible and would deliver as fine a sermon as he knew how.

Town Life. The people in the towns of the Virginia colony lived very different lives from the plantation owners. Most of the townspeople were traders and storekeepers who were not so wealthy as the planters who lived on the great plantations. In the early years of the colony, the townspeople had no schools and the only education which their children received was what they gained at home.

The townspeople had churches, however, which they were required to attend every Sunday. An old Virginia law, passed in 1610, assigned these penalties for staying away from church: "The first time a fine; for the second, a whipping; and for the third, to suffer death."

Thirteen years later the death penalty and the whipping were done away with, but for a long time any townsman who stayed away from church on Sunday had to pay a heavy fine. 

Virginia Becomes a Royal Colony. In the year 

1622, the Indians, who had long remained peaceful, de-

VIRGINIA 

71 cided once more to attack the settlers. Chief Powhatan was now dead and the chief who had taken his place had little love for the white people. Sudden attacks were made, and many plantation houses and small set- 

AN INDIAN ATTACK ON JAMESTOWN, 1622 

In 1622, bands of Indians suddenly raided Jamestown and other settlements in the Virginia colony. In this picture we see a fleet of Indian war canoes making an attack on Jamestown. 

The people of Jamestown were able to drive the Indians away, but many colonists who lived in the outlying settlements and plantations were killed. tlements were captured and burned. A large band of 

Indians in canoes even dared to attack Jamestown, but were driven away. In all, three hundred and fifty 

Virginia colonists were killed, including a great many women and children, and the people of the colony were much afraid.

Word of the terrible Indian attacks in Virginia soon reached King James I of England. Feeling that the London Company had not protected the colonists as it should, the king decided to do away with the company and to make Virginia a royal colony. This meant that the king would govern the colony himself through a governor whom he would appoint. At the request of the colonists, he allowed them to keep their voice in the government by continuing to hold meetings of the House of Burgesses. 

The Cavaliers. When King James I died, he was followed on the throne by his son, Charles I. Charles had his own ideas about running the government of England, and he soon quarreled with Parliament, the body of men who helped him govern the country. The quarrel grew into a war, and King Charles was defeated and thrown into prison. Not long afterwards his head was cut off, and for a while the English Parliament ruled the country without a king. 

Seeing what had happened to their king, many Englishmen who had fought on Charles' side against Parliament felt that it might be well for them to leave England. These friends of the king were known as 

Cavaliers. A large number of them came to Virginia, where the feeling of the people had always been in favor of the king. Most of the Cavaliers were well educated and wealthy and helped to improve the colony. 

Governor Berkeley. In 1660, the rule of Parliament in England had grown so weak that Prince Charles, son of the king who had been beheaded, was

VIRGINIA 

73 able to claim his father's throne and become king of England. He was known as Charles II. 

King Charles II sent Sir William Berkeley to 

Virginia as governor. Berkeley was a stubborn and selfish man. He took little interest in the problems of the colonists and had little sympathy for them. 

Once he even made this surprising statement, "I thank

God that there are no free schools, nor printing, and I hope we shall not have these for a hundred years, for learning has brought disobedience."

Bacon's Rebellion. During the years when Berkeley was governor, trouble with the Indians became more and more frequent. Lonely settlers living far from the towns were wakened at night by the terrifying war cries of the red men, and soon they and their families were lying dead among the smoking ruins of their cabins. Travelers between towns and between plantations were seized by bands of Indian warriors and often were put to death. The governor did nothing to stop these terrible conditions. He was lazy and did not care to start a hard and tiring war. Also, he was growing rich trading for furs with the Indians, and he did not want to have to stop this profitable line of business. 

Not far from Jamestown lived a rich young planter named Nathaniel Bacon. As word reached him of the different things which the Indians had done to the settlers, he became more and more angry. When at last his own plantation house was attacked and two of his servants were killed, he could stand no more. 

Hastily he gathered an army of settlers and went to

Jamestown to ask the governor's permission to fight the Indians.

To Bacon's surprise, the governor would not grant him the permission he requested. Nevertheless, the young planter was determined to end the Indian attacks whether the governor agreed or not. Through the forest he led his men to a fort which the Indians had built. There was a bloody battle at the fort, and the Indians were completely defeated and a great many of them were killed. Then Bacon and his men marched back to Jamestown.

Governor Berkeley, meanwhile, was enraged at Bacon's attack on the Indians. At first he started to raise an army with which to capture the young man, but he soon found that most of the people of Jamestown were more in sympathy with Bacon than with him, so he gave up the idea. When Bacon returned, he and the governor talked the matter over and at last agreed that if the governor would now grant Bacon permission to fight the Indians, Bacon would admit that he had been wrong in fighting them before. After 

Bacon had publicly admitted that he had been wrong, Berkeley still refused to grant him the permission he desired. Instead, the governor secretly sent men to arrest Bacon and throw him into prison. 

Bacon was warned of his danger just in time to escape and return to his plantation. There he gathered an army and marched straight back to Jamestown. 

As the governor saw the large band of armed men, he trembled with rage and rushed out to face them.

VIRGINIA 

75 

Baring his breast, he cried, "Here I am! Shoot me! 'Fore God, a fair mark! A fair mark! Shoot!"

Though Bacon was as angry as the governor, he managed to control his feelings and to speak calmly. "May it please your Honor," he said, "we have not come to hurt a hair of your head or any man's. We have come for a commission to save our lives from the Indians, which you have so often promised, and now we will have it before we go." The governor had no choice, and was forced to give Bacon the commission he demanded.

Once more Bacon led his men against the Indians, and once more he completely defeated them. Many of the Indian warriors were killed, and the others were so badly frightened that they no longer troubled the colonists.

On his way back to Jamestown, Bacon heard that Berkeley had raised an army to capture "the young rebel" and throw him into prison. Plainly Bacon's one hope of safety lay in defeating the governor and driving him from the colony. 

Along the road to Jamestown stood many plantation houses which were owned by men who had sided with Governor Berkeley. Each of these was visited by Bacon's men, and the wife and daughters of the owner were brought along with the army. Just outside of Jamestown, Bacon's followers stopped and began to dig trenches. As the governor's army came out of the town, Bacon's men lined up, between themselves and Berkeley's forces, the women they had captured.

THE BURNING OF JAMESTOWN, 1676 

Nathaniel Bacon, a young Virginia planter, led an uprising against Governor Berkeley because the governor would not protect the colonists against the Indians. Bacon and his followers drove Berkeley from Virginia, and burned Jamestown.

VIRGINIA 

77 The governor's men were afraid to shoot for fear of hitting the women, so they had to allow Bacon and his men to dig their trenches in peace. After the trenches were dug, the women were allowed to return to their homes.

The next day a fierce battle was fought between the two armies, and a good many men were killed on both sides. In the end, Berkeley and his followers were completely defeated and forced to flee for their lives. They boarded ships in the harbor and sailed away.

Bacon and his men now did a very strange thing. 

They burned Jamestown and left nothing but a smoking ruin of the first real English settlement in America. 

Then they returned to their plantations, hoping that their troubles were over. 

Governor Berkeley's Revenge. Only a month after 

Jamestown was burned, Nathaniel Bacon died suddenly. The cause of his death is not surely known. 

Some people say he died of fever, and others believe that he was poisoned. A little later Governor Berkeley once more returned to Virginia. There was now no leader to oppose him, and he quickly gained control of the colony. He sent word to the men who had fought on Bacon's side, promising that he would not punish them if they would give themselves up. Many did, only to find that the governor was not a man of his word. Without even a trial, more than twenty of the "rebels," as they were called, were hanged. 

Soon word of what Berkeley was doing reached

King Charles II. "That old fool has hanged more men in that naked country than I have done for the murder of my father," he shouted. Berkeley was recalled to England in disgrace, and there he died not long afterward.

Difficulties With the English Government. After 

Berkeley's removal the most important troubles which the Virginia colony had were with the English government. The members of Parliament felt that the House of Burgesses had too much power, and several times they attempted to reduce the privileges of the Virginia legislature. All of the attempts were resisted by the colonists, who succeeded in keeping for their representatives most of the rights which they had enjoyed, including the right to levy taxes. In 1689, when 

King William and Queen Mary came to the English throne, Parliament ceased its attempts to reduce the power of the House of Burgesses. 

William and Mary College. In 1693, William and

Mary College, the second college in the colonies, was founded at Williamsburg. This famous college, now nearly two hundred and fifty years old, is still one of the important educational institutions in our country.

The Virginia colony was founded in 1607 by a group of settlers sent out by the London Company of England. The first settlement was Jamestown.

VIRGINIA 

79 After many misfortunes had befallen the settlers, Captain John Smith, a young soldier, took charge of the colony. He made everybody work, and conditions were much improved. He also got the Indians to supply the settlers with corn.

In 1609, when Smith left the colony, conditions grew worse. Many of the settlers died during the winter of 1609.

The first four governors sent from England to the Virginia colony were Lord Delaware, Dale, Argall, and 

Yeardley. 

Tobacco, first planted by the Virginia colonists in 1612, became the most important product of the colony. 

Much of it was shipped to Europe and some of the colonists grew very rich.

In 1619 the Virginia colonists first received a voice in their government, for in that year Governor Yeardley established the Virginia legislature, or House of Burgesses. In that same year, a shipload of women came to Virginia. The first slaves were also landed in 1619. 

Many prisoners and indentured servants, or redemptioners, came to Virginia to work for the colonists. 

As the tobacco trade grew in importance, many of the colonists established huge tobacco plantations. The plantation owners were the wealthy members of the colony. Most of the townspeople of Virginia were traders and storekeepers.

After attacks by the Indians in 1622, the king of England made Virginia a royal colony. He allowed the House of Burgesses to continue its meetings.

When King Charles I was driven from the throne of England by Parliament and was put to death, many of the king's friends, called Cavaliers, came to Virginia. They helped to improve the colony. 

Sir William Berkeley became governor of Virginia in 1660. He was a stubborn and selfish man and had little sympathy for the colonists. 

Because Governor Berkeley would not protect the colonists against Indian attacks, a young planter named Nathaniel Bacon led a rebellion against him. The governor was defeated and fled from the colony, but later returned and put many of Bacon's men to death. 

For this he was recalled to England in disgrace. 

After Berkeley's removal, Parliament tried to reduce the privileges of the House of Burgesses. The colonists resisted and succeeded in keeping most of their rights.

CHAPTER FOUR MASSACHUSETTS 

"My ancestors came over in the Mayflower!" 

Have you ever heard anyone say this? Many people in our country are very proud that their ancestors crossed the ocean in this famous little boat more than three hundred years ago. Who came to 

America in the Mayflower, and what did they do that made them so famous? 

King James I. In 1603, when Queen Elizabeth died, King James of Scotland became the ruler of England. Scotland and England now had the same king, and ever since that time a single king has ruled both countries. The new ruler was known as King 

James I. 

King James was not a good ruler. He believed that God had given him the right to rule as he pleased, and he declared that neither the people nor Parliament should have anything to say about the way in which he ran his kingdom. This strange belief which King 

James held was known as "the divine right of kings. 99 Before the time of King James, England had given up being a Catholic country and had started a new church known as the Church of England. King James belonged to the Church of England and it seemed to him that all other Englishmen should also belong. He laid heavy taxes on the people to support his church, 83

THE NEW ENGLAND COLONIES OCEAN ATLANTIC 

The first Pilgrim and Puritan colonists made their settlements on the bleak New England coast. Later, colonists from these settlements moved inland and made their homes there. Turn often to this map as you read about the New England colonies.

MASSACHUSETTS 

85 and he declared that no one should attend any religious service except that of the Church of England. When members of any other church tried to worship as they thought best they were severely punished. 

The Separatists and the Puritans. There were two groups of people in England who belonged to the Church of England but who did not like its service. 

One of these groups was known as the Separatists. 

They disliked the Church of England service so much that they wanted to separate from the church and form a new one of their own. The other group was known as the Puritans. They did not want to separate from the Church of England but they did want to change, or purify, the service. They thought that a simpler service would be better. King James had no sympathy for either the Separatists or the Puritans, and punished them just as he did those who were not members of his church.

After a time the Separatists felt that they could no longer attend the king's church. On Sundays many of them gathered in the home of William Brewster, one of their leaders, who lived in the little town of Scrooby. 

There they later built a church of their own. 

Though the Separatists were doing him no harm, King James would not leave them alone. His punishments grew worse and worse, and many Separatists were thrown into prison. At last they could stand this treatment no longer, and they decided to leave England and go to some other country where they would have more freedom.

The Separatists in Holland. The little country of 

Holland, across the North sea from England, was one of the few countries in Europe where people were free to worship as they wished. It seemed to William Brewster and the other Separatists that they would be much happier in Holland than in England, so they packed up their possessions and sailed quietly away to that country. There they settled in the town of Leyden, not far from the coast. 

For ten years the Separatists lived peacefully in

Holland, worshiping in the simple way they liked so well. The Dutch, as the people of Holland were called, were very kind to them. The Separatists, however, were not happy. They were proud of being Englishmen, and it made them unhappy to see their children growing up to speak the Dutch language instead of English. Then, too, there was war in Europe, and armies were marching and fighting in Holland. Once more the Separatists began to wish that they might move to another land where they would be as free to worship as they had been in Holland, and where they would also be able to speak their own language and to live in peace. 

Thomas Weston's Company. It was easier for the 

Separatists to think about leaving Holland than to do it. In the first place few of them had money enough to travel very far, and in the second place they did not know where they could go. The other countries of 

Europe did not welcome members of new religions. 

The Separatists could not go back to England, for King James would punish them as he had done before. At

MASSACHUSETTS 

87 that time the only English colony in America was Virginia, and the Separatists could not go there because the people of Virginia wanted nobody in their colony who was not a member of the Church of England. 

Plainly if the Separatists wished to enjoy peace and religious freedom they would have to start a new colony of their own. But how could they do it? 

At this point an English business man named 

Thomas Weston took an interest in the Separatists.

Weston went to Holland to talk to the Separatist leaders, and to see if some arrangement could not be made by which he and some of his friends could help the Separatists and at the same time make a profit for themselves. At last an agreement was reached. It was decided that Weston would organize a company which would provide the Separatists with ships and supplies so they might cross the ocean and establish a colony in America. In return, all of the colonists were to work hard for seven years. No colonist was to own anything himself, but all crops that were raised, all furs that were bought from the Indians, all gold that was mined, and anything else of value that the colonists produced were to be put into a single storehouse. Everyone was to draw what he needed from the storehouse instead of keeping for himself what he produced. At the end of seven years, half the property of the colony was to go to the members of Weston's company, and the other half was to be divided among the colonists. 

Though the terms which Mr. Weston had made were very hard, the Separatists were so anxious to find a place

where they could live in peace and worship as they liked that they accepted them. At once Weston set about to organize his company. He also secured from the London Company a grant of land near the Hudson river, where the Separatists were to settle. 

The Voyage to America. On July 22, 1620, a large group of Separatists boarded the little ship Speedwell and sailed for Plymouth, England, where they were to get ready for their long journey across the ocean. 

There they were joined by other Separatists. 

Besides the Speedwell, Weston's company provided another boat, the Mayflower. The two little vessels set out together, but soon the captain of the Speedwell discovered that his boat had sprung a leak, and both ships turned about and sailed back to the port of Plymouth in England. There it was decided that the 

Speedwell was not a safe boat for an ocean passage, and all of the Separatists who were still eager to seek a new home in the New World crowded themselves aboard the Mayflower and once more set sail. 

It was September 16, 1620, when the Mayflower put to sea with her one hundred passengers. For more than two months the little boat sailed steadily to the west. The voyage was rough, and more than once it looked as though the boat would go to the bottom with everybody on board. But at last land was sighted, and the Mayflower came to anchor in the quiet waters of what is now the harbor of Provincetown on the coast of Massachusetts.

The colonists had not intended to come quite so far

MASSACHUSETTS 

89 north as they now found themselves. The land that the 

London Company had granted them was farther south, near the Hudson river. When they tried to sail south along the coast, however, they were driven back by 

THE MAYFLOWER IN PROVINCETOWN HARBOR 

In November, 1620, the Mayflower came to anchor in what is now the harbor of Provincetown, Massachusetts. Soon the 

Pilgrims left this harbor and sailed on in search of a better place for a settlement. They finally selected Plymouth. storms, and they finally decided to remain where they were, even though they had not been given permission to occupy land so far to the north. 

People who make long journeys for religious reasons are called pilgrims. The Separatists made a long journey for religious reasons when they crossed the ocean so that they might worship God as they saw fit in the

New World. So they called themselves the Pilgrims, and that is the name by which they have been known ever since. 

SIGNING THE MAYFLOWER COMPACT 

Before going ashore at Plymouth, the Pilgrims signed the Mayflower Compact, in which they declared themselves to be loyal subjects of the king of England, and promised to make good laws for the governing of their colony and to work together for the common good. 

After their ship dropped anchor, the Pilgrims went ashore. How good the firm ground must have seemed to them after so many weeks at sea! The women took with them bundles of clothes, and soon they were busy doing their washing along the shore. The men took

MASSACHUSETTS 

91 their guns in case they should meet savage natives or wild animals. 

The Mayflower Compact. Before dark all of the

Pilgrims returned to the Mayflower. There they held a meeting to determine what was to be done. Plainly they needed a leader and some sort of government. 

They drew up a paper in which they declared themselves to be loyal subjects of the king of England, and promised to make good laws for the governing of their colony and to work together for the common good. 

All of the men signed this Mayflower Compact, as it was called. They then elected John Carver, the oldest man among them, to be their governor. 

The Plymouth Settlement. After staying a short time where they first landed, the Pilgrims decided to search for a better place for a settlement. Sailing to the west they found a good harbor, and there they dropped anchor. Some years before, Captain John 

Smith had explored this coast and had made maps of it. He had given the name New England to the entire region and had named the little harbor where the Mayflower now lay at anchor, Plymouth. The Pilgrims decided to keep this name for their settlement. 

On December 21, 1620, the Pilgrims went ashore at Plymouth. At the water's edge was a large rock, and it is said that they used this rock in stepping to the shore. To this day people who visit Plymouth, Massachusetts, may still see Plymouth rock, which has been carefully preserved in memory of the landing of the Pilgrims.

Almost at once the men set to work to build a log cabin for the settlers to live in. Later they built a second cabin and a storehouse for their supplies. The winter was bitter cold and the Pilgrims suffered ter- 

THE FIRST DAY AT PLYMOUTH 

The Pilgrims landed at Plymouth in cold December weather. 

Until the men were able to build shelters, everyone suffered terribly. Many of the Pilgrims died during the first winter in the New World. 

ribly. Many of them became sick and died, and, by the time spring came, there were forty-six graves in their little burial ground. The Pilgrims were afraid that the Indians would see how many graves there were and would realize that there were not enough people left to

MASSACHUSETTS 

93 defend the little Plymouth settlement, so in order to hide the graves they planted corn over them. 

In April the Mayflower returned to England. The captain offered to take back with him any of the Pilgrims who no longer wished to remain in America. All had suffered and many of them must have longed to see their friends in England again, but when the Mayflower put out to sea not a single one of the Pilgrims sailed on it. Sadly they watched their little ship until it was out of sight, then turned back to go on with their work in the colony. 

Relations with the Indians. One day the Plymouth settlers heard a voice calling, "Welcome, Englishmen."

They rushed out of their houses to see who was calling to them, and saw an Indian who had walked out of the woods and was approaching the settlement. The Indian's name was Samoset. A few years before, he had learned some English from fishermen he had met along the coast. Samoset spent the night in Plymouth and on the next day he returned to the forests. 

Not long afterwards Samoset came once more to Plymouth. With him he brought another Indian named 

Squanto. At one time Squanto had been captured by some European traders and taken across the ocean to Europe. There he had learned to speak English. After many adventures he had been bought by a kind-hearted Englishman who had shipped him back to his home in the New World. Squanto was, therefore, friendly to the English and was glad to help the Plymouth settlers in any way he could.

From Samoset and Squanto the Pilgrims learned that an Indian chief named Massasoit was coming to visit them with a large group of followers. All of the 

Pilgrim men put their guns on their shoulders and marched out with beating drums to welcome the chief. 

The Indian leader was much pleased at this reception. 

He accompanied the Englishmen to their settlement and there talked with them for a long time with the help of Squanto and Samoset. Before he left he made a solemn treaty of peace with the Pilgrim leaders, and as a result the Pilgrims and Massasoit's people lived peacefully together for more than fifty years.

In the weeks which followed Massasoit's visit, Squanto proved to be very helpful to the English. He showed them how to set traps so as to catch the animals in the forests. He guided them to the best fishing places. He taught them how to make maple sugar and how to cook pumpkins. Best of all, he gave them some seeds and showed them how and when to plant corn, peas, and barley in order to raise the best crops. To the Pilgrims' surprise, they noticed that Squanto always put a dead fish in the ground with his corn to fertilize the soil and make the corn grow better. They did the same, and it was not long before they had some fine crops growing in their little fields. 

There was another tribe of Indians living not far away who were less friendly than the followers of Massasoit. Their chief was named Canonicus. He was much troubled to see the white men settling in the lands of the Indians, and he determined to make war upon

MASSACHUSETTS 

95 them and either kill them or drive them away. Canonicus' method of declaring war was to send to the Pilgrims a bundle of arrows wrapped in the skin of a rattlesnake.

As soon as the grim message from Canonicus was received, William Bradford, who was then governor, and Miles Standish, a soldier who had come to America with the Pilgrims, held a meeting to plan what should be done. Quickly they decided to resist the Indians and to show them that the Englishmen were not afraid. 

The arrows were removed from the snake skin and it was filled with powder and shot, and sent back to Canonicus. The Indian chief did not expect to find the 

Englishmen so brave and so ready to fight. He was startled. At once he ordered the snake skin to be taken away where he could not see it, and very quickly he changed his mind and decided not to have a war after all.

The Courtship of Miles Standish. Among the settlers who died during the first winter in America was the wife of Captain Miles Standish. Captain Standish wished to marry again, and chose beautiful Priscilla

Mullens for his wife. Being a bashful man, he sent handsome young John Alden to propose for him. John 

Alden was himself in love with Priscilla, but he did as he was asked. "Speak for yourself, John," said

Priscilla. Soon afterwards she became Mrs. John 

Alden, instead of Mrs. Miles Standish.

The Growth of the Plymouth Settlement. In the spring of 1621 many new log cabins were added to the

two in which the Pilgrims had spent their first winter. 

Most of these cabins had only a single room, heated by a large fireplace which was also used for cooking. 

There was little furniture. Blocks of wood covered 

THE HOME OF GOVERNOR BRADFORD IN PLYMOUTH 

At the right is Governor Bradford's house, surrounded by a palisade. At the left may be seen pathways leading to some of the other houses. The building on the hill served as a church and a fort. with fur served as chairs. Though a few of the Pilgrims had rough beds, most of them slept in blankets on the floor. There was no glass in the windows, but oiled paper was used to keep out the wind and rain. 

During the summer of 1621 the Pilgrims put up a new building on a mound in the midst of their settle-

MASSACHUSETTS 

97 ment. It had one story and was made of logs. It served as both a church and a fort. Inside were many rough benches for the congregation. Six cannons were mounted on the roof to protect the building against the Indians. 

PILGRIMS MARCHING TO CHURCH 

Each Sunday morning the Pilgrims gathered together and marched to church. The men carried their guns in case of an attack by the Indians. 

The Pilgrim Sunday. Every Sunday morning a drum was sounded and the Pilgrims gathered in front of the home of Captain Miles Standish. Each of the men brought his gun, as the Pilgrims did not want to be found unprepared in case an Indian attack took place. 

Led by the governor, the pastor, and Captain Standish, the Pilgrims marched to the church, where they sat for hours and listened to the long church service. At

noon there was recess of an hour for rest and the noonday meal, and then the service began again and lasted all afternoon. In the aisle stood one of the churchmen, known as the tithing man. In his hand he held a long wand with a rabbit's foot on one end and a feather on the other. If a lady fell asleep, the tithing man tickled her with the feather until she sat up and listened to the service once more. If a man fell asleep, he was rapped sharply on the head with the rabbit's foot. 

Sunday was a hard day for the churchgoers, but think what it must have been for William Brewster, the colony's first pastor! For nine years, Sunday after 

Sunday, he preached to his people nearly all day long. 

The First Thanksgiving. During their first sum mer in the New World, the Pilgrims were successful in raising good crops of corn, peas, and barley. Much of their success was due to the help and advice of Squanto, the kind Indian who had shown them the best way to raise plants in the New England soil. 

When the crops were harvested in the fall, the Pilgrims had plenty of food for the winter. On the last 

Thursday in November the settlers held a great feast and devoted the whole day to thanking God for his help. 

Many of the neighboring Indians came to the feast, and with them they brought deer, wild turkeys, and other game. This was the first Thanksgiving Day.

The Pilgrims Buy the Plymouth Settlement. The 

Pilgrims were earnest and thrifty people, and they were willing to work hard for the success of their colony. It distressed them to think that, by the terms of

MASSACHUSETTS 

99 their original agreement with Thomas Weston's company, they would soon have to divide their belongings with Weston and his partners. The only fair way to avoid doing this was to pay back the money which Weston's company had loaned them. So, by working hard, trading with the Indians, and borrowing money in England, the Pilgrims succeeded in buying from Weston and his partners their claim to the colony. 

Private Ownership of Land. Now that the colony belonged entirely to them, the Pilgrims made an important change in their manner of living. For a long time they had realized the disadvantages of having a common storehouse. People who did little work could receive just as much from the common storehouse as those who labored long and hard. There was, therefore, little reason for working harder than one's neighbors. 

Accordingly, Governor Bradford divided up the land of the colony, giving each family a section to work for itself. This proved very satisfactory. 

The Importance of the Colony. The Plymouth colony grew slowly. By the end of the tenth year the population was only three hundred. Nevertheless, the colony was very important. It gave England a foothold on the New England coast. Even more important, the Pilgrims granted freedom of worship and established a government in which all men had a voice. They thus planted the first seeds of our present system of government by and for the people. 

The Persecution of the Puritans. While the Pilgrims were battling to found their colony in the New

World, the Puritans who had remained in England were not having an easy time. King James died in 1625, but his son, Charles I, treated the Puritans just as 

THE PURITANS' FIRST SUNDAY IN AMERICA 

In 1628, a group of Puritans, led by John Endicott, came to America and founded the Salem settlement. They were very religious, and, on their first Sunday in the New World, held a long religious service, although they had no church in which to meet. badly as his father had. Charles tried his best to keep the Puritans from speaking or writing about their beliefs, and when many of them insisted on doing so without the king's permission he had them thrown into prison. The members of Parliament felt that this was

MASSACHUSETTS 

101 not fair, but their opinion did not trouble King Charles. 

He dismissed Parliament and forbade its members to meet again. Then he continued with his persecution of the Puritans.

The Massachusetts Bay Company. As the rule of 

King Charles grew more and more harsh, many of the Puritans began to consider following the Pilgrims to America where they could worship as they pleased. In 

1628, a colonizing company, known as the Massachusetts Bay Company, was formed. Its purpose was the sending of Puritan colonists to the New World. The six directors of the company were all Puritan leaders. 

The Salem Settlement. In the same year when the 

Massachusetts Bay Company was formed, John Endicott, a director of the company, crossed the ocean with a group of about fifty Puritans. They landed on the 

New England coast a little north of Plymouth and there founded a settlement known as Salem, a name which means peace. Like the Pilgrims, they bravely endured the troubles and hardships of life in the New World, and in time their settlement grew and prospered. 

The Massachusetts Bay Colony. At the same time there was in England another group of Puritans who were ready to leave their country and travel to America. Their leader was John Winthrop, also a director of the Massachusetts Bay Company.

Unlike the Pilgrims and the Salem settlers, Winthrop and his followers decided to try to get the king's permission to go to America, and they asked for a royal charter granting them the right to establish a colony

there. The king was only too glad to be rid of some of the troublesome Puritans, so he granted them the permission and the charter they desired. By the terms of the charter, the Massachusetts Bay colony, as it was called, was to extend north three miles beyond the Merrimac river, south three miles beyond the Charles, and west to the Pacific. It included Salem, which John 

Endicott had already established. 

Early in 1630 a group of seven hundred Puritan men, women, and children, led by John Winthrop, left England in eleven ships and sailed westward across the Atlantic. After a long voyage they reached Salem, where Endicott and his Puritan settlers made them welcome and prepared a feast in their honor. The newcomers, however, did not stay long in Salem but sailed south along the coast until they came to a broad bay into which flowed a river that John Smith had named the Charles. Beside the Charles river the Puritans founded a settlement which they called Charlestown. 

John Winthrop was elected governor of the Massa chusetts Bay colony. 

The Founding of Boston. Across the Charles river from Charlestown was a narrow neck of land which could easily be fortified against the Indians. Here, there lived a retired clergyman named William Blackstone who had come from England several years before in order to live in peace and quiet, far from the political troubles of his native land. William Blackstone liked the Puritans, and when he saw that many of them were ill because their Charlestown settlement was built on

MASSACHUSETTS 

103 land which was low and marshy, he invited them to move across the river and build a new settlement on the higher land near his home. Many of the Puritans, including Governor Winthrop, were very glad to do this. A number of cabins and a meeting house were soon erected and the new settlement was named Boston after the city of Boston in England.

The First Winter. During their first winter in 

America, the Puritan settlers suffered almost as much as the Pilgrims had. The cold, piercing winter winds swept through the walls of the cabins, and the colonists had trouble to keep warm. To make matters worse, the supplies began to give out. Just as it appeared certain that all of the colonists would die of starvation, an English ship sailed up over the horizon and dropped anchor at Boston. To their great joy, the Puritans found that the ship had brought them all the provisions they needed to last them through the remaining winter months.

The Growth of the Massachusetts Bay Colony. 

Unlike the Plymouth settlement, the Massachusetts

Bay colony grew rapidly. This was partly because of the large number of Puritans in England who desired religious freedom, and partly because of the increasing harshness of the king's rule. In the first year after the founding of Boston, more than a thousand people came to the colony, and by 1640 the number had increased to more than twenty thousand. Roxbury, Dorchester,

Cambridge, and many other towns grew up around Boston.

Puritan Towns. All of the towns in the Massachusetts Bay colony were much alike. In the center of each was a large open field called the common. Facing it was the church and meeting house, usually the same building. Around the common stood the small wooden houses of the townspeople, each on its own patch of ground. 

In the early days, some of these settlements were surrounded by palisades, or walls of pointed logs, as a protection against the Indians, but many of them were not. 

Government. The people of Boston and the other towns of the Massachusetts Bay colony had a share in the government of their towns. From time to time town meetings were held, at which all of the Puritan men were present. At these meetings officers were elected and all questions which concerned the governing of the town were discussed and settled. 

There were some questions, however, which concerned the citizens of more than one town, and these could not be settled by town meetings. If the towns were to act together for the common good, there must be some form of government for the entire colony. To meet this need the Puritans organized a legislature, or General Court, much like the House of Burgesses in Virginia. Representatives from each of the towns met in the General Court and decided how the affairs of the colony as a whole should be run. 

Occupations of the Puritans. Because the soil of 

Massachusetts was not well suited for agriculture, many of the Puritans turned to different lines of work. 

While some raised corn, barley, and such necessary

MASSACHUSETTS 

105 food plants, others made articles of iron or wove cloth. 

The sea off the coast was full of cod, haddock, and other kinds of fish, and an important fishing industry soon 000 

AN EARLY VIEW OF HARVARD COLLEGE 

Harvard, founded at Cambridge, Massachusetts, in 1636, was the first college in any of the English colonies in America. 

Some of the buildings shown in this picture are still standing. sprang up. A rich trade was also started with the West 

Indies, and lumber, which was plentiful in New England, was shipped out in exchange for sugar and molasses. Fishing and trading required ships, so shipbuilding became one of the leading industries of the colony. Indian trappers brought valuable furs to the towns and sold them to the settlers, who then shipped

them to England to be sold at a profit. There was plenty of work for everybody, and a number of the Puritan colonists became wealthy. 

Education. Many of the Puritan settlers were well educated, and they wanted their children to have the same advantage. In 1635, only five years after the founding of Boston, the Free Latin School was established to provide free education for Boston children. 

The next year Harvard College, the first college in the colonies, was founded in the nearby town of Cambridge. 

As early as 1647 the General Court passed a law requiring that there should be an elementary school, paid for by taxes, in each town of more than fifty families. Each town of more than a hundred families was required to have a school for older children as well. Thus the Massachusetts Bay colony early became a leader in educational progress. 

Religion. Although the Puritans had come to 

America seeking religious freedom, yet they did not permit religious freedom in the colony which they founded. The Puritan Church was established by law, and no other form of worship was allowed. Quakers, Catholics, and members of the Church of England could not vote in the town meetings or the General Court, and many of them were as cruelly punished for their religion as the Puritans had been in England. 

When some members of the Quaker religion insisted on holding meetings in Massachusetts, they were driven from the colony, and later some of them were put to death for daring to return.

MASSACHUSETTS 

107 One terrible event happened in the colony. Like other people of that time, the Puritans believed in witches. Witches were thought to be people who helped 

THE TRIAL OF A WITCH 

Many of the early colonists believed in witches-people who helped the devil in his work. In Salem, Massachusetts, a number of innocent people were put to death because the colonists thought they were witches. This poor woman is being accused of using magic spells to harm her neighbors. the devil in his work and who were given strange powers as a reward. In the little Puritan settlement of 

Salem some people began to suspect that certain of their neighbors were witches. A wave of fear swept

through the settlement. The suspected ones were tried, and many of them were put to death. Later the Salem settlers came to realize that they had put innocent people to death, and they were filled with sorrow. 

The Later Years of the Massachusetts Bay Colony. 

For more than fifty years after it was founded, the Massachusetts Bay colony continued to grow and prosper. More towns were established and, as we shall see, many settlers left the colony and traveled into the wilderness to establish new Puritan colonies of their own. For this reason Massachusetts is sometimes known as "the mother of colonies.' 99 Soon after 1680, the king of England became displeased with what he heard about the Massachusetts Bay colonists. Their colony was becoming too strong and too independent to please him. In order to stop this, he took away their charter in 1684. In 1691, the 

Plymouth and Massachusetts Bay colonies were combined into a single colony known as Massachusetts, and a new charter was granted which stated that members of all religions might vote. The new charter made Massachusetts a royal colony. From this time on the king, and not the colonists, had the right to select governors for the colony. 

King James I of England taxed his people heavily to support the Church of England, and declared that no one in his country should belong to any other church.

MASSACHUSETTS 

109 The Separatists were members of the Church of England who wanted to separate from the Church and form a new one of their own. The Puritans wished to change, or purify, the church service. King James disliked the 

Separatists and Puritans and punished them. 

Because the king mistreated them, a group of Separatists left England and settled in Holland. Ten years later, in 1620, they crossed the ocean in the Mayflower and founded the settlement of Plymouth, in what is now Massachusetts. They called themselves the Pilgrims. 

During their first winter in the New World many of the Pilgrims died, but in the spring not one of the settlers was willing to go back to England. 

The Pilgrims made a treaty with a friendly Indian chief, named Massasoit. Squanto, an Indian who had learned to speak English, helped the settlers in many ways. An unfriendly Indian tribe was prevented from declaring war by the boldness of the colonists. 

At the end of their first summer in the New World, the Pilgrims held a great feast to celebrate the successful harvesting of their crops and to thank God for his help. This was the first Thanksgiving Day.

The Pilgrims' voyage to the New World was made possible by an English company which furnished ships and supplies. In time the Pilgrims repaid the company, and the colony became their own. 

The Plymouth colony grew slowly, but it was very important for it gave England a foothold in New England and planted the first seeds of our present government by and for the people.

King Charles I of England treated the Puritans as badly as King James I had done. So in 1628 John 

Endicott, a Puritan leader, came to America with a group of about fifty Puritan followers. They founded the settlement of Salem, north of Plymouth. This was the first town in the Massachusetts Bay colony. 

In 1630, a large group of Puritan men, women, and children left England and sailed to America. They founded Charlestown, and elected John Winthrop as their governor. Later, Boston and other settlements were founded and the Massachusetts Bay colony grew rapidly.

The towns of the Massachusetts Bay colony were governed by town meetings. The colony itself was governed by the General Court, or legislature. 

The people of the Massachusetts Bay colony had many occupations, including agriculture, the weaving of cloth, fishing, trading, shipbuilding, and the manufacture of iron articles. In time some of the settlers became wealthy.

Schools and colleges were established in the Massachusetts Bay colony at an early date, and the colony soon became a leader in education. 

The Puritans did not allow religious freedom in their colony. The Puritan Church was established by law and no other form of worship was permitted. 

Members of other churches were not allowed to vote. 

In 1691 the Plymouth and Massachusetts Bay colonies were combined into the single royal colony of Massachusetts.

MASSACHUSETTS
