CHAPTER FIVE RHODE ISLAND, NEW HAMPSHIRE, AND CONNECTICUT 

If the Pilgrims and Puritans had been satisfied with conditions in England, they might never have founded the Plymouth and Massachusetts Bay colonies. And, if all of the people of these two colonies had found contentment in their new homes in America, the colonies of Rhode Island, New

Hampshire, and Connecticut might never have been established.

Puritan Life in Massachusetts. The Massachusetts 

Bay settlers were stern and God-fearing people. They believed in hard work and thought it sinful to spend much time in pleasure. Dancing, card playing, and other amusements were forbidden. On Sunday, people could not even leave their houses except to go to church. 

Everyone had to attend the Puritan Church. Only 

Puritans were allowed to vote. By nine o'clock every night all fires had to be covered or put out. 

Puritan Punishments. Very severe punishments were provided for all who broke the Puritans' laws. 

Men who drank too much, or beat their wives, or failed to go to church on Sunday were placed in the stocks or in the pillory. The stocks were a wooden frame with two holes in it. The person to be punished had to sit on a block of wood and thrust his feet through the two 113

holes, where they were securely fastened. Some stocks also had holes in which the prisoner's hands were fastened. 

FOR BEATING 

YGOODWYE 

A PURITAN COLONIST IN THE STOCKS 

"For Beating Ye Goodwife," this colonist must sit all day with his hands and feet fastened in the stocks. His wife seems to have forgiven him, for she is giving him a drink of water.

The pillory was another wooden frame, set on the top of a post about four feet high. The pillory had three holes, one for the prisoner's head and two for his hands. A person standing in the pillory was even more uncomfortable than one sitting in the stocks. Often the prisoners had to stay all day in the stocks or the pillory, and sometimes prisoners were kept there for several days. 

Women who gossiped or scolded or did other things of which the Puritans did not approve were punished by means of the ducking stool. This was a long beam of wood with a chair fastened to one end. The beam was attached near its middle to a post standing beside a pond or river. The woman to be punished was

RHODE ISLAND 

115 securely fastened in the chair, which was then swung out over the water and plunged beneath the surface. 

Sometimes the victim was held under water for a whole 

Courtesy of the Makers of "Listerine"

A PURITAN GIRL IN THE DUCKING STOOL 

Puritan women who gossiped or scolded were tied to a chair and ducked under water. It did not take many duckings to bring about a complete cure. minute. The number of times a woman was ducked depended upon what she had done. 

Murderers, robbers, pirates, and other people who committed serious crimes were put to death by hanging. 

There were a great many crimes which the Massachusetts Bay colonists thought deserved the death penalty. 

Rhode Island. Although most of the Puritans were contented with the life and customs in the Massachu-

setts Bay colony, there were some who were not. As time went on, numbers of these people left their homes and traveled beyond the borders of the colony to establish other colonies of their own, where they might live in the manner they thought best. Rhode Island was the first colony to be established in this way. 

Roger Williams. In 1635 there lived in the town of Salem a young minister named Roger Williams. 

Roger Williams had a very keen mind and a kind heart. The more he thought about the things the Puritans were doing and the manner in which they were living, the less he approved of them. In many of his sermons he spoke of conditions in the colony which he thought should be corrected. For example, he said that the Puritans had no right to make everyone attend their church or to limit the privilege of voting to men who were Puritans. He believed that the church and government should be run separately. He also felt that no one should be required to pay taxes for the support of the church. Most important of all, Roger Williams believed and freely stated that the Puritans had no right to occupy the land as they were doing, whether or not they had a charter from the English king. The land, he said, belonged to the Indians, and the Puritans should not live on it unless they paid the Indians for it. 

When the Puritan leaders in Boston heard what 

Roger Williams was saying from his pulpit, they decided that a man with such ideas was dangerous and should not be allowed to remain in the colony. So they sent word to him that he must travel to Boston and

RHODE ISLAND 

117 From "The Puritans," one of the Chronicles of America Photo plays. Copyright. By permission Yale University Press. 

ROGER WILLIAMS AT HIS HOME IN SALEM 

Because of his beliefs, this young Salem minister was driven from the Massachusetts Bay and Plymouth colonies. In 1636, he founded the settlement of Providence on Narragansett bay. 

Providence was the first settlement in the Rhode Island colony. from there be sent to England by the first boat. Williams refused to obey. This angered the Puritan leaders and they sent soldiers to arrest him and bring him to Boston by force.

When the soldiers arrived at Salem the young minister was nowhere to be found. Though the weather was cold and the ground was covered with snow, he had set out on foot through the forest, determined that no

one should send him to England against his will. After wandering for two weeks and suffering greatly from cold and hunger, he came at last to the village of Massasoit, the friendly Indian chief, and there he remained as Massasoit's guest until spring. 

When spring came, word reached the Puritan leaders that Roger Williams was staying with the Indians. Massasoit's village was not within the boundaries of the Massachusetts Bay colony but it was on land claimed by the Plymouth colony, and the Pilgrim leaders had no more use for the young minister than the Puritans had. They, therefore, sent word that he must leave at once, and Roger Williams wearily trudged forth to seek a new home. As he went along, he was joined by five companions, and together they made their way south until they came to the shores of Narragansett bay. There they built some log cabins and established a little settlement which Williams called Providence, "for God's providence to me in my distress.' 99 It was not long before Williams and his five companions were joined by other dissatisfied Puritans. A few at a time these families moved to Narragansett bay. 

Some of them settled at Providence and others established little settlements of their own nearby. Portsmouth, Newport, and Warwick were among the towns which grew up near Narragansett bay. 

Anne Hutchinson. Less than a year after Roger

Williams was driven from the Massachusetts Bay colony, another person began to express disagreement with the Puritan beliefs. This was a woman, Mrs. Anne

RHODE ISLAND 

119 Hutchinson. The Puritan leaders were troubled, and they told her that she must leave the colony. With her family and a few friends she left Boston and traveled south, finally making her home in one of the little settlements near Narragansett bay. 

The Rhode Island Colony. At first the little settlements around Narragansett bay were independent of one another. It was soon seen, however, that common laws and a common government were necessary. Roger 

Williams was chosen leader and the name Rhode Island was given to the entire group of settlements. 

Life in Rhode Island was very different from life in the Massachusetts Bay colony. Settlers were forbidden to occupy land unless they first paid the Indians for it. Every man was allowed to vote, no matter what his religion was. No one had to go to church unless he wished to. The harsh laws and severe punishments of the other Puritan settlements had no place in the life of the Rhode Island settlers.

In 1644, Roger Williams happened to be in England, where he was working on a book about the Indian languages. Word reached him that the government of the 

Massachusetts Bay colony had sent a petition to the king asking that the Rhode Island settlements be made a part of the Massachusetts Bay colony. That would never do! Williams himself went to the king and asked for a charter permitting the people of Rhode Island to govern themselves as an independent colony. The king gave Roger Williams the charter he requested and not long afterwards he returned to Providence with it.

The charter which Roger Williams secured gave the Rhode Island settlers the right to the land they had occupied and granted them the privilege of ruling themselves. It also provided for religious freedom within "the Rhode Island and Providence Plantations," which was the name by which the Rhode Island colony was known in England.

Rhode Island now grew rapidly. The religious freedom offered there attracted members of many different religions. One might think that this would lead to trouble and misunderstanding, but under the wise leadership of Roger Williams the Rhode Island settlers continued to live in peace and the colony prospered. Before 

New Hampshire. By the year 1622 the old Plymouth

Company no longer existed. Its place was taken by another company known as the Council of New England. The Council granted to two Englishmen, John 

Mason and Sir Ferdinando Gorges, a stretch of land along the New England coast north of Massachusetts, between the Merrimac and Kennebec rivers. long the two proprietors divided this land, Mason taking the southern part between the Merrimac and Piscataqua rivers, and Gorges taking the northern part between the Piscataqua and the Kennebec. Gorges' sec tion, the District of Maine, was sold to Massachusetts, but Mason's land, which he called New Hampshire, later became a separate colony, as we shall see. 

The first settlement on Mason's land was made in 1623, only three years after the founding of the Plymouth colony. Ten miles up the Piscataqua river, two

NEW HAMPSHIRE 

121 

EARLY SETTLERS IN NEW HAMPSHIRE 

In 1622 a section of land along the New England coast north of Massachusetts was granted to an Englishman named John Mason. In the years which followed, a number of little settlements grew up on Mason's land. King James II combined these settlements in 1679 into the English colony of New Hampshire. brothers named Hilton built houses for their families, and soon the little fishing village of Dover grew up about them. Not long afterwards other small fishing villages were established along the coast. 

John Wheelwright. In the settlement of Dorchester lived a Puritan minister named John Wheelwright. 

He was the brother of Mrs. Anne Hutchinson, and he

agreed with her in many of her beliefs. Like her, he disagreed with the religious teachings of most of the other Puritan ministers, and he lost no opportunity to argue with these ministers about questions of religion. The people of Dorchester and the other settlements became greatly interested in these arguments and, little by little, two groups were formed, one agreeing with John Wheelwright, and the other siding with his opponents.

At last it was decided to hold a meeting of ministers to discuss the religious questions which were in dispute. 

For a long time the ministers argued, but at last they decided on a series of beliefs most of which did not agree with what Wheelwright thought.

The leaders of the Massachusetts Bay colony were now sure that Wheelwright was in the wrong, and they were also very sure that many of his beliefs were dangerous. So they commanded him to leave the colony. 

With a number of his friends, the exiled minister traveled to New Hampshire and founded a town which he named Exeter. This was in 1638. Not long afterwards other groups of people left the Massachusetts Bay colony and established other settlements nearby. 

New Hampshire Loses and Regains Her Independ ence. As long as John Mason lived, the settlements in 

New Hampshire were, in a sense, his property, for they were built on land which the Council of New England had given him. In 1641, however, a few years after his death, the Massachusetts Bay colony laid claim to this whole section. Because they needed a strong

CONNECTICUT 

123 government, the New Hampshire settlers agreed to become a part of the Massachusetts Bay colony. 

For thirty-eight years, Exeter, Dover, and the neighboring settlements were thus a part of the Massachusetts Bay colony, though all this time the descendants of John Mason were protesting and trying to regain the land for themselves. At last, in 1679, King James 

II of England became tired of the dispute and made what had been Mason's land the royal colony of New Hampshire, with a legislature of its own and a governor whom he himself appointed. 

Connecticut. In the settlement of Cambridge, not far from Boston, lived a Puritan minister named 

Thomas Hooker. Thomas Hooker believed that every man should have the right to vote, whether or not he belonged to the Puritan Church. He and many of his followers were not content to remain in the Massachusetts Bay colony, where only the Puritans had any say in the government, and where the Puritan laws were so strictly enforced.

Nearly one hundred miles west of Cambridge is the Connecticut river, which flows across New England from north to south and empties into the waters of Long Island sound. The soil of the Connecticut valley is fertile, much better fitted for the growing of crops than the thin, stony soil around Massachusetts bay. 

Word reached Hooker that the rich valley of the Connecticut river was a splendid place to live, and in 1636 he and many of his friends started out to found a new home there.

THOMAS HOOKER AND HIS FOLLOWERS ON THEIR WAY TO THE CONNECTICUT VALLEY 

In 1636 Thomas Hooker, of Cambridge, left the Massachusetts

Bay colony and traveled westward with a number of followers. 

In the fertile Connecticut valley they founded Hartford.

CONNECTICUT 

125 The journey to the Connecticut valley must have been a hard one for many of Hooker's followers. All traveled on foot, for their few horses were needed to carry supplies and provisions. Mrs. Hooker was ill and had to be carried the entire distance on a stretcher. 

Day after day for more than two weeks, men, women, and children plodded wearily forward through the trackless New England forests. 

When they reached the Connecticut valley, Hooker and his followers found that a few other settlers were already there. A small group of Dutchmen had built a fort and a trading post, called Good Hope, on the Connecticut river. Not far away was the settlement of 

Wethersfield, founded by a group of Puritans from the Massachusetts Bay colony. Farther south, at the mouth of the river, was Saybrook, which had been founded a few months before by a group of Puritan settlers from 

England. North of Good Hope, a group of Pilgrims from Plymouth had built a little town named Windsor. 

For a while, Hooker and his followers stayed in the Windsor settlement, but before long they traveled south along the river and established the town of Hartford not far from the Dutch fort and trading post. 

The Connecticut Colony. Very soon after Thomas

Hooker and his followers traveled to the west and founded Hartford, several other groups of Puritans also came to the Connecticut valley. Some came by land, pushing boldly through the forests, and others came by sea. Many little towns sprang up in the rich valley, and for a few years each of these settlements

developed peacefully by itself without bothering very much about its neighbors. 

In 1639, however, it was decided to organize the little towns in the Connecticut valley into a single colony. Representatives from each town met in Hartford to frame a common government. After much discussion, a series of laws was drawn up known as the Fundamental Orders of Connecticut. These laws stated that throughout the colony the government and the church should be separate. This meant that not only Puritans but also members of all faiths might vote. It also meant that no taxes were to be raised for the support of the church. The government was to be carried on by a governor and a legislature elected by the people. One can easily see that under the Fundamental Orders the people of the Connecticut colony had many more rights and privileges than their neighbors in the Massachu setts Bay colony. The Fundamental Orders of Con necticut was the first written constitution in North 

America. 

The New Haven Colony. In 1638, two years after the founding of Hartford, a group of Puritans landed on the shore of Long Island sound a little to the west of the Connecticut river. Some of them came from the 

Massachusetts Bay colony, but most had come directly from England. Their two leaders, the Reverend John 

Davenport and a merchant named Theophilus Eaton, were very strict members of the Puritan Church. They declared that their people should be guided by laws taken from the Bible. Only Puritans should be

CONNECTICUT 

127 allowed to vote. Only certain kinds of clothes might be worn and certain games played. In some respects this group of Puritans was even more strictly governed than the people of the Massachusetts Bay colony. 

The spot which Davenport and Eaton chose for their settlement was given the name of New Haven. Here cabins and a church were built, and also a large sailing ship in which to carry furs and other products of the colony to England to exchange for supplies. It was a happy day for the New Haven settlers when their ship was at last finished and ready to sail for London, but their joy soon turned to grief, for on its first voyage the vessel was lost at sea.

Nevertheless, the New Haven settlement grew and prospered. Several other towns were built, among them 

Milford, Guilford, and Stamford. All of these were presently included as parts of the New Haven colony. 

The Connecticut Charter. Neither the New Haven colonists nor the Connecticut colonists had any real right to the land which they occupied, for the English king had not granted either group a charter. In 

1662, however, King Charles II issued a charter in which he combined the two colonies into one, with the name Connecticut. By the terms of this very liberal charter, the people were permitted to elect their own governor and legislature and lay their own taxes. The boundaries of the colony were extended as far west as the Pacific ocean.

Many years later another English king, James II, decided to take away the charter which Charles II had

THE KING'S OFFICER DEMANDS THE CONNECTICUT CHARTER 

King James II of England sent an officer and some soldiers to Hartford to take away the charter which King Charles II had given to the Connecticut colonists. In the midst of the discussion some of the colonists blew out the candles, seized the charter, and hid it in a hollow oak tree. granted, and to rule the Connecticut colony in a different way. The colonists did not want to give up the charter, but in spite of their protests an English officer and some soldiers were sent to Hartford to get it. 

There appeared to be no way in which the Connecti-

CONNECTICUT 

129 cut colonists could save their charter. Before giving it to the officer, however, several important men of the colony argued in favor of keeping the charter. Darkness fell. Candles were brought, and the speaking continued. At last the charter was laid in front of the 

English officer. Suddenly the candles were blown out, and in the darkness and confusion the charter disappeared. No one would admit having taken it, and it could not be found anywhere. It is said that one of the colonists had seized it and hidden it in a hollow oak tree, ever after known as the Charter Oak. When 

James II was no longer king, the colonists brought out the charter and used it again in governing the colony. 

The Massachusetts Bay settlers were stern, Godfearing people. They punished severely all who broke the laws.

Some Puritans who were not contented with the life and customs of the Massachusetts Bay colony established colonies of their own where they might live as they pleased. 

Roger Williams, a Puritan minister from Salem, was driven from the Massachusetts Bay colony because of the beliefs he held. He founded the settlement of 

Providence near Narragansett bay. Not long after wards other people from the Massachusetts Bay colony made settlements nearby, and all of these settlements combined to form the colony of Rhode Island.

J Among the important settlers in Rhode Island was Mrs. Anne Hutchinson, who was driven from the Massachusetts Bay colony because she disagreed with some of the Puritan beliefs. 

In 1644, Roger Williams obtained a charter from the king of England for the Rhode Island colony. 

Religious freedom was permitted in Rhode Island and the laws there were not strict. 

The Council of New England granted two Englishmen, John Mason and Sir Ferdinando Gorges, a stretch of land north of Massachusetts. Gorges' section, the 

District of Maine, was later sold to Massachusetts, but Mason's land developed into the colony of New Hampshire.

Dover, the first settlement in New Hampshire, was made in 1623. Soon other settlements grew up nearby. 

Exeter was founded in 1638 by the Reverend John Wheelwright, who was driven from the Massachusetts Bay colony because of his religious beliefs. 

For some years after John Mason's death, settlements on his land were governed as part of the Massachusetts Bay colony. Later, King James II made 

Mason's property the separate royal colony of New Hampshire. 

In 1636, Thomas Hooker, a Puritan minister from

Cambridge, founded the town of Hartford on the Connecticut river. There were already a few other settlements in this region, and more were founded not long afterwards. In 1639 these settlements were united into the Connecticut colony.

CONNECTICUT 

131 The New Haven colony was founded by strict Puritans from England and from the Massachusetts Bay colony. In 1662 it was combined with the Connecticut colony to form a single colony, called Connecticut.

CHAPTER SIX INDIAN WARS IN NEW ENGLAND 

How would you feel if bands of strange people moved into your neighborhood, claiming the best lands for themselves and completely spoiling your way of making a living? Of course you would be very angry, just as the Indians were when the white settlers moved into their lands. Let us see how the 

Indians of New England tried to solve this problem. 

The Pequot War. Along the coast of Long Island sound, east of the Connecticut river, lived the Pequot Indian tribe. Their chief was very much distressed when he saw that the English were pushing through the forests and making settlements in the Indian hunting grounds. Wild game was already becoming scarce, and the chief clearly saw that if the white men continued to take more land the Indians would soon have to move away and seek new homes. This, he felt, must be prevented at all costs. These lands had been the home of his people for many years, and he was determined to keep them.

Encouraged by their chief, the Pequot Indians began making attacks on the white men. Several traders were captured and killed, and people grew afraid to travel near the Pequot lands.

The white settlers, for their part, felt that the Indians should be punished, and a body of soldiers was 135

sent out by the leaders of the Massachusetts Bay colony to capture the Indians who had taken part in the killings. The murderers could not be found, but the soldiers attacked some Indian villages, killing a few Indians and wounding many more. They then returned to their colony feeling that justice had been done. 

The hatred the Pequot chief felt for the white men was greatly increased by this treatment of his people, and he decided to make war upon the settlers in earnest. 

Bands of warriors were sent into the Connecticut valley with the intention of driving the white men away from this rich section. Settlers were waylaid along the forest trails and killed. Houses were attacked at night, and the men, women, and children who lived in them were put to death. In a few weeks thirty people had been killed by the Indians, and no one knew when or where the next attack would come. 

The Attack on the Pequot Fort. Though the settlements in the Connecticut valley had only a quarter as many fighting men as the Pequots, the settlers determined to attack their enemies instead of waiting until reinforcements from the other colonies should arrive. 

An army of ninety men gathered together and set off down the Connecticut river in a fleet of small sailboats, under the command of Captain John Mason.

It was Mason's intention to attack the chief fort of the Pequots, which was near the shore of Long Island sound. Captain Mason wished to take the Indians by surprise, so he and his men sailed right by the fort as though they did not know it was there, and went

INDIAN WARS IN NEW ENGLAND 137 

THE MASSACRE OF THE PEQUOT INDIANS 

Because the Pequot Indians were attacking settlements in New England and killing the settlers, an army of colonists and friendly Indians was sent out to defeat them. Under cover of night a surprise attack was made on the main fort of the Pequots, near the shore of Long Island sound. The fort was burned, and almost all of the Indians in it were killed. ashore forty miles east of it. There they were joined by twenty Massachusetts colonists commanded by Captain John Underhill, and by two hundred friendly Narragansett Indians, led by Chief Canonicus.

The Pequots had asked the Narragansetts to join them in their war against the white men, but, on the advice of his friend Roger Williams, Canonicus decided to help the English settlers instead.

Westward along the shore went the little army. At first the Narragansett Indians marched in front, boasting of their bravery and of the manner in which they would defeat the Pequots. Later, as they approached the fort, they dropped back and let the white men lead the way.

It was two o'clock in the morning when the English reached the Pequot fort. No Indian sentries were standing guard, and the two entrances to the high palisade were unprotected. Several of the English were already inside before a dog barked and gave the alarm. 

Sleepy Indians rushed from their tents, to be met by the bullets of the English and the arrows of the Narragansetts. Torches were applied to the tents and soon the scene was lit by the glare of the burning Pequot village. Six hundred Pequots, men, women, and children, died that night, and only seven escaped. The

English, for their part, had two men killed and twenty wounded.

The New England Confederation. The Pequot War had shown the New England colonists that the Indians, once aroused, could be very dangerous enemies. There were other enemies, too, who were to be feared. In the valley of the Hudson river the Dutch had established settlements, and to the north were Quebec, Montreal, and other posts belonging to the French. At any

INDIAN WARS IN NEW ENGLAND 139 

time armies of Dutchmen or Frenchmen might invade the English lands. It was plain that no one settlement and no one colony acting by itself could meet these dangers nearly so well as a group of colonies acting together.

In 1643, representatives of the Massachusetts Bay, the Plymouth, the New Haven, and the Connecticut colonies met in the town of Hartford. After discussing the various dangers which threatened them, they made a definite written agreement to work together. By the terms of this agreement, prisoners or slaves who fled from one colony and took refuge in another were to be returned. In case of war each colony was to furnish a certain number of men, according to its population. 

Rhode Island and New Hampshire were not invited to join because the members of the other colonies did not like the customs and beliefs of the people who lived in these two colonies.

The New England Confederation, as the union was called, was important not only because of the protection which it gave to the colonists, but also because it marked the first attempt of any of the American colonies to work together for the common good. 

King Philip's War. After the Pequot War was over, the New England settlers had no more trouble with the Indians for forty years. Then a chief known as 

King Philip, who was Massasoit's son, began to go about among the Indian tribes, urging them to make war upon the white men.

The first Indian attacks were made on outlying set-

tlements in Rhode Island, but the Indians did not limit their warfare to any one region. From Rhode Island to New Hampshire, and from the ocean to the Connecti-

Lonely travelers were seized and cruelly put to death. 

Houses were attacked and the families in them killed. 

Many of the towns were taken and their people massacred. For a while it looked as though King Philip might succeed in driving the white men from New England.

INDIAN WARS IN NEW ENGLAND 

141 At last, however, King Philip's luck deserted him. 

His wife and his little boy were captured by the English and sold into slavery in the West Indies. His heart was almost broken by this, yet he continued to fight. 

One after another his Indian allies left him. Finally, the English attacked his last fort, and after a fierce battle captured it. King Philip himself was shot by one of his own Indians when he tried to escape. 

King Philip's War did not end for more than a year after the leader's death, but the remaining Indians never had a chance to win. When peace came, a great many of them had been killed, and the rest had fled to Canada or to other distant places. Never after that time were the Indians of New England powerful or dangerous.

After the Pequot Indians attacked white settlers in the Connecticut valley, a small army of colonists and friendly Indians captured the Pequot fort and killed most of the Pequots.

As a result of the Pequot War, the Massachusetts 

Bay, Plymouth, New Haven, and Connecticut colonies united in the New England Confederation and agreed to help each other in case of trouble. 

Forty years after the Pequot War, an Indian leader named King Philip made war on the white settlers. 

After much fighting, King Philip was killed and the Indian power in New England was broken.

CHAPTER SEVEN MARYLAND 

Land in America was cheap in colonial times. 

If you were a friend of the English king, he might give you all the land you wanted for the asking. 

Lord Baltimore asked King Charles I of England for a stretch of land on which to found a colony, and the king gave him more than six million acres. 

Let us find out what Lord Baltimore did with this tremendous gift of land.

During the reign of King Charles I of England, there lived in England a Catholic nobleman named George Calvert, whose title was Lord Baltimore. At that time the Catholics in England were being made to suffer just as the Separatists and Puritans were. They were forbidden to hold religious services of their own, and were required to attend those of the Church of England and to pay taxes for the support of this church. Any Catholics who refused to obey were severely punished by the king's officers. It troubled 

Lord Baltimore to see the Catholics being so badly treated, and he longed to do something to help them. 

It happened that Lord Baltimore was a personal friend of King Charles I, so he went to the king and asked his permission to establish a colony in Newfoundland where members of all religions would be free to worship as they pleased. The king granted his 143

request, and soon Lord Baltimore and a large number of settlers, both Catholic and Protestant, set sail across the Atlantic. Though they reached Newfoundland 

CECIL CALVERT 

Cecil Calvert, the second Lord 

Baltimore, was the founder of the Maryland colony. In 1633 he sent his brother, Leonard

Calvert, and a group of colonists to settle in Maryland. without difficulty, the climate there proved to be so severe that they were forced to give up their colony and return to 

England. 

The Maryland Char ter. Once more Lord Baltimore went to the king, and this time he asked permission to establish a colony farther to the south. As before, the king agreed, and promised him a large stretch of land north of Virginia. The colony was to be named 

Maryland in honor of the 

English queen, Henrietta 

Maria. Before the king's charter was drawn up, Lord Baltimore died. 

The king, however, gave the promised charter to Cecil Calvert, who inherited his father's title and became the new Lord Baltimore. 

The king's charter had many generous provisions. 

Cecil Calvert was to be the owner of the granted lands

MARYLAND 

145 and was given authority to rule his colony with almost as much power as a king. He was permitted to make the laws, to coin money, to lay taxes, and, in short, to run the colony very much as he pleased. 

Now, Cecil Calvert knew that if his colony in America were to prosper, some one with a good deal of influence should remain in England to look out for its interests. Though he would have liked to go to America, he felt that he would play a more valuable part in the colony's development if he stayed close to the English court. Consequently, he entrusted his younger brother,

Leonard Calvert, with the founding and management of the colony, and appointed him governor. 

The Journey to Maryland. In November, 1633,

Leonard Calvert and about two hundred colonists boarded two ships, the Ark and the Dove, and set sail for America. After about three months at sea, they reached the Virginia colony. Catholics were not welcome in Virginia, and Calvert and his colonists soon had to leave. They then sailed north up Chesapeake bay and the Potomac river until they reached the mouth of a small river. As this seemed to be an ideal spot to found a settlement, the party went ashore. 

The Founding of St. Mary's. Leonard Calvert and his followers were extremely fortunate in their choice of a place to settle. The land was high, so there were no swamps to breed disease. There were fertile fields which the Indians had already cleared of trees and cultivated. The Indians were friendly and were willing to sell the land.

Almost as soon as the Maryland settlers landed they set to work to establish their settlement, which they called St. Mary's. A storehouse, a fort, and a mill were built, and not long afterward a church, or chapel, was erected for the use of both Catholics and Protestants. With the help of the Indians, crops were planted in the fertile fields. When fall came, such a rich harvest was gathered that the settlers had all the corn they needed for their own use and had enough left over so that they were able to send a shipload to Plymouth in exchange for salted codfish and other supplies. 

Relations with the Indians. The Maryland settlers did not make the mistake of mistreating the Indians, as the members of so many other colonies did. Whenever they wanted new lands, they paid for them with axes, hoes, cloth, and other useful goods. They paid a fair price for the furs which the Indians traded to them. Indians accused of crimes were given fair treatment. As a result, the people of Maryland lived at peace with the red men and did not have to fear the sudden Indian attacks and massacres that made life so unpleasant in some of the other colonies. 

New Settlers in Maryland. Although Maryland had been founded as a place of refuge for Catholics, not many Catholics came to the colony. Instead, the religious freedom offered there attracted members of many other religions. A band of a thousand Puritans came from Virginia, where they were being persecuted, and founded a settlement which they first called Providence and, later, Annapolis. Other Puritans came

MARYLAND 

147 

THE BATTLE BETWEEN SHIPS FROM MARYLAND AND VIRGINIA 

William Claiborne of Virginia laid claim to Kent island, in Chesapeake bay. This was also claimed by Maryland. The quarrel grew very bitter and at last resulted in a battle between one of Claiborne's ships and two vessels from Maryland. The 

Maryland ships won. directly from England, as did many Baptists and members of the Quaker faith. Finally, a large number of Protestants crossed the ocean from Germany and settled in the new colony. 

Trouble with Virginia. The first serious troubles of the Maryland colonists came as the result of a dispute with Virginia over the ownership of Kent island.

Kent island lay within the boundaries of the territory which King Charles I had granted to Lord Baltimore.

Before the arrival of the Maryland settlers, however, a Virginia trader named William Claiborne had built a trading post on the island. He refused to admit that 

Kent island was a part of Maryland, and said that he intended to run the island as he wished, without regard to the rights and laws of the Maryland government. Not long afterward, one of his boats was found trading in Maryland waters without permission, and was seized.

When Claiborne heard that his ship had been taken, he mounted a cannon on another of his vessels and sent the armed ship out with orders to fire at any Maryland ship that should attempt to seize it. Soon 

Claiborne's ship met two armed vessels from Maryland, and a naval battle was fought in which a number of men were killed and Claiborne's vessel was defeated. 

Although his ship had been beaten in the battle, Claiborne was not willing to give up his claim to Kent island. The quarrel was taken to the courts of England, and at last it was decided that the island belonged to Maryland.

The Colony Prospers. From the very beginning, the Maryland colonists were happy and prosperous. 

Each of the gentlemen who came with Leonard Calvert was given two thousand acres of land, in return for which he had to pay only a small part of whatever crops he raised. Many of the workmen who came with the original settlers, as well as those who came later, had their passages paid by the owners of these estates, for whom they worked until the passage money had

MARYLAND 

149 been repaid. Then some of these people received lands of their own. In later years many slaves were brought to Maryland and were found to be very useful for work in the fields.

The soil of Maryland early proved itself to be well suited for agriculture. At first corn and wheat were raised, but later tobacco became the most important crop. As in the case of Virginia, many of the Maryland settlers grew very wealthy by selling their tobacco crops in England.

The Growth of Plantations. The development of the tobacco industry led to the growth of large plantations in Maryland. Though there were few roads in the colony, there were many rivers, and the plantation owners found it easy to travel about from place to place by means of boats.

The growth of large plantations in Maryland meant that few towns were established. For more than sixty years after the landing of Calvert and his settlers, St. Mary's was the only real town in the colony. 

Baltimore, which in time grew to be the most important city in Maryland, was not founded until 1729.

Political and Religious Freedom. The charter which Lord Baltimore had received from King Charles clearly stated that the proprietor of the colony had the right to make the laws. Therefore, Cecil Calvert wrote a code of laws to govern his people. The settlers, however, were not willing to have Lord Baltimore or anybody else write their laws, and refused to be bound by the code. Instead, they held a meeting of

representatives and drew up laws of their own. Although Lord Baltimore was distressed to learn that his colonists had not accepted his laws, he wisely decided to give up his right and to permit the colonists to do their own lawmaking. 

In 1649, there was war in England between King Charles I and his Parliament. Most members of 

Parliament were Puritans, and the Maryland settlers felt that if Parliament's armies won the war and gained control of England, the English government might take away their religious freedom and allow only Puritans to hold church services. In an attempt to prevent this, they passed a law, known as the Toleration Act, which granted freedom of worship to all Christians who settled in Maryland, no matter what their faith might be.

Civil War in the Colony. The colonists in Maryland were much interested in the quarrel between the king and Parliament, and some sided with the king and some with Parliament. Governor Calvert and many of the leaders in the colony favored the king, but the sympathies of the Puritan settlers were with Parliament. Seeing his chance to make trouble, William 

Claiborne of Virginia crossed into Maryland and urged the Puritan settlers there to take up arms against the 

Maryland government.

There followed a time of bloodshed and violence. 

Though no important battles were fought, many on both sides were killed in the frequent skirmishes which took place. Houses and plantations were attacked and

MARYLAND 

151 plundered. In the midst of all this trouble Governor 

Leonard Calvert died, and Claiborne and his Puritans were left in power.

The period of Puritan rule was an unhappy one for most of the Maryland settlers. The Toleration 

Act was repealed, and a new law was passed in its place providing that only the Puritans might hold church services. The Puritans even declared that Lord Baltimore had no claim to the colony, and that he should no longer play any part in its affairs.

After about four years of rule by the Puritans, Parliament came to the rescue of the Maryland colony. 

Freedom of worship was again established, and Lord Baltimore's rights in the colony were restored. This was the beginning of a new period of prosperity for the colonists, which lasted nearly thirty years. 

Maryland Becomes a Royal Colony. In 1689, King

William and Queen Mary came to the throne of England. Both were strong Protestants, and had little sympathy for Lord Baltimore and the other Catholics. 

In 1692, therefore, King William took away Lord Baltimore's rights in Maryland and made it a royal colony with a governor whom he himself appointed. 

The seat of government was removed from St. Mary's, and Annapolis became the new capital. Once more religious freedom was done away with, and all of the colonists were required to support the Church of England. As long as Maryland remained an English colony, the people living there were unable to regain their right to worship as they pleased.

During the reign of King Charles I the Catholics in England were persecuted because of their religion. 

A Catholic nobleman, George Calvert, Lord Baltimore, tried unsuccessfully to establish a colony in Newfoundland where there would be freedom of worship.

After the failure of the Newfoundland colony, the king granted Lord Baltimore a stretch of land north of Virginia. This land was named Maryland. 

Lord Baltimore died before the charter for the Maryland colony was drawn up, so it was given to his son Cecil Calvert. Leonard Calvert, Cecil's younger brother, was appointed governor of Maryland and arrived there with a group of colonists in 1634. Their first settlement was St. Mary's.

The Maryland settlers treated the Indians well, and were therefore able to live at peace with the red men.

Religious freedom was permitted in Maryland, and as a result Puritans, Quakers, German Protestants,

Baptists, and Catholics came to the colony. 

The soil of Maryland was well suited for agriculture. 

Tobacco became the most important crop, and many large tobacco plantations were laid out. Because of the growth of plantations, few towns were established. 

Although Cecil Calvert's charter permitted him to make the laws of Maryland, he gave up this right when the colonists insisted upon making their own laws.

MARYLAND 

153 In 1649, the Toleration Act was passed in the Maryland colony, granting religious freedom to all Christians.

When war broke out in England between the king and Parliament, the Puritans in Maryland gained control of the colony, and religious freedom was done away with. Later, freedom of worship was again. restored.

When King William and Queen Mary came to the throne of England, Maryland became a royal colony. 

Freedom of worship was no longer allowed.

CHAPTER EIGHT THE CAROLINAS What a strange situation! A colony in America was founded and governed by eight English noblemen who never came to the New World. The laws for the colony were written by an Englishman who knew little or nothing about the living conditions in America or the type of people who were to be guided by his laws. Could such a colony succeed? 

Did it? Let's find out.

In the year 1653 a group of discontented people from the Virginia colony loaded their possessions on pack horses and set out towards the south. Day after day they struggled through the swamps and pathless forests until they came to the banks of the Chowan river, where the soil was rich and the climate mild. 

Each of the travelers selected a spot for a home and unloaded the goods that he had brought with him. 

Soon all were hard at work building houses and clearing the land.

The settlers on the Chowan river did not build their houses close together, but lived on farms some distance apart. Each wanted plenty of land around his home on which to grow his crops. Here, as time went on, wheat and tobacco were grown, as in Virginia. Some of the settlers had herds of cattle to supply them with meat and hides. By far the most 155

important products of the Chowan settlement, however, came from the forests. These were pitch, tar, and turpentine, known as naval stores because they are useful in the building and repair of ships. 

North Carolina Department of

Conservation and Development AN OLD CHURCH IN NORTHERN CAROLINA 

This old brick church was built in colonial times. Many of the early Carolina colonists lie buried in its graveyard.

The Carolina Char ter. Ten years after the 

Chowan settlers had made their homes in the wilderness, an event occurred in England which was to have important results for them and for their descendants. King 

Charles II had eight friends of whom he was very fond. All of them were English noblemen. 

In 1663, the king granted to these eight men a huge stretch of land extending from Virginia as far south as Florida and as far west as the Pacific ocean. He also gave them a charter permitting them to make settlements on the land and to rule these settlements as they thought best. 

The eight gentlemen were to be known as proprietors and were to be the absolute owners of this land. In the charter granted to the proprietors, the name Carolina was given to the entire territory in honor of King Charles.

THE CAROLINAS 

157 

The Grand Model. The eight proprietors of Caro lina had their own ideas about how their colony should be run. They did not believe that the common people should have any share in the government or in the making of the laws. The proprietors themselves were to have full power of governing, and they chose a learned Englishman, named John Locke, to draw up the laws.

The laws which John Locke framed were not at all practical. According to his code, which was called the 

Grand Model, the people were to be allowed no rights. 

Though they could rent land, they were not permitted to own any. Locke's code even directed what kind of clothes the settlers should wear. Worst of all, no provision was made for changing any of the laws. 

New Settlers Come to Carolina. For several years after the arrival of the Chowan settlers, very few people came to Carolina. A few more Virginians drifted to the south and built homes along the Chowan and Roanoke rivers and Albemarle sound. At the end of seventeen years, however, there were still very few white people living in the entire region. Then, in 1670, a large group of settlers, sent by the eight owners of Carolina, arrived from England. These people landed far south of the Chowan settlement, along the banks of the Ashley river. Here they were joined by other settlers from England and from some English settlements in the West Indies. Some time later the settlement on the Ashley river was given up, and its inhabitants moved to a fine harbor on the seacoast,

where a new town, called Charleston, was established. 

To Charleston came many settlers from the countries of Europe. Dutch and Germans arrived, some to remain in Charleston and others to make little settlements of their own nearby. There were also many people from Scotland. From France came a band of 

Huguenots, or French Protestants, who had been forced to leave their own country because of their religion. They were intelligent, well-educated people, and their arrival did much to strengthen the colony. 

Most numerous of all were the English, who came not only from England but also from the other English colonies to the north. Though most of these newcomers settled in or near Charleston, some of them made homes farther north, near where the Chowan settlers lived. 

New Crops. For many years after the founding of Charleston, the only important crops raised in the southern Carolina settlements were tobacco, corn, and wheat. Then, however, a Dutch ship put in at the harbor at Charleston, and the captain presented the governor of the colony with a bag of rice. The governor planted the rice, and it grew so well in the fertile Carolina soil that many other settlers also planted some. Soon rice became the most important product of the Carolina colony, and shiploads of it were regularly sent not only to England, but also to the other 

English colonies along the North American coast. Not many years after the first rice grains were planted, there were a number of rice plantations near Charleston which rivaled the tobacco plantations of Virginia

THE CAROLINAS 

159 and Maryland in size and in the wealth which they produced for their owners.

Another valuable Carolina crop was introduced by a sixteen-year-old girl named Elizabeth Lucas. She believed that the soil of Carolina was suitable for many other crops besides the ones that were being grown there, and she experimented with several kinds of seeds. First she tried cotton and then ginger, but neither grew well. Later she received from the West 

Indies some seeds of the indigo plant, which is used in making a deep blue dye. Her first experiments were not successful, but she kept up her work and at last succeeded in raising plants which could be made to produce the rich blue color she desired. Other settlers followed her example, and within ten years so much indigo was being raised that it had become the second most important crop in the colony. 

Wars with the Indians. Although the Carolina settlers had little difficulty with the Indians at first, the red men became more and more discontented as they saw the white men spreading out and occupying their lands. At last the Tuscarora Indians, who belonged to the Iroquois nation far to the north, decided to go on the warpath. A body of five hundred Indian warriors made a surprise attack on one of the Carolina settlements, and in two hours killed one hundred and thirty settlers and burned their houses. 

At once the Carolina settlers prepared for war. 

In both northern and southern Carolina, armies were raised and sent against the Tuscaroras. After a num-

A BATTLE OF THE TUSCARORA WAR 

The Tuscarora Indians lived within the borders of Carolina. 

They attacked the settlers there and fierce fighting followed. 

After several battles, the Indians were defeated and driven from the colony. 

ber of small battles had been fought, the Indian leader and his men were trapped in their fort. For three days the white men besieged the Indian stronghold, and finally took it. Hundreds of Indians were killed, and the rest were so disheartened that they left Carolina and traveled to the north to join the other tribes of the Iroquois. 

Another serious Indian war broke out a few years later, when the Yemassee Indians went on the warpath. 

These Indians had long been friendly to the settlers,

THE CAROLINAS 

161 but it is thought that they were armed by the Spaniards in Florida and encouraged by them to attack the English. A series of terrible massacres took place not far from Charleston, and an army of settlers was sent out to defeat the Indians and drive them away. After several fierce battles the white men were at last victorious, and the hostile Indians were either killed or driven into the Spanish lands to the south. 

Pirates off the Coast. Now a new danger threatened the Carolina settlements, for a number of cruel and daring pirates made their headquarters on the islands off the coast, and preyed on the ships which passed by. One of the boldest of the pirates was Captain Teach, who was known as Blackbeard. On one occasion, Blackbeard waited outside of Charleston harbor and seized a ship on which were many of the city's leading citizens. At once he sent word to Charleston that unless certain supplies which he needed were delivered to him he would cut off the heads of the citizens and send them to the city. Naturally, the supplies were sent.

For many years, the pirates continued to terrify the people along the coast, but one after another they were destroyed. An English man-of-war attacked 

Blackbeard's ship, and, in the fighting which followed, the pirate leader was killed and so were most of his men. Other pirates were taken by the colonists themselves, often after terrible battles, and were hanged.

In the end, there were no pirates left along the Carolina coast, and shipping was once more safe.

PIRATES-UNWELCOME VISITORS TO CAROLINA 

Many cruel and daring pirates lurked among the islands off the Carolina coast, capturing and robbing the ships that came their way.

Sometimes they even raided towns and made the townspeople give them food, drink, and other supplies. 

Carolina Becomes a Royal Colony. The rule of the eight proprietors was never very satisfactory to the settlers of Carolina. Although the proprietors never succeeded in enforcing the elaborate rules of the Grand Model, they passed other annoying laws,

THE CAROLINAS 

163 

THE CAROLINA COLONISTS PROTEST AGAINST THEIR GOVERNMENT 

The people of Carolina did not like the laws which the proprietors of their colony made. In 1719, the citizens of Charleston seized their guns and threatened to make trouble if the governor continued to enforce these unpopular laws. The governor was made to obey the wishes of the people. which the governor tried to enforce. Finally, in 1719, the people of Charleston took their guns and gathered together in the public square of the town, determined to fight rather than submit longer to the hated laws. 

The governor tried to make the people put down their

guns and return to their homes, but they would not. 

So he himself was compelled to give in and to stop trying to make the people obey the laws which the proprietors had framed. 

Even after the revolt at Charleston, the proprietors of Carolina continued to run the colony in a way which did not please the settlers. In 1729, therefore, the settlers sent a petition to the king of England, asking him to take over the control of the colony. This he did. 

Later the king bought the lands of the colony from the proprietors, who were glad to sell them for they were not making as much profit as they had expected. 

The Division of the Colony. When the king took over the control of Carolina from the proprietors, he made an important change. The colony was divided into two colonies, North Carolina and South Carolina, each with a legislature of its own and a governor appointed by the king.

In 1653 a group of Virginia colonists moved south and settled on the banks of the Chowan river. 

In 1663, King Charles II of England granted to eight of his noblemen a huge stretch of land extending from Virginia as far south as Florida and west to the Pacific. This land was given the name Carolina. 

A learned Englishman named John Locke framed a code of laws for the governing of the Carolina colony. 

These laws were very impractical.

THE CAROLINAS 

165 A large group of settlers from England established a settlement on the banks of the Ashley river in southern Carolina in 1670. Later the settlers moved to the coast and established Charleston. 

Many settlers came to Charleston from Holland, Germany, Scotland, France, and England, and from the other English colonies in North America.

The two most important crops in the Carolina colony were rice and indigo. 

Because the Carolina settlers were taking their land, the Tuscarora Indians made war on the white men. 

In the fighting which followed, many of the Indians were killed, and the rest left Carolina and traveled to the north where they settled among the Iroquois Indians.

The Yemassee Indians also went on the warpath and attacked the white settlers. After several fierce battles they were defeated, and those who were not killed were driven into the Spanish lands to the south. 

Many pirates made their headquarters on the islands off the Carolina coast, preying on ships which passed by. At last the English navy and the colonists succeeded in ridding the coast of pirates. 

The rule of the eight proprietors of Carolina was never very satisfactory to the settlers. In 1719, the people of Charleston made the governor stop trying to enforce the proprietors' laws. Ten years later the king of England made Carolina a royal colony and then divided it into two colonies, North Carolina and 

South Carolina.

Between the southern colonies and New England lay the middle colonies of New York, New Jersey, Delaware, and Pennsylvania. Some of the settlements established here grew into important towns and cities which have played leading parts in our history.

CHAPTER NINE NEW YORK 

We have been reading about English colonies which were founded by Englishmen. Now we are to read about a colony which England claimed but which was founded by the Dutch. Why did people from Holland come to the New World, and how did they lose their colony to the English? How did the 

English treat their Dutch subjects when England gained control of the colony? 

Holland and the Dutch. In western Europe, along the shore of the North sea, lies a section of very low land called Holland. The Dutch people, who lived in this land, were thrifty and hard-working, and had a deep love for freedom. We have already seen how well they treated the Separatists from England who settled among them.

The Dutch East India Company. The Dutch coast is cut by many bays and rivers. These early tempted the people of Holland to build ships and to trade upon the sea.

For many years Dutch ships sailed along the west coast of Europe to Portugal, bringing back to Holland the spices and other goods which the Portuguese vessels had brought from the East Indies and countries of Asia. Then war with Spain came, and trade with Portugal was interrupted. A new way of getting goods from the East had to be found. 169

To handle the trade, a company was formed, known as the Dutch East India Company. This company sent ships far around the southern tip of Africa to India, to other countries of southern Asia, and to the East Indies. As the voyage was long and dangerous, the company was anxious to find a shorter route to the East.

Dutch Explorers and Traders. It was the Dutch 

East India Company, you know, which sent Henry 

Hudson exploring in 1609. Hudson discovered and explored the bodies of water which are now known as New York bay and the Hudson river. After claiming the river, the bay, and the lands about them for Holland, he returned to Europe, taking with him valuable furs which the Indians had given him. 

The very next year after Hudson's voyage of exploration, Dutch traders began coming to the New World to buy furs from the Indians. Some of these traders sailed far up the Hudson river, which they called the North river, and traded with the Iroquois Indians. In exchange for furs from the animals which the Indians trapped, the Dutch gave cloth, knives, axes, and sometimes even guns. The Iroquois were especially anxious to get guns. Only the year before, they had fought with Champlain and an Algonquin war party, and had learned to their sorrow how deadly the white men's weapons were. For this reason they were very anxious to make friends with the Dutch, in the hope that the white men would fight with them against the Algonquins and the French.

NEW YORK 

171 By the year 1614, many Dutch trading ships had visited the lands which Hudson had claimed, and there were two Dutch trading posts in this section. One was on the Hudson river, near where Albany now stands; the other at the southern end of Manhattan island. 

Though most of the Dutch who came to America were mainly interested in trading, some were also explorers. One of these, Captain Cornelis Mey, sailed south along the Atlantic coast as far as the southern tip of what is now New Jersey. Cape May, at that point, is named for him. Another, Captain Adrian 

Block, explored the islands and channels from Manhattan island to a little north of Cape Cod. 

As a result of their explorations, the Dutch claimed the lands about the Hudson river and New York bay and the area now included in New Jersey. To this whole territory they gave the name New Netherland. 

The Dutch West India Company. In 1621, twelve years after Hudson's voyage, a society was formed in Holland known as the Dutch West India Company. 

The founders of the company were a group of merchants who hoped to grow rich through capturing Spanish vessels, robbing Spanish settlements, carrying on fur trade with the Indians, and planting prosperous new colonies in various parts of the New World. The 

Dutch government helped and favored the company by granting it the sole right to trade with America and with Africa.

In 1623, the Dutch West India Company sent to the 

New World a party of Walloons-Protestants from

the lowlands along the North sea in and near Holland. 

Eighteen families sailed up the Hudson and settled at 

Fort Orange. Others settled on Manhattan island. 

Their settlement became known as New Amsterdam. 

Three years after the landing of the Walloons, the Dutch West India Company sent Peter Minuit, a German, to be governor of the New Netherland colony. 

Minuit visited the various settlements in New Netherland and at last decided that the best place of all for a strong settlement was the southern end of Manhattan island. There was already a fort at this point and a few families had made their homes there. But in spite of its favorable location New Amsterdam was one of the smallest settlements in the colony. 

Governor Minuit was both a wise man and a fair one, so he decided to buy Manhattan island from the Indians instead of taking it, which was the way so many of the white settlers gained their land. Accordingly, he called together the Indians who lived on Manhattan and showed them a large quantity of beads, knives, and other goods which seemed quite worthless to him but very valuable to the Indians. In exchange for the island, the red men were offered all of these objects. They eagerly agreed, and sold Minuit the entire island of Manhattan for about twenty-four dollars worth of trinkets. This was at the rate of about one-tenth of a cent an acre.

Peter Minuit proved to be a good governor. He was very kind, and he exercised for the best interests of the colonists the great powers which the Dutch

NEW YORK 

173 West India Company had given him. He did his best to establish a lasting friendship with the Indians and with his neighbors, the English colonists; and he built 

PETER MINUIT BUYING MANHATTAN ISLAND 

In 1626, the Dutch West India Company sent Peter Minuit to New Netherland to govern the colony. Governor Minuit bought Manhattan island from the Indians for trinkets worth about twenty-four dollars, and soon the important settlement of New Amsterdam grew up on the island. up a fine fur trade with the Indians. Nevertheless, the colony did not grow very rapidly nor did it pay large profits to the Dutch West India Company. So in 1631, after only five years in power, Minuit was recalled, and another governor was sent in his place.

The Patroon System. During Peter Minuit's governorship, an important change was made in the running of the New Netherland colony. Very few people were leaving Holland and going to the New World to make their homes, and this displeased the managers of the Dutch West India Company, who wanted their colony to become large and prosperous. 

To tempt colonists to go to America, the patroon system was established in 1629.

Under the patroon system, any member of the Dutch West India Company who would bring or send to New Netherland fifty grown-up colonists was to receive an estate on the Hudson river, or some other river lying within the boundaries of New Netherland. 

This estate might extend for sixteen miles along one bank of the river, or for eight miles along both banks. 

It was to extend back from the river as far as the owner could use the land. The owner was to be called the patroon, and was to be the ruler of the people whom he had brought to live on his estate. If he did not wish to leave Holland, he might send a manager to take care of his land and his people for him. 

Encouraged by the patroon system, many members of important Dutch families came to New Netherland as patroons. Among them were the Van Cortlandts, the Van Rensselaers, Livingstons, and Schuylers.

Many descendants of these patroons still live in New York state, and from the time of their arrival these fine old families have played an important part in the history of New York.

NEW YORK 

175 

Free Farmers Come to New Netherland. 

In 1638, a new plan was adopted to bring settlers to New Netherland. Farmers were encouraged to come to the colony and settle there on lands which were furnished for their use free of charge. Each was provided with free transportation from Europe and all of the equipment which he needed to run his farm. At the end of a few years, these free farmers were permitted to buy their land from the Dutch West India Company at a very reasonable rate. This plan proved popular among the common people of Holland, and many more of them came to New Netherland after 1638 than had come before this new system was put into effect. 

Wouter Van Twiller. The governor who followed

Peter Minuit in charge of the New Netherland colony was a fat, lazy young man named Wouter van Twiller. 

He was a nephew of Van Rensselaer, one of the leading patroons, so naturally he favored the patroons rather than the common people. Van Twiller was really much more interested in looking out for his own interests than for those of the colony. Though he became very wealthy during his stay in New Netherland, he did not improve the condition of the colony. The Dutch 

West India Company at last removed him and sent 

William Kieft in his place.

Governor Kieft's Administration. At once Governor 

Kieft began to make strict laws for the running of the colony. This was not pleasing to the people of 

New Netherland, but they were powerless to do anything about it.

War with the Indians. The worst blunder of 

Governor Kieft's administration was his treatment of the Indians. Under the earlier governors, the red men had always been friendly to the Dutch because they had been fairly treated. Governor Kieft, however, insisted that they pay taxes in furs and corn. They did not understand why, and were puzzled and angry. 

One of the Raritan Indians, who lived across the 

Hudson river from New Amsterdam, killed a Dutch settler. Because the Indians refused to give up the guilty warrior, Kieft sent a company of soldiers under cover of night to destroy their village. A dreadful slaughter followed. Almost all of the Indians in the village were killed-men, women, and children. Those who escaped bore the story of the tragedy to the other nearby tribes of Indians. 

As was to be expected, the red men went on the warpath, attacking farms and settlements, burning buildings, and killing all of the settlers they could find. 

Among those killed was Anne Hutchinson, who had moved from Rhode Island a little while before and had settled among the Dutch. 

The fighting was not ended until four years later, in 1645. In that year an English soldier named John 

Underhill, who had taken part in the Pequot War, came to New Netherland and led the Dutch army against the last of the Indian villages. The village was taken and most of the Indians were killed. 

Governor Kieft Is Recalled. The Dutch settlers were never much in favor of Governor Kieft, and they

GOVERNOR STUYVESANT REPLACES GOVERNOR

Governor Kieft, standing in the foreground beside his baggage, is talking to Governor Stuyvesant, the man with the wooden leg. Governor Kieft never reached Holland, for his ship was lost in a storm on the way home. had suffered a great deal during his Indian war. When he tried to tax them to pay for the war, they requested the Dutch government to recall him. The government turned the request over to the Dutch West India Company for consideration, for the colony belonged to the company and not to the Dutch government. 

After some delay, the company decided to recall Kieft
