PETER STUYVESANT-LAST DUTCH GOVERNOR OF NEW NETHERLAND 

The governor is seated in his home in New Amsterdam. In his left hand is an hour glass, used to tell the time. The feather which you see is Stuyvesant's quill pen, and beside it is an inkhorn, full of ink. The candlestick and mug are made of pewter, a soft metal much used in colonial times. and to send Peter Stuyvesant to New Netherland. On his way back to Holland, Governor Kieft was drowned when the ship on which he was traveling went down. in a storm.

Peter Stuyvesant's Administration. The new governor, Peter Stuyvesant, was a very picturesque character. He was a huge man, tall, heavy, and straight. 

His clothes were very rich, and he dressed in the height

NEW YORK 

179 of fashion. He had once been a soldier, and in a battle against the Portuguese he had lost his right leg below the knee. To replace it he wore a finely-carved wooden leg, decorated with silver bands and rows of silver nails. For that reason, he was sometimes referred to as "Old Silvernails."

Governor Stuyvesant soon won for himself a more fitting nickname "Headstrong Peter." He had no respect at all for anyone's opinion but his own, and he flew into a rage when anyone disagreed with him.

Naturally, a freedom-loving people like the Dutch were not happy to be ruled in this way, and Governor Stuyvesant soon became very unpopular. 

Although Peter Stuyvesant was a strict and unpopular governor, he was not a bad one. He was honest and patriotic, and he always did what he thought was best for the colony. During his rule many new houses and piers were built in New Amsterdam, and the fort at the southern tip of Manhattan island was strengthened.

Little by little, New Amsterdam grew from a small and unimportant town to an important city. New settlers came from Holland and other European countries and some even came from the nearby English colonies. At length the population became so mixed that all public notices had to be written not only in Dutch but also in French and English, so that the people could understand them.

Naturally, the French and English inhabitants of New Amsterdam were not so loyal to the Dutch gov-

ernment of the colony as Dutchmen would have been, and the presence of so many foreigners served to weaken Stuyvesant's hold over his colonists. Then, too, much of the population of New Amsterdam was here today and gone tomorrow, and people who are always moving from place to place do not usually make the best citizens. It is small wonder that, as more and more people from other nations settled in New Netherland, Dutch patriotism gradually died out and the Dutch company's hold on the colony became very weak. 

Trouble with the Indians. During Governor Stuy vesant's administration there was trouble with some Indians who lived to the north, along the Hudson river. 

An Indian woman was caught stealing fruit from a settler's orchard, and was shot dead for this small offense. The other Indians of her tribe were quick to go on the warpath. Down the river they came and attacked New Amsterdam at a time when Governor Stuyvesant and most of the soldiers were away. There were fighting and bloodshed in the streets before the 

Indians at last left. From New Amsterdam the red men crossed the bay and attacked another Dutch settlement on Staten island, killing many people and burning most of the houses. Then they went home, satisfied that they had gained revenge.

Governor Stuyvesant proved to be more sensible in his handling of Indian affairs than Governor Kieft had been. He realized that the Indians had had some excuse for making their attack, and that they were now satisfied because they felt that they had had

NEW YORK 

181 

INDIANS RAIDING A DUTCH SETTLEMENT 

During Peter Stuyvesant's administration, a band of Indians raided the Dutch settlements on Manhattan and Staten islands, killing many settlers and burning their houses. After this happened, Governor Stuyvesant made a treaty with the red men. 

Because the old man in this picture was a friend of the Indians, he and his family were spared, but his three hired men were killed. revenge. Instead of starting a long and cruel war against the red men, therefore, he sent men to make a treaty of peace with them so that there would be no more trouble. He also cautioned his own settlers to "reform themselves"; that is, to be more careful and considerate in their dealings with the Indians. 

Peter Stuyvesant was a firm believer in preparedness, and although he had made peace with the Indians

he now made sure that no more such disastrous raids should occur. Along the northern edge of the New 

Amsterdam settlement he had a twelve-foot palisade of pointed logs built, fitted with gates which could be closed in time of danger. Along the foot of this wall there ran a street which soon became known as Wall street. Although the wall has long since gone, the street is still there. Today Wall street is one of the most important business streets in the world. 

Relations with Other Colonies. Both the Dutch settlers in New Netherland and the English in Connecticut claimed Long island and the lands between the Connecticut and Hudson rivers. Some Dutchmen had settled in these sections, but there were many more English, and in time they began to crowd the Dutch out of the disputed lands and even to make settlements within the borders of New Netherland itself. At last 

Peter Stuyvesant traveled to Hartford to make a treaty, so that both the Dutch and the English would know which lands rightly belonged to them. A boundary line was agreed on which was almost the same as the present boundary between Connecticut and New York. 

In 1655, Governor Stuyvesant, with a Dutch army, conquered a Swedish colony on Delaware bay. These 

Swedish lands thus became a part of New Netherland. 

The English Take New Netherland. In the days when Peter Stuyvesant was governor of New Netherland, Charles II was king of England. It troubled him to see a thriving Dutch colony planted between his New England colonies and the English colonies farther to

NEW YORK 

183 the south. Moreover, Holland had been rapidly growing in power and was now a strong rival of England. 

This was not at all to England's liking. Anything that he could do to weaken the power of Holland would be very much to his country's advantage, thought King Charles. If he could only gain possession of New 

Netherland he would be killing two birds with one stone, by both strengthening England and weakening her greatest rival.

Holland and England were not at war, but this did not disturb King Charles. By way of an excuse for seizing New Netherland, he declared that the Dutch had been trading with the English colonies in America in spite of the Navigation Acts, which forbade the colonies to trade directly with foreign countries. This charge was true, but it was a very poor excuse for conquering a peaceful neighbor's colony. As though they were his to give, King Charles made a present of all Dutch lands in America to his brother, the Duke of York, and supplied the duke with warships and soldiers to use in taking possession of his new lands. 

One day in 1664, four English warships sailed into the harbor at New Amsterdam and dropped their anchors. Colonel Nicolls, the English commander, sent word to the governor that he had come to take possession of the colony. Stuyvesant was enraged. He tore up Colonel Nicolls' letter and threw the pieces on the floor. Never, he said, would he surrender his colony to the English! He would resist until the last man was dead!

www 

GOVERNOR STUYVESANT DESTROYS THE ENGLISH COMMANDER'S LETTER 

In 1664, an English fleet appeared before New Amsterdam, and the commander sent Governor Stuyvesant a letter demanding the surrender of the New Netherland colony. Although the governor tore up the letter in a rage, he was forced to surrender 

New Netherland to the English. 

But Peter Stuyvesant did not have the support of his people. He had bullied them too much, and they were thoroughly sick of his harsh rule. A committee of citizens called on the governor to learn what terms of surrender Colonel Nicolls had proposed in his letter.

NEW YORK 

185 When Stuyvesant would not tell, the citizens collected the pieces of the colonel's letter and put them together, and so read the message. To their surprise, they found that the terms were very generous. The people were to be permitted to keep their own property. They were to have many more rights in the government than they had had under Dutch rule. They were not to be denied freedom of worship. With such generous terms, many of the citizens felt that they would rather live under the rule of the English than under such strict 

Dutch governors as Kieft and Stuyvesant. 

Poor Peter Stuyvesant had no choice but to turn New Netherland over to the English. So, without the firing of a shot, the Dutch flag was hauled down and the English flag was raised over New Amsterdam. 

Three years later, in 1667, the government of Holland officially granted New Netherland to the English in exchange for land in South America.

Governor Nicolls' Administration. Once they were in command, the English divided New Netherland into three colonies-New York, New Jersey, and Delaware. The name New York was also given to New 

Amsterdam, in honor of the Duke of York. Fort 

Orange became Albany, and the names of many other little Dutch settlements were changed to English names. 

Even today, however, you will find many towns in New York state whose quaint names were given to them by the early Dutch settlers. 

Colonel Nicolls, commander of the English fleet which took New Amsterdam, became the first English

governor of the colony. He was a just man and tried to see that everyone was treated fairly. 

The Dutch Recapture New York. In 1673, war broke out between Holland and England, and a Dutch fleet entered New York harbor and captured the colony. 

The name New Netherland was restored. Though no attempts were made by the English to recapture the colony by force of arms, it was peacefully returned to England by the treaty which ended the war. 

The Administration of Edmund Andros. The first 

English governor of New York after the colony was again surrendered by the Dutch was Edmund Andros. 

Like Peter Stuyvesant he was a harsh and headstrong man, but he was honest and able and did much to improve the colony. 

Perhaps the most important event of Governor Andros' administration was the appointment of a committee at Albany to keep the Iroquois Indians friendly with the English. This committee was to see that the 

Indians were fairly treated in all of their dealings with the white men, and to settle any quarrels which occurred between the settlers and the Iroquois. The committee members worked quietly and patiently, and their work proved very valuable, for the Iroquois continued to remain friendly to the English. 

Like all strong men, Governor Andros made many enemies. Some of these accused him of misusing the tax money of the colony, and he was forced to go to England to defend himself in the courts. The colonists seized this opportunity to send a petition to the Duke

NEW YORK 

187 of York, asking for a greater share in the running of their government. The privilege they wanted most was to have a voice in questions concerning taxation. 

To make sure that the duke paid attention to their petition, the citizens who signed it said that they would not pay any taxes or duties until they were given a chance to vote for or against all proposed taxes. 

The Duke of York was very indignant at the protests of his colonists. He consulted with Governor 

Andros and others of his advisers, and all of them told him that it would be wise to give the colonists the privileges that they asked. Although the duke hated to do it, he hated even more to lose any of the tax money from the colony, and at last he agreed to see that the people got their rights. 

A new governor was sent to New York to take the place of Governor Andros, who was still too busy with affairs in England to leave that country. 

EDMUND ANDROS 

Edmund Andros was the first 

English governor of New York after the colony was returned to the English by the Dutch. He was harsh and headstrong but did much to improve the colony. 

Governor Dongan's Administration. Thomas Don gan, the new governor of New York, was a very able

man. In 1683, soon after he took office, he held a meeting of the first New York Assembly, or legislature. 

THOMAS DONGAN Thomas Dongan followed Ed mund Andros as governor of 

New York. He made an important treaty with the Iroquois Indians and gave the colonists a voice in their government besides many other privileges which they had not had before. 

Seventeen chosen representatives of the people gathered in New York 

City and passed fifteen acts, one of which provided for a set of laws the Charter of Liberties. 

By the terms of this charter, the Assembly, made up of representatives of the people, was to meet at least once every three years for the purpose of helping the governor make laws and settle the problems of the colony. 

No laws were to be made and no taxes were to be levied without the consent of the Assembly. All of the men in the colony, except slaves, were to have the privilege of vot- ing. There was to be complete religious freedom, so that members of all Christian churches should have the privilege of worshiping as they thought best. It was further provided that no one in the colony was to be imprisoned without a fair trial by jury. The seventeen

NEW YORK 

189 members of the Assembly and Governor Dongan signed the Charter of Liberties. It was then sent to England to be submitted to the Duke of York for his approval.

Besides the drawing up of the Charter of Liberties, the Assembly did other important work. It divided the colony into counties, each with its own officers to run the local government. The forming of the counties not only improved the administration of affairs in the colony but also provided a fair way of representing the various parts of the colony in the Assembly. Each county was to be represented by at least one representative.

The Treaty with the Iroquois. Governor Dongan was troubled to learn that the French colonists living to the north were making serious attempts to win the friendship of the Iroquois Indians. If they succeeded in this, there was danger that a combined army of French and Indians might sweep down on the colony and conquer it. The governor, therefore, called a meeting of the chiefs of the Iroquois tribes and persuaded them to remain loyal to the English. In the end the 

Iroquois chiefs signed a solemn peace treaty and announced that King Charles II of England was now their Great Chief. As the success of the New York colony depended very largely on the friendship of the Iroquois Indians, this treaty of Governor Dongan's was really very important. The treaty also gave the 

English a claim to the land occupied by the Iroquois tribes, and was the excuse upon which they later took

for themselves all of the land now included in the state of New York. 

The Dominion of New England. In the year 1685 

King Charles II died, and the Duke of York took his place upon the throne as King James II of England. As king of England he now owned not merely 

New York, New Jersey, and Delaware, but all of the 

English colonies in America. 

Soon King James II made an important change in the administration of several of his American colonies, including New York. He declared that the colonies of New York, New Jersey, Massachusetts 

Bay, Plymouth, Connecticut, Rhode Island, and New

Hampshire should all be united, and should be known as the Dominion of New England. A single governor was to rule this whole great area, and was to be aided by deputy, or assistant, governors in the various colonies. The governor and his deputies were to have absolute power, and the people were not to be represented in any way. Edmund Andros was sent from 

England to be the first governor of the Dominion. 

Naturally, the colonists were much distressed by the creation of the Dominion of New England and by the discontinuing of representative government. They soon saw that King James II was an even worse tyrant than the three kings who had gone before him, and they longed to free themselves from his oppressive rule. 

The people of England were no more contented with their king than the colonists were, and in 1688 they arose and drove him from the country. In his place

NEW YORK 

191 they put his daughter, Mary, and her husband, Prince 

William of Orange, the head of the Dutch republic.

In 1689, when word of the revolution reached America, the colonists proclaimed their loyalty to King William and Queen Mary, and set about to overthrow Andros and the deputy governors whom King James had placed in charge. Governor Andros happened to be in Boston at that time, and he was promptly seized and thrown into prison. This act marked the end of the Dominion of New England. 

Leisler's Rebellion. The deputy governor in charge of the New York colony was a weak man. When he heard that King James had been driven from the throne, he did not know what to do. As he believed there might be trouble in the colony, he hastily resigned, and not long afterward sailed to England. 

One of the boldest men in the New York colony at this time was a German merchant named Jacob Leisler. 

It seemed to him that a strong leader was needed to hold the colony loyal to King William and Queen Mary, and he was willing to be that leader. Even before the deputy governor left the colony, Leisler took command of the small army stationed in New York City, seized the fort, and called a meeting of the Assembly, which had not met since King James had recalled Governor Dongan. Under the influence of Leisler's strong personality, the Assembly granted him much power in directing the affairs of the colony, so that for a while he was able to serve as the absolute ruler of New York.

After Leisler had been in power for some months,

an event occurred which showed that he had the interests of the colony at heart and that he was able to govern well. An army of French and Indians swooped down from the north and attacked and burned the settlement of Schenectady, not far from Albany. At the same time another attack by the French and Indians 

NEW YORK CITY IN 1704 

By 1704, New York was a thriving little city-one of the most important in the English colonies. The fort which you see was built to protect the city from attack. took place in New England. At once, Leisler sent messengers to the Massachusetts Bay, Plymouth, Connecticut, and Maryland colonies, requesting representatives from each to meet in New York to consider what should be done about the new danger from the north. The representatives arrived, and as a result of the meeting a united army of colonists was sent out to find and destroy the French and Indian raiders. Although the army of colonists failed to find the enemy, the meeting

NEW YORK 

193 which Leisler called in New York was of great importance. It was one of the first attempts to unite the colonies in a common cause, and it showed clearly that when necessary the members of the various colonies could act together. 

Jacob Leisler confidently expected that he would receive a commission as governor from King William and Queen Mary. So he was bitterly disappointed when a ship appeared in New York harbor carrying the news that a new governor from England was on his way to take over the colony's government. On the ship were two companies of the new governor's soldiers, and when these came ashore they found that Leisler and some of his men had locked themselves in the fort and refused to open the gates. The commander of the soldiers demanded that the gates of the fort be opened, but Leisler answered that he would surrender the fort only to the new governor himself. Even when the governor's soldiers attacked the fort, Leisler resisted, and two men were killed before the gates were at last forced open and Leisler and his men were overpowered and captured.

Not long afterwards, Leisler was put to death for his part in the rebellion. Many of the people in the colony felt that this was a cruel way to treat a man who had governed the best he knew how, and for many years there was bitterness because of Leisler's execution. 

The People Gain a Share in the Government. The new governor, Henry Sloughter, had been instructed by King William and Queen Mary to grant to the peo-

ple the right of sharing in the affairs of government, and this he did soon after his arrival. A meeting of the Assembly was called, and ever since that time the people of New York have had a voice in their government. As time went on, the Assembly became more and more powerful and better able to take care of the interests of the people. It was no longer possible for dishonest governors to grow rich at the people's expense or for cruel governors to oppress the people and take away their rights.

The Trial of John Peter Zenger. Nearly fifty years after Leisler's rebellion an event occurred in New York City which was to have important results in every one of the English colonies in America. The governor at that time was a man named Cosby. Governor Cosby was greedy for wealth and power. He controlled the only newspaper in the colony, The New York Gazette, and he was therefore able to decide what news might be printed for the people to read, and what might not. In general, The Gazette was allowed to publish only information favorable to the governor.

In 1733, a new newspaper was established, The New York Weekly Journal. The editor and printer, John 

Peter Zenger, was not controlled by the governor, and he was interested in seeing that the people were kept informed of all facts, whether or not they were favorable to the governor and his friends. Some of the statements which he printed about Governor Cosby displeased the governor, who promptly had Zenger thrown into prison.

NEW YORK 

195 In Philadelphia there lived a famous lawyer named 

Andrew Hamilton. He heard of Zenger's plight and realized that much more was at stake than the imprisonment of a single man. If Governor Cosby were allowed to keep 

Zenger in jail for printing true but unpleasant things about him, no printer would dare to give a fair account of the news for fear of being thrown into prison. Although he was then a very old man, Andrew Hamilton offered to travel from Philadelphia to New York to help 

Zenger win the trial. So well did he plead Zenger's cause that the printer was released from prison. 

Throughout the colonies, the freedom of the press was now assured. 

Sir William Johnson.

Soon after the Zenger

Hamilton won the case, and in doing so he established the freedom of the press in the colonies. trial, there came to America a young Irishman who was to become an important figure in the affairs of the New 

York colony. His name was William Johnson.

William Johnson was just the type of man that the Indians liked. He was brave, honest, and considerate. 

Because he never cheated them, they trusted him. They were pleased when he learned their language and came to visit them. When the Indians had furs to trade they brought them to Johnson, and before long he became enormously wealthy.

As the years went by, Johnson gained much land for himself along the Mohawk river. Here he built himself a beautiful home, Fort Johnson, which later became known as Johnson Hall. Frequent feasts were held at Johnson Hall, and to them came Indians from the surrounding lands. They were just as welcome as Johnson's white neighbors, both English and Dutch, who also enjoyed his hospitality. 

Johnson's most valuable work for the New York colony lay in his dealings with the Indians. Whenever trouble arose, he called together the Iroquois chiefs and urged them to keep their friendship with the English. No other white man had more influence with the Indians. They were always glad to listen to 

William Johnson. It was largely due to him that the 

Iroquois stood by the English and fought hard against the French in later wars between the French and English colonies in America.

The people of Holland were thrifty and hard-working and had a deep love of freedom.

NEW YORK 

197 

Henry Hudson, exploring for the Dutch East India 

Company, discovered the Hudson river and New York bay in 1609, and claimed these waters and the lands about them for Holland. Soon other Dutch explorers and traders came to the New World. The lands claimed by the Dutch were called New Netherland. 

In 1623 the Dutch West India Company sent a party of Walloons to New Netherland, where they established settlements on the Hudson river and on 

Manhattan island. The Manhattan island settlement was named New Amsterdam. 

Peter Minuit, the first governor of New Netherland, purchased Manhattan island from the Indians, and established a fine fur trade with the red men. He was followed by Wouter van Twiller and William Kieft, two governors who did little for the New 

Netherland colony. 

In order to encourage people to move to New Netherland, the Dutch West India Company offered large estates to any members of the company who would take or send fifty grown-up settlers to New Netherland. The owners of these estates were known as patroons. After a time the company began to send free farmers to America.

The last Dutch governor of New Netherland was 

Peter Stuyvesant. He was harsh and headstrong, but he tried to govern for the best interests of the colony. 

By the terms of a treaty which Stuyvesant made with the Connecticut settlers, a boundary was established between New Netherland and Connecticut.

In 1655, Stuyvesant conquered a Swedish colony on Delaware bay and added it to New Netherland. 

In 1664 an English fleet entered the harbor of New Amsterdam, and Peter Stuyvesant was forced to surrender his colony to the English. 

After the English captured the New Netherland colony, they gave the name New York to both the city of New Amsterdam and to one of the three colonies into which they divided New Netherland. 

Colonel Nicolls, the commander of the English fleet which took New Netherland, was the first English governor of New York. Under him the people were given many rights which they had not enjoyed before. 

In 1673, a Dutch fleet appeared in New York harbor, and the English were forced to surrender the lands that had been New Netherland. Later this territory was given back to the English. 

Edmund Andros was the first governor of New York after the colony was again surrendered by the Dutch. He was harsh and headstrong, but served honestly and ably and did much to improve the colony. 

Governor Dongan replaced Governor Andros. In 

1683, he held the first meeting of the New York Assembly, or legislature. The Assembly divided the New 

York colony into counties, each of which was to be represented in the Assembly. Governor Dongan also made a treaty of peace with the Iroquois Indians. 

In 1685 the Duke of York became King James II of England. He combined the New York, New Jersey, Massachusetts Bay, Plymouth, Connecticut, Rhode Is-

NEW YORK 

199 land, and New Hampshire colonies into the Dominion of New England. Edmund Andros was the first governor of the Dominion. Under the new government, the people were not represented in any way. 

When King James II was driven from the throne, the Dominion of New England ceased to exist. Jacob 

Leisler, a New York merchant, seized control of the New York colony and ruled with much power. When a new governor arrived from England, Leisler was put to death because he refused to surrender the fort on 

Manhattan island. 

An important event during Leisler's administration was a meeting of representatives from the New York, Massachusetts Bay, Plymouth, Connecticut, and Maryland colonies to arrange for defense against the French and Indian raiders from the north who were attacking settlements.

Governor Sloughter, who replaced Leisler, granted the people of New York a voice in their government.

When John Peter Zenger published true but unpleasant facts about Governor Cosby of New York, the governor had him thrown into prison. Andrew 

Hamilton, a famous Philadelphia lawyer, defended

Zenger successfully in his trial, and by so doing established the freedom of the press in the English colonies in America. 

Sir William Johnson, an Irishman who had settled in the Mohawk valley, helped the English cause by making friends with the Iroquois Indians.

CHAPTER TEN  

When the English took New Netherland, they divided it, as we have seen, into three colonies. We now know what happened in one of these colonies -New York-under English rule. The history of the other two colonies was very different. Let us learn what happened there.

New Jersey. The Dutch colony of New Netherland included not only the lands around New York bay and the Hudson river, but also the lands south of New 

York bay, along which Cornelis Mey sailed in 1614.

Two years after Mey's voyage along the coast, the Dutch claim to this land was strengthened when a 

Dutchman named Cornelis Hendricksen rounded Cape

May and explored Delaware bay and the lower Delaware river.

Dutch Settlements. In 1617, soon after the first 

Dutch settlements in New Netherland were made, a party of Dutchmen crossed the Hudson river and founded a trading post on its west bank, where Jersey City now is. They called their new settlement Bergen. 

Six years later, in 1623, a group of settlers from Holland arrived at the New Amsterdam settlement to seek homes in the New World. Though some of them settled in New Amsterdam, most of them soon left and traveled south to the Delaware river. On the river's east bank 204

they built a fort, which they named Fort Nassau, and around the fort a little settlement grew up. 

Not long afterwards other parties of Dutchmen from New Amsterdam crossed the Hudson, or North, river and founded a few little trading posts. Except for these, the Dutch made no attempt to colonize the southern section of New Netherland during the time when the colony was in their hands. 

The English Capture the Colony. In 1664, when 

New Netherland was captured by the English, the entire colony, including the southern lands, came under English control. Almost at once the Duke of York sold the land between the Hudson and Delaware rivers to two of his friends, Lord John Berkeley and Sir 

George Carteret. Since Sir George Carteret had once been governor of the island of Jersey, off the French coast, and had defended the island against the enemies of the king's father, the king gave the name New 

Jersey to the land held by the two noblemen.

The Settlement of New Jersey. Sir George Carte ret never visited the New Jersey colony, but in 1664 he sent his nephew, Captain Philip Carteret, to establish settlements and to serve as governor of the colony. 

Early the next year, Governor Carteret and a small group of colonists landed on the eastern shore of New Jersey at the point where some other English settlers had already established a little village, which Carteret named Elizabethtown.

Captain Philip Carteret proved to be a good governor. When he first stepped ashore on the New Jersey

coast, he carried on his shoulder not a gun, but a hoe, to show that he intended to share in the work of his people. This he did. He also enforced well and fairly 

GOVERNOR CARTERET OF NEW JERSEY 

Captain Philip Carteret was the first governor of New Jersey. 

Here we see him about to take possession of his colony. Over his shoulder he carries a hoe, to show that he intends to share in the work of his people. the liberal laws which Sir George Carteret and Lord John Berkeley had framed for the governing of their colony. 

The year after Governor Carteret landed in New Jersey, a group of people from the Connecticut colony settled on the banks of the Passaic river. There they founded a number of settlements, one being Newark.

These settlers were strict Puritans. In the towns which they established only Puritans could vote. 

The Division of New Jersey. In 1673, when the 

Dutch recaptured New Netherland from the English, New Jersey once more passed into Dutch hands. Soon, however, the colony was restored to England. 

The question now arose whether New Jersey still belonged to Carteret and Berkeley or whether the treaty had transferred the ownership of the colony to the king of England. Before the question was settled, Berkeley sold his claim to New Jersey to two members of the Quaker religion, John Fenwicke and Edward Byllynge. Carteret kept his interest in the colony, which he hoped would be restored to him. 

After much discussion, the lawyers and judges of England decided that the colony belonged to the king and not to the proprietors. As he had done before, King Charles II promptly gave New Jersey to his brother, the Duke of York. The duke, for his part, divided the colony into two sections. The northeastern section, called East Jersey, he gave to Sir George Carteret, who restored his nephew, Philip Carteret, to the governorship. The duke gave the southwestern section, called West Jersey, to Fenwicke and Byllynge. 

The Settlement of West Jersey. Quakers were then being persecuted both in England and in most of the American colonies, and Fenwicke and Byllynge wanted to establish a colony where Quakers could worship as they wished. Neither of the new proprietors, however, had enough money to carry out their purpose, and

they soon sold West Jersey to three other Quakers.

One of these, William Penn, became a very important man in the English colonies in America. We shall learn more about him when we read about Pennsylvania. 

In 1677, a set of laws was drawn up for the West Jersey colony. These laws were largely the work of 

William Penn. They provided for an Assembly of one hundred members to be chosen each year to represent the people. This Assembly was to have most of the governing power of the colony. The laws also provided for complete religious freedom. 

In the same year when Penn's code of laws was drawn up, an English ship sailed up the Delaware river with 230 Quaker colonists. They landed on the river's east bank and established the settlement of Burlington. The new settlement grew rapidly, for many hundred new colonists arrived during the first year. Burlington was soon a thriving town. 

Unfortunately, the Quaker settlers of West Jersey were never actually governed by Penn's fine code of laws. Soon after they arrived, the Duke of York gave 

Edward Byllynge the sole right of governing the colony. Byllynge at once appointed a deputy governor, and the two of them called a meeting of the West Jersey Assembly. When the Assembly met, its members passed a new set of laws providing for government by a governor and an advisory council.

Trouble with Governor Andros. When Edmund 

Andros was governor of the New York colony, he was very anxious to gain control of East and West Jersey

as well. Even though he knew that the Duke of York had given these lands away, he regarded the two Jersey colonies as part of New York, and he felt that the New Jersey settlers should pay taxes and duties to New York's governor. This they refused to do. 

In 1680, therefore, Governor Andros sent soldiers to the Elizabethtown settlement to arrest Governor Philip Carteret. The soldiers dragged poor Governor 

Carteret out of his bed and took him to New York, where he remained in prison for four weeks. At the end of that time he was released, but he was told that he must not perform his duties as governor until the question of the taxes which he and his people owed to the New York government was settled. 

A month after Governor Carteret was released from prison, the Assembly of East Jersey met, and Governor Edmund Andros appeared before it. As before, Andros claimed that Carteret and other New Jersey citizens owed taxes to New York. The members of the Assembly, however, did not believe that Andros was right, and they refused to order Carteret or the others to pay any taxes or duties to New York.

The West Jersey colony, too, had trouble with Governor Andros, who claimed the right to lay a tax on all goods which the colonists imported from England. The colonists, of course, refused to pay the tax, and took the matter to the English law courts. 

The case was decided in their favor, and Andros was unable to collect any money from the West Jersey people.

The Sale of East Jersey. When Sir George Carteret died in 1680, he had a good many debts. For two years a group of eight men ran his colony in the hope of making some money from it to pay these debts. 

They did not succeed, and in 1682 they offered the entire East Jersey colony for sale at auction. William 

Penn and eleven other Quakers bought it for about $17,000. Later each of these twelve proprietors sold half of his share in the colony to another Quaker, so that East Jersey was owned by twenty-four people. 

The twenty-four Quakers who owned East Jersey drew up a set of laws and established a government for their colony. All free men were permitted to vote and to worship as they pleased. All local government was to be in the hands of the people. Everyone should pay a share of the taxes. No person should be imprisoned without trial by jury. The government of the colony was placed in the hands of a governor and a body of directors known as the American Board of 

Proprietors. 

New Jersey Becomes a Royal Colony. For two years, 1688 and 1689, both sections of New Jersey were included in the Dominion of New England. 

Though the proprietors of East Jersey and West Jersey regained their rights when the Dominion was dissolved, they finally, in 1702, sold their rights of governing the colony to the English king. The king, in turn, united East and West Jersey into the single royal colony of New Jersey, with an Assembly to represent the people. Until 1738 the governor of New York

also served as the governor of New Jersey, though the two colonies were separate. At length, however, the people of New Jersey protested against this practice and were given a governor of their own. 

Delaware. We now come to the story of Delaware, the smallest English colony in America with the exception of Rhode Island. Before Delaware became an 

English colony, it was for a time a colony of two other countries. Let us learn how this came about. 

Early Explorers. It is believed that the first white man to visit what is now Delaware was Henry Hudson, who sailed into Delaware bay in 1609, while searching for a passage across the North American continent to the Pacific. After exploring the bay and satisfying himself that this was not the passage he sought, Hudson left Delaware bay and sailed on to the north where he soon afterwards discovered New York bay and the Hudson river.

Two years later an English explorer, Lord Delaware, also sailed into Delaware bay. Delaware did not remain to carry on extensive explorations, but in later years Delaware bay, the Delaware river, and the colony of Delaware were named in his honor. 

Five years after Lord Delaware's explorations, a 

Dutch explorer, Cornelis Hendricksen, visited Delaware bay. Hendricksen carried his explorations much farther than either Hudson or Delaware, for he not only explored the shores of the bay but also sailed for some distance up the river itself. The glowing account which he carried back to Holland made the

Dutch more anxious than ever to establish settlements in the New World. 

The First Settlement. In 1631, a small group of 

Dutch colonists from Holland landed just south of 

KING GUSTAVUS ADOLPHUS In 1624, King Gustavus Adolphus of Sweden issued a charter to the South Company, which was formed to establish Swedish colonies in the New World. 

Delaware bay and established a settlement. Not. long afterwards a band of Indians fell upon the settlement and completely destroyed it, killing all of the inhabitants.

Although this first little settlement came to a sad and early end, it had one very important result. At the time when it was settled, the English were planning to found the Maryland colony a little to the south, and the presence of the

Dutch colonists estab lished a Dutch claim to the land about Delaware bay, and kept the Mary- land colonists from making any settlements in that section. 

The South Company. Sweden at this time was being ruled by a strong king, Gustavus Adolphus.

Gustavus Adolphus was interested in planting Swedish

colonies in the New World, and in 1624 he issued a charter to a trading and colonizing company known as the South Company. Though at first this company included Swedish, Dutch, and German members, the Swedes soon bought out the rights of the Dutch and the Germans, and the company came entirely under 

Swedish control. 

The Swedes Come to America. In 1638, after the death of King Gustavus Adolphus, the South Company sent a group of Swedish colonists to the New World under the command of Peter Minuit, the first governor of New Netherland, who had bought Manhattan island from the Indians. 

Near Delaware bay, Minuit and his colonists founded 

Fort Christina, named in honor of Christina, the child queen of Sweden. As he had done before in New 

Netherland, Minuit won the friendship of the Indians by buying from them the land where he proposed to settle. The entire territory which he bought was given the name New Sweden. 

Unfortunately, Governor Minuit did not live long enough to give the little colony of New Sweden the benefit of the experience he had gained in New Netherland. In 1638, the very year when his colonists landed in the New World, the governor set out on a trading expedition to the West Indies. A storm occurred while he was at sea, and no trace was ever found of the ship or anyone on board.

For five years the Swedish government did nothing to encourage its colony in the New World. Then, in

SWEDISH SETTLERS LANDING IN AMERICA 

In 1638, a band of Swedish settlers sent out by the South Company landed on the shores of Delaware bay and founded the first settlement in the colony of New Sweden, which later became the Delaware colony. 

1643, a new governor named Johan Printz arrived. 

Governor Printz established some new settlements, the most important of which was Upland. 

Trouble with the Dutch. The Dutch, as you remember, had a claim to the Delaware region because of the explorations of Hendricksen, and because they had established a settlement there before the arrival of the Swedes.

When Peter Stuyvesant became governor of New Netherland, the Dutch began to take a new interest in these lands to which they had a claim. Before long, Governor Stuyvesant sent a group of Dutchmen to the Delaware region, and they built a fort at the present site of Newcastle. They named it Fort Casimir. 

When the Swedish governor saw the Dutch coming into New Sweden, he felt that he must stop them if he could. So he led his Swedish soldiers against 

Fort Casimir and captured it without much trouble. 

It was renamed Fort Trinity. 

As might be expected, Peter Stuyvesant was furious when he heard what had happened to his fort. With the encouragement of the Dutch West India Company, he sent a force of seven warships and seven hundred soldiers to conquer all of New Sweden. The Swedes had no warships and very few soldiers, so they made no attempt to resist the Dutch. The colony was peacefully surrendered and became a part of New Netherland in 1655. Most of the colonists did not move away, but remained and became subjects of the New Netherland government.

The English Take the Colony. In 1664, when the 

English under Colonel Nicolls took New Netherland, the land that had been New Sweden passed into their possession. This section was then given the name 

Delaware in honor of Lord Delaware, who had explored the region a number of years before. 

Delaware was included in the lands granted by Charles II to the Duke of York, who made Newcastle

the capital of the colony. Since the Duke of York was anxious not to have people from other colonies settle near Newcastle, he established a northern boundary line, all points of which were twelve miles from the capital. No settlers from other colonies were permitted to build homes within this boundary. This accounts for the curved northern boundary line of the present state of Delaware. 

In 1680, the Duke of York, wishing to make a profit from his Delaware colony, leased it to William Penn, the Quaker leader. Soon it became a part of a new colony, Pennsylvania, which William Penn established. 

The people of Delaware and of Pennsylvania, however, did not get on well together, and in 1691 Delaware was placed under a deputy governor, who was responsible to the governor of Pennsylvania. In 1704, Delaware was granted a separate legislature. Many years later the colony secured a governor of its own and became completely independent of Pennsylvania. 

 The explorations of Mey and Hendricksen, Dutch explorers, gave the Dutch a claim to the lands south of New York bay. These lands were part of New 

Netherland. 

Not long after the first settlements were made in New Netherland, a number of Dutch traders crossed to the west bank of the Hudson and founded Bergen.

Later Fort Nassau and other settlements were founded.

In 1664, when New Netherland was captured by the English, the land between the Hudson and Delaware rivers passed into their hands. The English called this territory New Jersey. It was sold by the Duke of 

York to Sir George Carteret and Lord John Berkeley. 

Sir George Carteret chose his nephew, Captain 

Philip Carteret, to govern New Jersey. In 1665, a party of colonists headed by Governor Carteret settled at Elizabethtown.

The year after Carteret and his colonists landed in New Jersey, a group of Puritans from Connecticut. settled on the banks of the Passaic river and founded some settlements, including Newark. 

In 1673, the Dutch recaptured New Netherland. 

When the colony was returned to the English, Lord John Berkeley sold his share of New Jersey to two Quakers. The colony was divided into East Jersey, owned by Carteret, and West Jersey, owned by the two Quakers.

The two Quakers to whom West Jersey was sold. resold it to three other Quakers, one of whom was. 

William Penn. Not long afterwards several groups of colonists settled in West Jersey. 

Both the East and West Jersey colonists had trouble with Governor Andros of New York, who tried unsuccessfully to collect taxes from them. 

In 1682, after the death of Sir George Carteret, the East Jersey colony was sold to William Penn and eleven other Quakers. Twelve more Quakers later bought shares in the colony.

In 1702 the king of England combined East and West Jersey into the single royal colony of New Jersey. 

Henry Hudson, Lord Delaware, and Cornelis Hendricksen were the first explorers to visit Delaware bay. 

The first settlement in what is now Delaware was made in 1631 by a group of Dutch colonists. This settlement was soon destroyed by the Indians. 

In 1638, the South Company of Sweden sent a group of Swedish colonists to the New World. They founded Fort Christina near Delaware bay and named their colony New Sweden. Peter Minuit was the governor.

The second governor of New Sweden, Johan Printz, improved and strengthened the colony. In 1655, however, the Swedes surrendered New Sweden to the Dutch. It thus became a part of New Netherland. 

In 1664, when the English took New Netherland, all the land that had been New Sweden passed into their possession. They named it Delaware. 

William Penn leased the Delaware colony from the Duke of York in 1680. For a time, Delaware was part of Pennsylvania, but later became a separate colony.

CHAPTER ELEVEN PENNSYLVANIA 

Two hundred and fifty years ago many kings believed that they had been chosen by God and that they were better than other men. People were supposed to remove their hats, bow very low, and be most respectful in the presence of a king. If they did not, they were likely to be severely punished, and perhaps even put to death. The colony of Pennsylvania was founded by a man who would not even take off his hat in the presence of the king of England. 

George Fox and the Friends. Not long after the

Pilgrims came to the New World and founded the Plymouth colony, a boy was born in England whose name was George Fox. During his boyhood, George tended sheep. As he wandered about the countryside, guiding his sheep, he carried with him a Bible which he often read. The more he read about the way in which Christ had lived, the surer he became that the world was a sinful and wicked place, and that people would be much happier if they would only follow Christ's example and live together in peace and brotherly love. The more Fox thought about this the better it seemed to him. At last he decided to try to lead others to his way of thinking.

Soon George Fox had many followers-peace-loving people who were convinced that the ways of the world 221

GEORGE FOX PREACHING IN THE STREETS OF AN ENGLISH TOWN 

George Fox, an English shepherd, founded the Society of

Friends. The members of this society, who were called Quakers, did not believe in war, in personal pride, or in taking oaths.

PENNSYLVANIA 

223 were sinful, and that all men should live together in perfect friendship. They called themselves the Friends. 

The Friends believed that all men were equal, so they would not take off their hats to show respect even to the king. Because the term "you" was a title of respect, they used the words "thee" and "thou." They refused to wear the rich clothes of the day, for they thought that fine clothes were a cause of pride and of envy. The 

Friends felt that every man had a conscience and that this conscience, or "inner voice" as they called it, should be his guide, and not the teachings of the ministers. Because the Bible says, "Swear not at all," they would take no oath, not even the oath of loyalty to the king. And because the Bible says, "Blessed are the peace-makers," they would not fight for any cause. They also refused to pay taxes, because much of the tax money was used to support the army, the navy, and the Church of England, which the Friends did not approve of. 

QUAKERS 

The Friends, or Quakers, dressed in clothes of gray, brown, and other quiet colors.

The Friends' Church Meetings. The church meet ings of the Friends seemed very strange to the other people of England. The Friends' churches were plain, without steeples or elaborate decorations. All the men sat on one side of the church and the women on the other. There was no set form of service and no sermon. Often many minutes would go by, while the people sat in silence, thinking religious thoughts. 

All at once the silence would be broken as a man or woman rose and addressed the others, giving some message inspired by "the inner light of conscience."

How the Quakers Got Their Name. The Friends believed that it was their duty to interfere with the church services held by people of other religions. One 

Sunday, George Fox walked into a church where the Church of England service was being carried on. 

Without even taking off his hat, he interrupted the service, speaking in a loud voice about his beliefs. He was at once arrested and taken before a judge. 

Even in court, Fox would not remove his hat.

Looking the judge straight in the eye, he said to him, "I bid thee tremble before the word of the Lord!" "I bid thee quake before the law!" the judge answered. From that time on, George Fox and the other Friends were known as "Quakers." 

The members

The Persecution of the Quakers. 

of the Church of England did not understand the Quakers, and many thought that they were inspired by the devil. So the Quakers were forbidden to hold church services, and were fined, imprisoned, and pun-

PENNSYLVANIA 

225 ished in many cruel ways whenever they dared to speak in public. This was true not only in England but in most of the English colonies in America. In Massachusetts, the Puritans even put to death four Quakers who dared return to Boston after having been sent away. Only in Rhode Island and Maryland were the 

Quakers permitted to settle and hold their services. 

William Penn. At the time when the Quakers were first preaching their beliefs in England, there was a student at Oxford University named William Penn.

He was a son of Admiral William Penn, one of the boldest and most skilful commanders in the English navy. Young William was a strong, handsome youth, a leader in both studies and athletics. During his early student days he enjoyed the many pleasures of life at Oxford just as any young man might do. 

Then a Quaker preacher came to Oxford and lectured to many of the students. William Penn liked the things the preacher said, and decided to become a Quaker. From that time on, he refused to attend the University chapel. He took off his student's gown because he regarded it as "a badge of pride." Other 

Quaker students did the same thing, and they even began to remove the gowns from fellow students who were not Quakers. This the University would not allow. Penn and the other young Quakers were expelled from Oxford.

Admiral Penn had always wanted his son to follow in his footsteps and become a commander in the navy. 

He was, therefore, much upset when he heard that

William had become a Quaker. He sent the boy to 

France, in the hope that the gay life at the French court would make him give up his Quaker ideas. After being in France for a time, William served in the English army during a war against the Dutch, and still later he helped to manage an estate of his father's in Ireland. All seemed to be going as the Admiral wished, but in Ireland William attended a Quaker meeting, and once more became an enthusiastic Quaker. 

This time Admiral Penn was even more angry than he had been before. He told William, "You may 'thee' and 'thou' whom you please, except the king, the Duke of York, and myself." William would not agree to these three exceptions, and he was driven from his father's house. From that time on he served as a 

Quaker preacher, making frequent speeches and writing many pamphlets. Though he was often arrested, he was usually released after a short time because of his father's influence. One day he went to see King

Charles II, and, in his Quaker way, walked into the king's presence without removing his hat. The king gazed at him for a moment, then removed his own hat. "Friend Charles," said Penn, "why dost thou remove thy hat?" "Because," answered the king, "where I am, it is the custom for only one to remain covered." 

William Penn Gains Lands in the New World. 

King Charles owed William Penn's father a great deal of money, and when the Admiral died the young man inherited this debt. He knew that his chances

PENNSYLVANIA 

227 of being paid in money were not very good, as the king had many debts and was constantly adding to them by spending more money than Parliament would grant him. As William thought about the debt, a plan occurred to him for doing a great service to the Quakers of England. The king, he knew, had broad lands in North America for which he had very little use. If the king could be persuaded to give up some of these lands in payment of the debt, a colony might be founded where people of all religions could worship as they pleased without fear of punishment. Penn regarded the founding of such a colony as "a holy experiment."

The king was delighted to cancel the debt, and gave Penn a great stretch of land between the New 

York and Maryland colonies. Penn named his new possession Sylvania, which means "woodland," but the king changed the name to Pennsylvania in honor of Admiral Penn. 

In 1681 The First Settlers in Pennsylvania. 

William Penn sent to his colony a shipload of colonists -mostly Quakers. Some of these settled at Newcastle on Delaware bay, and some at Upland, later called 

Chester, on the Delaware river.

Newcastle and Upland were not included in the territory of Pennsylvania which King Charles II had granted to William Penn. The Duke of York, however, had leased the Delaware colony to Penn in 1680, so his colonists had a right to settle there. As there were already a good many Swedish and English people

living in Newcastle and Upland, the first Quaker colonists did not suffer the hardships of building homes in the wilderness, as was the case with the Jamestown and Plymouth settlers.

The next year Penn and a hundred more colonists sailed from England on the ship Welcome. It was a terrible voyage, for smallpox broke out while the ship was in mid-ocean, and before land was sighted more than thirty of the colonists had died. On October 12, 1682, the Welcome arrived at Newcastle, and Penn and his settlers went ashore.

The Great Law. Not long after his arrival in the 

New World, William Penn called a meeting at Upland of all the Swedish, Dutch, and English settlers. After making a short friendly speech, the Quaker leader told the people about the constitution, or set of laws, under which they were to be governed. 

Penn's constitution was known as the Great Law. 

It provided that the colonists should be free to worship in any way they saw fit; that all settlers who paid taxes had the right to vote; that every male member of any Christian church might hold office; that all children should begin training for some trade or useful occupation at the age of twelve; that only two crimes, murder and treason, should be punishable by death; and that all prisoners should be put to work at some useful trade so that they might become good citizens. 

These last two provisions of Penn's Great Law are especially interesting in view of the fact that in England at this time there were over two hundred

PENNSYLVANIA 

229 crimes punishable by death, and that no one had ever thought before of making a prison anything but a place in which to lock up people who had done wrong.
