The Founding of Philadelphia. William Penn was not content to settle in 

Newcastle or Upland, but wished to establish a settlement of his own on the land which King Charles 

II had given him. Even before his arrival, some of the men whom he had sent from England had traveled north and selected as a fine place for a city the spot where the 

Schuylkill river flowed into the Delaware. Here 

Penn and many of his followers went soon after their arrival in America. 

MANHEIM LANCASTER CONESTOGA VALLI CONESTOGA GERMANTOWN SUSQUEHANN PHILADELPHIA UPLAND NEWCASTLE KEY TO CHANGED NAMES FORT CASIMIR-NEWCASTLE FORT TRINITY=NEWCASTLE UPLAND - CHESTER LAWARER. 

PHILADELPHIA AND VICINITY 

Most of the early settlements in Pennsylvania were made in the southeastern part of the colony. This map shows a num- 

For the new city which he proposed to build, William Penn chose the name Philadelphia 

Greek word meaning ber of these settlements. "brotherly love." It was his sincere hope that the people of Philadelphia would live together in peace. 

Unlike most cities, Philadelphia did not just grow up. It was carefully and intelligently planned. Too

many European cities of those days had dark, narrow, dirty streets, with many buildings crowded close together. In such cities epidemics of disease were common, and there was constant danger of terrible fires that would spread for miles and leave thousands of people homeless. Philadelphia, Penn said, should be "a clean country town, which will never be burned and always be wholesome."

Straight, broad streets were laid out for the new city of Philadelphia, some running north and south and some east and west. There were five open squares, one in the center of the city where the City Hall now stands, and one in the center of each of the four quarters of the town. The city blocks were divided into large lots, so that each house would have much land around it to reduce the danger of fires spreading. As the city grew and many houses were built, trees were planted about them, and broad green lawns were laid out. These trees and lawns added much to the attractiveness of Philadelphia.

Relations with the Indians. The many Indian tribes of Pennsylvania belonged to the Algonquin and the Iroquois nations. In the southern part of the colony lived Algonquin tribes. The more northerly lands were held by the Iroquois. Most of the Indians living near Philadelphia were Algonquins, belonging to a tribe known as the Delawares. 

Soon after William Penn's arrival in the New 

World, he held a meeting with the neighboring Indians.

The Indians came dressed in their war paint and

PENNSYLVANIA 

231 carrying bows, arrows, and spears. Penn and his followers were dressed in clothes of quiet colors, and were unarmed. Penn told the Indians that the Quakers 

PENN'S TREATY WITH THE INDIANS 

William Penn believed in treating the Indians fairly. Soon after his arrival in Pennsylvania he made a treaty with the red men, which was never broken while he remained alive. never carried arms, never made war, and wished to be friends of the red men.

Before the meeting ended, Penn made a treaty with the Indians. It was agreed that the settlers would buy from the red men whatever land they needed, instead of taking it by force. Whenever there was a

dispute between an Indian and a white man, it was to be settled by a council made up of six Indians and six colonists. The Indians, for their part, showed that they could understand kindness and fair treatment, for they said, "So long as the sun and moon shall shine, the Indians and the English will live together in love and peace." Penn's treaty with the Indians was never broken by either the red men or the Quakers as long as he remained alive.

The Walking Purchase. As new colonists arrived in Pennsylvania, William Penn bought small sections of land from the Indians, paying for them with cloth, trinkets, knives, and other things which the Indians liked. Finally, he decided that instead of buying many small plots of land it would be better to buy one large area and divide it up into smaller sections as the need arose. He, therefore, held a meeting with the Indians, and they agreed to sell him, in exchange for several chests full of goods, a stretch of land extending from the Delaware river as far to the west as a man could walk in three days.

Accompanied by a few Quakers and some Indians, Penn set out to the west. He walked slowly, stopping occasionally to rest, and when he had traveled for a day and a half he announced that he now had all the land he needed, and that he would do the rest of the walking some other time. Many years later this "walking purchase" was completed by the colonists.

The manner in which it was done caused hard feeling on the part of the Indians, as we shall see.

PENNSYLVANIA 

233 

New Settlers in Pennsylvania. Although Pennsyl vania was settled long after most of the other English colonies in America, it grew much faster than any of them, and soon became one of the most important and prosperous. One reason for this was the advertising campaign which William Penn carried on in Europe. 

Thousands of pamphlets were printed, telling of the cheap, fertile land, the liberal government, and the religious freedom of the new colony. As a result, thousands of Germans from the Rhine valley crossed the ocean and settled in the fertile lands in the southeastern part of the colony. The Scotch-Irish also came in large numbers-bold, vigorous people who some time before had moved from Scotland into northern Ireland. Most of these scorned to settle in the quiet, fertile east, but traveled on to the uninhabited western part of the colony and founded new towns in the wilderness. In addition, a constant stream of Quakers came from England, and there were smaller groups of settlers from many other European countries. By 

1684, there were nearly 8,000 people in Pennsylvania, of whom more than 2,000 lived in the thriving city of Philadelphia.

Pastorius and the Mennonites. Many people who were being persecuted in Europe because of their beliefs were attracted to Pennsylvania by the promise of religious freedom. In 1683, only a year after Penn himself had landed, a German religious leader named 

Francis Daniel Pastorius arrived from Europe with a band of pilgrims known as the Mennonites. These

people settled at Germantown, not far from Philadelphia, and were soon joined by so many others from the Rhine region of Germany that Germantown became the most important German settlement in the New 

World. Today it is a part of Philadelphia. 

Most of the Germantown settlers had been linen weavers in Europe, and they planned to carry on this occupation in America. They soon found, however, that the flax plant from which linen cloth is made would not grow in Pennsylvania, so they turned their attention to the raising of sheep, and established the woolen industry at Germantown, where it is still important. 

Kelpius and the Pietists. Also from Germany there came a group of people known as the Pietists, led by John Kelpius. Like the Quakers, the Pietists believed in living as Christ had lived, and in being kind to all men. They thought that the common worldly pleasures were sinful, and they were strongly opposed to dancing, to the theater, and to public games of all sorts. Soon after their arrival, Kelpius and his Pietists built a monastery on the Wissahickon river near Philadelphia. 

Becker and the Dunkers. Another group which early came to Pennsylvania were the Dunkers, members of the German Baptist Church who were being persecuted in their own country. Under the leadership of Peter Becker, a group of Dunkers left Germany and settled at Germantown. Within the next few years other groups of Dunkers left Europe and came to the

PENNSYLVANIA 

235 colony. As their numbers increased, the members of the church established settlements of their own, one of which was Ephrata, where they built a monastery. 

The Dunkers who settled at Ephrata were the strictest members of their religion. They did not believe in missionaries, in Sunday schools, or in advanced education. For a long time they denied themselves all pleasures and all luxuries. They carried on very little trade with the other settlers, for their settlement at Ephrata was intended to be self-supporting. Shoes, clothes, dishes, tableware, and, in short, almost all of the necessities of life were made by members of the Ephrata settlement. They even printed their own books from paper made in Ephrata. 

Count Zinzendorf and the Moravians. Still another religious group played an important part in the history of Pennsylvania. These were the Moravians. The purpose of the Moravians was "to know God, to love Him, to do His Commandments, and to submit to His will." The rules of the Moravians divided the people into three groups according to personal behavior-the Perfect, the Proficient, and the Beginners. Led by a 

German nobleman named Count Zinzendorf a group of Moravians came to Pennsylvania and established 

Bethlehem, Nazareth, and other settlements.

One of the most important activities of the Moravians was the sending out of missionaries, and the Bethlehem settlers soon began to send members of their religion in every direction. Many Iroquois and 

Algonquin Indians were taught the Christian religion,

and it was not long before some Moravians even pushed through the forests of northern North America to the shores of the Arctic ocean to preach to the Eskimos. 

One group of Moravians who came to Pennsylvania was led by Baron Henry Stiegel, a wealthy German nobleman. Stiegel and his followers settled near 

Ephrata and founded a little town called Manheim.

Here workshops were built for the manufacture of glass and ironware. The products of the Manheim shops were soon in use in many of the English colonies in America. 

Dr. Muhlenberg and the Lutherans. On the heels of all these groups came the Lutherans. They, too, found life in Germany very hard, because of their beliefs. Their leader was Dr. Henry Muhlenberg. 

Most of the Lutherans settled along Perkiomen creek. 

The "Pennsylvania Dutch." With so many people coming to the Pennsylvania colony from Germany, it is small wonder that Pennsylvania soon became more German than English in population. Today the greater part of the "Pennsylvania Dutch" are descendants of the original German settlers who crossed the ocean to avoid religious persecution.

Welsh Settlers. From the little country of Wales, west of England, came a band of Quakers to settle in Pennsylvania. They purchased a large section of land not far from Philadelphia and there established a number of settlements. The thrifty, hard-working 

Welshmen proved to be good farmers, and soon many of them were very well-to-do. In later years many

PENNSYLVANIA 

237 more Welsh people came to Pennsylvania. Some of them settled in the mining districts in the central part of the colony. 

The Redemptioners. We have seen how the buying of Negro slaves began in Virginia and the other southern colonies because the settlers needed workers to labor in the cotton and tobacco fields. In Pennsylvania and the colonies farther to the north it was not profitable to have slaves. There the climate was not suited to the growing of cotton and other plantation crops, and during the long, cold winters Negro workers had little useful labor to do, yet had to be fed and sheltered. 

Nevertheless, servants were needed for housework and to help on the farms, and a great many poor people were brought from Europe for this purpose. Most of these were redemptioners.

William Penn's Return to England. After spending two years in the New World, William Penn returned to England to settle some personal affairs there and to visit his family. Though he expected to stay in England only a short time, he found so many important things to do there that he remained for many years.

Not long after Penn's return to England, his friend, King Charles II, died, and the king's brother, James

II, came to the throne. James was not popular and, after a brief rule, was overthrown and driven from the country. Both King Charles and King James had liked William Penn, and during their rules he had had much influence. The new king and queen of England,

William and Mary, were not friendly to Penn and did little to help him. Several times during his stay in 

England he was imprisoned for his Quaker beliefs, and much of his wealth was spent in paying fines. 

Another serious blow was the death of his wife.

Affairs in the Colony. In the meantime, affairs in the colony had not been going well. Thomas Lloyd, the governor whom Penn had left in charge, did his best to govern the colony successfully. After some years in office, however, he was removed by an order from the English government. 

The order from the English government came because King William and Queen Mary had decided to take the Pennsylvania colony away from William 

Penn. For two years, Penn's settlers in the New 

World were ruled by the governor of New York.

After the colony had been for two years under the leadership of New York's governor, William Penn was again given control of it. At once, he placed his cousin, William Markham, in charge. Markham was a pleasant, easy-going man, but he was not a good governor, and the colonists had many troubles during the time he was in office. 

Penn's Return to the Colony. William Penn had now been in England for fifteen long years. When word reached him of the trouble that Governor Markham was having, he at once returned to America, bringing with him two of his sons and a daughter, and a new wife whom he had married while he was in England.

PENNSYLVANIA 

239 It was Penn's intention to spend the rest of his life in Pennsylvania. He had two houses in the colony, one in Philadelphia and one on a great estate beside the Delaware river. This second house was one of the 

WILLIAM PENN'S COUNTRY HOUSE 

This house, in which Penn lived, was one of the most beautiful homes in the entire Pennsylvania colony. most beautiful in the entire colony. It was a brick building, two stories high, with a roof of tile, and a large reservoir for holding the water needed in the house. This reservoir was considered a great luxury, for most people had to go with pitchers or buckets to the public well whenever they needed water. 

The lands about William Penn's house were well

cared for. There were beautiful lawns and gardens. 

Not far from the house were stables, workshops, a bakery, and even a brewery for the making of beer. 

In the stables were many horses and a large and comfortable coach, and in the river before the house a six-oared barge was anchored.

Penn's Second Trip to England. Much to Penn's disappointment, his affairs in England once more called him back across the ocean after a stay of only two years in his colony. This time he appointed James 

Logan to govern during his absence. Penn did not take his wife, her baby boy, and his older children with him, but left them in Pennsylvania. 

Poor William Penn was never able to return to

America. As year after year went by and matters in 

England held him, he grew discouraged. At last he decided to sell Pennsylvania to King William and 

Queen Mary. A price was agreed on-a low price, not even equal to the amount which King Charles II had owed Penn's father-and the sale was ready to be completed, when Penn was suddenly taken ill. 

He never recovered sufficiently to complete the sale, and at his death the colony of Pennsylvania became the property of his wife, who left it to his sons. 

Trouble With the Indians. Some time after Penn's death, the treaty which he had made with the Indians was broken. This was not the fault of the Indians, but of the Englishmen. Some of the leaders in the colony were not honest and fair in their dealings, and their actions led to bitter feelings.

PENNSYLVANIA 

241 

The "Walking Purchase" Is Completed. You will recall that when William Penn made his "walking purchase" of land from the Indians, he walked for only a day and a half of the three days allowed him, and said that he would walk the rest of the distance some other time. Fifty years later the walk was completed, but not by William Penn.

It is said that one of Penn's sons, Thomas, was responsible for completing the "walking purchase."

This time the walk was no friendly, leisurely journey through the woods, with frequent stops. First, a long straight path was cleared through the forest, and three of the fastest walkers in the colony were carefully trained. When the day came to start, the three white walkers set off at such a fast pace that their Indian companions had to run to keep up with them. Hour after hour they walked, with very few stops, and as they went deeper and deeper into the forest the Indians became more and more distressed. At the end of the first day the red men refused to go any farther. "No sit down to smoke," they said, "no shoot a squirrel, but run, run, all day long!" Nevertheless, the three white walkers kept on, and at the end of a day and a half they had traveled seventy miles.

The Indians were so indignant at the way in which Thomas Penn and the other white men had acted that they refused to move from the land which the white men now claimed. Thomas Penn knew, however, that the Delaware Indians were very much afraid of the Iroquois, and he threatened to call upon the Iroquois

to drive them out if they did not give up their lands to his settlers. The Indians were forced to agree, and moved away, but from that time on there was little good feeling between the red men and the white in 

Pennsylvania. 

The Conestoga Indian Affair. Some distance to the west of Philadelphia, on the Susquehanna river, was a little settlement called Paxton. One night some white settlers living near Paxton were murdered, and the people decided that the crime must have been done by Indians from an Indian village called Conestoga. 

So a group of armed men from Paxton swept down on Conestoga when most of the warriors were away, and destroyed the village completely, killing men, women, and children.

At the time of the massacre, some of the Conestoga Indians were in the settlement of Lancaster, and the people there promptly put them in prison. They did so for the double purpose of keeping the Indians from attacking the white men who had destroyed their village, and of keeping the white men from massacring more of the Indians. But in spite of this, to Lancaster came a group of mounted men from Paxton. Straight to the jail they went, overpowered the guards, and killed the Indians. Then they went on to find the rest of the warriors and kill them too. They were prevented from doing this by a company of soldiers from Philadelphia who met them at the Schuylkill river and forced them to return home.

The affair of the Conestoga Indians meant the

PENNSYLVANIA 

243 breaking of William Penn's treaty and the end of peaceful relations between the white men and their red-skin neighbors.

The Cornplanter Indians. The Cornplanter Indians were a group of Iroquois whose half-white chief was named Cornplanter. For a long time they were unfriendly to the Pennsylvania settlers, and they fought actively against them in the wars which later came between the French and English colonists in America, and between the English colonists and their mother country. In time, however, Cornplanter and his followers once more became friendly neighbors. 

The Founding of Harrisburg. About twenty years after William Penn first landed in the New World, an Englishman named John Harris crossed the ocean and settled in Philadelphia. After a while he moved to the west and built a house on the Susquehanna river at a shallow place where it was not difficult to cross on foot or on horseback. As many Indians came to this point on the Susquehanna in order to cross the river, Harris was able to build up a profitable trade with the red men without traveling far from his home, as other traders usually had to do. 

Some years later, when trade with the Indians began to die out, Harris' son, also named John, ran a flatboat, or ferry, back and forth across the river, carrying men and goods. This means of crossing dry shod attracted people to Harris' landing place, and, in time, the town of Harrisburg grew up there. Today, Harrisburg is the capital of the state of Pennsylvania.

Pennsylvania's Relations with Other Colonies. 

The kings of England who granted charters to men who wished to establish colonies in the New World usually knew very little about the land which they were giving away. For this reason, the wording of their charters was often vague and indefinite. As a result there were many quarrels between the people of the different colonies over the ownership of land.

The Dispute with Maryland. The colony which bordered Pennsylvania on the south was Maryland. 

Not long after the first settlers arrived in Pennsylvania a serious dispute broke out between the people of the two colonies over the location of the boundary line. A broad strip of land, including the city of 

Philadelphia, was claimed by both colonies. Though 

William Penn and Lord Baltimore twice met to settle the dispute, they could not agree, and the question remained unsettled.

The boundary question became serious when representatives of the Maryland government tried to collect taxes from people who believed that they were living in Pennsylvania. Fighting broke out, and a few people were killed on both sides. It is said that one of the 

Maryland leaders was captured and taken to Philadelphia. Looking about him with satisfaction, he declared that in his opinion this was the finest city in all Maryland.

After the quarrel had been going on for more than fifty years in the courts of both England and America, an agreement was finally reached, and the hard feeling

PENNSYLVANIA 

245 died out. But it was not until the year 1763 that two English surveyors named Mason and Dixon were employed by Maryland and Pennsylvania to determine the exact boundary line between the two colonies. This they did, in spite of many difficulties, including attacks by the Indians. Every five miles they set up a stone pillar, bearing on one side the coat-of-arms of Lord Baltimore and on the other side the coat-of-arms of 

William Penn. In later years the Mason and Dixon line, as it was called, became famous as the boundary line between the slave states and the free states of our country.

The Dispute with Connecticut. The charter which 

Connecticut had received from the king of England stated that the colony should extend westward as far as the Pacific ocean. As the southern boundary of 

Connecticut was much farther south than the northern boundary of Pennsylvania, this gave Connecticut a claim to a broad strip of land across the entire northern part of the Pennsylvania colony. At first the people of Pennsylvania did not take this claim seriously. But when Connecticut settlers began coming into their land and establishing settlements in the beautiful Wyoming valley, there was hard feeling and finally fighting. Not only the settlers, but also the nearby Indians, took part in this "Yankee-Pennamite War," as it was called. A great many people on both sides were killed. At last, after much trouble, the land was awarded to Pennsylvania, and the Connecticut settlers were paid for giving it up.

A MASSACRE IN THE WYOMING VALLEY 

Both Pennsylvania and Connecticut claimed the beautiful Wyoming valley in northeastern Pennsylvania. When people from Connecticut tried to settle in the valley, there was bitter fighting in which the neighboring Indians took part. At last the valley was given to Pennsylvania, and the Connecticut settlers were paid for giving up their claims. 

Pennsylvania's Claims to New Jersey and Delaware. As you have already learned, William Penn was one of the proprietors of East and West Jersey, and he also had a claim to Delaware because he had leased it from the Duke of York. In 1702, Penn sold to the king and queen of England his rights to govern the Jersey colonies, and two years later he granted Delaware a separate legislature which made it, in effect, an

PENNSYLVANIA 

247 independent colony. In this way Pennsylvania gave up its claims to the lands of both New Jersey and 

Delaware. 

Benjamin Franklin. In 1706, a boy named Benjamin Franklin was born in Boston. He was one of a large family, for he had nine older brothers and seven sisters.

When Benjamin was eight, he was sent to a grammar school, where he studied for a year. The next year was spent at another school, where he learned writing and arithmetic. These two years spent in study were the only years of schooling that Franklin ever had. 

At the age of ten Benjamin was set to work in his father's shop, making candles. He worked hard and did very well, but he never learned to like candle-making. He wanted to be a sailor, and his father was afraid that he would run away to sea. Hoping to find a business that Benjamin would like better, the father set him to work for his brother James, the printer and publisher of a newspaper known as the New England Courant.

Benjamin liked printing better than candle-making, and he did not run away. He wanted to write articles for the paper, but feared that James would not print them. Soon Benjamin discovered a way. He wrote a clever little story and stuck it under the front door of the shop. James found it and liked it. He printed it and many others before he at last discovered that Benjamin was their author. After that no more of 

Benjamin's stories were printed in the Courant.

When his brother was arrested because of some things which he had printed in the Courant, the boy had courage and skill enough to carry on the newspaper during James' absence.

Although Benjamin liked the printing business, he did not like his brother James. The two were constantly quarreling, and sometimes James even beat the boy. At last Benjamin could stand no more, and he told his brother that he would not work for him any longer. James was very angry. He went around to the other printers in Boston and made each of them promise not to give Benjamin any work. 

Since he could not find work in Boston, Benjamin decided to seek his fortune in New York. He had no money, but he did have some books, and these he sold for enough money to pay for his passage. After three days at sea he reached New York, and there learned that there were no jobs open for printers. He was advised to travel farther, to Philadelphia, so off he started once more on his journeys. 

The trip to Philadelphia was much harder than the trip to New York had been, for Franklin's money was now almost gone. He crossed from New York to Perth

Amboy by boat, and then started out on foot for Philadelphia. In two days he covered fifty miles, and, tired, dirty, and footsore, he came to the Delaware river north of Philadelphia. With several other travelers, Franklin hired a boat and in it reached his destination. 

There was little in the appearance of the seventeenyear-old boy who entered Philadelphia to suggest that

PENNSYLVANIA 

249 

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN ENTERS PHILADELPHIA 

Deborah Read, the smiling girl in the doorway, does not even suspect that this awkward seventeen-year-old boy from Boston, walking along the streets and munching a loaf of bread, will some day be her husband and the greatest man in Pennsylvania.

he might some day be the city's greatest citizen. His clothes were very dirty, and his pockets bulged with the few possessions he had brought with him. Under each arm he carried a loaf of bread, and as he walked along he munched on a third loaf which he held in his hand. A pretty girl named Deborah Read stood at the door of one of the houses and laughed at him as he went by. Little did she realize that a few years later she would be his wife.

In Philadelphia, Franklin found work with a printer named Keimer, and for several months all went well. Then, one day, two well-dressed strangers entered the shop. One of them was Governor Keith of 

Pennsylvania. The governor advised Benjamin to set up a shop of his own, and suggested that perhaps the boy's father would supply the necessary money. Now 

Mr. Franklin was not willing to do this, for he justly felt that his son was too young to have a shop of his own. The governor then said that he himself would be glad to supply the money and suggested that Benjamin go to London to buy himself a printing press. The governor said that he would send the money to the boat so that Benjamin might have it for use in London. 

Benjamin was delighted with the idea, and when the time came he eagerly boarded the ship that was to carry him to England. For some reason the governor had changed his plans and sent no money, a fact that Franklin did not find out until too late to leave the ship. 

When the boat reached England, the boy was without friends and without money. He did have his printing

PENNSYLVANIA 

251 skill, however, and he soon found work in a London printing shop. In time he made friends with a number of people, and he even considered remaining in London for the rest of his life. But at last a merchant who wished to open a store in Philadelphia persuaded him to return to that city and to go to work in the store. 

Back he traveled across the ocean, hoping to make his fortune in America. 

Unfortunately, the merchant soon fell sick and died, and Franklin was again without a position. He was now twenty years old-a studious, hard-working, intelligent young man. Once more he went to Mr. 

Keimer, the printer, and once more he was given work. 

The next year he borrowed some money and set up a shop of his own.

Franklin's Writings. Benjamin Franklin was not only a printer, but also a writer of much ability. He started a newspaper-the Pennsylvania Gazette-and it was immediately successful. In his paper he printed the latest news and also many clever little articles which appealed to his readers. He printed advertisements, too, although newspapers in those days seldom had them.

Soon the young printer brought out a second publication, a yearly booklet called Poor Richard's Almanack. In his almanac he printed much valuable information: tables showing the rise and fall of the tides, and the movements of the moon and the planets; weather predictions; lists of the kings and queens of the different countries, and the days upon which they

Poor Richard, 1733. 

held court; a calendar; and many other things.

THE COVER OF POOR RICHARD'S ALMANACK 

This yearly booklet, written and printed by Benjamin Franklin, contained much valuable information. It was read throughout the colonies. saved is a penny earned;" "A rolling stone gathers no moss;" "Early to bed and early to rise makes a man healthy, wealthy, and wise." Poor Richard's Almanack was so widely read throughout the English colonies in

America that Franklin is sometimes spoken of as "the school teacher of the 

American people."

Franklin's Public 

Services. Thirteen years after Benjamin Franklin entered Philadelphia without friends or money, he was made clerk of the

General Assembly of Pennsylvania. The fol lowing year he was advanced to the position of 

Deputy Postmaster-Gen eral of the colony. While holding this position, he found time to organize a good police force and a fire

PENNSYLVANIA 

253 department for the city of Philadelphia. For his policemen he selected strong, honest men who would protect the citizens well. His firemen were carefully trained, and each was equipped with a leather bucket and a basket. The buckets were for carrying water to throw on the fire, and the baskets were used to carry articles out of burning buildings. 

Later, Franklin was made Postmaster-General of all the English colonies in America. He now had a splendid opportunity to improve the mail service between the colonies, which at that time was slow and uncertain. The mail was sent from colony to colony by horsemen known as postriders, or by boat. Almost at once Franklin improved the service by having more horsemen and more boats. In the following chapters of this book we shall learn of other public services which Benjamin Franklin did for the English colonies in America.

Franklin's Inventions. Besides his many other fine characteristics, Benjamin Franklin was also a skilful inventor. The houses in his day were heated by log fires built in great fireplaces. Such fires used much wood, but did not heat the house very well. Sitting in front of the fire, one might roast one's face and freeze one's back at the same time. This poor method of heating displeased Franklin, and he set to work to improve it. The result was the first stove. Though Franklin's stoves were crude, they made the houses much warmer and used less fuel than the old-fashioned fireplaces, which had been the means of heating up to this time.

In Franklin's day a few learned men were beginning to experiment with electricity. It occurred to Franklin 

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

This great citizen of Pennsylvania had only two years of schooling, yet he gained undying fame as a writer, a statesman, and an inventor. that lightning might be one form of electricity. 

Before this time people had never known exactly what lightning was. 

Franklin made a kite and to it he fastened a long cord of hemp. On the end of the cord he tied a metal key. The next time there was a thunderstorm he flew the kite up among the storm clouds. 

Franklin believed that if lightning were really electricity, some of it would follow the hemp string from the clouds down to the metal key.

Placing his knuckle near the key, he got such a shock that all doubt was removed from his mind. 

Now that Franklin knew what lightning was, he was able to make a very useful invention. This was a lightning rod, a metal pole which extends from the roof of a house to the ground. Instead of the lightning striking and burning the house, it follows the rod to the ground where it does no harm. It is said that this

PENNSYLVANIA 

255 invention of Franklin's has saved many houses from destruction by lightning. 

Benjamin Franklin-American. Only in America was it possible for such a man as Franklin to become successful and respected. Had he lived in Europe he would never have been heard of, for no workman's son could hope to rise high there, no matter how able he might be. In the new civilization which was arising in 

America, titles and family names counted for less than industry and intelligence. There was a chance for every able man, and Franklin took advantage of that chance.

The society of Friends was founded by George Fox, an English shepherd. The Friends, or Quakers, did not believe in fighting, in taking oaths, or in personal pride. They believed that all men were equal, and they showed no more respect to one man than another. 

The Quakers were persecuted in England and in all the English colonies in America except Rhode 

Island and Maryland.

King Charles II owed a good deal of money to Admiral Penn of the English navy. When the admiral died, his son, William Penn, inherited the debt. Instead of paying the debt in money, the king gave Penn a large section of land in the New World between the 

New York and Maryland colonies. 

William Penn was a Quaker, and he planned to

found a colony in the New World where Quakers and members of all other religions would be free to worship as they pleased. The name Pennsylvania was chosen for the colony. 

In 1681, William Penn sent to his colony a shipload of colonists, mostly Quakers. The next year he and a hundred other colonists sailed from England on the ship Welcome.

William Penn drew up a very liberal code of laws, known as the Great Law, for his colonists. 

At the point where the Schuylkill river flowed into the Delaware, Penn founded the city of Philadelphia.

This city was very carefully planned and laid out. 

Penn always treated the Indians fairly and bought from them all of the land which his settlers occupied. 

Soon after his arrival in the New World he made a treaty of peace with the red men which was never broken as long as he remained alive. 

Many new settlers came to Pennsylvania in the early years of the colony. Among these were Germans, Scotch-Irish, and Welsh. Most of the Germans who came belonged to the Mennonites, the Pietists, the

Dunkers, the Moravians, the Lutherans, and other religious groups.

Much of the work in Pennsylvania was done by indentured servants, or redemptioners. 

Although William Penn planned to remain in

Pennsylvania for the rest of his life, his affairs in England compelled him to spend more time there than in his New World colony.

PENNSYLVANIA 

257 After William Penn's death his treaty with the Indians was broken, and there was hard feeling between the white men and the red. 

Harrisburg, now the capital of Pennsylvania, grew up on the Susquehanna river at the point where John 

Harris, an early settler, established a trading post.

The Pennsylvania colonists had disputes with the people of Maryland and Connecticut over the location of boundary lines. These disputes were at last settled. 

Two English surveyors, Mason and Dixon, determined the location of the boundary line between Pennsylvania and Maryland. 

Benjamin Franklin was one of Pennsylvania's greatest citizens. He became famous as an author, a statesman, and an inventor.

CHAPTER TWELVE GEORGIA 

We have read about colonies founded to make money for their proprietors, and colonies founded by people in search of religious freedom. All of the first twelve English colonies in North America were founded for one or both of these reasons. 

Now we come to the thirteenth colony, Georgia, which was founded for very different reasons. 

General Oglethorpe. It used to be the custom in many European countries, including England, to throw men into prison because they owed debts which they could not pay. Often these men were honest and industrious, but in spite of this they were locked up in prison with thieves and murderers, and sometimes were kept there for the rest of their lives. Their situation was desperate, for while they were in prison they had no way of raising money, and unless they could raise money to pay their debts they had no way whatever of getting out.

Among the many unfortunate people imprisoned for debt was one well-born gentleman who had the misfortune to be placed in a cell with some prisoners who had smallpox. As was to be expected, he took the disease and died. People were constantly dying in the English prisons, so to most Englishmen a death more or less meant very little. To one influential Englishman, how- 260

GEORGIA 

261 ever, the death of this poor prisoner was very shocking, for the prisoner was a close personal friend. 

The friend of the dead prisoner was General James 

Oglethorpe, an English officer who had distinguished himself in fighting against the French. It seemed terrible to him that honest men could be held in prison because they owed money, and he determined to see what he could do to remedy the situation.

Oglethorpe's Plan for a Colony. As General Oglethorpe considered the plight of the unfortunate debtors, it occurred to him that it might be a splendid idea if they could begin life over again in some distant land. The more he thought of this plan the better he liked it. So he decided to ask King George II if he might not start a new colony in America, and take to it honest men who were in prison for debt in England. 

Courtesy of Oglethorpe University GENERAL OGLETHORPE 

This kind and far-seeing English soldier founded the Georgia colony in 1733 as a refuge for debtors, and as a protection for the other English colonies against the Spaniards in Florida. 

General Oglethorpe was not at all sure that King

George would approve of his plan for founding a new colony, for the English ruler had little interest in what

happened to debtors. Some other reason had to be found which would appeal to the king. General Oglethorpe was not long in finding it. When he went to see the king, Oglethorpe told him of his plan to help the debtors, but he stressed more strongly the need of a powerful, well-armed English colony between South Carolina and the Spanish land of Florida. If there were such a colony, he said, the English settlers to the north would be relieved of the constant danger of attack by the Spaniards. This idea appealed to King 

George II, and he granted to General Oglethorpe a broad stretch of land between the Savannah and Altamaha rivers, extending westward as far as the Pacific ocean. The new colony was called Georgia in honor of 

King George II. 

The Settling of Georgia. General Oglethorpe's first step in the founding of his colony was to appoint a board of trustees who were to serve for twenty-one years.

Next came the selection of the colonists. For four months General Oglethorpe and members of his board of trustees went through the prisons, talking to those who had been jailed for debt. Only honest, able men who were anxious to begin life anew were selected. At last, late in 1732, General Oglethorpe and 130 colonists set sail from England in the ship Anne. 

Early in 1733, the Georgia colonists arrived at Charleston, South Carolina. The governor of the colony welcomed them, and they were given shelter in the houses of the Charleston settlers. They stayed only a

GEORGIA 

263 few days, however, and then sailed south to the settlement of Beaufort, South Carolina, where they were kindly received. 

GENERAL OGLETHORPE MAKES FRIENDS WITH THE INDIANS 

While searching for a good place to found a settlement, General James Oglethorpe and a few companions came upon an Indian village beside the Savannah river. The Indians at once prepared to fight the white men, but Oglethorpe managed to make friends with their chief and so avoided a battle. Near the Indian village the general founded Savannah, the first settlement in the Georgia colony. 

While the colonists remained in Beaufort, General Oglethorpe and a few companions traveled south along the coast, and then made a canoe trip up the Savannah river. Eighteen miles from the sea, they found a high bluff, at the top of which the land was dry and level.

Here, thought Oglethorpe, would be a splendid place to found a settlement.

At the point selected, there was an Indian village ruled by a chief named Tomochichi. Tomochichi had seen something of the Spaniards in Florida, and as a result he trusted no white man. Fearing that Oglethorpe and his companions might attack his village, he told his warriors to prepare for battle. Luckily one of the Indian women had somewhere learned to speak English, and with her help General Oglethorpe was able to convince Tomochichi that the Englishmen had come as friends and not as enemies.

The Founding of Savannah. As soon as he had made peace with the Indians, Oglethorpe returned to Beaufort in order to take his colonists to their new home. On February 12, 1733, they arrived at the place which he had selected, and went ashore. 

In laying out the town, which General Oglethorpe called Savannah, he followed almost the same plan that William Penn had used for Philadelphia. After the forest trees were cleared away, broad, straight streets were laid out, some running parallel to the river, and others running straight back from the river's bank. 

At first the settlers lived in tents, but it was not long before many log houses were built. There was also a fort, for General Oglethorpe was too good a soldier to take any chance of attack.

General Oglethorpe's Laws. Although General Ogle thorpe was a kind-hearted man and treated his colonists fairly, he and his board of trustees made many strict

GEORGIA 

265 laws for the running of the colony. Every man had to work with his hands. No one could have slaves. The buying and use of rum and other strong drink were forbidden. The colonists were not allowed to have any voice in their government during the first twenty-one years of the colony's existence, while the board of trustees was in charge. No one who could not serve in the army when the colony was in danger was permitted to own land. Besides all these strict provisions, there was one liberal one, for complete religious freedom was granted to all Christians, except members of the Catholic Church.

New Settlers in Georgia. Now that his colony was well started, General Oglethorpe went back to England to urge more colonists to come to Georgia. When he returned to America, the general brought back with him many new colonists. Among the English settlers who came to Georgia were John and Charles Wesley, two very religious young men, who later founded the Methodist Church. For a few years the Wesley brothers remained in Georgia, preaching the Christian religion to the Indians. Then they returned to England to carry on the work of organizing their new church. 

The religious freedom in Georgia attracted not only the Scotch, Germans, and English, but people from other countries. Many Swiss and Portuguese arrived, and one small group of Jews also crossed the ocean and established a settlement of their own. In later years, after some of the strict laws had been repealed, hundreds of settlers came to the colony from Virginia,

North and South Carolina, and other colonies farther to the north. 

Georgia Department of Archives CHARLES WESLEY PREACHING TO THE INDIANS 

Two religious young men, John and Charles Wesley, came to the Georgia colony from England to teach the Christian religion to the Indians. After a brief stay in the colony, they returned home and founded the Methodist Church. 

General Oglethorpe's War with the Spaniards. Al though slavery was not permitted in Georgia, it was allowed in South Carolina, and from time to time slaves escaped from Charleston and other South Carolina settlements and made their way across Georgia to the Spanish lands in Florida. At first the Spaniards paid

GEORGIA 

267 little attention to these escaped Negroes, but later they decided to make some use of them. A number of Negroes were armed by the Spaniards and sent to attack their former masters.

The Negroes were few and were poorly armed, and the attack they made was neither dangerous nor important. The southern colonists, however, became very bitter against the Spaniards because of their part in the affair. General Oglethorpe ordered his soldiers to arrest any Spaniard found in Georgia, and he established a well-defended settlement, called St. Simon's, on an island at the mouth of the Altamaha river not far from the Spanish settlement of St. Augustine. 

The Spanish Plot to Arouse the Indians. When the Spaniards realized that the English had built a settlement so near St. Augustine, they planned to destroy the settlement and massacre the inhabitants. To this end they sent messengers to the Indian tribes, urging the red men to make war on the English settlers. 

News of this reached General Oglethorpe, and he and some companions set out immediately for the chief Indian village, well over one hundred miles from Savannah. After a journey of many days through the wilderness, they reached the village, and the general held a conference with the Indian chiefs. After much talking and an exchange of presents the Indians decided to remain friendly to the English. 

The Siege of St. Augustine. So far General Ogle thorpe had been content merely to defend his colony against the Spaniards. Now, an event occurred which

THE SIEGE OF ST. AUGUSTINE, 1741 

Because Spaniards from Florida attacked his settlers, General Oglethorpe laid siege to the Spanish town of St. Augustine. 

While the siege was going on, a Spanish fleet appeared, and Oglethorpe and his men had to retreat to Georgia.

GEORGIA 

269 made him want to drive the Spaniards out of their own settlements. A Spanish ship, sailing along the coast, came upon a small island where two of the Georgia settlers had their homes. Although the settlers made no attempt to resist the Spaniards, they were put to death by the Spanish soldiers.

When Oglethorpe and the other settlers heard of this cruel deed, they were very angry. At once the general gathered together all the fighting men he could, in order to make an attack on St. Augustine. The Spaniards were driven into their city, and the Georgia settlers began a determined siege which they hoped would end with the surrender of the Spanish city. While the siege was being carried on, a strong Spanish fleet arrived from the West Indies, and General Oglethorpe was forced to give up the attack and lead his army back to Georgia.

Changes in the Georgia Colony. After the trouble with the Spaniards was over, General Oglethorpe left Georgia and returned to England to live. Both the colonists and the Indians were sorry to see him go, for he had been a kind and fair governor.

Not long afterwards, many of the settlers began to demand some changes in the strict laws of the colony. 

Most of all, they wanted to be allowed to have slaves. 

They pointed out that the Virginia and Carolina colonies were prosperous because slave labor made it possible to grow tobacco and cotton. At last the trustees decided that the colonists were right, and they changed the law so that some slavery was allowed. A strict limit, how-

ever, was placed on the number of Negro slaves who might be brought to the colony, and a law was passed providing that all slaves must be kindly treated. 

Next the colonists protested against the law forbidding the buying of rum. Rum was one of the most important products of the West Indies, and, unless the Georgia colonists could buy it, there was little hope of establishing a profitable trade with these nearby islands. 

So this law was repealed. 

Not long afterwards, in 1752, the trustees of the colony gave up their power, and the king of England appointed a governor and a council of twelve advisers to help govern Georgia. Provision was also made for an assembly to represent the people. In this way 

Georgia became a royal colony-the last of the thirteen colonies to come under the king's direct control.

The Georgia colony was founded by General James Oglethorpe as a place of refuge for English debtors, and as a protection for the other English colonies against the Spaniards in Florida.

King George II of England granted General Ogle thorpe a large stretch of land between the Savannah and the Altamaha rivers, and there the general took a group of colonists early in 1733. Their first settlement was named Savannah.

General Oglethorpe and the board of trustees which he appointed to help him run the colony made many

GEORGIA 

271 strict laws. None of the colonists were allowed to have slaves or to buy rum or any other strong drink. The people were given no voice in the affairs of the colony. On the other hand, religious freedom was granted to all Christians except members of the Catholic 

Church. 

The religious freedom in Georgia attracted people from many European countries as well as from some of the English colonies.

Among the early Georgia colonists were John and Charles Wesley, the founders of the Methodist Church. 

The Georgia settlers had trouble with the Spaniards in Florida, and Oglethorpe and his men made an unsuccessful attempt to capture St. Augustine.

After General Oglethorpe returned to England, his trustees allowed slavery and the purchase of rum. In 

1752, Georgia was made a royal colony.
