CHAPTER THIRTEEN LIFE IN THE ENGLISH COLONIES 

Two hundred and fifty years ago, the airplane, the automobile, the locomotive, and the steamboat were unheard of. There were no radios, telephones, telegraphs, or electrical instruments of any sort. 

Even stoves and furnaces had not yet been invented, and not a single house had running water. 

Imagine living without such modern conveniences! In this chapter we shall learn how our colonial ancestors lived under these conditions. 

Social Classes in the Colonies. What sort of peo ple came from Europe to the English colonies in America? Very few titled lords and nobles came, for they were powerful in their own countries and so were satisfied to stay there. Nor did many people from the lowest classes cross the ocean, for usually they had neither the money nor the ambition. The great majority of the colonists were people of the middle class, who had left their European homes to travel to the New World in search of religious freedom or the opportunity to begin life again under improved conditions. 

The Upper Class. There were, of course, exceptions to this rule. The Cavaliers who came to Virginia were high-born gentlemen who were forced to leave their homes because they were the friends of a king 273

who had been driven out of power. Many of the patroons living in the New York colony were members of wealthy and powerful Dutch families. At first these groups formed the highest social class in the colonies. 

In time, people of wealth and influence, the successful merchants and the owners of profitable plantations who had gained their wealth in the New World, were included in this class. All during colonial times the wealthy and the high-born families formed the upper class of society in the colonies. 

The Middle Class. Most of the colonists belonged to the middle class-farmers, storekeepers, clerks, traders, and workmen of various sorts. These people were to be found in every colony, and they formed the most important group, for they greatly outnumbered both the upper class and the servant class. 

The Servant Class. The servant class was divided into three levels. The highest was made up of the free white servants who worked for wages and could leave their employers' service whenever they wished. Next came the indentured servants, or redemptioners, who sold their services for a certain number of years in order to pay for their transportation to the New World. Some of the indentured servants had been prisoners who had been released from prisons in the Old World to work in the colonies. Generally they worked without wages until the time of their original prison sentence came to an end. They were then freed. 

The lowest level consisted, of the slaves, Negro servants who were bought by the colonists, and who

worked without pay for the rest of their lives. The first of these Negro slaves were brought from Africa to the Virginia colony in 1619. In the years which followed, thousands of them were bought by the plantation owners of the other southern colonies. Some of the northern colonists also had slaves as house servants, but as there were no large plantations in the northern colonies slavery never gained a strong foothold there. 

Colonial Settlements and Houses. Except for Phil adelphia and Savannah, which were laid out according to definite and carefully prepared plans, the settlements in each of the thirteen colonies were at first much alike. 

As soon as the settlers landed, log cabins were built to provide shelter. Usually, a fort and a palisade were erected as soon as possible for protection against the Indians. Almost always a church was among the first buildings, for most of the early colonists were extremely religious.

Settlements and Houses in the Southern Colonies. 

As they grew and developed, however, the settlements of the various colonies became very different in appearance. In the South, where huge crops of rice, cotton, and tobacco were raised on broad fields, the great plantation houses were built far apart. They were tall, stately buildings, with wide halls, large, high-ceilinged rooms, and many windows. The verandas were broad and sometimes extended all the way around the houses. 

Usually the veranda roofs of the southern mansions were supported by massive pillars which gave an air of dignity and beauty.

MOUNT VERNON, IN VIRGINIA, THE HOME OF GEORGE WASHINGTON 

The plantation owners of the South lived with their families in large beautiful homes like this. There were many rooms, and broad verandas with tall stately pillars. The Negro slaves lived apart in small cabins on the edges of the plantations. 

Not far away from the main dwelling were "the quarters," the little cabins where the slaves lived. 

Every plantation had a blacksmith's shop, a carpenter's shop, and a shoemaker's shop. There were also warehouses for storing cotton bales, stables for the horses, and sheds where the seeds were removed from the cotton. Each southern plantation was really a little village by itself. Because of this, few towns grew up in the southern colonies.

Settlements and Houses in New York. Farther to the north, in New York, the colonists lived in two very

different kinds of homes. Along the Hudson river were the beautiful mansions which the patroons had built, set in the midst of their broad lands. These estates were not unlike the southern plantations, for besides the beautiful homes of the owners there were many smaller buildings-workshops, mills, and the little cottages of the tenant-farmers, who worked the land as the slaves did in the South.

Most of the New York colonists, however, lived in villages. Their homes were tall Dutch houses of brick, with steep roofs, many gables, and high chimneys. In 

New York and the other settlements founded by the Dutch, the houses were built close together, for the Dutch people wished their towns in the New World to be as much as possible like the crowded little towns of Holland. The presence of Dutch windmills, with their long, sweeping arms, gave a picturesque touch to New York and the other Dutch settlements, making them look like little towns transplanted from Holland. This 

Dutch appearance of the settlements continued to be noticeable long after the English took the colony. 

Settlements and Houses in New England. In New 

England the towns developed on still another plan. In the center of each settlement was an open field of grass. 

This was the common, where the soldiers drilled and where public meetings were sometimes held. In some of the New England villages, there were great rambling wooden houses with many windows, but most of the homes were little one-story cottages with wide, gently sloping roofs and small dormer windows set with many

A NEW ENGLAND COTTAGE 

Miles Standish lived in this house in the early days of the Plymouth settlement. Most New England homes were much smaller than those of the South. panes of glass. Such houses were easy to heat, and were therefore practical in New England, with its bitter cold winters.

The New England farmhouses, too, were built with the cold season in mind. To the low, wooden houses of one or two stories where the farmer and his family lived were joined the barns, the stables, and the storehouses for the farm supplies. This was done so that it would not be necessary to wade through deep snow in order to carry on the daily farm tasks in winter. 

Settlements and Houses in the Other Colonies. In 

Pennsylvania, New Jersey, Delaware, and the southern colony of Maryland, most of the people lived on farms.

The farmhouses were small frame buildings with steep, gabled roofs. Usually these houses were not joined to the barns and stables, as in New England, but stood some distance away.

In the towns, most of the buildings were low, rambling houses of stone or brick, set in rows along the broad tree-shaded streets. The stores and office buildings were small structures of wood or brick. 

Backwoods Settlements and Houses. In the unsettled parts of all of the colonies, where the forests were still uncut and there were few settlers, the people lived in log cabins. These were small buildings, usually having only one room. They were made of logs cut from the forest trees, with roofs of rough boards, chopped from logs. The chinks between the logs of the walls were filled with clay, to keep out the wind and rain. 

Usually the entire family slept in the single room of the cabin. Sometimes there was a loft, much like a broad shelf, across one end of the cabin. In some houses, the loft was used as a place to store provisions. 

In other homes it served as a sleeping-place. 

How Colonial Homes Were Furnished. The fur nishings in the homes differed almost as much as the homes themselves. Some of the richer colonists who lived on the plantations or in the towns brought from England finely carved furniture of oak and mahogany. 

There were beautiful tables and chairs, massive sideboards, and high four-posted beds over which canopies were stretched like cloth roofs. At night, curtains, hung from the canopies, were let down and closed about the

sleeper, for the people of colonial times made the mistake of thinking that the night air was bad to breathe.

Even in the homes of the wealthy colonists there were few pictures and few rugs, and wallpaper was 

William Thompson, Saugerties, N. Y.

A BEDROOM IN A SOUTHERN PLANTATION HOUSE 

Most of the wealthy people of colonial times used fine furniture brought from Europe. The beds had canopies, and many of them had curtains which could be drawn together about the sleeper to protect him from the chill night air. almost unknown. Woven cloth tapestries served to decorate the walls, and sometimes the floors were protected by a covering of sand or rushes. When the first rugs were brought to the colonies, people often walked carefully around them to avoid soiling or mussing them. 

If we should visit the home of a wealthy colonist,

we would miss a great many of the conveniences to which we have become accustomed. We would find no bathrooms with running water, for plumbing was practically unknown. Most people had to take a bucket or pitcher to the nearest well whenever they needed water for any purpose. If our visit were made in the winter time, we would find the house very cold. Furnaces had not yet been invented, and, until Benjamin Franklin invented his stove, the only means of heating was by fireplaces in the various rooms. At night we would find no electric lights, or gas jets, or even kerosene lamps to give us light, for these things had not yet been invented. Instead we would have to depend on the faint, flickering light of candles or whale-oil lamps.

The homes of the middle-class colonists were furnished much the same as those of the wealthy people, though the furniture was not so fine nor so well made. 

The farmhouses and the homes of the backwoods settlers had even fewer conveniences. There the furniture was usually very crude, for it was all made from rough planks cut from the forest trees. The tables were rough and uneven, and the chairs were often only blocks of wood. Some farmhouses and cabins had hard, uncomfortable beds, called bunks, but most people slept upon piles of fir-tree boughs laid on the cabin floor. If light were needed at night, it was furnished by pine torches or by green reeds dipped in fat and lighted. 

Some of the backwoods settlers had candles which they themselves made from tallow, but many people did not have even this poor light.

The Fireplace in Colonial Times. In colonial times the fireplace played a much more important part in the daily lives of the people than it does today. Except in 

THE FIREPLACE IN COLONIAL TIMES 

In colonial days, family life centered about the fireplace in many a home. Here we see one of the women of the household rolling dough for piecrust, while the man sitting near her is paring apples for the pies. Another woman is cooking food over the fire, a third is spinning thread, and a woman in the next room is churning butter. Notice the crane for holding pots and kettles, and the built-in oven at the left of the fireplace. the finest houses, there was usually only one fireplace to heat the house. Often it was big enough to hold sixfoot logs, and around it on winter evenings the family gathered for warmth and companionship. There was

no other warm place in the house, and even the distant corners of that one room were cold and drafty. The hearth was truly the center of the home. 

Besides supplying warmth, the fireplace had another use, for in it was cooked all of the food for the family. In the earliest fireplaces a bar of green wood ran from side to side above the flames, and from it were hung the pots and kettles in which the vegetables and other food were cooked. Green wood burns slowly, and it was often many months before one of these bars burned through and dropped a meal into the ashes. 

Toward the middle of the Colonial Period the wooden bars in many fireplaces were replaced by iron bars, and later an iron rack, called a crane, was invented. This was fastened to one side of the fireplace, and from it pots and other cooking utensils were hung. Because the crane was hinged at one end, it could be swung out into the room so that the pots and kettles could be hung on it or removed from it with very little trouble. When meat was to be roasted, it was stuck on a sharp iron rod called a spit, which extended from one side of the fireplace above the flames. Many clever devices were invented to keep the spit turning so that the meat would roast evenly on all sides. 

While the vegetables were stewing in the pot above the fire and the meat was roasting on the spit, the housewife often used the fireplace to prepare still other foods. 

Corn was roasted in the hot ashes, bread was baked in a little metal oven built into the stonework of the fireplace, and sometimes waffles were made with the help

of long-handled waffle irons held above the glowing coals. Surely the fireplace played a most important part in the life of every colonist. 

Colonial Tableware. The tableware of colonial times was far different from what we have today. Only in the homes of very wealthy people were china dishes to be found. Even pewter dishes, cups, and bowls were not common, for they were very expensive. In most homes the food was served in shallow wooden plates known as trenchers. Soup, porridge, stew, and other partly-liquid foods were eaten with large wooden spoons, and more solid foods, such as meat and potatoes, were eaten with the fingers. Forks were unknown in the colonies, and even in Europe not many people had yet begun to use them. Glasses were rare, and most people drank from tall, wooden cups known as tankards. 

Leather and metal tankards and jugs were also used.

The Clothes of the Colonists. In colonial times, the various classes of people dressed in very different ways, depending upon their occupations, their wealth, and the colonies in which they happened to live. 

Costumes of the Backwoods Districts. In the backwoods districts the men usually wore rough jackets and long trousers made of deerskin. In cold weather, undergarments of wool, linen, or a widely-used cloth called linsey-woolsey were worn beneath the deerskin costumes. Linsey-woolsey was made of a mixture of linen and woolen threads, the linen threads running one way and the woolen threads the other. 

The women of the backwoods settlements wore long,

plain dresses of wool, linen, or linsey-woolsey, and undergarments made of the same materials. Practically all of the cloth used in the backwoods and farming 

A COLONIAL GIRL WEAVING CLOTH 

In colonial times, spinning thread and weaving cloth were among the most important duties of the girls and women. Much of the clothing in those days was made of homespun cloth, woven in the homes of the colonists. districts was woven by hand from woolen and linen threads spun from the wool and flax which the colonists raised. Spinning and weaving were two of the most important tasks of the colonial housewife. Cloth made at home was known as "homespun."

How Wealthy Colonists Dressed. In most of the colonies the wealthy people dressed very elaborately in clothes which they imported from England. This was especially true of the southern colonies, where the 

COLONIAL COSTUMES OF 1665 

At the left we see a colonial governor and his wife. The other couple are a southern plantation owner and his wife. The cloth for such fine costumes was usually brought from England. wealthy plantation owners took great delight in their elaborate costumes. Usually the men wore broadbrimmed hats decorated with flowing feathers. The coats were trimmed with lace and sometimes with gold braid. Knee breeches of rich cloth, high boots, and

often a beautiful, decorated sword completed the costume. In the northern colonies high boots were less frequently worn. There the colonists wore rich silk stockings and low-cut shoes with broad buckles. 

COLONIAL COSTUMES OF 1665 

At the left are two Puritan colonists from Massachusetts. At the right are a Virginia Cavalier and his wife. Compare these costumes with those shown on pages 223 and 286. 

The dresses of the wealthy women had long, very full skirts. Much fine lace and silk were used for decoration. Because a delicate, white skin was considered beautiful, huge sunbonnets, silk or velvet masks, and long gloves were worn during the summer to protect

the skin of the face, hands, and arms from the hot rays of the sun. High-heeled shoes were worn, the heels sometimes being so high that the ladies were unable to walk down stairs without removing their shoes, for fear of pitching forward.

There were, of course, many exceptions to these styles of dress. Even the wealthiest Quakers wore plain clothes with few decorations, and many of the Puritans dressed simply. But, generally speaking, the clothes of colonial times were much more picturesque than those we wear today.

The Clothing of Middle-Class Colonists. Through out the colonies, the people of the middle class dressed very much alike. Here is a description of the costume of a Boston printer who lived during the Colonial Period: "He wore a pea-green coat, white vest, nankeen (brownish-yellow) small clothes (knee breeches). white silk stockings, and pumps fastened with silver buckles which covered at least half the foot from instep to toe. His small clothes were tied at the knees with ribbon of the same color in several bows, the ends reaching down to the ankles. His hair in front was loaded with grease, and was frizzled or crimped, and powdered. Behind, his natural hair was added to by the use of a large queue, called vulgarly a false tail, which, enrolled in some yards of black ribbon, hung half-way down his back." 

Wigs and Hairdressing. Perhaps the strangest part of a colonial man's costume was his hair. In early colonial times, the men usually men usually wore their hair

long. The Virginia Cavaliers were especially proud of their long curls, which often hung down well below their shoulders.

In time, it became unfashionable to "wear one's own hair," and huge, fluffy, powdered wigs were popular.

For more than a hundred years these expensive and uncomfortable coverings were worn on the heads of all men who could afford them. Then, men took to powdering their own hair, and to braiding it behind to form a queue, or pigtail. In order to make the powder stay on the hair, a large amount of heavy grease was used.

Children's Costumes. As soon as they were no longer babies, the children of colonial times usually dressed much as their parents did. On the farms and in the backwoods districts, the boys had suits of deerskin and the girls had long dresses of wool, or linen, or linsey-woolsey. The children of wealthy colonists wore elaborate costumes much like those of their parents, except that the boys did not wear swords or wigs. The little colonial girls, in their long, full dresses, looked like dainty little ladies. But think how uncomfortable they must have been!

Laws About Clothing. Many laws were passed in colonial times to prevent the wearing of fine clothes by people of the lower classes. Although rich people in most of the colonies were permitted to dress as elaborately as they wished, many poorer people were arrested for wearing silk clothes, or gold braid, or lace decorations. It was felt that in wearing such costumes they

were trying to rise above their social position. This attitude, a result of the strong class feeling which had long existed in Europe, gradually died out in the colonies. Today we would consider it very "un-American" to try to enforce laws allowing some people to dress better than others.

Colonial Occupations. In reading about the various colonies, we have already learned something of the ways in which the colonists made their living. In the 

South, the wealthiest men were the plantation owners, who raised cotton, tobacco, or rice on their broad lands. 

In the northern colonies, especially in New England, shipbuilding, fishing, and trading goods with other colonies and other countries were important occupations. 

In every colony there were storekeepers, traders, lawyers, and large numbers of farmers. The backwoodsmen in the outlying districts in all the colonies, both north and south, made their living by hunting, trapping, raising a few vegetables, and carrying on fur trade with the Indians.

Colonial Children. The children of colonial times had fewer toys and amusements and less time for play than modern children have. Before they could play, the farm boys and girls had endless duties, or chores, to do. For the boys, there was wood to be cut for the fireplace, fires to be kindled, livestock to be tended, and many other tasks in which they were expected to help their fathers. Among the duties of the girls were the weaving of cloth, the making of candles, and a share in the work of cooking.

Even the sons and daughters of rich families had certain things which they must do. In the South, the boys usually helped their fathers to oversee the work on the plantations, and in the North they learned business methods in their fathers' offices or shipyards. The daughters of rich people did fancy-work-embroidery stitches in many and varied patterns, lace making, and knitting. Both boys and girls had to practice long and hard to learn to stand and sit correctly, and to become familiar with the many polite manners of the day. 

In colonial times, it was the custom for parents to be very stern, and to punish the slightest disobedience. In those days people really believed that "children should be seen and not heard," so the young folks. usually sat quietly and demurely in the presence of their parents and other grown-ups, and spoke only when they were spoken to.

Life was by no means 

Although there were no

Colonial Amusements. 

all work in the colonies. radios, phonographs, moving pictures, or other modern amusements such as we have today, the children of the colonists managed to enjoy life thoroughly. When farm work was done, there were husking bees, where the young people got together and laughed and sang as they husked the corn. These husking bees were often merry occasions, for it was the custom for each boy who husked a red ear of corn to kiss the young lady whom he liked best. There were quilting parties at which the girls talked over the affairs of the day as they made household quilts.

The children also had many sports and games. The boys took part in contests of wrestling, boxing, boatracing, foot-racing, horse-racing, swimming, and a game not unlike our modern football. Both boys and girls 

WINTER SPORTS IN COLONIAL TIMES 

Skating and sleighing were favorite winter sports for both children and grown-ups in the northern colonies. alike played croquet, chess, tag, and other games. Skat ing and sleighing were popular in the winter. 

The colonial men and women also had many pleasures. There were dances, especially in the South, at which finely-dressed men and women with powdered wigs performed the graceful, old-time dances. Many games of cards were popular, and afternoon card par-

ties, particularly among the women, were nearly as common as they are at present. Bowling, an old Dutch game, spread from New York until colonists both north and south were playing it. Wooden pins were set up,  

A COLONIAL RECEPTION 

Receptions, teas, dances, and other social gatherings were popular in many of the colonies. Notice the costumes of the men and women and the furnishings of the room. usually on a smooth lawn outdoors, and the men rolled heavy balls to try to knock them over.

One of the most popular of the colonial sports was cock-fighting-battles to the death between trained fighting roosters. Around the cockpit would gather the colonial men, each eager to bet on his favorite cock.

At a given signal the roosters would be released. They would rush at each other, striking with their beaks and with their sharp, pointed spurs. After a hard and exciting battle, one would sink bleeding to the ground, and the other would flap his wings and crow the cry of victory. As you can see, cock-fighting was a cruel sport. Even in those days many people were opposed to it, and today it is forbidden by law. 

Another cruel sport of colonial times was bear-baiting. A live bear was chained to a post, and a pack of dogs was turned loose to attack him. Usually there was a furious battle. Before it was over, many of the dogs would be killed and sometimes the bear, too. Fortunately, bear-baiting is no longer practiced anywhere in the world.

As might be expected, the Puritans of New England led the soberest lives and had the fewest pleasures. 

Cock-fighting was forbidden, and dancing was discouraged in many places in New England. It was the Puritan belief that an evening spent with friends about a fireside "chatting, eating nuts and apples, and enjoying a quiet time" was about as far as a self-respecting person should go in search of fun. The stern New England "Blue Laws" forbade most forms of enjoyment. Some of these laws are still enforced in a few places. 

Nevertheless, even the Puritans had their days of celebration. The greatest of these was the day when the militia, or volunteer soldiers, were mustered, or ordered out. All of the citizens who had received military training gathered together and drilled. Sham bat-

tles were often carried on to show the colonists how fighting should be done in actual war. There were feasting and drinking, as well as tugs-of-war and other rough games. Weddings, birthdays, and other celebrations were usually marked by feasts, some of which lasted for several days. This was true not only in 

New England but in all of the colonies. 

Books, Almanacs, and Newspapers. Throughout the colonies, reading was a favorite form of amusement. 

Books were few, and there were no libraries from which reading material could be borrowed. Nevertheless, people who owned books read and re-read them, and took delight in lending them to their friends. Yearly almanacs, such as the one Benjamin Franklin published, were to be found in almost every home. There were no daily newspapers, but weekly papers were popular, even though the news they contained might be many months old. As there were no cables, radio sets, steamships, or trains in colonial times, news traveled from colony to colony only as fast as horsemen could carry it, and news from Europe crossed the ocean slowly in sailing ships.

The Church in Colonial Times. Religion played a very important part in the life of all of the English colonies in America. As we have seen, many of the colonies were founded for religious reasons, and in all of them lived many people who had left Europe to escape religious persecution.

As one might expect, the Sabbath was more strictly observed in the Puritan colonies in New England than

A PILGRIM CHURCH SERVICE 

In colonial times the church services were very long and often tiresome. It was not unusual for a minister to preach a sermon several hours in length. The men kept their guns close beside them, even in church, in case of an Indian attack. anywhere else. There the Sabbath began at six o'clock

Saturday afternoon and lasted until sundown on Sunday. During that time no games or pleasures of any sort were allowed. One could not even take a walk, or carry a package, or do any work except that which was absolutely necessary, such as putting coal on the fire to keep it from going out, or milking the cows at the regular milking time. A colonist could not even leave his house except to go to church, and every colonist had to attend Sunday service.

In the church, which was usually a large wooden building without an organ and with rows of straight, uncomfortable, uncushioned benches, the members of the congregation had to sit perfectly still and listen to the long, long sermon. Hour after hour, the minister talked on, cautioning the people to lead good lives, warning those who did wrong, and describing the rewards and the punishments which awaited people after death. In their pulpits many of the ministers had hour glasses. It took an hour for all of the sand to flow from the upper chamber of an hour glass to the lower, and often an hour glass was turned upside down several times during the sermon. 

Although the people of the other colonies were not so strict in keeping the Sabbath as were the New Englanders, yet Sunday was carefully observed in all of the colonies. In the northern settlements the people gathered in church and listened patiently to the long services. Only severe sickness or some equally good reason was accepted as an excuse for absence from the service. As a matter of fact, most people enjoyed going to church and would not have stayed away even if they could.

In the South, the distances between the plantation houses and the nearest churches were so great that many families were unable to attend church services. 

Nevertheless, divine worship was conducted on Sunday. 

The owner of each plantation gathered his family, his servants, and his slaves together, led them in the singing of hymns, and even preached a sermon. In the

southern towns, regular church services were held. 

Crimes and Punishments. We have learned that in 

A PILLORY 

Sometimes a colonist who had committed a small offense had to stand for hours with his head and his hands fastened in the holes of a pillory.

William Penn's colony of Pennsylvania only two crimes, murder and treason, were punishable by death. The other twelve colonies were not so gentle with lawbreakers as Pennsylvania was. 

Most of the colonies based their laws on those of England, where death was the penalty for more than two hundred different crimes. In these colonies, not only murderers and traitors, but also thieves, pirates, witches, bandits, and many other offenders were executed. 

Prisoners who were condemned to death were usually killed by hanging. Almost every town had its gallows, or hanging frame, in plain sight where all could witness the executions. Sometimes the person to be executed was drowned or was crushed to death by means of heavy stones, but such cases were rare.

Even small and unimportant crimes were often the cause of severe punishment. The stocks and pillory were much used throughout the colonies for many small offenses. The whipping post was also widely used, especially for the punishment of runaway slaves and indentured servants. The unhappy victim was chained to the whipping post, and was then horsewhipped for his misdeeds. It was the custom for drunkards to have the letter "D" branded on their foreheads with a hot iron. People who stole even small amounts were likely to have their ears cut off. A man who swore might have a hole bored through his tongue, or might be made to sit in the stocks or stand in the pillory all day with his tongue painfully clasped in a split stick. Women who gossiped or nagged were punished by means of the ducking stool. For such a slight offense as staying away from church, people might be fined or whipped, and an early Virginia law even provided that people who continued to stay away from church might be put to death. 

Travel in the Colonies. In the early years of the 

Colonial Period there were no roads, and people who wished to travel had to choose between going by boat and walking. As most of the early settlements were built along the coast or on the banks of rivers, travel by boat was the most popular means of getting about. 

This was especially true in the southern colonies. 

Although some colonists had wooden rowboats which had been brought from England, most people depended on birch-bark canoes or on dugouts.

Travel on Horseback. When horses were first brought to the colonies, travel on land became practical for the first time. There were still no roads, but the narrow paths through the woods were widened and cleared of underbrush so that horsemen could ride in comfort. Along many of the more important paths the trees were marked to show the way. 

When the colonists began to travel by land, crossing rivers became an important problem. There were, at first, no bridges, and travelers had to find a shallow place and ford the stream, or else swim their horses across. Canoe ferries early appeared on the principal rivers. The travelers were carried across the rivers in canoes, while their horses swam alongside. Later, larger ferry boats were built which could carry both the horses and the riders, but it was many years before carriages could cross any but the shallowest rivers.

In colonial times it was a very common sight to see two people mounted on one horse. One person sat in the saddle and rode the horse in the usual way, and a second person rode behind him on a cushion called a pillion. Usually the pillion-rider sat sideways, with both legs on the same side of the horse, and held on to the rider for support. For a long time during the Colonial Period, travel by horse was the only method of reaching one's destination by land, unless one wished to walk.

When heavy supplies or goods had to be carried from place to place and it was impossible to send them by boat, pack horses were used. The goods to be sent

were carefully wrapped and fastened to the backs of the pack horses, which were then led along either by a man on foot or by a rider on horseback. Sometimes long lines, or trains, of pack horses could be seen wending their way along the forest trails. 

Mail in those early days was carried on horseback by men known as postriders. The service was slow, uncertain, and infrequent, and it cost a great deal to send a letter even a short distance. Often a postrider carried with him a passenger seated on a pillion behind the saddle.

Highways and Coaches. In time some of the forest trails over which the horsemen rode were widened into highways, and when this happened travel by stagecoach began. These stagecoaches were slow and uncomfortable. At first they took seventy-two hours to cover the ninety miles between New York and Philadelphia.

Later, when better roads were built, the time was cut to about thirty-six hours. To us this seems extremely slow, but the colonists were so delighted by the speed of the stagecoaches that they referred to them as "the flying machines." Today an automobile can cover this same distance in two hours or less.

Benjamin Franklin, who improved the mail service in the colonies by increasing the number of postriders, now also played an important part in improving the roads. A new highway, called the Post Road, was built from Philadelphia to Boston, and Franklin personally took charge of marking off the distances on this road. 

To one of the wheels of his coach he fastened a machine

A PRIVATE COACH OF COLONIAL TIMES 

Travel in colonial days was slow, and the bad roads made riding uncomfortable. Coaches furnished the best way of traveling by land, but even these took thirty-six hours or more for the short trip from New York to Philadelphia. which he had invented for registering the distance traveled. At the end of each mile, Franklin stopped and waited for a cart full of milestones which followed his coach. At each point where he stopped a numbered milestone was set in place along the road so that people could tell how far they had traveled and how much farther they had to go to reach their destination. 

Taverns. The development of the stagecoach lines was followed by the building of many inns, or taverns,

along the road. Here the travelers could get meals, a bed, and a stable for their horses. Since many people could not read, each tavern sign contained a picture as well as the tavern's printed name. Thus an inn called "The Blue Anchor" would have upon its sign a picture of an anchor painted blue. Many of the taverns had very picturesque names and signs, such titles as "The 

Red Lion," "The Three Ships," and "The Crown and 

Cannon" being very popular.

The Conestoga Wagons. As the roads continued to improve, freight wagons came into use. The first of these were put into service in the Conestoga valley of Pennsylvania, and they were therefore known as Conestoga wagons. Some of them were large enough to carry six tons of freight, and they were pulled by either two or three teams of horses. On the top of each Conestoga wagon was a wooden framework, and over this was stretched a canvas cover, like a tent. Until the coming of the railroads, long after the end of the Colonial Period, the Conestoga wagons continued to be used in the East. And for many years after there were railroads in the East, the wagons carried freight in the West, where they were known as "covered wagons." 

They have been described as "the finest wagons the world has ever known.

Travel by Water. While travel on land was developing, travel by water was also being improved. In time the canoes and dugouts were largely replaced by small sailing boats. Somewhat larger sailboats, called packet boats, made regular trips between Boston and

New York, New York and Albany, and other towns on the seacoast or the rivers. But until long after the end of the Colonial Period travel by water was slow and uncertain, for it depended on wind and weather. 

Education in the Colonies. Massachusetts was the leading colony in the development of education. As early as 1647, a law was passed there providing that every town of fifty families should have a free school for younger pupils, and that every town of a hundred or more families should also have a school for older pupils. The money to support these schools was raised by taxation, so that everyone had a share in paying for them. The parents of the children who attended the schools, however, paid a larger share of the school expenses than other people of the colony. 

Compared with the schools which we have today, these early colonial schools were not very good. The school building was usually a small log cabin, often with a dirt floor. There were no desks, only a shelf along one of the walls. The pupils sat on stiff wooden benches while reading, or reciting, or listening to the teacher. When the time came to write, each child took a sheet of birch-bark and a quill pen which the teacher had made from a goose feather. Seating himself on a bench before the writing shelf, the pupil dipped the quill into a little cup of home-made ink and wrote as best he could on the birch-bark. In winter, the school building was usually very cold, for the only source of heat was a fireplace in which wood was burned whenever the parents of the pupils remembered to supply it.

The schoolmaster was seldom a well-educated man. 

He lived, in turn, at the homes of the different pupils, and what little pay he got came from the tax money used to support the school. If the schoolmaster happened to be a harsh or impatient person, the duller pupils often had a hard time of it, for in colonial times it was customary to whip the pupils who did not learn their lessons.

Very few subjects were taught in these early schools. 

Rarely did the pupils learn any geography or history, and the teaching of drawing, music, and such subjects was unheard of. Reading, writing, and arithmetic were the only subjects which were regularly taught. 

In the early years of the Colonial Period only boys were permitted to attend the schools, and the girls had merely what education they could get at home. Later, both girls and boys were admitted to the lower grades, though it was a great many years before girls were allowed to share in the higher education of academies and colleges.

School books were scarce in colonial times. The first lessons in reading were usually taught with the aid of a hornbook. This was not really a book at all, but a wooden paddle on which was fastened a piece of paper covered by a thin, transparent sheet of horn. On the paper were printed the alphabet, the numbers from 

1 to 9, and usually the Lord's Prayer.

When they were finished with the hornbook, the pupils studied The New England Primer. This was a little book containing the letters of the alphabet. Each

letter was illustrated with a crude picture and some such verse as this: 

"L The Lion bold 

The Lamb doth hold." "M The Moon gives light 

In time of night." 

The other northern colonies followed Massachusetts and gradually opened schools not unlike those in the Puritan colony. The one real difference was that these schools were supported entirely by the parents of the pupils who attended them. Later the Massachusetts system was adopted, and the schools were supported by public taxes.

In the South, too, after the first years, there were schools in the larger towns, but the sons of the plantation owners usually lived too far away to attend them. 

These boys had to depend on private tutors for their education until they were old enough to travel to 

Europe or to one of the northern colonies to attend school. The girls on the plantations had little education. From their mothers they learned to read and write and to be good housekeepers, but they seldom had very much "book learning."

Colonial Colleges. The first college in the colonies was established in Cambridge, Massachusetts, in 1636, for the purpose of training ministers. Because the 

Reverend John Harvard left some money and half of his library to this new college, it was named. Harvard

in his honor. Today Harvard is still one of our most important educational institutions. 

Fifty-seven years later, in 1693, the second college was founded. This was William and Mary, in the Virginia colony. In 1701, Yale was founded at New Haven, Connecticut, and not long afterwards three more col- 100000 

ONE OF THE OLDEST BUILDINGS AT WILLIAM AND MARY COLLEGE 

William and Mary College, founded at Williamsburg, Virginia, in 1693, was the second college to be established in the 

English colonies in America. 

leges were established-King's College, now Columbia, in New York; the College of New Jersey, now Princeton, in New Jersey; and the College of Philadelphia, now the University of Pennsylvania, in Pennsylvania. 

All of these institutions grew rapidly in size and importance, and soon it was no longer necessary to send boys to Europe to receive an advanced education.

Colonial Government. We have seen that the English colonies in America started with several different forms of government. Some, such as Pennsylvania, the 

Carolinas, and Georgia, were ruled directly by their owners, or proprietors. Others, such as Virginia, were governed by colonizing companies which had founded them. Still others, such as Massachusetts and Rhode 

Island, were governed by their own people. 

In time, all of these English colonies were claimed by the king of England, turned into royal colonies, and given the same general form of government. Each had an Assembly, consisting of representatives chosen by the people to help make the laws. Each of them also had a governor who represented the English king, and who worked with the Assembly to make the laws and to run the affairs of the colony. Usually the governor had even more power than the Assembly, for he could veto, or forbid, any of the laws which the Assembly passed. 

The English king, however, made no attempt to change the local government in the different colonies. 

In New England, this local government was carried on by town meetings. Once a year all of the voters in a town gathered together to consider whatever problems had arisen, and to elect the town officers. The usual problems which had to be discussed were the building of new roads and schools, the appointing of teachers, the repairing of town property, and the collecting and spending of the tax money. Another important duty was the selection of two men to represent the town in

the colonial Assembly. Even today, many New England towns are governed by town meetings. 

In the South, where towns were few and far between, a different form of local government was developed. 

Here the various colonies were divided into sections known as counties. Each county included at least one fair-sized town, the county seat, and sometimes there were several towns in a single county. At least once a year a Court Day was held at the county seat, and people journeyed there from all parts of the county. The procedure on Court Day was much the same as in a New England town meeting. County officers were elected, new taxes were laid, and the people voted on how money was to be spent. Charges against prisoners and disputes between colonists were settled in the County Courthouse. Finally, two men were selected to represent the county in the Assembly. 

In New York, Pennsylvania, and some of the other middle colonies, both town and county governments were to be found. In that case, the town looked after the enforcement of some laws and the county looked after others. Each collected its own taxes and spent its own money. When both forms of government were in use, the representatives sent to the Assembly usually represented the county rather than the town. 

Who Could Vote? We have seen that in Massachusetts and most of the other Puritan colonies only men who were members of the Puritan Church had any voice in the government. In Virginia no man who was not a member of the Church of England could vote.

Most of the other colonies allowed every free man above twenty-one years of age to have a voice in town or county government and in the affairs of the colony. 

This meant that only indentured servants, prisoners, slaves, and women were denied a vote. By the end of the Colonial Period there was even greater freedom, and in all of the colonies all men except slaves and prisoners were allowed a voice in the government. 

How Life Has Changed in America. Our life today is very different from that which the colonists lived. If one of our relatives who lived about the year 1700 were to visit us today he would be struck with wonder at almost everything he saw. Let us imagine, for the moment, that such a thing is possible, and that we are to receive a visit from our Quaker cousin Jonathan, who lives in colonial Philadelphia.

We go to meet Jonathan in our automobile, and at the first sight of it his eyes almost pop out of his head. 

When he has recovered somewhat from his astonishment, he asks how it is possible for a coach to travel without horses. We explain to him as best we can how gasoline motors work, but he still looks at us as though he thinks we are strange beings from another world. 

As our automobile swings out onto the highway and we start home, Jonathan is at first alarmed by the terrific speed at which we are traveling. Never before has he ridden in a coach which could go more than ten miles an hour, and now he is traveling fifty! He can scarcely believe us when we tell him that some automobiles have been made which travel over two hundred

miles an hour, and airplanes have been known to go more than four hundred. Airplanes! The very thought of a machine which can carry men through the air makes him surer than ever that this is a land of magic. 

As we drive through a big city on our way home, Jonathan gazes in wonder at the many astonishing things going on about him. He asks about the flashing electric signs outside of the stores and theaters, and looks thoroughly puzzled even after we have explained to him how they work. The word "electricity" means nothing to him, for it was unknown in his time. His mouth hangs open, and he can hardly believe his eyes when he sees his first tall building. It is not surprising that he is in a high state of excitement by the time we reach our home.

Even our ordinary home is a fairyland to Jonathan. 

The cheery, even warmth which the furnace spreads throughout all the rooms seems marvelous to him. We turn on the electric lights, and he blinks unbelievingly at us. "How did thee kindle these lights?" he wants to know. "What makes them so bright? Where is the fire? Why is there no smoke?" We try to tell him, but we can see that he still does not understand. Even the striking of an ordinary match astonishes him, and he wants to know how it is possible for us to make fires so quickly. Why, in his home a person who wanted to start a fire had to strike pieces of flint and steel together patiently, time after time, until he was lucky enough to strike a hot spark directly into some soft, dry tinder. 

Usually it was easier to walk a couple of miles and bor-

row some hot coals from a neighbor than it was to start a fire by the flint and steel method. 

The running water in our home delights him. He has heard, he says, that in William Penn's big house beside the Delaware river a tank was installed in the attic to hold water for the use of the family. Even in 

Mr. Penn's house, however, there was nothing like this -no running water which could be turned on or off at will, no hot water to be had by a mere turn of a faucet. 

We try in vain to make Jonathan understand how electricity, our useful servant, can not only light our homes, but can also produce heat to toast our bread, can make ice in our electric refrigerator, can carry our voice through wires to distant places, and can even bring us music and entertainment out of the very air. 

Electric heaters, refrigerators, telephones, and radios were undreamed of in Jonathan's day. Even the electric doorbell astonishes him, for he cannot understand how pushing a button in one place can ring a bell in another. 

Jonathan spends two weeks with us. Every day he sees something new and astonishing-elevators, trains, moving pictures. At the end of his visit, as we drive him back toward his home, we ask him what his family and friends will think when he tells them of the marvelous things he has seen. For a moment he gazes at us in silence, and then he says, a little sadly, "I shall tell them about thee, my friends, but I shall not try to tell them of the marvels I have seen." Astonished, we ask him why. "Because," says Jonathan, "if I tried

to tell them of these things they would think I had been visiting with witches, and I would not have them think so of thee."

Most of the English colonists in America were members of the middle class. Few titled lords and few of the lowest social classes of Europe came to America. 

The highest social class in the colonies consisted of the high-born and wealthy colonists. The middle class was made up of farmers, storekeepers, traders and other workmen. The lowest class included the servants and slaves.

Although most of the early settlements in the English colonies were at first much alike, they became in time very different. In the South, huge plantations appeared, and in the northern colonies the settlements grew into villages and cities.

The houses of the colonists differed greatly, depending upon the wealth of their owners and the colonies in which they were located. The houses of the wealthy and middle-class colonists contained furniture from England. The furniture in the farmhouses and in the backwoods cabins was generally very crude. 

In colonial times the fireplace was very important. 

It was almost the only source of heat, and it was also used for cooking the food.

The tableware of colonial times was far different from what we have today. Trenchers, tankards, and

wooden spoons were common. Some pewter dishes were used. Glasses were rare, and forks were unknown in the colonies.

The various classes of colonists dressed in different ways, depending upon their occupations, their wealth, and the colony in which they lived. Children usually dressed much as their parents did. 

In the South, the principal occupations were the raising of cotton, tobacco, and rice. In the North, especially in New England, shipbuilding and fishing were important. Every colony had storekeepers, traders, lawyers, and large numbers of farmers. The backwoodsmen made their living by hunting and trapping, raising vegetables, and trading with the Indians. 

The children of colonial times had fewer amusements and less time to play than modern children have. 

They enjoyed husking bees, quilting parties, and many games and pastimes. The colonial men and women entertained themselves with dances, cards, bowling, cock-fights, bear-baiting, and other sports. The Puritans of New England had the fewest pleasures. 

Religion played an important part in the daily lives of all the colonists. The Sabbath was more strictly observed in New England than in the other colonies. 

Except in Pennsylvania, very severe punishments were provided for all lawbreakers.

In the early years of the Colonial Period there were no roads in the colonies, and people who wished to travel had to walk or go by boat. Later, paths were cleared, roads were built, and people could travel on horseback

or by stagecoach. Freight was carried in Conestoga wagons. As travel increased, taverns were built along the roads. Sailing vessels of many kinds came into use for travel by water.

Massachusetts was the leading colony in the development of education. Later, schools and colleges were established in the other northern colonies and in the towns of the South. Few subjects were taught. For many years, girls were not permitted to attend school, but later they were allowed to do so. 

At first the English colonies in America had several different kinds of colonial governments. In time, all of the colonies came to have assemblies to represent the people, and governors to represent the English king. 

In New England, local government was carried on by town meetings. In the South, county government developed. In the middle colonies both town and county governments were to be found. By the end of the 

Colonial Period all men except slaves and prisoners were allowed a voice in the government. 

A CHOOSING EXERCISE ON THE ENGLISH COLONIES 

Read the following sentences aloud, using the word or words in parenthesis which make each statement correct. 

1. Most of the people who came from Europe to the English colonies in America were members of the (highest-middlelowest) social class. 

2. In the highest social class of colonists were members of the (Cavalier and patroon-Puritan) families, and successful merchants and plantation owners. The middle class was made up

of (free servants-storekeepers, traders, and other workmen). 

The lowest class consisted of (servants and slaves-workmen from Europe). The (highest-middle-lowest) class was the largest, and so was the most important. 

3. The (New England-southern) colonies were made up largely of plantations and a few towns. Most of the New York settlers lived in (log cabins-towns founded by the Dutch). The (southern-New England) settlements were built around (ponds open fields) known as commons. The backwoods settlers lived mostly in (tents-log cabins). 

4. Some of the richer colonists had fine furniture (made in 

America-brought from Europe). In the farmhouses the furniture was (crude-graceful and beautiful). Light was furnished by (kerosene lamps-candles and whale-oil lamps). 

5. In colonial times, most of the cooking was done in (stovesfireplaces). Meat was cooked on (cranes-spits), and vegetables were cooked in (iron ovens-pots hung over the flames). 

There was (little-much beautiful) tableware. Wooden (spoons-plates) called trenchers were used. Forks were (common-not used) in the colonies. 

6. The different classes of colonists dressed (alike-very differently). In the backwoods, (deerskin-satin) suits and (silkhomespun) dresses were worn. The wealthy colonists usually dressed (elaborately-simply), in clothes which they (made at home-imported from Europe). The Quakers and many of the Puritans wore (plainer-more stylish) clothes than other colonists. Children usually dressed much (as-more simply than) their parents did. 

7. In the (southern-New England) colonies, fishing and shipbuilding were important occupations. (Cotton, tobacco, and rice-Coffee and sugar cane) were raised on the plantations of the (North-South). The (merchants-backwoodsmen) of all colonies made their living by hunting, trapping, raising a few vegetables, and trading with the Indians.

8. The children of colonial times had (more-fewer) amusements and (less-more) time to play than modern children. 

Husking bees, quilting parties, (baseball, basketball, and hockey-wrestling, boxing, and croquet) were among their pleasures. The colonial men and women enjoyed (dancing, card playing-bicycling) and bowling. (Cock-fights-Bullfights) and (fish-bear) baiting were popular. The Puritans had (fewer more) pleasures than the other colonists.
