CHAPTER FOURTEEN LIFE IN THE DUTCH, FRENCH, SPANISH, AND PORTUGUESE COLONIES 

Now that we know how the English colonists lived in the New World, let us see what life was like in the French, Spanish, and Portuguese colonies, and in the Dutch colony of New Netherland in the years before it was taken by the English. 

Our first visit will be to New Netherland. 

Life in New Netherland. The Dutch people, like the 

English, were a home-loving race. As they came to 

America to found lasting settlements, the men brought with them their wives and children, as well as many Dutch ideas and customs. Even their towns were built to look like small Dutch cities. The tall brick houses were close together, and here and there a windmill could be seen with its long arms slowly turning in the wind. The streets were kept very clean, for the 

Dutch have always been famous for their cleanliness. 

Many of the houses were furnished with finely-carved furniture brought from Europe. The estates of the patroons, with their broad lands and beautiful, big houses, were as attractive as any estates to be found in America. 

Social Classes. The patroons, of course, formed the highest social class. Next came the storekeepers, millers, traders, and other free workmen. In the lowest 318

class were the tenant farmers, who lived on the patroons' estates and had few privileges. 

Clothes. In the early years of the Dutch settlements, men, women, and children all wore the quaint costumes which in those days were worn in Holland. 

NEW AMSTERDAM ABOUT 1660 

This is how the Dutch settlement of New Amsterdam looked during the last few years of Dutch rule. Notice the windmill, the quaint Dutch houses made of brick, the fort, and the gallows from which prisoners were hanged. 

The men dressed in broad-brimmed hats; cloth jackets with white collars; short, full pantaloons; long stockings; and low-cut shoes. The women wore long, full dresses, usually of brightly-colored cloth; white, lacetrimmed collars and aprons; and white, starched caps with flaring edges. The clothes of the poorer colonists were usually made of homespun cloth, but many of the wealthy Dutchmen's clothes were made of rich cloth

brought from Europe. In place of low-cut leather shoes, some of the poorer people wore the clumsy wooden shoes which the Dutch peasants in Europe were wearing at that time. After New Netherland was taken by the 

English, the costumes worn were like those in the other 

English colonies. 

Occupations and Pastimes. The occupations of the 

Dutch colonists did not differ much from those in the English colonies. There were storekeepers, innkeepers, shipbuilders, millers, and members of many other everyday businesses. There were also many farmers and gardeners who raised crops to be used as food throughout the colony. 

Nor were the games and pleasures in New Netherland very different from those in the nearby English colonies. Bowling, which later became popular throughout all of the colonies, had its start in New Netherland, and in much the same way husking bees, quilting parties, and the various games popular in the other colonies gradually spread to the Dutch settlements. 

Food. It was even more important for the Dutch colonists in America to raise their own food than it was for the English colonists. The English could send food from colony to colony in case supplies ran short, while the Dutch had only the single colony of New Netherland, and so could not do this. Corn, beans, potatoes, and several kinds of animals were raised to supply food for the people. The Dutch people were good farmers, and, after the colony was well started, there was never a food shortage in New Netherland.

Religion. As we have learned, the Dutch were a freedom-loving people, and they permitted all who came to their colony to worship as they pleased. The official church of Holland was the Dutch Reformed Church, but the colonists did not have to support it or attend its services. 

Travel, Punishments, and Education. In a great many respects, New Netherland was almost exactly like the English colonies. Traveling was done on foot or horseback, and later in carriages or in sailboats. 

Prisoners were punished by means of the whipping post or the gallows. The little Dutch schools in the early days of the colony were not very different from the schools of Massachusetts and the other English colonies. Until after the Dutch lost their control of the colony they had founded, there were no colleges in it. 

Government. The government of New Netherland was largely in the hands of governors appointed by the Dutch West India Company. The people had very little say in any of the colonial affairs. In this way the 

Dutch colony differed a good deal from many of the English colonies, where the people were granted a voice in their government. 

Life in the French Colonies. The people who came from France to the French colonies of Louisiana and New France were rather different in type from the English and the Dutch. Most of them were not homelovers, intent upon making new homes in the wilderness. Although settlements grew up at Quebec, Montreal, Three Rivers, and New Orleans, they remained

THE FOUNDING OF QUEBEC 

Here we see Champlain, the "father of New France," directing the building of a palisade at Quebec. In all the French lands in 

America, there were only four really important towns-Quebec, Montreal, Three Rivers, and New Orleans.

small during the period when the French held the land, for few French families cared to cross the ocean and settle down in the New World. Most of those who came were bold, adventurous men who wished to make their fortunes and then return to France. 

The Seigniors and Habitants. King Louis XIII 

was troubled to see so few French settlers in the New World. So, to encourage his people to go to America, he planned a colonizing system much like the patroon system of New Netherland. Large estates along the 

St. Lawrence river were given to certain settlers, known as seigniors. The French government then offered to give French farmers free transportation across the ocean if they would live and work on the seigniors' estates. The farmers who accepted this offer were known as habitants.

Usually the houses of the seigniors were not so large and so richly furnished as those of the patroons, for most of the seigniors had little wealth. The farm lands along the St. Lawrence river had fertile soil, but so few habitants came to the New World that New France never became a great farming district, in spite of the king's attempt to make it so. Though some crops of wheat and oats were grown for food and some sheep were raised to provide wool for warm clothing, much of the food and clothing had to be brought from France. 

The fur trade continued to supply almost all of the wealth of the French colonists.

On the estate of each of the seigniors was a little village where the habitants lived in small houses of

wood or stone, crowded close together along a single narrow street. Most of the houses had two rooms, a living room where all the members of the family ate, slept, and spent whatever time they were indoors, and a storeroom where provisions were kept. 

In addition to the houses of the habitants, each little village contained a church, or chapel, and a windmill. 

In the chapel, the villagers, practically all of whom were Catholics, gathered for church services. Every estate had its priest, usually a Jesuit. In the round, stone windmill building was the machinery for grinding grain. Many of the windmill buildings were also fitted with strong doors and loopholes, so that they could serve as forts in time of danger.

Towns. Besides the little villages on the seigniors' estates, there were the more important towns of Quebec, Montreal, Three Rivers, and New Orleans. The first three of these were on the St. Lawrence river, and the fourth was near the mouth of the Mississippi. All were much alike. There were a few narrow winding streets crowded with quaint little houses like those of France. Each town had one or more churches, and a fort to protect the townspeople. Along the river were wharves and warehouses. Ships might be found at the 

New Orleans wharves at any time of the year, but the towns on the St. Lawrence could be reached by boat only during the late spring, the summer, and the early fall, because the river froze over in winter. 

The Woods Runners. Many of the French colonists lived neither on the estates of the seigniors nor in the

towns along the rivers, but deep in the forests. These people were the "woods runners"-trappers and traders who made their living in the depths of the forests trading with the Indians. In their small log cabins far from civilization the woods runners lived the whole year round, coming to the settlements only when they needed supplies or had furs to sell. Sometimes one of these hardy men would go for many months without seeing another white man. It is small wonder that they came to know the Indians well, and that many of them married Indian women.

Clothing. The clothing of the French colonists was noted for its brilliant colors. Many of the less wealthy seigniors and their habitants dressed much alike. In summer the men would wear short jackets, long trousers, and deerskin moccasins. The trousers and jackets might be any color of the rainbow, depending on the taste of the wearer. Usually they did not match, but were of different colors, and often a brilliant sash of a third color gave an added touch of vividness. In winter, the costumes were the same except that a long coat was worn over the jacket, high deerskin boots replaced the moccasins, and a bright woolen cap was worn on the head.

The wives of the less wealthy seigniors and of the habitants also dressed in brilliant colors. They wore long skirts, tight-fitting jackets, and shawls and scarfs of wool. Woolen stockings and deerskin moccasins completed the costume.

In the towns, the clothing of the middle class of peo-

ple was much like that of the habitants and the less wealthy seigniors. The government officials, the army officers, the wealthy merchants, and the rich seigniors, however, wore finer clothes. They dressed much as the wealthier English colonists did, in long coats and knee breeches of rich material, silk stockings, low shoes with silver buckles, and broad-brimmed hats. Sometimes a flowing cloak of some rich color was also worn. The wives of these wealthy colonists dressed much like the wealthy ladies of the English colonies.

Like the backwoodsmen of the English colonies, the French woods runners usually dressed in deerskin coats, trousers, and moccasins. In winter they wore fur caps on their heads and high deerskin boots on their feet. They also used snowshoes in winter whenever they left their cabins, for the deep snow in the northern woods made travel impossible without them. 

Pastimes. Although the French are a pleasure-loving race, the French colonists in the New World had few forms of amusement. Like their English neighbors to the south, colonists who lived near enough together sometimes gathered for husking or quilting bees or for feasts. Story-telling and singing were other favorite pastimes. In the larger settlements, dances were sometimes held.

Religion. The Catholic Church played a most important part in the history of New France. As practically all of the settlers belonged to this church, it served to draw them close together by giving them a common interest. The Catholic priests who came from France

worked well to keep the settlers loyal to their king and their government across the sea. 

Even more important was the work of the missionaries who went out to preach to the Indians. These tireless workers, living among the Indians and helping 

A FRENCH MISSIONARY SETTLEMENT 

In little mission-forts like this, the French missionaries endured the hard life of the wilderness in order to teach the Catholic religion to the Indians. them in many ways, were able to gain and hold for France strong redskin allies and friends. Many of the missionaries also explored and claimed sections of the New World for the French government. 

Travel. Travel in the French lands of America was slow and difficult. There were no good roads at all. 

Men mounted on horses could follow the Indian trails

without much trouble, but wagons and coaches were useless. For this reason, there was very little travel throughout the French lands. The woods runners depended upon their own strong legs and upon their birch-bark canoes for traveling from place to place. In the river settlements, canoes and boats were used almost entirely.

Government. When King Louis XIV was king of 

France he decided that his colony of New France was not being managed very well, so he established a new type of government for it. He appointed a committee of fifteen colonists in whom he had confidence, and ordered this group to meet every Monday morning and to send him a complete report of what was going on in the New World, and what should be done to improve the colony. From time to time new members were appointed to serve on the committee, to replace members who died, resigned, or were removed.

The king's committee governed New France for more than a hundred years. All important questions which arose were discussed and settled by the committee members. Criminals were tried by them, and were either released or sentenced. If his crime were serious, a criminal was imprisoned for a long period of time or was hanged. The French made little use of the stocks, pillory, ducking stool, and other forms of punishment so popular in some of the English colonies. 

Although the king of France read very carefully the long reports sent to him by his committee in America, he did not often interfere with the committee's

work. He was content to let well enough alone, and as a result the French colonists in America did not suffer the constant royal interference which the English colonists experienced.

Education. With the French colonists scattered about as they were, it was very difficult to educate their children. Only the boys and girls in a few of the larger settlements could attend schools, for there were no schools on the seigniors' estates or in the wilderness where the woods runners made their homes. 

The priests, who were educated men, did their best to make up for this lack. Besides teaching religion to the Indians and the habitants, they also taught reading and writing, and sometimes a little arithmetic. It was the priests who founded the few schools in the larger settlements, and the teachers there were mostly priests and nuns. In Quebec the Jesuits even founded a college which has now grown into Laval University. 

Nevertheless, there was very little chance for education in most parts of the French colonies.

How People Lived in the Spanish Colonies. The 

Spaniards, you remember, sent explorers to the New

World before the English, French, or Dutch, and they were also the first to make settlements in America. 

Like the French, most of the early Spanish settlers who came to the New World were interested in becoming wealthy and then returning to Europe to live. In time, however, some of the settlers brought their wives and families and decided to remain in New Spain, as the Spanish lands in America were called. In Florida, in

RUINS OF THE OLD SPANISH FORT AT ST. AUGUSTINE 

This grim old fort still stands at St. Augustine, Florida. The town, founded by the Spaniards in 1565, was the first permanent settlement in what is now the United States. 

Mexico, in the southwestern part of what is now the United States, in South America, and in the West Indies the Spaniards settled and established the Spanish language and civilization. 

Social Classes. In the Spanish colonies there were three distinct social classes. The highest class consisted of pure-blooded Spaniards who had been born in Spain and who had moved to America. These people were very proud, and they took for themselves the best of everything. The best offices and the highest salaries

were theirs, and as a result they became the wealthiest class.

Next in line came the Creoles. These people, too, were pure-blooded Spaniards, but they were born in the New World instead of in Spain. There was no law which said that the Creoles should have any fewer opportunities or advantages than the Spaniards from Spain, but they usually did. In time the Creole people became the middle class in the Spanish colonies-the merchants, traders, and workmen. 

To the lowest social class belonged the many thousands of people who were not of pure Spanish blood. 

Some of these were Indians who had been living in New Spain before the Spaniards claimed it. Some were Negroes who had been brought as slaves. Many were mixtures, or half-breeds, part Spanish and part Indian, or part Indian and part Negro. At first, all of the Indians and Negroes and many of the half-breeds were slaves, but the Spanish government early passed laws forbidding slavery in the colonies. Though this made the slaves free, they still had very few rights, and in many ways were not much better off than they had been.

Settlements and Buildings. Most of the important towns of the Spanish colonies were not started by the Spaniards at all but by the Aztec or the Inca Indians, who had owned the land before the coming of the Spaniards. In these towns were many stone buildings which the Indians had built, as well as houses, churches, and other buildings of Spanish design.

As all of the Spanish colonies were in warm lands, the houses were built to shut out the heat rather than the cold. Many houses of the better class had open courtyards, with fountains, plots of grass, and trees to provide cool shade. The buildings were low, rambling structures, usually made of adobe, or baked clay. Some were very plain and homely, but most of them were decorated with beautiful woodwork and with skilfully wrought iron grills and balconies. The best homes had finely made furniture brought from Spain. 

The people of the poorest class also lived in houses of adobe, but they were far different from the homes. of the Spaniards and the Creoles. They were, in fact, tiny huts with adobe walls, dirt floors, and little or no furniture. In some of the poorer houses, there were no windows at all, and only one doorway, through which the members of the family and their chickens and pigs wandered at will.

The Spanish churches, missions, and public buildings were large and very beautiful, for the adobe walls were regularly whitewashed, and there were many decorations of carved wood and wrought iron. 

Clothing. As we might expect, the Spaniards and the Creoles dressed in the beautiful and elaborate fashions that were then popular in Europe. The men wore high boots, beautifully embroidered coats and trousers of satin, broad-brimmed hats with flowing plumes, brilliant cloaks, and usually swords with decorated hilts. The women wore the lovely costumes of 

Spain-long, graceful dresses trimmed with lace, gaily

colored shawls called mantillas, fine silk stockings, dainty shoes with high heels, and, in their hair, high 

Spanish combs. 

The people of the lower class had no special costumes. In general, they wore rags, and often not very many rags, because the weather was warm. 

Occupations. The Spaniards, as a race, have never been fond of hard work. The first who came to America came in search of treasure, which they took from the Indians whom they conquered. When they had seized the riches of the Indians, the Spaniards formed companies to work the gold and silver mines. Even so, they did little of the work themselves but had it done by the 

Negroes, Indians, and half-breeds.

In time, some of the Spanish settlers developed another kind of industry. They brought from other countries the seeds of grapes, lemons, oranges, coffee, rice, cotton, and sugar cane, and discovered that these plants would grow well in the soil of the New World. 

Soon plantations were laid out, and before long, fruits, vegetables, and other agricultural products were shipped to Europe. On the plantations, as in the mines, the hard work was done by the Negroes, Indians, and workmen of mixed blood.

Religion. By far the hardest workers among the pure-blooded Spaniards who came to America were the priests. These men came with a sincere desire to teach the Indians the Catholic religion. Throughout the 

Spanish settlements they established missions, where the Indians could come and hear the teachings of the

AN OLD SPANISH MISSION IN CALIFORNIA Throughout their New World colonies, the Spaniards built missions where the Catholic religion was taught to the Indians. 

Catholic Church. The priests also helped the 

Indians in other ways, for they taught the red men how to farm, and they often took care of the sick in the Indian villages. 

Education. As in the 

French colonies, it was the priests who did most of the work of educating in New Spain. Schools were started, where the children of the Spaniards, the Creoles, and even the poor workmen could learn to read and write the Spanish language. In some of the schools, arithmetic, music, and painting were also taught. But the most important subject of all was religion.

In the founding of colleges, the Spaniards far outdid the people of any other European nation in America. In their

lands they founded fourteen universities and a great many small colleges. One of the most famous universities was at Mexico City, and others were at Lima, Peru, and Havana, Cuba. All of these are still in existence.

Pastimes. The Spaniards, like the French, are a pleasure-loving people, and to their New World colonies they carried many of the sports and pastimes of Old Spain. There were many parties, or fiestas. Dances were held in all of the larger towns and cities. In some of the colonies, especially in Mexico, the Spanish national sport of bullfighting was introduced and became very popular. Bullfights are still held today in Mexico. 

Travel. Unlike the other Europeans who settled in 

America, the Spaniards found good roads already made in some of their colonies. The Incas of Peru were especially remarkable in this respect, for they made flat, level, stone-paved roads sometimes twenty or twentyfive feet in width. These roads were better than even the Spaniards themselves could build. It was not difficult for the Spaniards, therefore, to build up fine transportation routes between the different settlements of these colonies. They did not, however, build roads connecting one colony with another, for a reason which we shall soon learn.

Over their roads, the Spaniards traveled on horseback and in carriages. In some of their colonies they also made use of small sailboats to travel from settlement to settlement along the coast or on the rivers. 

Trade and communication with Europe were carried on

by means of large sailing ships called galleons. Because of the dangers from pirates and English sea rovers such as Sir Francis Drake, these galleons did not ordinarily sail alone, but in armed fleets. As these ships carried so much treasure from the New World to the Old, the fleets of galleons were known as "the silver fleets." 

Punishments. In their manner of punishing criminals and other prisoners, the Spaniards used much more cruel methods than the people in the other New World colonies. Though some prisoners were put to death quickly and easily by hanging or by having their heads cut off, others were locked in iron cages and left in the public square to die of hunger, thirst, and exposure. When they wished to make a stubborn prisoner talk, the Spaniards made use of the rack, a framework on which the prisoner's body could be slowly stretched out by means of ropes fastened to the hands and feet, until the pain became greater than he could stand. The thumbscrew, a vise in which the fingers were pinched, was another favorite Spanish machine of torture.

Food. Just as the Spaniards made use of the roads of the Indian nations whom they conquered, so did they also make use of their foods. The beans, corn, grains, and other crops of the Indians soon became a regular part of the Spanish diet. The wealthiest 

Spaniards, however, continued to have their wines, their spices, their olives, and certain other special foods brought to them from Europe, for these could not be obtained in the New World.

Government. The government of the Spanish colonies was carried on by governors known as viceroys, a title which means "assistant kings." That is exactly what the viceroys were. Each one of them had to obey the orders of the king of Spain, but each ruled in one of the Spanish colonies in the New World with all the power of a king. When the king of Spain wished to send orders to one of his viceroys, he wrote a letter and sent it by special messenger. Usually it took several months for the messenger to reach the viceroy, so messages were sent only when it was necessary, and the viceroys were left alone to rule much as they wished. 

The people themselves had little or no voice in the government.

There was one thought that continually troubled the Spanish king. Suppose his American colonies were to revolt against him, and unite with each other to overthrow his control! To prevent their doing such a thing, the king forbade the colonies to have any communication with one another. That is why roads were not built from one colony to another. Each of the viceroys was responsible only to the king and to the Spanish government, and was expected to play no part in the affairs of any colony but his own. This may be one reason why the Spanish colonies did not unite against Spain until long after both France and England had lost most of their lands in the New World.

How People Lived in the Portuguese Colony of 

Brazil. The Portuguese, you remember, were great explorers. It was Prince Henry of Portugal who

thought of the idea of ships reaching India by sailing south around Africa. It was Vasco da Gama, another 

Portuguese, who first reached India by following the route Prince Henry had thought of. In time, many other Portuguese ships sailed around the south of Africa, and a prosperous trade was built up. 

The Discovery and Settlement of Brazil. In 1500, not many years after the beginning of the Portuguese trade with India, a Portuguese sea captain named Cabral set out in his ship to reach India. To avoid calms off the African coast, he changed his route and sailed hundreds of miles west of his course. When finally he sighted land, he believed that it must be an unknown island never before reached by a man from Europe, and he claimed it in the name of Portugal. 

The king of Portugal was interested in his possession in the New World, and he gave it the name Brazil. 

The very next year after Cabral's discovery of Brazil, another Portuguese expedition went there, headed by Americus Vespucius. Vespucius sailed along the coast and discovered that Brazil was not an island but part of the mainland of South America. After many weeks of sailing, he entered a beautiful harbor, which he named Rio de Janeiro. Then he turned to the east and sailed back to Portugal, reporting to the 

Portuguese king what he had seen.

Portugal at this time was engaged in developing her colonies in the East Indies, and for thirty years no more interest was taken in Brazil. In 1532, however, King John III of Portugal decided to develop his

American lands. To each of fifteen Portuguese noblemen he gave one hundred and fifty miles of coast, each section to extend inland an indefinite distance. fifteen noblemen were known as donatarios. The donatarios were given the right to found cities, levy taxes, and to manage their own sections just as they pleased. The king reserved for himself the right to levy duties on goods shipped in or out of Brazil, and he also claimed for himself the sole right to trade in dyewood and spices. 

Several years after the founding of his colonial system in Brazil, King John came to realize that it was very unsuccessful. Only ten of the donatarios had even shown interest enough to occupy their lands, and, The 

AMERICUS VESPUCIUS- FOR WHOM AMERICA WAS NAMED 

In 1501, Americus Vespucius, an Italian sailing under the flag of Portugal, explored the eastern coast of South America, where the Portuguese colony of Brazil was later developed. of these ten, eight were having much trouble and making no profit. The few colonists who had left Portugal and gone to Brazil were discontented, for the donatarios were proving to be hard masters, and the people on their

lands had few rights indeed. Attacks by Indians and occasional raids by French pirates served to make a bad situation worse.

For these reasons, King John III appointed a governor-general to run the affairs of the entire Brazilian colony. This move took most of the power away from the donatarios, though they still held their lands. At once conditions improved. Forts were built as a protection against the pirates and Indians, and the little town of Bahia, which the governor-general selected for his capital, soon grew into a thriving city. Now, for the first time, colonists began to travel to Brazil in large numbers.

Religion. King John III was very anxious to establish the Catholic religion firmly in his new colony, and soon after the appointment of the first governor-general he sent a number of missionaries, mostly Jesuits, to Brazil. These men worked hard to convert the Indians, and a great many of the red men in time accepted the Catholic faith. The missionaries also strongly opposed the seizing of Indians for slaves, a cruel practice which had been started in the Brazil colony. They at last won the victory, for the Portuguese king made a new law declaring that no Indians were to be held as slaves unless they had been captured in war. 

Occupations. The chief industries of the Brazil colony were the gathering and exporting of dyewood, and the raising of sugar and coffee. All three of these industries were profitable, and some of the coffee and sugar planters grew very wealthy. When King John's

PORTUGUESE SOLDIERS RAIDING AN INDIAN VILLAGE 

In the early years of the Brazil colony, bands of Portuguese soldiers often raided the Indian villages, burning the houses, killing any Indians who resisted, and taking many as slaves. 

Thanks to the work of the Jesuit missionaries, the Portuguese king finally forbade these cruel raids. new law about the Indians was passed, however, the planters suffered from a shortage of laborers. As a result they started importing Negro slaves from Africa, and before long slavery gained a firm foothold in Brazil. 

Life in Brazil. In the hot, moist country of Brazil, the Portuguese colonists lived very much as the Spaniards lived in their warm colonial possessions. The houses and public buildings were low, rambling struc-

tures of stone or brick, usually built around an open courtyard. Most of them were whitewashed on the outside, for this helped to keep out the blazing heat of the sun.

The clothing of the colonists, too, was suited to the warm climate. There were no elaborate costumes, such as were worn in Europe. Even the wealthiest colonists were satisfied with plain trousers and jackets of light cloth, and the Negroes and Indians wore nothing more than a piece of cloth tied about the waist. 

Like the Spaniards, the Portuguese were not fond of hard work. The pure-blooded Portuguese, who formed the highest social class in the colony, did little or no work themselves but merely supervised the work of the Indians, the Negro slaves, and the half-breeds. 

Though the climate and soil of Brazil were well suited to coffee and sugar production, grains, vegetables, and many other crops would not grow there very well. For this reason, the colonists raised little of their own food, but imported practically all of it from Europe. 

There were no universities in the Portuguese colony of Brazil, and very few schools. Though the Jesuits did some teaching, the only way that any of the colonists could receive a good education was by going to 

Europe and studying there. 

Travel. One of the main reasons why Brazil developed slowly was the difficulty of traveling from place to place within the colony. There were no roads at all, and very few paths. Most of the land was covered by thick, tropical jungles through which it was almost im-

possible to make one's way. The only means of transportation was by boat along the many streams and rivers. As a result, the settlements which grew up were along the coast and river banks. The greater part of Brazil was left to the Jungle Indians, who still live there almost undisturbed to this very day. 

Dutch and French Attempts to Take Brazil. For a period of sixty years, from 1580 to 1640, Brazil was a Spanish rather than a Portuguese colony. This was not because the Spaniards captured it from the Portuguese, but because the countries of Spain and Portugal were united under the same government during that period, with the king of Spain as the ruler of both lands. During this period, the Dutch occupied part of 

Brazil and tried to make it a colony of Holland. At last they gave up the attempt, for the Portuguese settlers there resisted them at every turn. The French, too, twice tried to take Brazil, but both times the Portuguese drove them away.

The Discovery of Minerals. In 1693, gold was discovered in the interior of the colony, and not long afterwards diamonds, too, were found. These discoveries led many people from Europe to travel to South America, and the population of the colony grew. 

Pombal's Reforms. About 1750, the Marquis of 

Pombal, who was the Prime Minister of Portugal, made a number of important changes in the colony of Brazil. 

The few remaining privileges of the donatarios were taken away and given to the governor-general. The 

Indians were given the same rights as the white settlers.

The capital of the colony was moved from Bahia to Rio de Janeiro. All of these changes were real improvements. With them, however, went another move that seems cruel and unjust, for Pombal issued an order that the Jesuits must leave Brazil and Portugal.

He did this because the Jesuits had opposed certain of the changes he was making in the Brazilian government. Those of the Jesuits who refused to leave were thrown into prison, and a few were hanged for daring to disobey the government. 

Brazil Becomes Independent. Like the Spanish colonies, Brazil remained loyal to her mother country long after England and France had lost most of their New World possessions. But after many years, the 

Brazilians revolted and declared their independence. 

Though more than a hundred years have passed since that happened, the Portuguese language and the Catholic religion, which King John's settlers established there, are still the language and religion of practically all of the people of Brazil.

The Dutch were a home-loving race who came to the New World to establish lasting settlements. In New 

Netherland they built towns like those in Holland. 

At first the Dutch colonists wore the quaint, oldfashioned costumes which were worn in Holland, but after their colony was captured by the English they adopted English styles of dress.

In occupations, pastimes, travel, punishments, and education, the Dutch were much like the neighboring 

English colonists. 

All colonists in New Netherland were permitted to worship as they pleased. 

The government of the Dutch colonists was largely in the hands of the governors. The people had little voice in colonial affairs.

The French colonists who came to the New World were adventurous men who wished to make their fortunes and return to France.

In order to encourage settlement, the king of France gave large estates to certain settlers known as seigniors. 

French farmers, called habitants, were sent to live and work on the seigniors' estates. 

New France never became a great farming district. 

Most of the wealth of the French colonists came from the fur trade.

There were only four important towns in the French colonies in North America. These were Quebec, Montreal, Three Rivers, and New Orleans. 

Many of the French colonists lived in the forest and gained their living by trading with the Indians. They were known as woods runners. 

The clothing of the French colonists differed greatly according to wealth and occupations. All of the colonists dressed in brilliant colors. 

The French colonists had few forms of amusement. 

Husking bees, quilting parties, feasts, dancing, storytelling, and singing were their pastimes.

Practically all of the French settlers belonged to the Catholic Church. Missionaries went out among the 

Indians and preached the Catholic religion to them. 

There were no good roads in the French lands in America, and travel was slow and difficult. 

For about one hundred years the colony of New France was governed by a committee of fifteen men appointed by the French king. 

There was only one university and few schools in the French colonies in America. Most of the educational work was done by priests and nuns. 

The earliest Spanish settlers in the New World were interested in becoming wealthy and then returning to Europe to live. Later, some settlers brought their wives and families and settled down. 

In the Spanish colonies there were three social classes the pure-blooded Spaniards born in Spain, the Creoles, and the half-breeds. 

Most of the important towns in the Spanish colonies contained stone buildings built by the Indians, and buildings, made of adobe, put up by the Spaniards. 

The Spanish churches and public buildings were large and beautiful.

The Spaniards and Creoles in the Spanish colonies dressed in beautiful and elaborate clothes. The people of the lowest class usually dressed in rags. 

The most important occupations of New Spain were the mining of valuable minerals and the growing of fruits and other agricultural products. 

Many Catholic priests and missionaries came to the

Spanish colonies, where they taught the Catholic religion to the Indians and also established schools. 

There were a number of universities in New Spain. 

The Spaniards carried to their colonies many of the sports and pastimes of Spain, including parties, dancing, and bullfighting.

In some of their colonies, the Spaniards found good roads which the Indians had made. Transportation within these colonies was good, but no roads joined one colony to another. 

The Spanish colonies were governed by viceroys. 

The people had little voice in the government. In order to keep the colonies from uniting, each viceroy was made responsible directly to the Spanish king. 

Brazil was discovered by Cabral in 1500, and the Brazilian coast was explored by Americus Vespucius the next year.

In 1532, King John III of Portugal divided the Brazilian coast into fifteen equal sections, which he gave to fifteen of his noblemen, known as donatarios. 

Some years later he took away much of the power of the donatarios and appointed a governor-general to rule the entire country. Brazil then became more prosperous.

King John sent to Brazil many missionaries, mostly Jesuits. These taught the Catholic religion to the 

Indians, and they also succeeded in having a law passed forbidding the colonists to use the Indians as slaves unless captured in war. As a result of this law, many 

Negro slaves were brought to Brazil.

The houses of the Portuguese colonists in Brazil were low, rambling structures of stone and brick, usually built around an open courtyard. 

The chief industries of Brazil were the gathering and exporting of dyewood and the raising of sugar and coffee. Later the mining of gold and diamonds also became important.

From 1580 to 1640, the king of Spain ruled Portugal, and Brazil was a Spanish colony. During this period both Holland and France tried unsuccessfully to gain control of Brazil.

After many years as a colony of Portugal, Brazil revolted and became independent. The Portuguese language is still spoken in Brazil, and the Catholic religion is still followed by most of the people.

CHAPTER FIFTEEN THREE INTERCOLONIAL WARS IN AMERICA 

Between 1689 and 1748, three important wars were fought in Europe. In each of them, England fought on one side and France on the other. The 

English and French colonists in America, loyal to their mother countries, carried on these wars in the 

New World. 

Conditions in Europe. During the time when the 

European colonies in America were growing and developing, conditions in Europe were far from peaceful. 

In France, King Louis XIV had the strongest army in all Europe. Spain, Holland, and most of the other 

European countries were weak. But England, with a strong navy and army, was a serious rival of France. 

We have already learned that in 1688 the people of England arose and drove King James II from the throne. In his place the English people put his daughter Mary and her husband, Prince William of Orange, who thus became a leader in the governments of both England and Holland. James II took refuge in the court of King Louis XIV, who was a friend of his. 

While James was king, England and France had been friendly, but when King William and Queen Mary came to the throne the friendship between the two countries ended. 351

As head of the Dutch Republic, Prince William of 

Orange had long been an enemy of King Louis XIV. 

Some years earlier, King Louis had tried to conquer Holland. Though the attempt was not successful, Prince William did not forget. One of his first acts after he became king of England was to prepare for a war against France.

King William's War. The war began in 1689 and lasted for eight years. On one side was France, and on the other side were Holland, England, Austria, Sweden, and Spain. The countries opposed to France formed a league the League of Augsburg. So in Europe the war had the high-sounding title of the War of the League of Augsburg. In the colonies it was known by a much simpler name. The colonists called it King 

William's War.

When news of the war in Europe reached the English colonies, the people did not at first know what to do. They were loyal Englishmen, and were anxious to do anything they could to help their mother country. 

On the other hand, they had no desire to start a needless and bloody war with the French in North America. 

While they were still trying to make up their minds what to do, the question was decided for them. 

Raids by the French and Indians. Count Frontenac was the French governor of New France, or Canada, as it was now called. He regarded the war in Europe as a splendid excuse for attacking the neighboring English colonies, and he lost no time in organizing a number of armed expeditions.

THREE INTERCOLONIAL WARS 

353 A band of Algonquin Indians, led by Frenchmen, moved south from Canada through the New York woods towards the little village of Schenectady, not far from  

THE SCHENECTADY MASSACRE 

The fighting in King William's War began when a band of Frenchmen and Indians from Canada attacked the village of Schenectady, New York, killed the people, and burned the houses. 

Albany. It was night when they reached the settlement. Never dreaming of an attack, the villagers had posted no guards and had even neglected to close the gates of their palisade. Into the sleeping town crept the French and Indians under cover of darkness. Sud-

denly the dreadful Indian war whoop rang out. People sprang from their beds, only to be struck down by the tomahawks of the Indians or the bullets of the French. 

Most of the men, women, and children in the town were killed, the houses were destroyed, and little indeed was left of Schenectady when the raiders again turned their faces to the north.

At about the same time that Schenectady was being destroyed, other bands of French and Indians raided Haverhill, Massachusetts, and other towns in New England. Many of the English settlers were killed, and a few prisoners were taken back to Canada. 

Attacks by the English Colonists. Naturally, the 

English colonists were horrified. As we already know, Jacob Leisler of New York called a meeting of representatives from the New York, Massachusetts Bay, Plymouth, Connecticut, and Maryland colonies, and raised an army to defeat the French and Indian raiders. 

Though this expedition was unsuccessful, a group of 

Massachusetts volunteers led by Sir William Phips succeeded in capturing Port Royal, in Acadia.

Encouraged by the success of their attack on Port Royal, the Massachusetts colonists now planned to capture the most important French fortress in America- Quebec. A fleet of warships was sent along the coast and up the St. Lawrence river, but the fort proved stronger than expected, and the attack failed. Then winter came, and the cold weather forced the attackers to return to their homes without having accomplished anything.

THREE INTERCOLONIAL WARS 

355 The Treaty of Ryswick. For several years the war went on in Europe, but no more important battles were fought in America. From time to time bands of French and Algonquins raided the English settlements, and these visits were usually repaid by the Iroquois, who sent several bands of warriors into Canada to massacre their French and Indian enemies. At last the fighting stopped on both sides of the Atlantic ocean. Neither 

France nor the League of Augsburg had succeeded in winning a decisive victory. The Treaty of Ryswick, which was signed in 1697, restored to the nations which had fought in the war all lands they had lost. Thus 

Port Royal, which Phips and his volunteers had so bravely captured, was given back to the French. 

Queen Anne's War. In 1701, about four years after the end of King William's War, trouble once more broke out in Europe. The king of Spain had died, leaving his throne to Prince Philip, a grandson of King 

Louis XIV. Most of the nations of Europe objected to having Philip as ruler of Spain, for it was felt that, with a member of King Louis' family on the Spanish throne, France would become dangerously powerful. 

So England, Holland, Austria, Portugal, Prussia, and a few other countries prepared for war against France. 

War was declared in 1702.

This time France did not have to fight alone, for 

Spain and Bavaria joined with her. Because the war was brought about by a quarrel over the choice of a new king for Spain, it was known in Europe as the War of the Spanish Succession.

England had a better reason than her allies for making war on France. King William and Queen Mary were now dead, and Queen Mary's younger sister, Anne, had been chosen by the English Parliament to rule the country. King Louis XIV of France protested. He declared that the son of his old friend, James II, should be the ruler of England. So England entered the war, not only to settle who should be ruler of Spain, but also to protect the throne of Queen Anne. Since the English colonists in America were much more interested in who occupied the English throne than in who became king of Spain, they referred to the war as Queen 

Anne's War. 

Raids by the French and Indians. As in King William's War, the first fighting in the colonies began with French and Indian attacks made on English settlements. Just as the Frenchmen and their savage followers had swept down on Schenectady under cover of night, they now swept down on Deerfield and Haverhill, Massachusetts. In both of these towns, the settlers were taken completely by surprise, and many were cruelly murdered. The houses were burned, and the few people who were captured alive were marched off to Canada. Whenever a prisoner weakened on the journey and was unable to keep up with the rest of the party, his sufferings were quickly ended by a blow from an Indian tomahawk.

Attacks on Port Royal and Quebec. During the first few years of Queen Anne's War, the English in Europe were so busy fighting that they could not help

THREE INTERCOLONIAL WARS 357 

their colonists in America, but at last they sent a few warships to the colonies. At once plans were made to strike an important blow at the French. A fleet carrying many soldiers sailed from Boston to Acadia, where Port Royal was taken without much trouble. 

As in King William's War, the capture of Port

Royal encouraged the English soldiers and colonists to believe that they might also take Quebec. Once more an expedition set out by sea, and once more the attack failed dismally. This time the fleet did not even reach 

Quebec, for many of the ships ran on the rocks near the mouth of the St. Lawrence river, and nearly a thousand men were drowned. Sorrowfully the remaining soldiers returned to Boston.

The Treaty of Utrecht. In Europe, the war went badly for King Louis and his allies. At last, after a dozen years of fighting, the Treaty of Utrecht was signed. By the terms of the treaty, Louis' grandson, Philip V, was allowed to occupy the Spanish throne. In 

England, Queen Anne was permitted to remain undisturbed on the English throne. And in America, not only Port Royal, but all of Acadia and the island of 

Newfoundland were turned over to England. Port

Royal was renamed Annapolis, and Acadia became the 

English colony of Nova Scotia. 

King George's War. For nearly thirty years after the end of Queen Anne's War there was peace in Europe. Then, in 1740, another European throne was left without a king when the Emperor of Austria died. The

WHERE THE WARS BETWEEN THE FRENCH AND ENGLISH COLONISTS IN AMERICA WERE FOUGHT

THREE INTERCOLONIAL WARS 

359 emperor's daughter believed that she should rule her father's country, but the rulers of France, Spain, Prussia, and Bavaria had other ideas. They wanted to seize Austria and divide it up among themselves, and they declared war for the purpose of doing this. 

At once England and Holland came to Austria's assistance, and again all Europe was at war. Because the war was the result of a quarrel over who should rule Austria, it was known in Europe as the War of the Austrian Succession. In the colonies, to which the war spread in 1744, it became known as King George's War, because George II was now king of England. 

The Fortress at Louisburg. On Cape Breton island, commanding the southern entrance to the St. Lawrence, was the French settlement of Louisburg. Here the 

French had a powerful fortress, guarded by many guns and protected by stone walls ten feet thick. Next to 

Quebec, Louisburg was the strongest French fortress in America. It was so strong that the men in England who commanded the English army and navy felt that it could not be captured.

In the opening years of King George's War, the French used Louisburg as a naval base from which to send their warships out against the English. Many a vessel bound for Boston or New York was attacked and destroyed by a fast French raider from the stronghold on Cape Breton island. Attack followed attack, until the English colonists decided that they must besiege Louisburg, whether or not it was possible to capture it.

Led by Colonel Pepperrell, an army of four thousand men from all of the New England colonies set out in 103 ships to take the fortress. On the way, they were joined by four English warships. These proved useful in blockading the harbor and keeping French warships from coming to the assistance of the fort. 

For six weeks Colonel Pepperrell and his men laid siege to Louisburg, never for a minute relaxing their attack. Because their cannons were too small and weak to damage the walls from a great distance, the attackers dragged the guns through the slime and mud of a swamp almost to the very walls. As the bombardment continued, the French guns were put out of action, one after another. At last resistance was no longer possible, and the French commander sadly turned his fortress over to the English colonists who had fought so hard to take it. 

The Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle. When word reached the English colonies that Louisburg had fallen, there was great rejoicing. Everyone felt that the colonies would now be much safer, since this strong French naval base had passed into English hands. Alas, the rejoicing came too soon. When the Treaty of Aix-la- 

Chapelle was signed in 1748, Louisburg and Cape Breton island were given back to the French in exchange for some French lands in India. Many of the colonists were bitter about this move, for they felt that the English government cared little for the safety of the colonists if it was willing to leave so powerful a stronghold in the enemy's hands.

THREE INTERCOLONIAL WARS 361  

During the time when the European colonies in America were growing and developing, England and France were bitter rivals in Europe. 

In 1689, the War of the League of Augsburg began in Europe. In the colonies it was known as King William's War.

The fighting in the colonies began when bands of Frenchmen and Indians from Canada attacked English settlements and massacred the inhabitants. The English colonists made an unsuccessful attempt to find and defeat the raiders. An army of Massachusetts volunteers captured Port Royal, Acadia, but a later attack on the French stronghold of Quebec failed. When the war was ended by the Treaty of Ryswick, Port Royal was given back to France.

In 1702, the War of the Spanish Succession broke out in Europe. In the colonies it was known as Queen 

Anne's War.

The war began in the colonies when French and Indians from Canada attacked English settlements in New England. Once more the English colonists captured 

Port Royal and made an unsuccessful attack on Quebec.

When the Treaty of Utrecht ended the war, Acadia and Newfoundland were added to the English possessions in America.

In 1740, the War of the Austrian Succession broke out in Europe. In America it was known as King 

George's War.

In the opening years of King George's War the 

French used Louisburg, on Cape Breton island, as a naval base from which to make attacks on English ships.

An army of New England colonists, commanded by Colonel Pepperrell, attacked and captured Louisburg, but this stronghold was given back to the French when the Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle was signed at the end of the war.

CHAPTER SIXTEEN THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR AND PONTIAC'S CONSPIRACY 

West of the thirteen English colonies lay a vast area claimed by both England and France. When 

English colonists began to cross the Appalachian mountains and settle in the disputed lands, it was clearly seen that a serious war would result. 

The first three wars between the French and English colonists in America were only the beginning of a great conflict-a contest which was to decide whether France or England should control most of the 

North American continent.

Conflicting Land Claims. As we have already learned, the charters which the English kings granted to several of the English colonies in America stated that the colonists might occupy the lands from sea to seathat is, from the Atlantic ocean to the Pacific. This meant that the claims of a number of the English colonies extended right across the great territory of Louisiana, which La Salle had claimed for France. 

In order to strengthen their claim to Louisiana, the French had built a string of forts extending from Quebec westward along the Great Lakes and southward to the mouth of the Mississippi. Except for these forts, the land was occupied only by a few colonists, the mis- 365

sionaries, and the fur traders who lived in the forests and traded with the Indians. Most of the French colonists in America lived in New France. 

For a long time, the English colonists made no effort to build settlements in what they regarded as their western lands. While the colonies were young, no more land was needed than the narrow strip which lies along the Atlantic, east of the Appalachians. Furthermore, the Appalachian mountains formed a barrier to westward expansion, as there were only a few places where they could be crossed easily. But the time was to come when the English would be forced to strike a blow in defense of their western claims.

The Fur Trade. Another cause for trouble between the French and English colonists in America was the fur trade. Most of the French colonists, as no doubt you remember, made their living by buying furs from the Indians and trading them for money or supplies. 

The English backwoodsmen, too, made their living from the fur trade. As a result, a bitter rivalry grew up between the English and the French over the question of who should get the best furs from the Indians. 

The French and English Colonies Compared. Let us see how the French and English colonies were prepared for the coming struggle. The French lands hemmed in the English colonies on the north and west, and included the two great waterways of the continent, the St. Lawrence and Mississippi river systems. These lands, however, were very thinly populated. In all of New France there were only about 80,000 settlers.

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 367 

The English colonists far outnumbered their French neighbors, being nearly fifteen times as many. 

The greatest strength of the French lay in the fact that they were united. All of New France was ruled by one governor. Practically all of the people were 

Catholics and were held together by their common religion. The English, on the other hand, belonged to a number of different churches, and each of the thirteen colonies had its own government. 

Most of the men in the French colonies were woodsmen who were used to enduring hardships and were skilful in the handling of rifles. Only a very small number of the English colonists had had such good training for war, for most of them lived in the cities. These advantages of the French did much to make up for the difference in numbers. 

Both the French and English had Indian allies who would help them in case of war. The French had the 

Algonquins, who had never forgotten how much Champlain had helped them in their war against the Iroquois. The English had the Iroquois, who had not forgotten Champlain either. Both the Algonquins and the 

Iroquois were cruel and bloodthirsty in their methods of war. Besides the Algonquins, the French also had many allies among the red men of the Mississippi valley, where their woods runners and missionaries had made friends with the Indians.

English Settlers and the French Forts. At last so many settlers came to live in the English colonies that most of the good farm land was taken and there was

an urgent need for more space. A few at a time, daring pioneer families made their way through the mountain passes, and began to clear the land and to build homes for themselves west of the Appalachians. Then, in 

1749, a group of Virginia colonists went into partnership and formed the Ohio Company, for the purpose of taking possession of rich lands in the Ohio river valley and later selling them to colonists. The Ohio 

Company sent out an experienced scout and fur trader named Christopher Gist to select the best places for settlement.

When the French governor in Canada heard about the Ohio Company's plans, he was very angry. The 

Ohio land was claimed by France, and he intended to hold it for his king. He promptly sent a party of 

Frenchmen into the Ohio territory to warn the Englishmen who were building homes there that they must leave the French lands at once.

Knowing that the English would pay little attention to his warning unless the French claims were protected by guns and soldiers, the French governor also sent out men to build three forts in the territory. These, he believed, would prove that the French meant business, and would keep the English from attempting to make settlements.

Virginia Attempts to Expel the French. The Ohio 

Company appealed to Governor Dinwiddie of Virginia for help. At once the governor sent word of the situation to England, and the English government sent back a letter. The letter said: "Build forts near the

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 369 

Ohio if you can get the money. Require the French to depart peacefully; and if they will not do so, we do hereby strictly charge and demand you to drive them off by force of arms. 99 It was easy for the English king and his ministers to give such an order, but it was very difficult for the governor of Virginia to carry it out. Winter had now set in, and between the Virginia settlements and the French forts lay two hundred miles of trackless, snowcovered wilderness. Governor Dinwiddie selected a young man twenty-one years old, a surveyor by trade, to inform the French that they must leave the Ohio valley. This messenger was a younger brother of two members of the Ohio Company. His name was George Washington.

With a small party of companions, Washington set out from Virginia for the French forts. The long journey took weeks-weeks of suffering, hunger, and endless tramping through the woods. At last the men reached Fort Le Boeuf, where they gave the French commander Governor Dinwiddie's message. After some delay, the commander's answer was delivereda flat refusal to leave-and Washington and his men started back. 

Because his companions traveled so slowly, Washington decided to take one of them and push on ahead.

The man selected was Christopher Gist, the same man whom the Ohio Company had sent out to explore its lands in the Ohio River valley. On they went, day after day, suffering many hardships and having several nar-

WASHINGTON AND GIST CROSSING THE ALLEGHENY RIVER 

In 1753, George Washington and a party of men were sent to warn the French to leave the Ohio valley. The French refused to go.

On the way back to the English colonies, Washington and a woodsman named Gist went on ahead. Washington nearly lost his life when he fell into the Allegheny river while trying to cross it on a raft. row escapes from death. Once an Indian fired at them, but hit neither one. Another time, trying to cross the 

Allegheny river on a raft, they were caught in the ice. 

Washington slipped from the raft into the river, but with his companion's aid was at last able to struggle back to safety. Finally, they reached their destination

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 371 

and delivered the French commander's message to Gov ernor Dinwiddie. 

The French and Indian War. Since the French would not move away peacefully, the English colonists were now faced with the task of driving them out by force. It seemed that the best way to do this was to build in the same region some English forts from which attacks could be made upon the French. So in February, 1754, Governor Dinwiddie sent out a company of men to build a fort at the point where the Allegheny and the Monongahela rivers join to form the Ohio. 

This location had been selected by George Washington on his way back from Fort Le Boeuf. 

Great Meadows and Fort Necessity. Then, on

April 2, a small force of men led by George Washington was sent to occupy the fort which the workmen were building. Before they even reached the fort they received word that it was in the hands of the French. 

A party of five hundred French soldiers had driven the English workmen away, had completed the fort, and now occupied it themselves. They had given this new stronghold the name of Fort Duquesne. 

Even though he could not hope to recapture Fort 

Duquesne with his little force, Washington decided to push on and see what he could do. On the way, an 

Indian came to him and told him that some French soldiers were camping nearby. Fearing that the French might attack his men and capture them, the young leader hastily marched to a broad clearing known as Great Meadows, and there dug some trenches for pro-

tection. For some time he waited, but the French did not appear. When he grew tired of waiting, he decided to attack them.

The French were encamped in a hollow in the woods, and Washington and his men had little trouble in surrounding them. Suddenly one of the French soldiers. saw an Englishman moving in the forest and raised his gun. At once shots rang out, and for fifteen minutes a furious battle was fought. At the end of that time the 

Frenchmen surrendered.

Well knowing that the French would strike back as soon as they could, Washington now led his men back to Great Meadows and there did what he could to strengthen the defenses which he had built. At best, the shallow trenches and low earthworks gave little protection, but there was no time to build better ones. 

Fort Necessity was the name which the English chose for their sorry little stronghold.

As Washington had foreseen, the French soon appeared and made an attack. For all of one day a battle was fought, but from the very beginning it was plain that the English had no chance. They had few supplies and little powder, and before the day was over more than fifty of their small band lay dead or wounded.

As darkness fell, a Frenchman with a white flag came forward to ask the English to surrender. If Washington and his men would give up the battle, and would promise to build no more forts in the region of the Ohio river for at least a year, the French would permit them to return unharmed to Virginia. Though Washington

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR
