GEORGE WASHINGTON AT FORT NECESSITY 

This picture shows George Washington, on the right, talking to one of his officers before the French attacked and defeated his small group of colonial soldiers at Fort Necessity. hated to accept these terms, he knew there was little to be gained by continuing the unequal struggle for Fort Necessity. He, therefore, sorrowfully agreed, and led his little army back to Virginia. 

The two small battles which Washington and his men had fought against the French were to have most important results. They marked the first fighting in a war which was to spread from the American colonies to Europe and even farther-to the French and Eng-

lish colonies in all parts of the world. The great struggle between the English and the French had begun. 

The Albany Congress. When it was seen that a long and bitter struggle with the French colonists was surely coming, the English colonists began to consider what they might do to strengthen themselves and to present a more united front to the enemy. In 1754, representatives from Massachusetts, Connecticut, New 

Hampshire, Rhode Island, New York, Pennsylvania, and Maryland met at Albany, New York, to talk over the situation and to decide how they might best work together against the French. 

One of the Pennsylvania representatives at the Albany meeting was Benjamin Franklin. He proposed that a single governor, called the president-general, should be given charge of all English forces from the colonies. To work with him, the president-general should have a body of representatives including men from each colony. This plan would provide a single governing body to oppose the French, instead of thirteen separate colonial governments which had never worked together. Franklin clearly saw that unless the colonies gave up their own selfish interests and helped each other it would be very difficult to defeat the 

French. 

Unfortunately the Albany Plan of Union was not adopted. The people of the different colonies opposed it because it meant giving up some of their control over affairs in their colonies. The king was also opposed to the plan because it provided for a single colonial gov-

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 375 

ernment, which would be harder to control than thirteen separate governments. So the colonies continued to remain apart, and for a long time they were unsuccessful in the war. 

There was, however, one very valuable result of the Albany Congress. The chiefs of the Iroquois tribes were there, and it was soon seen that they were not so anxious to help the English as they had been in years past. Said one of the chiefs, "The French are men and build many forts, but the English are women and are unprotected." If the Iroquois had joined the French and opposed the English, the chances of the English would have been slight indeed. William Johnson, however, with his tact and influence, was able to convince the Iroquois that once more they should come to the assistance of their English allies. 

Braddock's Defeat. In the spring of 1755, an English general named Braddock arrived in the Virginia colony. With him he brought 1600 regular English soldiers. To the disgust of George Washington and other colonial officers, the colonial troops were also put under the command of Braddock and his English officers. 

This brought the general's total force up to 2200 men. 

Soon after his arrival, General Braddock started from Virginia to capture Fort Duquesne. Ahead of the army went a group of woodsmen, clearing a road through the wilderness. Slowly the long column of 

English soldiers moved towards the French stronghold. 

In vain did George Washington urge General Braddock to send out scouts to discover any enemy that

might be hiding along the trail. Braddock would not listen. He was used to the open battlefields of Europe, and he felt that no American woodsman could tell him anything about carrying on a war. 

And so General Braddock's army marched into a terrible trap. Not far from Fort Duquesne, the first soldiers in the ranks saw a Frenchman standing in the middle of the trail. The Frenchman raised his arm, and immediately from the forest on both sides came a volley of musket shots. The English were brave, but they did not know what to do. Man after man dropped, but not an enemy could be seen. Raising their muskets to their shoulders they sent volley after volley crashing into the woods in a vain attempt to destroy their hidden enemies.

Still the slaughter continued. The bravery of the 

English soldiers gave way to fear, and fear gave way to panic. They huddled together like sheep, not knowing where to turn or what to do. Only the fact that George 

Washington and his colonial troops hid themselves behind rocks and trees and fought back in Indian fashion kept the battle from being more one-sided. 

Throughout the battle General Braddock rode bravely back and forth, encouraging his men. At last he realized that the cause was hopeless, and ordered a retreat. Even as he did so he fell to the ground mortally wounded, with a bullet through his chest. Back went the shattered English forces, leaving hundreds of dead and dying on the trail. The colonial troops protected the retreating soldiers as best they could, and

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 377 

kept the defeat from becoming a massacre. Long before Virginia was reached, brave, foolhardy General Braddock died of the wound he had received. His body 

THE DEATH OF GENERAL BRADDOCK 

While leading his English army against Fort Duquesne, General Braddock was suddenly attacked by an army of French and Indians. The general himself was mortally wounded, and his army was badly beaten. In this picture we see General Braddock, in the cart, dying, while his army retreats. was buried in the road, and men and wagons passed over it so that the Indians would not find his grave. 

Attempts to Invade French Lands. There were only three routes through which it was practical for the English armies to invade the lands of the

French-by way of the Cumberland pass, the Mohawk valley, and the Champlain valley. Not long after Braddock's defeat, an army of English soldiers and colonists was sent through the Mohawk valley against Fort Niagara, called the gateway to the Great Lakes. There were no roads, and long before the French stronghold was reached the attackers gave up their original plan. 

Turning to the north, they marched to Fort Oswego, an English outpost on the shores of Lake Ontario. There they set to work to enlarge and strengthen the fort so that the French would not be able to take it. 

Another attempt to invade French lands was made by William Johnson. At the head of a large force of colonial soldiers and Iroquois Indians, Johnson started towards the Champlain valley, building forts as he went. 

Near Lake George, an advance guard of Johnson's soldiers ran into a trap much like the one which the French had set for General Braddock near Fort Duquesne. From behind trees and rocks, volleys were poured into the ranks of the colonial troops, and hundreds of men were killed. Expecting an easy victory, the French and Indians then pushed on to complete the destruction of Johnson's forces.

But this time the French were not dealing with a panic-stricken army. They found the main body of the colonial army waiting for them behind some rough earthworks which had been thrown up along the shores of Lake George. The charging French and Indians were swept back with frightful slaughter, and the

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 

379 French commander was wounded and captured. Back went the French and Indians in full retreat, with Johnson's forces after them.

Then, once more, the tide of the battle turned. The retreating French and Indians rallied their forces and poured upon their pursuers such a deadly fire that the colonists were forced to retreat to their earthworks. 

Here they built Fort William Henry, a valuable English outpost on the borders of the French lands. 

The War Spreads to Europe. Although the French and English colonists in America had been fighting for months, England and France remained at peace until 1756. In that year, however, they declared war upon each other. In Europe, the war became known as the 

Seven Years' War. 

No sooner had war been declared in Europe, than both the English and the French sent new commanders to their colonies in North America. One after another, the English sent unskilled generals who happened to be friends of those in power in England. The French, on the other hand, sent one of their best commanders, General Montcalm. 

The Campaigns of 1756. The year 1756 was a disastrous one for the English. Montcalm besieged and captured Fort Oswego. As the English made no move to attack the French, the Iroquois Indians began to feel that the war was already lost, and that they might as well give up their alliance with the cowardly English. 

Bands of Iroquois warriors began to make raids on English settlements in the Mohawk valley, and thou-

sands of the settlers left their homes and crowded into Albany and other towns which were protected by forts. 

In spite of all that Sir William Johnson could do, the Iroquois Indians at this time were more of a danger than a help to the English. 

The Disasters of 1757. The year 1757 proved no bet ter for the English than 1756 had been. A strong English army moved north from Albany to capture the French stronghold of Crown Point on Lake Champlain. 

Before the soldiers even reached their destination, they were defeated and driven back by the French. 

General Montcalm was not slow to follow up this advantage. Leading a force of French and Indians south from Fort Ticonderoga, he besieged and captured 

Fort William Henry near Lake George. Not only the soldiers in the fort but also the women and children were slain by the Indians, although the French officers did their best to prevent the massacre. 

At the same time that Fort William Henry was falling into French hands, an English fleet under Lord Louden was moving toward Louisburg on Cape Breton island. Before Louisburg was reached, word came to 

Lord Louden that there was a French fleet in Louisburg harbor and that more soldiers had arrived to help defend the fort. Discouraged by the news, he turned his fleet about and sailed back to New York without even attempting to attack Louisburg.

William Pitt. In the meantime, important affairs were happening in England. An able statesman named 

William Pitt was placed in charge of the war. Pitt was

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 381 

not a man to put weak favorites in positions of authority. He was disgusted at the type of men who had been managing the campaign in America, and he determined to carry on the war with vigor and intelligence. 

Fresh troops and fresh supplies as well as a fleet of warships were sent to the colonies. Most important of all, two able leaders, Lord Jeffrey Amherst and General

James Wolfe, were sent to take command of the English and colonial forces. From this time on, the fortunes of war definitely favored the English. 

The Capture of Louisburg. Before Amherst and 

Wolfe left England, they had a long conference with William Pitt about the plan of campaign which should be followed. All three agreed that before England could win the war, Louisburg had to be captured, the French had to be driven out of the Champlain valley, and Fort Duquesne at the head of the Ohio river had to be taken. Once these three things were accomplished, the English would be in a position to move against the French strongholds along the St. Lawrence river.

In June, 1758, General Amherst led a fleet and an army against Louisburg. The French warships in the harbor were destroyed, and after a siege of six weeks the walls of the fort itself were so battered and broken that the English soldiers were able to charge in and take the town. In order to make sure that the French would never again make a powerful stronghold of 

Louisburg, General Amherst ordered his soldiers to tear down the walls and to destroy all of the defenses.

THE CAPTURE OF LOUISBURG 

In 1758, General Amherst led an English fleet and army against the French fortress of Louisburg, on Cape Breton island, and captured it. The French warship at the right has been set on fire by the shots of the English ship at the left, and the members of the crew are being picked up by the small boats from the English vessel.

The First Attack on Ticonderoga. The next move of the English was against Fort Ticonderoga. General 

Abercrombie and Lord Howe were placed in charge of the army which moved up the Champlain valley. 

As the English army neared Fort Ticonderoga, the French came out to meet them, and then retreated again towards the fort, firing as they went. One of the French bullets struck Lord Howe and killed him. General

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 383 

Abercrombie was now left in command, and he was not long in showing what a foolish leader he was. Near the walls of the fort, the French had built a strong system of earthworks, and these Abercrombie tried to take by means of a bayonet charge. Bravely his soldiers rushed forward, but they had no chance of taking the earthworks. Cannons and muskets thinned their ranks, and they were forced to fall back. Five times Abercrombie repeated the foolish order to charge, and each time hundreds of English soldiers and colonists gave up their lives uselessly. At last, beaten and discouraged, the army retreated to Albany, leaving Ticonderoga in the hands of the French.

The Capture of Fort Frontenac. The year 1758 was not permitted to end with a French victory. A brave colonial leader named John Bradstreet gathered an army of colonists and sailed along the shore of Lake Ontario until he reached Fort Frontenac. Taking the French by surprise, Bradstreet and his men stormed the fort and hoisted the English flag over it. 

The Capture of Fort Duquesne. While all of these affairs were going on in the northern colonies, an English army led by General Forbes was moving against 

Fort Duquesne. The French had only one thousand regular soldiers in the fort with which to oppose 

Forbes' six thousand men, but when they heard of

Forbes' advance they hastily gathered together a large army of Indians to aid them. For weeks the defenders waited, hoping to repeat the victory gained three years before over General Braddock. Forbes' army moved

so slowly, however, that at last the Indian warriors, growing impatient at the continued delay, set out for home. Desperate because of this unforeseen turn 

THE ENGLISH TAKE FORT DUQUESNE, 1758 

As General Forbes and his English army approached Fort Duquesne, the French abandoned the fort and blew it up. Here we see the English flag being hoisted over the ruins. of affairs, the French abandoned Fort Duquesne and blew it up so that it would be of no use to the English when they arrived.

When at last General Forbes and his army reached the site of Fort Duquesne, they found nothing but ruins. 

At once they set to work to build a new fort on the same spot, naming it Fort Pitt in honor of that great English statesman, William Pitt. Today the city of Pitts-

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 385 

burgh, Pennsylvania, stands where Fort Duquesne and 

Fort Pitt once stood.

The Capture of Fort Niagara. In June, 1759, an army of English soldiers and Iroquois Indians, under 

Sir William Johnson and Colonel Prideaux, attacked

Fort Niagara. The siege lasted for several weeks, during which time a French army from Canada tried to relieve the defenders and drive the English and Indians away. When this attempt was unsuccessful, the commander of the fort realized that there was no longer any hope, and he surrendered.

The Capture of Ticonderoga and Crown Point. 

While Prideaux and Johnson were besieging Fort 

Niagara, Lord Jeffrey Amherst was leading an English and colonial army north into the Champlain valley. 

The French forces which he met were not able to hold him back, and without difficulty he captured Fort

Ticonderoga and Crown Point, which were deserted by the French at his approach.

The Siege of Quebec. General Wolfe in the mean time had taken his fleet and his army north along the coast, and had sailed up the St. Lawrence to Quebec. 

Late in June he began the siege of this great French stronghold, the most powerful fortress in all North 

America. 

Quebec was not an easy place to take. Although part of the town lay on a narrow plain along the river, most of it and the fort itself stood on top of a steep cliff three hundred feet in height. In the fort General Montcalm had as many soldiers as Wolfe, and he had the

advantage of being at the top of the cliff and having the strong walls of the fort to protect his men. 

All summer the siege went on. Wolfe landed some of his troops and guns and succeeded in destroying many buildings in the lower part of the town, but the fort and the upper part of the town were safely out of his reach. A few attempts were made to climb the cliff and attack the French, but every time the English were driven back with terrible loss. July and August passed, and still there was no sign of success. It seemed impossible to take Quebec.

Still, General James Wolfe was not a man to give up easily. He was young and determined. Although his body was frail and his health was bad, he had a keen mind. Continually he studied the fort and the cliff through his telescope, searching for a weak point in the defenses. At last he found what he was looking for. 

A few miles from the fort he discovered a narrow path winding up the face of the cliff. The way was steep, but it could be climbed. All that General James Wolfe wanted was a chance at success. 

One dark September night the attempt was made.

Below the fort the English warships moved in close to the shore, and boatloads of soldiers rowed back and forth near the land to make the French believe that they were going to make an attack at that point. In the meantime, the real attack was beginning two miles upstream. Boat after boat carried soldiers to the land near the narrow path which led up the cliff. 

Finally, when the army was landed, a band of

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 

387 twenty-five daring men quietly climbed to the top. The 

French guards there were taken by surprise. A few were captured, and the others were driven back. Then, as rapidly as possible, the 4500 soldiers whom General Wolfe had himself selected for the attack made their way up the cliff to the level land at the top. When the morning sun arose, Montcalm and his French officers saw the long lines of English soldiers drawn up on the level Plains of Abraham before the very walls of 

Quebec. 

The defenders now found themselves in a desperate situation. With the English on the plains above the cliff, the Frenchmen in the fort could no longer get supplies, and sooner or later they would be forced to surrender or starve. Plainly, the only chance of holding Quebec now was to defeat the army which had climbed the cliff. General Montcalm decided to risk everything on a single battle. He ordered his men out to meet the enemy.

What a stirring picture the two armies must have made as they faced each other on the Plains of Abraham, the English in their blazing red coats and the French all dressed in blue! Here was an Old World battle fought in European style, without trees or bushes to hide behind. Standing in their solid ranks, the two armies opened fire on each other.

Early in the fight General Wolfe was wounded, but he continued to lead his men until a second bullet pierced his chest. A third bullet struck him, and he fell and was carried to the rear. Scorning a doctor's

help, he went on giving commands. At last he saw the blue lines of the French break before his advancing soldiers. "Now, God be praised, I will die in peace, he said, and soon the brave English leader was dead. 99 

THE DEATH OF GENERAL WOLFE 

Leading his men up a steep and narrow path, this young English commander, General Wolfe, surprised the French and captured Quebec-the most powerful French stronghold in America. General Wolfe, however, lost his life in the battle. 

Montcalm, too, showed great bravery in the battle. 

As he tried in vain to rally his defeated troops, he was struck by a bullet from an English gun. He fell to the ground, and as his officers gathered around him they heard him say, "I am happy that I shall not live to see

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 389 

the surrender of Quebec." Four days after the battle the French surrendered the fort to the English. 

With the fall of Quebec, the French cause became hopeless. England now controlled the St. Lawrence river. Louisburg, Fort Ticonderoga, Crown Point, Fort Niagara, Fort Frontenac, and Fort Duquesne had been taken. In 1761, Montreal, the last French stronghold in America, was captured by the English. 

The War in Europe Continues. The capture of 

Montreal meant the end of the war in North America, but not in Europe. France still continued to fight against the English, though growing steadily weaker as battle after battle was fought. Spain now became interested in the war, because the Spanish felt that if England won she would become too strong. Accordingly, in 1762, Spain entered the war on the side of France.

The declaration of war against England proved to be a very foolish step on the part of Spain. Neither her army nor her navy was able to win victories from the English. The English, on the other hand, sent a fleet and an army to the West Indies, and captured the Spanish city of Havana without much trouble. At about the same time, another English fleet conquered the Philippine islands. Before long both France and 

Spain were beaten and were forced to make peace. 

The Treaty of Paris. The treaty which ended the war was signed in Paris in 1763. As England had won such a complete victory, the English were able to make peace on their own terms. France was forced to turn

over to England all of her land in North America east of the Mississippi river, with the exception of the city of New Orleans and two small islands off the coast of 

Newfoundland. Spain, for her part, surrendered

Florida to England in exchange for Cuba and the Philippines, which the English had taken. 

Shortly before the signing of the Treaty of Paris, France and Spain had signed a secret treaty between themselves. By the terms of this treaty France turned over to the Spaniards the city of New Orleans and all French lands west of the Mississippi. For some time this treaty was kept a secret, but at last the world came to realize that France no longer held any important possessions in North America, and that the only two countries having lands there were powerful England and feeble Spain.

Pontiac's Conspiracy. We have seen that the western Indians favored the French and fought against the English. They were not pleased, therefore, when the 

French soldiers gave up their forts, and English soldiers took possession of them. The English charged higher prices than the Frenchmen for guns and ammunition, and would not let the warriors enter the forts, a privilege the Indians had had when the French held the land. Worst of all, the English soldiers were followed by English settlers who cut down the trees and began to make farms and establish villages. 

A powerful Indian chief named Pontiac soon came to realize that the westward movement of the English settlers would mean the end of the Indian hunting

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 391 

grounds. He was very bitter, and went about from tribe to tribe urging the warriors to rise and kill the 

English. Most of the western Indians were willing to join in Pontiac's conspiracy. It was agreed that on the second day of May, 1763, the English forts to the west of the Appalachian mountains would be attacked. 

Pontiac's conspiracy came very near to succeeding. 

By clever tricks the Indians gained admission to most of the English forts, and captured them without trouble, killing every Englishman they could find.

Only three of all the English forts west of the Appalachian mountains were not captured. Fort Niagara was not attacked, for it was too near the lands of the Iroquois, who were the friends of the English. The commander of Fort Pitt was warned of the coming attack, and had barely time to prepare for the siege. 

Most important of all, the strong fort at Detroit was not taken.

In the end, the Indians were defeated. Though many of the regular English troops had already returned to Europe, there were still some left in the colonies, and these were sent to the relief of the besieged forts. An army of 1500 English regulars defeated the 

Indians in the Ohio valley, and Pontiac became discouraged and at last signed a treaty of peace. 

Results of the Wars Between England and France. 

The series of wars between England and France had very important results. They gave England extensive colonies not only in North America, but also in Asia.

England now had more colonies than any other Euro-

pean nation, and the great English colonial empire was firmly established. In North America, the results of the wars meant that English ideas and customs would be followed and the English language spoken. 

Although the wars gained for England extensive colonies in North America, they also greatly weakened her hold on her thirteen original colonies. The colonists had learned how to fight and had discovered that they could unite against a common enemy. The confidence which they gained gave them a feeling of independence, and this feeling was further strengthened by the fact that there were now no powerful and dangerous neighboring colonies.

Finally, the wars plunged England deeply into debt, and it was these debts of the mother country that in time led England to lose the thirteen American colonies. 

The first three wars between the French and English colonists were the beginning of a great struggle to decide whether France or England should control most of the North American continent. The conflicting land claims of the French and English and trouble over the fur trade at last resulted in war.

Although the English held less land than the French, they had more colonists. The French were better united because they had a common religion and because all of New France was under one government. Most of the 

French colonists were woodsmen, used to handling

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 393 

guns, while most of the English were townspeople. The 

English had only the Iroquois Indians to help them, while the French had the Algonquins and a number of western tribes.

In order to hold the Ohio valley for France, the governor of Canada sent men to warn the English settlers in the valley to leave. The French also built forts to strengthen their claim to the land. 

A party of men led by George Washington was sent to Fort Le Boeuf to command the French to leave. The 

French refused.

There was little fighting in the French and Indian War in 1754. The French captured without bloodshed a fort which some English workmen were building at a point where the Allegheny and Monongahela rivers join to form the Ohio. Later a force of English colonists, led by George Washington, defeated and captured a group of French soldiers. When attacked by another French army, Washington and his men were forced to surrender. This was the beginning of the 

French and Indian War. 

In 1754 representatives from a number of the colonies met at Albany to decide how they might best work together against the French. At this meeting Benjamin Franklin proposed a plan of union, which was not accepted. William Johnson succeeded in renewing the 

English alliance with the Iroquois Indians. 

In 1755 General Braddock, leading an English and colonial army, made an unsuccessful attack on Fort Duquesne. Another English army started out to attack

Fort Niagara, but gave up the attempt and turned aside to Fort Oswego, an English outpost on Lake Ontario. Sir William Johnson attempted to lead an

English army north through the Champlain valley, and fought an indecisive battle near Lake George. After the battle, Johnson built Fort William Henry.

In 1756 the French and Indian War spread to Europe, where it was known as the Seven Years' War.

The English promptly sent a number of unskilled generals to the colonies, while the French sent one of their best commanders, General Montcalm. 

The year 1756 was a disastrous one for the English. 

General Montcalm besieged and captured Fort Oswego, and bands of Iroquois warriors began to desert the English cause and to raid English settlements in the 

Mohawk valley.

In 1757 an English army moving north through the Champlain valley was defeated and driven back, and the French followed up this advantage by besieging and capturing Fort William Henry. Lord Louden led an unsuccessful English expedition against Louisburg. 

When William Pitt was placed in charge of the war, fresh troops and supplies, a fleet of warships, and two able leaders, Amherst and Wolfe, were sent to the 

English colonies. 

In 1758 General Amherst besieged and captured

Louisburg. General Abercrombie and Lord Howe led an unsuccessful expedition against Fort Ticonderoga. 

Fort Frontenac and Fort Duquesne fell into the hands of the English.

THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 395 

Sir William Johnson and Colonel Prideaux besieged and captured Fort Niagara in 1759. At the same time an English army led by Lord Jeffrey Amherst was taking Fort Ticonderoga and Crown Point. 

After besieging Quebec all summer, General James Wolfe finally captured it. Both General Wolfe and 

General Montcalm were killed during this attack on 

Quebec. 

In 1761 the English captured Montreal. This ended the fighting in North America.

The Seven Years' War continued in Europe after the fighting ended in the colonies. Spain joined the side of France, but was soon defeated. 

The Treaty of Paris was signed in 1763. By its terms France turned over to England all of her North American possessions east of the Mississippi river, except New Orleans and two islands off Newfoundland.

Spain surrendered Florida to the English. Before this time, France had secretly given Spain the French lands west of the Mississippi river, and also the city of New 

Orleans. 

The western Indians were not pleased when the English took possession of the French lands. An Indian chief named Pontiac formed a plot to capture and destroy the English forts. All but three were captured. 

At last Pontiac was defeated and had to make peace. 

The wars between England and France gave England colonies in both North America and Asia, but they weakened England's hold on her thirteen original colonies in North America, and also plunged the mother

country deeply into debt. In later years, these debts led to England's loss of her thirteen American colonies.

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN GROWTH OF  

We have seen how bravely the English colonists in America fought for their mother country during the wars against France. Yet only twelve years after the signing of the Treaty of Paris, the people of the thirteen colonies were ready to take up arms and fight against England. What caused this great and sudden change in the feelings of the colonists? 

The Freedom of the Colonists. During the early part of the Colonial Period, the English colonists in America were allowed a great deal of freedom. The 

English government was very busy with affairs in Europe, and consequently the king and Parliament paid but little attention to their American possessions. 

So the colonists grew accustomed to settling matters for themselves and to laying their own taxes. True, England had passed the Navigation Acts in an effort to control the colonial trade, but these Acts were not well enforced, and, in spite of them, the colonists traded with other countries than England and with their colonies in America. The fact that England made so little effort to interfere with the freedom of the colonists resulted in the growth of a spirit of self-reliance and independence.

At the time of the French and Indian War, practi- 399

cally all of the English colonists living in America had been born in the colonies and had never even seen England. There were no telephones, telegraphs, or radios 

KING GEORGE III 

King George's attempts to tax his colonies for the benefit of the mother country caused hard feeling between England and the 

English colonies in America. 

in those days, and the only way of communicating with England was by means of sailing vessels, which were very slow. It cost a good deal of money to send even a letter to the mother country, so there was little correspondence between the colonists and the English on the other side of the 

Atlantic. Only the merchants, ship owners, seamen, and government officials had any direct contact with England. 

King George III. In the year 1760, while the

French and Indian War was going on, a new king, George III, came to the 

English throne. He was stubborn man, who believed in enforcing his will against that of his people. 

When the war ended, King George and his advisers began to consider how England's tremendous war debts should be paid and what should be done with the vast

new empire which England had won for herself through wars in Europe and America.

Somewhere, somehow, money had to be raised to repay that which had been spent on warships and soldiers and supplies. The people of England were already being taxed heavily, and little more money could be gained from them. There were, however, England's colonies in different parts of the worldespecially those in North America. Their early days of poverty and struggling were now practically over, and many of the colonies were prosperous. Since the 

French and Indian War had been largely fought in behalf of the American colonists, why should they not pay a share of its cost?

England's Attitude toward Her Colonies. In the time of King George III, it was the belief of most European governments that colonies should be used for the benefit of their mother countries. The new king of England saw nothing wrong with this idea. He decided that laws should be passed and other steps taken to bring the English colonies in America under closer control and to make them profitable to England. 

One of the men most active in putting through the new laws was an English nobleman named George

Grenville, who became England's Prime Minister not long after King George came to the throne.

The Laws Affecting Trade. The first step taken by the English government to control the colonies was an attempt to enforce the Navigation Acts and

some old laws restricting certain industries carried on in the colonies. By the terms of these old laws, the 

American colonists were forbidden to export from one colony to another or to other countries manufactured articles which competed with those made in the mother country. Among these articles were woolen garments, hats, and iron goods. The purpose of the laws was to encourage the colonists to ship their raw materials to the mother country and to import manufactured goods from England.

To offset the effects of the laws restricting manufacturing, other laws had been passed which favored the colonists. Bounties, or rewards, were paid for such naval stores as pitch, tar, and turpentine, and for other colonial products which England needed. The people of Ireland were forbidden to grow tobacco, so that the planters in the southern colonies would not have to lower their prices to meet this competition. And the law that goods shipped into or out of the colonies should be carried only in English or colonial ships greatly helped the New England colonies, where shipbuilding was a leading occupation. 

The Sugar and Molasses Act. Another law aimed at colonial trade was the Molasses Act, which had been passed back in 1733. This law forbade the colonists to buy molasses or sugar from the French and Spanish colonies in the West Indies. The aim of the law was to force the American colonists to buy these products from the English colonies in the West Indies and to pay a tax on all they bought. Though a great deal of

molasses was imported into the colonies, much of it was smuggled and few people paid the tax.

The colonists resented the Molasses Act and the other laws which interfered with their trade, and there were grumbling and hard feelings. As yet, however, the dissatisfaction was not serious. 

Then, in 1764, Grenville had the Molasses Act repealed and had a new law passed, known as the Sugar and Molasses Act, which also placed duties on coffee, silks, and wine. The Sugar and Molasses Act was to be strictly enforced. This law especially aroused the colonists, who carried on a very profitable trade with the Spanish and French colonies in the West Indies, shipping out fish, lumber, corn, wheat, and other products to the islands and getting sugar and molasses in return.

Writs of Assistance. In order to enforce its laws against smuggling, the English government began to issue general search warrants known as Writs of Assistance. Ordinarily when a policeman or a government officer wishes to search property, he has to have a paper called a search warrant stating which house he is to search and what he is to search for. The Writs of Assistance did not contain this information. Officers who held them could search any house or shop at any time for smuggled goods and were not compelled to tell the owner what articles they were searching for. 

The Writs of Assistance aroused great resentment among the colonists, most of whom felt that the English government had no right to issue such warrants. James

Otis of Boston made a stirring speech before the Massachusetts Legislature, opposing the Writs of Assistance. 

Nevertheless, they continued to be issued, and the English officers rummaged through houses where they thought they might find smuggled goods.

The Stamp Act. Grenville next planned to station an English army in the colonies. The reason he gave was that the army was to defend the colonists against Indian attacks. To help pay the expenses of maintaining the English troops, Grenville suggested a direct tax to be placed on the colonists. 

There was at that time in England a law that all newspapers, pamphlets, wills, mortgages, and other legal or public documents must bear government stamps. The cost of the stamps for each document depended upon the value and nature of the paper. The sale of these stamps in England brought a great deal of money into the English treasury.

It was Grenville's idea to pass a Stamp Act taxing the American colonists in the same way. Before submitting the matter to Parliament, he invited the colonists to comment on the Stamp Act and to suggest any form of taxation that would be more acceptable to them. 

As the colonists had no better plan to suggest, Grenville submitted his American Stamp Act to Parliament, and in March, 1765, the Stamp Act was passed. 

Opposition to the Stamp Act. At once, there was strong opposition in the colonies. Merchants, bankers, lawyers, and publishers-those whose businesses were most seriously affected-were the leaders in opposing

PATRICK HENRY SPEAKING IN THE HOUSE OF BURGESSES 

This fiery young orator of Virginia was one of the leaders in opposing the Stamp Act and other English laws and taxes which oppressed the colonists. the law. In Virginia, a young lawyer named Patrick 

Henry arose in the House of Burgesses and declared that only the General Assembly of the colony had the right to tax the Virginia citizens. 

James Otis, too, was very active in opposing the Stamp Act. At his suggestion, a body of representatives from nine of the colonies met in New York in October, 1765. This Stamp Act Congress, as it was called, drew up a paper known as the Declaration of

Rights and Grievances, denying that anybody except the representative assemblies of the colonies themselves had any right to lay taxes on the colonists. Copies of 

AN ENGLISH STAMP DISTRIBUTOR ATTACKED BY A MOB 

The Stamp Act was very unpopular in the colonies, and the English stamp distributors were forced to resign. In Boston, a mob attacked the man chosen to distribute the stamps and burned his office. the declaration were sent to the king and to both of the Houses of Parliament. 

There were serious riots over the Stamp Act in New York, Pennsylvania, and all of the New England colonies. In Boston, the man who had been chosen to

distribute the stamps was driven from his office, and the office was burned. All of the stamp distributors were forced to resign their positions. Throughout the colonies, people banded together and vowed that they would never buy a single one of the hated stamps.

Even more important, many of the colonists pledged themselves to buy no more goods from England until the Act should be repealed. This was a severe blow to the English merchants and manufacturers, who promptly joined with the colonists in opposing the law. 

At last, in March, 1766, Parliament voted to discontinue the Stamp Act. At the same time, however, a new act was passed stating that Parliament had the right to "bind the colonies in all cases whatsoever." provision was known as the Declaratory Act.

This When news reached the colonies that the Stamp Act had been repealed, there was great rejoicing. Messages of thanks were sent to the king and to his ministers. 

None of the colonists seem to have paid much attention to the Declaratory Act, with its threat of future taxation.

Why the Colonists Objected to the English Taxes. 

One thing which we should keep very clearly in mind is the attitude of the colonists toward taxation. They did not object to paying taxes. Then, as now, taxes were necessary in order to pay the salaries of government officials, to build roads and public buildings and keep them in repair, to provide police protection, and to do many other necessary things. The colonists knew this and were quite willing to pay the taxes voted by

their own legislatures. What they were not willing to do was to pay taxes laid upon them by the English Parliament, for the colonists had no voice in choosing the members of Parliament, and they therefore felt that they were not represented in that body. "Taxation without representation is tyranny" became their cry.

The Townshend Acts. The new taxation threat ened by the Declaratory Act was not long in coming. 

Early in 1767, the Townshend Acts were passed, placing a duty on all window glass, paper, paint, and tea brought into the colonies. These were four things which it was believed the colonists could not do without. The money paid to the English government through the collection of these taxes was to be used to support an English army in America and also to pay the salaries of governors, judges, and other English officers in the colonies. It was felt that if these men were in England's pay they would be entirely dependent on the English government and would have to work for England's interests rather than for those of the colonists. The 

Townshend Acts also provided that cases of smuggling in America should be tried in court without a jury and declared that the Writs of Assistance were legal and should continue to be used.

The colonists were just as quick to resist the Townshend Acts as they had been to resist the Stamp Act. 

Letters were sent from colony to colony urging the people not to import English goods. Nevertheless, the 

Townshend duties continued to be enforced, and the

only immediate result of the opposition was the bringing of two regiments of English soldiers from Halifax, Nova Scotia, to Boston to uphold the government officials and preserve the peace. 

The Boston Massacre. The arrival of the soldiers in Boston had anything but a peaceful effect. The citizens of the town objected to them, and whenever the soldiers appeared they were greeted with jeers and catcalls. Small boys delighted to pelt them with stones or snowballs and, because of their red coats, to yell, "Lobsterbacks!" as they passed.

On March 5, 1770, a crowd of Boston citizens gathered and began to jeer at an English soldier who was standing guard before a building. Other soldiers came to his assistance. Finally sticks and stones began to fly through the air in place of words, and several of the soldiers were struck. This was more than they could stand. Raising their muskets, they fired a volley into the crowd, killing five citizens and wounding several others.

Immediately all Boston was aroused. A large number of citizens gathered at Faneuil Hall and chose a patriotic leader named Samuel Adams to make a protest to the English governor of Massachusetts. To the governor's astonishment, Adams demanded that all of the soldiers be immediately removed from Boston. 

When the governor hesitated, Adams shouted, "There are three thousand people yonder in the town meeting, and the country is rising. Night is coming on, and we must have our answer." The governor yielded, and the

English troops were at once removed from the city and stationed in a fort on an island in Boston harbor. 

The Boston Tea Party. Early in 1770, Lord North became the Prime Minister of England. He was a close friend of King George's, and together they considered what might be done to make the colonists admit that England had the right to tax them. It was very plain that the colonists could never be forced to pay taxes. 

So instead of trying to use force, the king and Lord 

North decided to play a trick upon the colonists.

During the three years since the Townshend duties had been put into effect, the English government had spent a tremendous amount of money in order to collect a small amount of taxes and duties. England's trade with the colonists, too, had fallen so low that once again English merchants and manufacturers were protesting bitterly. All of the duties provided in the Townshend 

Acts were, therefore, removed, with the exception of a very low tax on tea, which the king insisted should be left. This tax was so low that the colonists could buy tea from England, even with the tax, for less than they could get it in any other way. The king felt that they would be so glad to get tea at this low price that they would pay the tax and in that way would admit that the English government had a right to tax them. Once they had admitted this, it would be a simple matter to force them to pay other taxes. By way of carrying the point, loads of tea were shipped to America in 1773. 

The king and Lord North failed to realize how bitterly the American colonists were opposed to taxation

without representation. When the tea ships from England arrived, they were rudely received. At Philadelphia and New York, the ships were not even allowed 

THE BOSTON TEA PARTY 

In 1773, three English ships laden with tea came to Boston. 

The people would not accept the tea and pay the English tax on it. A number of leading citizens disguised as Indians went aboard the ships and dumped the tea into the harbor. to dock. In Charleston, South Carolina, the tea was unloaded but was intentionally stored in damp cellars where it would spoil.

It was the reception of the three tea ships sent to Boston that caused the greatest excitement in England. 

At first the Boston citizens tried to get the governor

to send the cargoes back to England, but he would not. 

When all other methods failed, a large band of the town's leading citizens, dressed as Indians, boarded the tea ships and dumped the entire cargo into the harbor. 

This affair became known as the Boston Tea Party. 

The Intolerable Acts. When King George III 

heard about the Boston Tea Party, he was enraged. It seemed to him that almost every unfortunate thing that happened in the colonies had its beginning in the capital of Massachusetts. It was in Boston that a mob had burned the office of the chief stamp officer and had tarred and feathered other English officials. It was there that the king's troops had been attacked by a crowd of citizens and had been forced to fire upon their attackers. And now came this Boston Tea Party, adding insult to injury!

Moreover, Boston was the home of five of the leading patriots who opposed the English taxes-John 

Hancock, John Adams, James Otis, Joseph Warren, and, worst of all, Samuel Adams. It was Samuel 

Adams who had sent a circular letter throughout the colonies, urging resistance to the acts of the English government. He had even organized Committees of 

Correspondence in all thirteen of the colonies to send information and encouragement from colony to colony whenever there was trouble with the English. The king regarded Adams and most of the other citizens of 

Boston as dangerous and unreasonable characters.

Boston and the Massachusetts colony, the king decided, must be punished, and it was largely at his

A BOSTON COLONIST RESENTS THE QUARTERING ACT 

This colonist strongly objects to taking into his home a group of English soldiers, who, by the terms of the Quartering Act, have been sent to live in his house. urging that Parliament passed a new series of laws in March, 1774. These laws were especially aimed at the citizens of Boston, where they soon became known as the Intolerable Acts. 

The first of the Intolerable Acts, the Boston Port 

Act, closed the port of Boston to all trade until the people would admit that they were wrong in destroying the tea and would pay for it. The second act forbade

the people of Massachusetts to hold town meetings except for the election of officers, and it placed practically the entire control of the government in the hands of an officer of the English army. The third act provided that any English soldier or official accused of a serious crime should not be tried in the colonies but should be tried in England, where everything would be in his favor. The Quartering Act provided that the officers and soldiers of the English army stationed in Boston should be quartered in the public buildings of the town and the private homes of the citizens. The 

Quebec Act cancelled claims held by Massachusetts and the other colonies to the lands north of the Ohio river and added this vast territory to the Province of Quebec. 

When news of the Intolerable Acts reached the colonies, the people were angry and indignant. Citizens from the other colonies sent messages to Massachusetts, offering to help the people there by furnishing them with the supplies they could no longer get from England. Massachusetts was being punished, but the other colonies felt that the cause was theirs as well. 

The First Continental Congress. On September 5, 

1774, a meeting of representatives from all of the colonies except Georgia was held in Philadelphia to consider what should be done about the Intolerable

Acts. After a long and heated discussion of the situation, the members present drew up a Declaration of Rights in which they demanded the right to levy their own taxes and requested the king to undo the wrongs from which they were suffering. The members also

agreed not to carry on any trade with England until their wrongs had been righted. Groups of people were to be formed in the colonies to persuade all the colonists to take this same stand.

The meeting held in Philadelphia was known as the 

First Continental Congress. It clearly indicated that the deeds of the king and his Parliament were driving the American colonies to act together, and that they were rapidly becoming a single nation instead of a group of thirteen separate colonies.

The English king and Parliament saw fit to dis regard the requests of the First Continental Congress. 

The rebellious colonies were to be forced to submit. The next year, when the Second Continental Congress met, it was not to protest and petition, but to assume the authority of governing the colonies and to organize resistance to England.

During the early part of the Colonial Period, the English colonists in America were allowed a great deal of freedom. Though they regarded themselves as 

Englishmen, few of them had ever seen England or had any direct contact with the mother country.

George III became king of England in 1760. He believed that colonies existed for the benefit of the mother country and that they should be used to build up the trade of the mother country and also to help her pay her debts.

Soon after he came to the throne, King George decided to enforce the Navigation Acts and some old laws restricting the manufacture of certain goods in the colonies.

In 1764, the Molasses Act of 1733 was repealed and the Sugar and Molasses Act passed in its place. This required the colonists to pay duties on coffee, silks, and wine as well as on sugar and molasses.

In order to enforce its laws against smuggling, the English government issued general search warrants, known as Writs of Assistance. Although James Otis and other colonists opposed these Writs of Assistance, they continued to be issued and used. 

Parliament passed the Stamp Act in 1765, requiring that all public papers and documents must bear government stamps. The colonists objected to this tax, and there were riots in many of the colonies. A body of colonial leaders met in the Stamp Act Congress, and many of the colonists pledged themselves not to buy goods from England until the Stamp Act was repealed. 

Because of the opposition to it, Parliament repealed the Stamp Act in 1766 but passed the Declaratory Act, stating that Parliament had the right to "bind the colonies in all cases whatsoever."

Early in 1767, Parliament passed the Townshend Acts, placing duties on window glass, paper, paint, and tea. Part of the money raised in this way was to be used in supporting an English army in the colonies. 

When the colonists protested against the Townshend Acts, two regiments of English soldiers were

sent to Boston to keep the peace. The soldiers were not popular with the people of Boston. At last, in 1770, a group of soldiers fired on a crowd of Boston citizens. 

As a result of this action, the people compelled the governor to remove the soldiers from the city.

The Townshend duties were finally removed except for a low tax on tea. The colonists would not pay even this low tax. When English tea ships arrived in the colonies, none of the colonists would purchase the taxed tea. In Boston, a group of citizens boarded the tea ships and threw the tea into the harbor. This affair was known as the Boston Tea Party. 

To punish the people of Massachusetts and Boston, King George III had Parliament pass the Intolerable

Acts in 1774. The first of these Acts closed the port of 

Boston until its citizens should pay for the tea. The second Act placed the government under an English army officer. The third provided for the trial of 

English soldiers and officials in England. The fourth directed the quartering of soldiers in public buildings and private homes.

The Intolerable Acts angered the colonists. Repre sentatives from all colonies except Georgia met at

Philadelphia and drew up a Declaration of Rights, which they sent to the king and to Parliament. This meeting was known as the First Continental Congress.

The next meeting of representatives from the colonies was the Second Continental Congress, which met for the purpose of organizing resistance to England.

CHAPTER EIGHTEEN THE  

It was one thing for the American colonists to protest against the manner in which the English government was treating them, but it was quite another thing for them to attempt to remedy their troubles by force of arms. England had a large army and a splendid navy and was backed by the resources of a mighty empire. The colonies had no army, no navy, and very little wealth. How dared the colonists defy their powerful mother country? 

Preparations for War. As soon as it became plain that the American colonists would not submit to his unpopular laws and taxes, King George III began preparations to force his subjects in America to obey him. His first move was to issue an order forbidding any of the colonies to trade with any foreign nations. 

He then planned to send thousands of soldiers to America. These additional forces were to land at Boston, where General Gage already had several thousand 

English troops at his command.

The colonists, for their part, also got ready for the coming struggle. Patriotic colonists were organized into companies, which met from time to time to drill and to learn what they could of the methods of carrying on a war. These men were known as "minutemen" because they agreed to be ready at a minute's notice to fight against the English soldiers. Muskets, powder, 420

bullets, and even a few cannons were collected and stored at Concord and other towns not far from Boston. 

. CONCORD LEXINGTON MBRIDGE HIL 

DORCHESTER HEIGHTS General Gage's Plan. 

General Gage heard of the military stores the colonists were gathering at Concord. He also learned that two of the colonial leaders, Samuel 

Adams and John Hancock, were staying in Lexington, which was on the way to Concord. If only 

Adams and Hancock could be seized and the military stores destroyed, the resistance of the colonists might be ended at once, thought General 

Gage, and he planned accordingly. 

BREED'S HILL CHARLESTOWN BOSTON 

1080 

WHERE THE REVOLU- TIONARY WAR BEGAN 

The first fighting of the Revolutionary War took place not far from Boston. Find the important places mentioned in the text.

Paul Revere and Wil liam Dawes. About midnight on April 18, 1775, a large body of English troops commanded by Major Pitcairn quietly left Boston and set out for Lexington and Concord. The colonists, however, had not been caught napping. No sooner were the 

English troops under way than two lanterns were hung in the tower of Old North Church in Boston. Within sight of the church tower, two horsemen were waiting.

PAUL REVERE'S RIDE 

On the night of April 18, 1775, Paul Revere rode from Charlestown to Lexington and Concord, warning the minutemen that an English army was on the way to seize Samuel Adams and John Hancock and to destroy the military supplies which the colonists had gathered.

To them the two lanterns meant "The English troops are on the move!" The expected march to Lexington and Concord had begun! Into their saddles leaped

Paul Revere and William Dawes and set out at a fast gallop to warn the minutemen.

Paul Revere's route led him straight from Charlestown to Lexington and Concord. As he rode, he spread the alarm "To arms! The regulars are coming! The 

English are marching to Lexington!" Dawes, riding by way of Roxbury, spread the same warning to all of

the minutemen who lived along that route. Behind the two riders, lights appeared in the farmhouses as the minutemen hastily put on their clothes and seized their guns.

The Battle of Lexington. Early in the morning of 

April 19, the English troops reached Lexington. There was no chance for them to seize Adams and Hancock, for the two colonial leaders had been warned. Instead, the English found a small band of minutemen drawn up on the village common, facing them. Captain Parker, who commanded the minutemen, spoke to his little army in a low voice. "Stand your ground," he said. "Don't fire unless fired upon, but if they mean to have a war, let it begin here!"

The war did begin there. Major Pitcairn, in charge of the English troops, spurred his horse toward the minutemen, shouting, "Disperse, ye rebels!" When they did not move, the English soldiers raised their muskets and fired a volley. Eight minutemen fell dead.

Knowing well that his little band could not resist so many English soldiers, Captain Parker ordered a retreat. Firing a few shots as they went, the minutemen left the field, and the English continued their march toward Concord.

The Battle of Concord. At Concord, the English found that most of the supplies had been taken away and hidden. But there had not been time to remove everything, and the soldiers managed to seize and destroy two cannons and a small quantity of supplies. 

They then started to march toward the northern end

MINUTEMEN 

The minutemen were colonists who agreed to fight the English soldiers at a minute's notice. At Concord, a force of these valiant patriots drove back the English regulars and won the first Amer ican victory in the Revolutionary War.

of the town, where there was a wooden bridge across a narrow river.

At the bridge, the soldiers were met by a second group of minutemen. Again, as at Lexington, the 

English opened fire, but this time the minutemen did not fall back. Instead, they returned the fire with such deadly effect that the soldiers were forced to retreat. 

The minutemen were by no means satisfied with this small victory. Running along through the woods and fields, they kept up with the English columns and poured shot after shot into the ranks. Many of the redcoats fell, and it seemed to the English as though every tree and every stone wall along the way concealed a minuteman with a rifle and a deadly aim.

The orderly retreat of the English became a rout. 

Into the town of Lexington they streamed, beaten and almost exhausted. If they had not met a strong body of English troops at Lexington, it is likely that all of 

Major Pitcairn's English soldiers would have been killed or captured before they could reach Boston.

The Second Continental Congress. On May 10, 1775, the Second Continental Congress met at Phila delphia, with John Hancock of Massachusetts as president. Congress at once called for troops from the different colonies, and, in June, placed George Washington of Virginia in command of the colonial army. 

Washington's experience in the French and Indian War made him well fitted for this position. For almost six years, the Second Continental Congress was the only central government which the colonies had.

The Capture of Ticonderoga and Crown Point.. 

Ever since the French and Indian War, the English had held powerful Fort Ticonderoga on the shores of Lake Champlain. There were not a great many soldiers in the fort, but there were huge supplies of ammunition and muskets and also many cannons. It occurred to a young patriot named Ethan Allen that these supplies would be very helpful to the American cause. 

Early in the morning of the very day when the Sec ond Continental Congress met in Philadelphia, Ethan 

Allen and a band of minutemen known as "Green 

Mountain boys" quietly approached the fort. An English sentinel challenged them but was overcome before he could give the alarm. Through the gates poured the Americans. The astonished English soldiers, springing from their beds, had no chance to offer resistance. Ethan Allen himself roused the sleepy 

English commander and demanded the immediate and unconditional surrender of the fort and all of the supplies in it. Without a struggle, Ticonderoga and all of its supplies were turned over to the Americans. 

Soon after Ticonderoga fell, another band of Green 

Mountain boys seized the strong English fort at Crown

Point on Lake Champlain. Large supplies of guns and ammunition were won, and plans were made at once to send these supplies and those from Ticonderoga to the American forces which were gathering about the city of Boston. 

The Battle of Bunker Hill. In the meantime, affairs were moving rapidly in eastern Massachusetts. The

news of the battles of Lexington and Concord spread like wildfire through the colonies, and troops began to pour in from all directions to reinforce the minutemen who were guarding the roads leading out of Boston. 

Though his source of supplies from the surrounding country was now cut off, General Gage made no attempt to leave Boston but remained in the city and awaited the arrival of reinforcements which were on their way from England with Generals Howe, Clinton, and Burgoyne. When at last these arrived, General Gage felt that the English were in a position to overcome the untrained colonists.

General Gage had long had in mind the fortifying of Bunker Hill and Breed's Hill, which stood across the Charles river, near Charlestown, and commanded the city of Boston. The colonists learned of the plan, and on the night of June 16 a large body of minutemen quietly moved to the top of Breed's Hill and began to dig trenches. By morning, they had an entrenchment extending across the top of the hill. 

It was plain to General Gage that the American troops on the hill must quickly be driven away. If they were allowed to hold their position until a battery of cannons could be brought up, they would be able to prevent English ships from passing in or out of Boston harbor, and, even more important, they could bombard the city itself. He immediately sent General Howe with a force of three thousand English regulars to drive the Americans away.

Early in the afternoon, the English army began its

attack upon the American trenches. Up the steep slopes of the hill marched the three thousand English regulars, firing as they went. Waiting for them in the trenches at the top were fifteen hundred Americans. 

Powder was scarce, and the American officers told their men not to fire until the English were so close that the whites of their eyes could be seen. The advancing ranks of red had almost reached the trenches when the command to fire was given, and a volley rang out from the 

American lines. Hundreds of redcoats fell dead, and the remainder beat a hasty retreat down the hill.

At the bottom of the hill, the English re-formed their ranks and once more advanced upon the American trenches. As before, the defenders held their fire until the redcoats were close at hand. Then again a deadly volley burst forth, and the English ranks were ripped apart and the survivors driven down the hill. 

The English soldiers were brave men. Again they formed their ranks, and made a third assault. General Howe himself walked up the hill at the head of his troops, giving his soldiers a splendid example of bravery. This time there was no deadly volley. The 

American supply of powder was exhausted, and as the English swarmed in triumph over the entrenchment the minutemen were forced to fall back before them. still battling gamely with the butt ends of their rifles, with clubs, and even with rocks. This battle became known as the battle of Bunker Hill.

The English had taken the hill, but they had lost more than one thousand men in doing it. Said one of

the American officers after the battle, "I wish that we could sell them another hill at the same price." 

THE ENGLISH TROOPS LEAVE BOSTON 

When General Washington placed batteries of heavy guns on Dorchester Heights, overlooking Boston, the English were forced to abandon the city. They boarded their warships and sailed to 

Halifax, Nova Scotia. 

The Capture of Boston. For many months following the battle of Bunker Hill, there was no more fighting around Boston. General Howe, who succeeded General Gage in charge of the English army, was content

to hold the city. Washington, who in July had taken command of the colonial army, was content to keep the English bottled up in Boston until the cannons and supplies which he knew were coming from Ticonderoga and Crown Point should arrive. They reached him early in March, 1776. At once, Washington sent a strong body of his men to fortify Dorchester Heights, overlooking Boston harbor and the city itself.

When General Howe saw the American guns on 

Dorchester Heights, he knew that he could no longer hold Boston. Rather than wait for Washington to bombard the city, Howe loaded his men onto their ships and sailed away to Halifax.

The Invasion of Canada. During the winter of 

1775, while the siege of Boston was going on, a part of the colonial army under General Montgomery and a young officer named Benedict Arnold made a daring invasion of the English lands in Canada. The odds were too great. Although Montreal was captured, an attempt to take the strong fortress at Quebec ended in failure, and the colonial troops were forced to retreat from Canada. 

The Attack on Charleston. On June 28, 1776, an

English fleet appeared in the harbor of Charleston, South Carolina. The English commanders expected to take Charleston easily, for the city was defended by only a single fort-tiny Fort Moultrie, made of logs.

The men in Fort Moultrie were determined to die rather than surrender to the English. For ten hours the guns of the English warships hammered the walls.

THE BRAVE DEFENSE OF FORT MOULTRIE 

When the English attempted to take Charleston, South Carolina, in June, 1776, the colonial soldiers in little Fort Moultrie, which guarded the harbor, bravely resisted. The English fleet was defeated and forced to withdraw. of the fort, and for ten hours the gunners in the fort gave them shot for shot.

When darkness fell, the English had had enough. 

Their ships were full of holes, and the decks were red with the blood of scores of their men, wounded or dead. 

Under cover of night, they sailed away. 

The Breach Widens. Even after the early battles of the war, most of the American colonists did not want to become completely independent of England. All they wanted to do was to bring King George to his

senses so that he would restore to them the liberties they had enjoyed before he came to the throne. Only a few of the most vigorous patriots, such as Samuel Adams and John Hancock, were anxious for complete separation from England. 

In the late months of 1775 and the early part of 1776, however, there occurred a series of events which led many of the colonists to feel that they wanted to break all ties with England.

The Hessians. England's war against her American colonies was not popular in the mother country. 

When King George called for volunteers to fight the Americans, very few Englishmen offered to serve in the army. At last, in desperation, the king hired 30,000 

German soldiers from the prince of Hesse and sent them to America to help in putting down the rebellion in the colonies.

The king's action in hiring these Hessians, as they were called, angered the American colonists and led to an increase in the size of the colonial army. Many men enlisted who would not otherwise have done so, for they now felt that they were not fighting against their own countrymen, but against hired foreign troops. 

The Burning of Falmouth. Only a month after the hiring of the Hessians, the anger of the colonists was increased when an English warship sailed into the harbor of Falmouth, Maine, and the ship's crew burned the town. This cruel and useless deed made many people homeless just as the harsh New England winter was beginning.

"Common Sense." In January, 1776, an Englishman named Thomas Paine, who had come to America, brought out a pamphlet entitled Common Sense. In this pamphlet, Paine called upon the American colonists to give up their loyalty to England and to realize that they were already, in effect, an independent nation fighting for existence. Paine's arguments were powerful, and they convinced thousands of the colonists that they should fight to secure their independence. 

The Declaration of Independence. The desire for freedom grew by leaps and bounds. Finally, in June, 1776, Richard Henry Lee, representing Virginia in the Continental Congress, introduced the resolution, "that these united colonies are, and of right ought to be, free and independent states." This motion of Lee's was seconded by the patriotic John Adams of Massachusetts. Representatives from several other colonies urged that the resolution be adopted at once, but a number of representatives preferred to discuss so serious a move before giving their votes.

While the discussion was going on, a committee consisting of Thomas Jefferson, Benjamin Franklin, Roger Sherman, John Adams, and Robert R. Livingston was appointed to draw up a Declaration of Independence to be used in case Lee's motion was carried. 

Though all of the committee members helped in the work, the actual writing of the Declaration was done by Thomas Jefferson.

On the fourth of July, 1776, the Declaration of Independence was adopted by Congress, and the English

colonies in America no longer existed. In their place was a new nation, the United States of America. 

The official adoption of the Declaration of Independence took place in the Pennsylvania State House in Philadelphia, now known as Independence Hall. As soon as the Declaration had been adopted, the great bronze bell in the tower began to peal out the joyful tidings. Ever since that time, this bell has been known as the Liberty Bell. 

The Revolutionary War began in April, 1775, when the English commander in Boston, General Gage, sent troops to seize John Hancock and Samuel Adams at Lexington and to destroy colonial supplies at Concord. 

Paul Revere and William Dawes warned the minutemen of the Englishmen's approach, and battles were fought at Lexington and Concord. During their retreat from Concord, the English lost many men. 

On May 10, 1775, the Second Continental Congress. 

met at Philadelphia, with John Hancock as president.

Congress called for troops from the different colonies, and, in June, appointed George Washington as commander of the colonial army. 

In May, 1775, a band of Green Mountain boys, led by Ethan Allen, captured the English fort at Ticonderoga with all its supplies. Another band seized the 

English fort at Crown Point. 

In June, 1775, the Americans fortified themselves

on Breed's Hill overlooking Boston harbor. After two unsuccessful attempts, the English succeeded in driving the Americans away, but lost many men in doing it. 

This battle became known as the battle of Bunker Hill. 

Soon after the battle of Bunker Hill, General George Washington took command of the colonial army. 

In March, 1776, cannons and supplies arrived from 

Ticonderoga and Crown Point, and General Washing ton fortified Dorchester Heights, overlooking Boston.

This led General Howe and his soldiers to abandon the city of Boston.

During the winter of 1775, an American expedition under General Montgomery and Benedict Arnold invaded Canada. The Americans were unable to take 

Quebec, and the expedition was forced to retreat to the colonies. 

On June 28, 1776, an English fleet made an unsuccessful attack on Charleston, South Carolina.

Late in 1775 and early in 1776, there occurred a series of events which made many of the colonists want to break all ties with England. The first was the hiring of Hessian troops by King George to put down the rebellion. The second was the burning of Falmouth, Maine, by the crew of an English warship. The third was the publication of a pamphlet entitled Common 

Sense in which the author, Thomas Paine, called upon the American people to give up their allegiance to England and to realize that they were already in effect an independent nation fighting for existence. 

On July 4, 1776, the Declaration of Independence,

which had been written by Thomas Jefferson, was adopted by the Second Continental Congress. The Eng lish colonies in America had now been replaced by a new nation, the United States of America.

A CHOOSING EXERCISE ON THE EARLY DAYS OF THE REVOLUTIONARY WAR

CHAPTER NINETEEN THE  The signing of the Declaration of Independence made an important change in the nature of the Revolutionary War. No longer were the Americans merely a group of English colonists fighting for their rights. Instead, they were members of a new nation, striving to throw off the yoke of powerful 

England across the sea. How did the Americans fare in their struggle for independence?

Washington's Campaign of 1776. When General 

Howe and his English troops sailed from Boston to 

Nova Scotia, they had no intention of staying there.

Washington was well aware of this, and he felt very sure that the next point of attack would be New York.

So he moved his army from Boston to New York City. 

North of the city he built two forts, Fort Lee on the west bank of the Hudson and Fort Washington on the east bank. He also dug entrenchments on Brooklyn 

Heights, on the western end of Long Island, and there awaited the enemy. 

Washington and 

Soon after the

The Capture of New York City. 

his men did not have long to wait. entrenchments on Brooklyn Heights were finished,

General Howe with his army and a strong English fleet arrived at New York harbor. With a force of 20,000 men, Howe landed on Long Island and started toward 439

WASHINGTON CONFERRING WITH HIS OFFICERS 

After the battle of Brooklyn Heights, General Washington held a meeting of his officers in New York City. At this meeting, it was decided that New York City must be abandoned to General Howe and his army. Washington is the man at the left of the table. 

the American position. A small force of Americans which tried to stop him was easily defeated, and when he reached Brooklyn Heights the English general spread his army out in a great half-circle about the American entrenchments. 

Washington found himself in an awkward position. 

In front of him was an English army much larger than his own. Back of him were the waters of Long Island sound. If the English fleet were to sail up the East river and into the sound, he would be bottled up on all sides, and the American army would surely be defeated

and captured. Something would have to be done, and done quickly.

As night fell, the English could see the Americans hard at work strengthening their position. All during the night, the sound of picks and shovels told the English sentries that the Americans were still on Brooklyn Heights. When dawn came, however, and the English stormed the trenches, they found the Americans gone.

Under cover of fog and darkness, Washington had ferried his army across to New York City while a few men worked on in the trenches to deceive the enemy. 

Now that Brooklyn Heights had been lost, Washington could not hope to hold New York City, so he abandoned it to the English troops. 

The Retreat Across New Jersey. During the next two months, Washington retreated slowly up the east bank of the Hudson and crossed the river at Stony 

Point. Then he headed southwest toward New Jersey to place his army between the English and Philadelphia, which was then the capital of the United States. 

Hot upon Washington's trail came an English army led by Lord Cornwallis.

On the east side of the Hudson was an American army of about 7,000 men led by General Charles Lee. 

Washington ordered Lee to join him, but that general, jealous of his commander, disobeyed. Without Lee's aid, Washington's forces could not face Cornwallis' army, and the retreat into New Jersey continued. 

Southwest through New Jersey went Washington, with Cornwallis close behind him. Sometimes the two
