Much of the fighting in the Revolutionary War took place in New York, New Jersey, and Pennsylvania. Study the map and fix in mind the locations of the different battles and the routes followed by Washington and by the English commanders. armies were only about a mile apart, so close was the pursuit. At last, Washington reached the Delaware river and crossed it, taking with him every boat that could be found for miles along the stream. Cornwallis could not follow him and was forced to halt. It was now December, and the English could only wait and

hope that in the cold winter weather the river would freeze hard enough so that they could cross. 

The Battle of Trenton. So far, Washington's campaign of 1776 had been a disastrous one. He had been driven from Brooklyn Heights and from New York

City, and his army had been pursued across New Jersey without daring to make a stand against Cornwallis' superior forces. Fort Lee and Fort Washington had been captured by the English. To Cornwallis and to most people, it looked as if the war were about over. 

General Washington, however, did not share this belief. He was biding his time, waiting for a favorable opportunity to strike, and at last he got it. Cornwallis stationed his English troops at several points along the Delaware to keep Washington from returning, and then went back to New York to enjoy the Christmas holidays. 

On Christmas night, Washington and a force of 2,500 picked men secretly re-crossed the Delaware and marched toward Trenton, where a large body of Hessians was stationed. It never occurred to the Hessians that the ragged, beaten army that had been driven across New Jersey would ever strike back. They were, therefore, taken by surprise when Washington and his men suddenly fell upon them. The battle was short. 

After a brief resistance, the Hessians threw down their arms and surrendered. This victory gave the Americans needed guns and ammunition and put new heart into the army. 

Back from New York rushed Cornwallis, hopeful

THE SURRENDER OF THE HESSIANS AT TRENTON 

On Christmas night, 1776, General Washington made a sudden attack on the Hessian soldiers stationed at Trenton, New Jersey, and defeated them. Here we see the commander of the Hessians surrendering his sword to Washington. of capturing Washington and ending the war. He came very near to succeeding. At the head of a powerful army, he managed to trap Washington and his little force of Americans between the English army and the Delaware river. "I will bag the old fox tomorrow,' said Cornwallis confidently, and pitched his camp. 99 But Washington once more escaped, just as he had from Brooklyn Heights. All night long, brilliant campfires burned in the American camp to show the English sentries that Washington and his army were still there.

Cornwallis and his men believed the story that the campfires told them, but when morning dawned they found the fox was gone. Washington had led his army around the English forces and was already on his way toward Princeton.

The Battle of Princeton. At Princeton was stationed another force of Hessian soldiers. Like the soldiers at Trenton, they were taken by surprise when Washington and his men appeared. Hastily the Hessians seized their arms, and for a time put up so fierce a fight that the Americans were driven back. Washington rallied his troops, and before long the Hessians were forced to surrender. In quick succession, Washington had won two brilliant victories over the English. 

He now led his troops into winter quarters at Morristown, New Jersey. 

Robert Morris. While Washington was fighting the 

English with bullets, his friend Robert Morris was fighting them with dollars. Morris was a Philadelphia banker. He well knew that in spite of the victories at

Trenton and Princeton the American army was in a desperate plight. There was no money to pay the soldiers, and many of them wished to go home. If the war were to continue, money must be raised. 

Early in the morning of January 1, 1777, Robert

Morris went about Philadelphia, calling on all of his friends and asking for help. In this way, he managed to gather $50,000 in gold and silver which he sent to 

Washington. The American commander was overjoyed by this unexpected help, for it meant that he would

be able to hold his army together through the winter and to carry on the war at least a little longer. 

The New English Plan of Attack. During the winter, the English officials in London who were directing England's part in the war thought up an elaborate new plan for overcoming the Americans. In New York state, there were many Tories, or English sympathizers, who might be persuaded to join the English army. 

With their help and with the aid of Indian allies, New York might be captured. New England would thus be separated from the southern colonies, and the northern and southern colonists would find it hard to send help to each other. The first task of 1777, therefore, would be to conquer New York.

It was planned that General Burgoyne was to move south from Canada, capturing Ticonderoga on his way and finally taking Albany. At the same time, Colonel 

St. Leger was to cross Lake Ontario from Canada by boat, land at Oswego, and make his way down the Mohawk valley to Albany. General Howe was to move up the Hudson river and join the other two commanders. 

When this had been accomplished, all important posts in New York would be in English hands, and New England would be cut off from the southern colonies. 

Burgoyne's Expedition. South from Canada came

General Burgoyne at the head of his English army. 

Slowly and painfully he struggled through the northern forests until he reached Ticonderoga, which he captured. So far he had met with little resistance from the Americans. Now, however, Burgoyne's troubles

commenced. An army of Americans under General 

Schuyler began to block his way, chopping down trees and placing them across the roads and destroying bridges. Though these acts did not seriously slow up the English soldiers, they did hold back the supply wagons, and suddenly Burgoyne and his men found themselves without sufficient food. 

Not far away was the town of Bennington, and Burgoyne sent a thousand soldiers there to get food and other supplies. Instead of food, the soldiers found an 

American army under Colonel John Stark. Stark and his men fought so well that they defeated and captured most of Burgoyne's thousand troops. Less than a hundred of them managed to escape and rejoin Burgoyne. 

Sorely in need of supplies, General Burgoyne determined to push on to Albany, where he expected to receive help from General Howe. On September 19, 1777, the English came upon Schuyler's American army, which was fortified near Saratoga. Schuyler, however, was no longer in command, for he had been succeeded by General Gates. The English attacked at once but were driven back with heavy losses. General 

Burgoyne then decided to wait for General Howe and his army from New York. After waiting for almost three weeks, he became discouraged and once more attacked the Americans, with no better success than before. His plight was desperate. Many of his men had been killed, the rest were starving, and he was surrounded by American troops. On October 17, he surrendered.

St. Leger's Expedition. Colonel St. Leger, meanwhile, had been trying to carry out his part of the plan to conquer New York. He landed at Oswego and marched to Fort Stanwix, a small American fort at the western end of the Mohawk valley. As the Americans would not surrender, St. Leger and his men laid siege to the fort.

Not far east of Fort Stanwix lived an old patriot named General Herkimer. When he heard that the 

English were besieging Fort Stanwix, he proceeded at once to gather an army and go to the relief of the fort. 

At Oriskany, Herkimer and his men met an English force which St. Leger had sent out to stop them. In the midst of a heavy rain and terrific thunderstorms, the two armies fought for hours. Very early in the battle General Herkimer was wounded in the leg, but he sat with his back against a tree and continued to direct the fighting until at last the enemy was forced to retire.

In the meantime, the Americans in Fort Stanwix took advantage of the absence of the soldiers who had gone to fight Herkimer, by making a sudden attack upon the English camp. The attack was successful, and large stores of food and ammunition were brought back within the walls. Now that they had these badly needed supplies, the defenders were prepared to resist the English siege for as long as necessary. 

While this was going on, word had come to General Schuyler at Saratoga that Fort Stanwix was in danger of being captured. At once he asked for volunteers to

go to the rescue of the defenders. Among the first to volunteer was brave young Benedict Arnold. At the head of a company of picked soldiers he marched west from Saratoga and started up the Mohawk valley. 

Hearing that a large American army was on its way to defeat him, St. Leger speedily withdrew his forces to 

Oswego and from there sailed back to Canada. 

General Howe's Orders. Both Burgoyne and St. 

Leger had failed in their invasions of New York. The orders which should have been sent to General Howe had found their way into a pigeonhole in a desk in England and were there forgotten. That is the reason why 

Burgoyne waited in vain for the reinforcements which should have been traveling north along the Hudson. 

The American Flag. During the campaigns of 

1777, the American flag-the Stars and Stripesappeared for the first time in battle. The design was the work of a committee headed by George Washington, and the first flag was made by Betsy Ross of Philadelphia. Probably St. Leger's troops besieging Fort Stanwix were the first English soldiers to see the Stars and Stripes, for the new flag floated over the fort throughout the siege.

The Capture of Philadelphia. Although General 

Howe did not join Burgoyne and St. Leger, he was not idle. It occurred to him that the capture of Philadelphia would be a splendid and valuable move, since Philadelphia was not only the capital of the United 

States but also the largest city.

So General Howe with his army left New York City

BETSY ROSS MAKING THE FIRST AMERICAN FLAG 

The American flag-the Stars and Stripes-was designed by a committee headed by George Washington. The first flag was made by Betsy Ross of Philadelphia.

and started southwest through New Jersey. The going was hard, for Washington and the American army continually threatened his advance. Though the Americans were not strong enough to engage in open battle, they kept so closely on Howe's trail that he feared his army would be cut off from its supplies. At last, in disgust, Howe turned about and retired to New York. 

General Howe next attempted to reach Philadelphia by sea. Out of New York harbor sailed his ships, south along the coast, and up Chesapeake bay. Finding the mouth of the Delaware defended by two forts, he landed his soldiers at the head of the bay and set out overland to attack Philadelphia.

The Battle of Brandywine Creek. At Brandywine creek, Washington and his army made a desperate attempt to stop Howe's English forces, and for some time the English were unable to cross the creek in the face of the American fire. At last, however, Howe learned of an unprotected ford some distance up the stream. Crossing at this point, the English routed the 

Americans and drove them back toward Philadephia.

The Occupation of Philadelphia. When it was seen that Philadelphia was about to be taken, the members of Congress hastily fled to Lancaster, Pennsylvania, and later to York, making these two towns temporary capitals of the United States. The Liberty Bell was also removed from Philadelphia to keep it from falling into English hands and was taken to Allentown, where it was hidden in the basement of a church for safekeeping. But nothing could save Philadelphia itself

from the English. Washington and his army withdrew from the city, and the English entered and occupied it in September, 1777, without meeting any further resistance. 

The Battle of Germantown. Now that Philadelphia was captured, General Howe divided his army, leaving some of his soldiers stationed at Germantown, just outside of the city, and sending the remainder to capture the two American forts at the mouth of the Delaware river. After severe fighting, the forts were taken. 

Washington, in the meantime, had seized the chance offered him by the division of Howe's troops. Dividing his own army into four groups, he made a sudden attack from four different directions on the English forces in Germantown. At first the Americans were successful. The English were driven back, and it seemed as though the same thing were about to happen that had taken place at Trenton and Princeton. Then two things occurred to spoil the American plan. One was the heroic defense of the old Chew house, which was bravely held by a small group of English soldiers in spite of repeated American attempts to take it. The other event, even more unfortunate, was a mistake made by the Americans themselves. A dense fog covered the town, and when two divisions of American troops met they mistook each other for the enemy and opened fire. Many of the soldiers were killed, and the 

American forces were so weakened that they were obliged to fall back. Philadelphia remained in English hands.

THE ATTACK ON THE CHEW HOUSE, GERMANTOWN 

After abandoning Philadelphia to General Howe in 1777, Washington attacked the English at Germantown. The attack failed, and the American army was forced to retreat. Here we see the Americans vainly trying to capture the Chew house, the center of the English defense.

The Winter at Valley Forge. Following the battle of Germantown, Howe's army settled down for the winter in Philadelphia, and Washington led his forces into winter quarters at Valley Forge, northwest of the city. While the English were enjoying themselves, the

AMERICAN TROOPS DRILLING AT VALLEY FORGE 

Though the American troops suffered from cold, hunger, and lack of clothing during the winter at Valley Forge, they received splendid training from their officers. This picture shows Baron 

Steuben, a German nobleman, drilling the soldiers of Washington's army. 

American soldiers endured a terrible winter. The weather was bitter cold, supplies were low, and there was no money to pay the soldiers. The few log cabins in the camp provided only a pitiful shelter. Many of the men had no shoes, and as they walked through the

snow their bloody footprints told the story of their suf ferings. 

In spite of all hardships and troubles, the American army somehow managed to live through the winter at Valley Forge. They even profited from the long winter. With the army were a number of skilled army officers from Europe, and these men devoted most of their time to turning the untrained American troops into well-drilled soldiers. General La Fayette, General 

De Kalb, Baron Steuben, and other officers all did their parts in drilling the American troops. When spring came at last, the American army was better trained than it had been at any time before.

France Sends Help. Early in 1778, an important event happened which proved of invaluable help to the 

American cause. For over a year, Benjamin Franklin had been in France urging the king and his ministers to take the part of the colonies against France's old enemy, England. France had secretly been lending money and supplies to the Americans, but she hesitated to openly declare an alliance, for she feared that the Americans were not strong enough to fight successfully. Then the news of General Burgoyne's surrender reached Paris, and the French immediately made a treaty in which they recognized the independence of the United States and agreed to lend money and to send troops and warships to America.

The Battle of Monmouth. General Clinton was now the commander of the English troops in Philadelphia. When he heard that a French fleet was on its

way to America, he decided to move his army to New York City, which he could defend more easily than he could Philadelphia. So he led his soldiers through New 

Jersey toward New York.

General Washington was quick to realize that if the English army could be attacked while on the move there was a good chance of defeating it. So Washington and his army pursued the English across New Jersey and at last caught up with them at Monmouth. Here Washington ordered General Charles Lee and his division to start the attack, but the treacherous Lee, instead of obeying, ordered a retreat. The timely arrival of

Washington on the scene stopped the retreat, but the battle which followed failed to become an American victory because of General Lee's action. Neither side gained any real advantage. During the night, Clinton withdrew his army and continued his march to New 

York City. Washington and his troops retired to

Morristown. 

Indian Massacres. During the summer and fall of 

1778, the inhabitants of western Pennsylvania and western New York suddenly found themselves faced with a new and terrible danger. The Iroquois Indians, who had been lending occasional help to the English, began to make sudden raids on the American settlements. It is said that they were encouraged in this activity by the Tories. At any rate, many settlements were captured, and the men, women, and children were put to death, often after horrible tortures. Two of the worst massacres by the Indians were at the town of

Cherry Valley, New York, and in the Wyoming valley of Pennsylvania.

In order to protect the remaining settlers in the stricken regions, General Washington sent an army of five thousand men led by General John Sullivan. At 

Newtown, near the present site of Elmira, Sullivan and his men came upon the Indian raiders. In the battle which followed, the Indians were completely defeated, and a great many of them were killed. This broke the power of the Indians in the East. 

The War in the West. Soon after the French and 

Indian War, King George III had passed a law forbidding any of the colonists to settle west of the Appalachian mountains. There were colonists, however, who paid no attention to this law. Led by Daniel Boone, James Robertson, John Sevier, and other pioneers, small bands of settlers crossed the mountains into the lands now included in Kentucky and Tennessee, and before long a number of little backwoods settlements grew up there.

The English and the Indians. The English, meanwhile, held a number of forts in the region west of the Appalachians. When the war began to go against them in the East, they encouraged the western Indians to make attacks upon the American settlers in their lands. 

The man who did the most to stir up the Indians was Colonel Henry Hamilton, the commander of the fort at Detroit.

George Rogers Clark. Among the American settlers west of the Appalachian mountains was a young

backwoodsman named George Rogers Clark. Clark traveled to Virginia to lay before Patrick Henry, the Virginia governor, a plan by which the entire section north of the Ohio river between the Mississippi and the Appalachians could be conquered. Most of the people living in that section were Frenchmen or of mixed French and Indian blood, and Clark felt that they were not loyal to the English and could easily be won over by the Americans.

Governor Henry and the other leaders in the Virginia government approved of Clark's plan, and in June, 1778, the young leader set out with a small band of men. After sailing down the Ohio on flatboats, Clark and his men landed at a point about a hundred and twenty miles southeast of Fort Kaskaskia and trudged the long distance overland. They surprised the soldiers at the fort, who surrendered without a shot. Before 

Clark reached Forts Cahokia and Vincennes, the

French troops there hauled down the English flags and ran up American flags in their place.

When Colonel Hamilton, the English commander at Detroit, learned the news, he quickly started out with a large force of English soldiers to retake the forts. 

Clark was still at Kaskaskia when Hamilton arrived at Vincennes and captured the fort there. It was now winter, and the English commander decided to wait until spring and then attack Clark's army. 

When Clark heard that Vincennes was again occupied by the English, he at once made plans to attack it. 

Taking only 130 picked men, he set out as fast as pos-

From "Vincennes," one of the Chronicles of America Pho toplays. Copyright. By permission Yale University Press. 

THE SURRENDER OF VINCENNES 

At the head of a small band of men, George Rogers Clark set out in 1778 to capture the English forts west of the Appalachian mountains. He took Kaskaskia and Cahokia without trouble and soon afterwards compelled Colonel Hamilton and his English soldiers to surrender Vincennes. sible for the fort. It was a terrible journey. The weather was cold, and much of the land which they had to cross was under water. At the end of sixteen days,

Clark's little army at last reached Vincennes, tired, weak, and hungry.

Hamilton and his men would probably have had little difficulty in defeating and capturing Clark's small band if the Americans had launched an immediate at-

tack upon the walls. Instead, the American leader kept his men concealed so that the English would not know how few there were. He then boldly advanced and demanded that the English surrender the fort. If Hamilton and his men would give up Vincennes, said Clark, they would not be harmed, but if the Americans were forced to take the fort by assault every Englishman would be slaughtered. Hamilton asked for a truce of three days, but Clark would not grant it. Once more he repeated his demand that the English either surrender immediately or be put to death. Hamilton's courage failed him, and he surrendered Vincennes without the firing of a shot. 

Great credit should be given to George Rogers Clark and his men. Though they did little real fighting, their bloodless victories led to the United States' receiving the tremendous territory between the Appalachian mountains and the Mississippi river when the war finally ended.

The Capture of Stony Point. In the spring of 

1779, the English captured the American fort at Stony Point, which guarded the upper Hudson. General 

Washington assigned the task of recapturing Stony Point to a young Pennsylvania officer who was known as Mad Anthony Wayne because of his reckless daring. 

At the head of a small army of American troops, Wayne closed in about the fort under cover of night. 

Suddenly, without warning, the Americans swarmed into the fort, and after a brief resistance the stronghold fell.

The War on the Sea. Because the United States had no navy at the beginning of the war, most of the earliest fighting took place on land rather than on the sea. The Americans, however, had many merchant vessels, most of which were armed with cannons to guard against pirates. With the consent of Congress, these vessels, called privateers, soon began to prey on English shipping both at home and abroad, and were successful in capturing a large number of English ships and their cargoes. Congress also outfitted a small fleet of warships. Though they gave good service for a time, it was not long before the English navy captured or destroyed them all.

John Paul Jones. When France entered the war, Benjamin Franklin persuaded the French government to lend the Americans some warships. One of these, the Bon Homme Richard, was commanded by Captain

John Paul Jones. Accompanied by a few smaller ships, the Bon Homme Richard started out from a French seaport to capture English vessels in the English channel. A number of ships were taken, and a successful raid was made upon an English seaport. Then, on the way back, the French and American ships came upon a fleet of forty English merchant vessels under the pro tection of two warships, the Serapis and the Countess of Scarborough.

Leaving his allies to take care of the Countess of 

Scarborough, John Paul Jones immediately engaged in a fierce battle with the Serapis. After a long and bloody fight, the English commander hauled down his

THE CAPTURE OF THE SERAPIS 

In 1779, the American warship Bon Homme Richard, commanded by John Paul Jones, defeated and captured the English warship Serapis. This picture shows the American sailors board ing the Serapis.

flag and surrendered. Jones and his sailors then abandoned their own battered ship and sailed triumphantly back to port in the conquered Serapis. 

It is hard to over-estimate the effect of John Paul Jones' victory. Both Spain and Holland were jealous of the English naval power, and they seized this opportunity to declare war on England. England now had four nations against her in place of one, and this fact prevented her from throwing all of her energies into the war against the United States. 

The Treason of Benedict Arnold. One of the most brilliant American officers in the war was Benedict Arnold. Several times he had proved his ability-in the invasion of Canada, in the battle of Saratoga, and in the rout of St. Leger's troops at Fort Stanwix. 

Now he was to bring sorrow and distress to his countrymen.

In 1780, Arnold was in command of the important 

American fort at West Point, on the Hudson river.

Dissatisfied with the advancement he had received, and feeling that he had been unjustly treated, Arnold had secretly agreed to sell to the English the plans of the fort and a record of the number of soldiers defending it. In exchange, he was to receive a large sum of money -about thirty thousand dollars-and a high office in the English army. 

Major André, a brave English officer, volunteered to pass through the American lines, get the papers from Arnold, and take them to the English headquarters. On the way back to the English lines, André was captured,

and the papers were found in his possession. Not long afterward he was hanged as a spy. 

Word of André's capture reached Arnold as he was eating his breakfast at West Point. He immediately left the table, leaped upon his horse, and escaped to the safety of the English lines. There he received his reward and was made a brigadier-general in the 

English army.

The Invasion of the South. The English leaders now decided upon a new plan of attack. In the South, they knew, there were a great many Tories who needed but little urging to join the English army. 

It was planned to send an army through the southern states, gathering all the men that could be found. When a vast army had been gathered, it was to sweep to the north, defeating the Americans and capturing all of their important forts and cities. 

The Conquest of Georgia. The English already had a foothold in the South. In December, 1778, a fleet of 

English warships had attacked and captured the city of Savannah. An attempt by the Americans to recapture Savannah had been unsuccessful, and before long all of Georgia had been conquered. 

The Capture of Charleston. In 1780, the next step was taken in the conquest of the South. Charleston was captured in much the same way that Savannah had been taken. General Lincoln, the American commander, fought bravely to defend the city, but he had less than three thousand troops to oppose the thirteen thousand English under Cornwallis and Clinton. When

at last Charleston fell, General Lincoln and his soldiers were taken as prisoners. 

The Battle of Camden. General Clinton now re turned to New York and left Cornwallis to complete the capture of the Carolinas. Inland marched the 

English, straight to the town of Camden, which they took with little trouble. Meanwhile, the American army in the South had been placed under the command of General Gates. Gates at once led an attack on the 

English forces at Camden in the hope of retaking the town. So poorly did he organize the attack, however, that his army was entirely routed, and he and his forces fled to North Carolina.

The March through South Carolina. Cornwallis and his troops now pushed on through South Carolina, destroying towns and crops as they went along. But the English did not find the going as easy as they had expected. Under such skillful leaders as Francis 

Marion and Thomas Sumter, bands of patriots made sudden attacks on the English when least expected, and managed to do considerable damage. The heroism of these brave men kept alive the spirit of resistance in the South.

The Battle of King's Mountain. When Cornwallis at last reached North Carolina, he sent out an army of over a thousand men under Major Ferguson to defeat the bands of patriots who had been attacking him. 

Word spread among the backwoodsmen that the English were coming, and a large body of Americans gathered to resist them. Soon the English found them-

selves trapped on the top of a high hill known as King's Mountain. Here a desperate battle was fought, in which Major Ferguson was killed and his army was forced to surrender.

The Battle of Cowpens. Following Gates' defeat by the English at Camden, General Nathanael Greene, a much abler man, was placed in charge of the American army in North Carolina. This move proved to be a wise one.

Early in 1781, a large body of Cornwallis' troops, commanded by Colonel Tarleton, met a part of General Greene's army, commanded by Morgan and Pickens, at a place called Cowpens. The Americans fought a brilliant battle, and most of Tarleton's forces were either captured or destroyed. 

The Battle of Yorktown. Cornwallis had been dealt a staggering blow at Cowpens. Nevertheless, he decided to go on into Virginia and subdue the Americans there. In Virginia, General La Fayette and an evergrowing force of Americans held Cornwallis at bay. 

Finally, the English general decided to retire with his army to Yorktown, which was situated on a narrow peninsula extending into Chesapeake bay. Here he erected fortifications and awaited reinforcements from 

New York.

The English reinforcements did not arrive. Instead, a French fleet suddenly appeared in Chesapeake bay and cut off Cornwallis from all communication by sea. 

In the meantime, General Washington had made a sudden forced march from New York and had joined

THE SURRENDER AT YORKTOWN 

Trapped between the American army and the French fleet, General Cornwallis was forced to surrender his English army to 

Washington at Yorktown, Virginia, on October 19, 1781. His surrender marked the close of the War for Independence. 

La Fayette and his men, who were now camped before Yorktown.

Cornwallis was trapped between the French fleet and the American army. For a week, he resisted the combined attacks of the two forces, but he knew there was little hope. At last, on October 19, 1781, the

English flag was hauled down, and Cornwallis and his men marched out and surrendered. 

The End of the War. Cornwallis' surrender marked the end of the War for Independence. While 

Cornwallis was carrying on his campaign in Virginia, the American general Greene had succeeded in winning

back the Carolinas and Georgia, with the exception of Savannah and Charleston. These two towns were given up by the English during the following year. New 

York, the only other point held by the English, was surrendered in 1783 after the signing of the treaty which formally ended the war. 

War between France and England. The French continued to fight against England for two more years. 

In the West Indies, a tremendous naval battle was fought, and the French were defeated. Thus England won the war from the French while losing to the Americans.

The Treaty of Paris. By the terms of the treaty of peace, which was signed in Paris in 1783, the English colonies in America were granted their independence.

All English land between Florida on the south and Canada on the north and between the Atlantic ocean on the east and the Mississippi river on the west passed into American hands. England also gave Florida back to Spain. France was the only nation that fought against England and did not profit by the war. 

With the signing of the Treaty of Paris, the United States became a free and independent nation. 

In 1776, General Washington's American army was defeated by the English at Brooklyn Heights and was driven from New York City. After a long retreat across New Jersey, Washington made surprise attacks

upon the Hessian forces stationed at Trenton and Princeton and defeated them. He then led his army into winter quarters at Morristown. 

Robert Morris, a banker of Philadelphia, helped the American cause by raising money in Philadelphia and sending it to General Washington.

In 1777, the English attempted to conquer New York state. An English army under General Burgoyne marched south through the Champlain valley and captured Fort Ticonderoga but was defeated at Saratoga and forced to surrender. Another English army, under 

Colonel St. Leger, failed to take Fort Stanwix and at last retreated into Canada.

General Howe was supposed to lead his army north from New York City to Albany to join Burgoyne and

St. Leger, but he failed to receive his orders and made an attack on Philadelphia instead. He defeated the

Americans at Brandywine creek and took the city of Philadelphia.

Washington's army was defeated by the English at Germantown and spent the winter of 1777 at Valley Forge. The men suffered terribly but received valuable military training.

Early in 1778, word was received that France would send help to the Americans. This was largely the result of Benjamin Franklin's work. Money, troops, and warships were sent to help in the fight against the 

English. 

While General Clinton was attempting to move his army from Philadelphia to New York, General Wash-

ington attacked him at Monmouth. Neither the English nor the Americans gained much advantage in the battle. 

At Newtown, an American army under General Sullivan defeated bands of Iroquois Indians who had been massacring people in western New York and Pennsylvania.

Although King George had made a law forbidding the American colonists to settle west of the Appalachian mountains, bands of pioneers led by Boone, Sevier, Robertson, and others had established settlements in what is now Kentucky and Tennessee before the Revolutionary War. 

George Rogers Clark, a young backwoodsman, led a small American force west and captured the English forts at Kaskaskia, Cahokia, and Vincennes. 

An American force led by Anthony Wayne recaptured the American fort at Stony Point, which had been taken by the English.

At the beginning of the Revolutionary War, the United States had no navy. A few warships were outfitted, and these, together with many armed merchant ships called privateers, made raids on English shipping and captured a large number of English vessels and their cargoes. All of the American warships were at last captured or destroyed by the English navy.

The most important American naval victory during the war was the capture of the English warship Serapis by the American Bon Homme Richard. The defeat of the Serapis resulted in Spain and Holland declaring war against England.

Benedict Arnold, who had been a brilliant American officer early in the war, tried to betray the fort at West Point into English hands. His plan was discovered, and Arnold fled to the English lines. 

Late in 1778, an English fleet captured Savannah, Georgia. In 1780, Charleston, South Carolina, was taken, and the American army defending the city was captured. 

The English next took Camden, South Carolina.

An American army under General Gates attempted to retake Camden but was badly defeated.

Bands of patriots under Marion, Sumter, and other American leaders made frequent raids on the English army as it marched northward. Two divisions of Cornwallis' army were defeated by American forces at 

King's Mountain and Cowpens.

Cornwallis and his English army invaded Virginia and fortified themselves at Yorktown. Here they were trapped by the American army and a French fleet and were forced to surrender. The battle of Yorktown marked the end of the American Revolution. 

After the battle of Yorktown, the French continued to fight against the English. A French fleet in the West 

Indies was badly beaten by the English warships. 

The Revolutionary War was formally ended when the Treaty of Paris was signed in 1783. By its terms, England granted her American colonies their independence together with all of the land from Florida to Canada and from the Atlantic ocean to the Mississippi river. Florida was returned to Spain. France was the

only country which fought against England and gained nothing in return.

SIGNING THE CONSTITUTION 

Ewing Galloway, N. Y.

These men are members of the Federal Convention which drew up the Constitution of the United States. Their work finished, they proudly sign their names.

CHAPTER TWENTY THE DEVELOPMENT OF THE CONSTITUTION 

"We must all hang together, or we shall all hang separately," said Benjamin Franklin at the begin ning of the Revolutionary War. During the war, the colonies did "hang together," but when the war was over they were no longer united. Instead of a strong country with a central government, there were thirteen separate states, each with its own government and each jealous of the others. What a tremendous task it must have been to bind these thirteen states together under a single government! 

The Second Continental Congress. The Second 

Continental Congress, which had governed the colonies during the Revolution, was a government without definite powers. It was made up of representatives from each of the thirteen colonies, meeting at Philadelphia.

The different colonies varied in the number of representatives which they sent to Congress, but each had only a single vote. Though the Congress had no legal right to do so, it raised the colonial army, appointed officers, declared independence from England, raised money by begging and by borrowing it, signed a treaty of alliance with France, and finally accepted the Treaty of Paris which ended the war. These were all necessary moves, and Congress assumed the power to make them only because they had to be made and 475

there was no one else to make them. In doing this, the members of Congress showed real courage. 

The Articles of Confederation. In July, 1775, only a few weeks after the battles of Lexington and Concord, Benjamin Franklin submitted to the Second Continental Congress a plan of government known as the 

Articles of Confederation and Perpetual Union. It was much too early for such a plan to be accepted. 

Although the colonists were at war with England, most of them still regarded themselves as Englishmen fighting for their rights, and few wanted lasting independence. Franklin's plan was, therefore, not accepted. 

After the Declaration of Independence, however, the people of the United States had no thought or hope of again becoming English subjects. A committee was appointed, with John Dickinson of Pennsylvania at its head, "to prepare a plan of confederation and transmit it to the respective colonies for their consideration and approbation." This plan, called the Articles of Confederation, was submitted to Congress. It was approved by Congress in November, 1777, and then sent to the various states to be ratified, or approved. 

Under the Articles of Confederation, the United States were to be governed by a Congress made up of delegates from the different states. Congress was given the power to declare war and peace, to make treaties, to pass laws, and to borrow money. It was expected that the ratification of the Articles would not take long, but it was not until 1781 that the last of the states ratified them and they became the law of the land.

THE CONSTITUTION 

477 The state of Maryland was largely responsible for this long delay in the acceptance of the Articles of Confederation. The Maryland Assembly found no fault with the Articles themselves, but held up their adoption as the best way to force the settlement of a very important problem.

The question which Maryland wished to have settled concerned the lands between the Appalachian mountains and the Mississippi. Massachusetts, Connecticut, New York, Virginia, North Carolina, South Carolina, and Georgia all had claims to sections of these lands. 

Other states, including Maryland, New Jersey, and Pennsylvania, had no claim to western lands. 

The people of Maryland held that since the western lands were gained as a result of a war in which all of the states had fought, the lands should be granted to all of the states together. For a long time, the states which claimed the lands would not give up their claims. At last, New York and Virginia offered to surrender their claims. In later years, the other states followed their example. As soon as New York made its offer, Maryland ratified the Articles of Confederation, and they became the form of government for the United States. 

This was in March, 1781.

The Weaknesses of the Articles of Confederation. 

The government provided by the Articles of Confederation soon proved itself to be a very feeble one. Congress was given far too little power, and the states were allowed to keep altogether too much. Congress had no power to enforce its laws and no courts in which to try

people who would not obey them, no control over trade and commerce, no power to lay taxes, and no authority to regulate and control the paper money issued by the states. The only way in which Congress could obtain money to meet debts or other expenses was by asking the states for it and then accepting whatever they were willing to contribute. In 1781, for example, Congress asked the states for $5,000,000. At the end of the year, all the money that had been contributed was $450,000, and there was no way of making the states give any more. Another weakness of the Articles was that they could not be amended, or changed, without the consent of every state.

Difficulties with Foreign Countries. It is not surprising that the governments of the powerful European nations failed to take the feeble government of the United States very seriously. After the war, England insolently refused to give up her forts and trading posts west of the Appalachian mountains. She also refused to make favorable trade treaties with the United States, so American merchants were unable to carry on profitable trade with the English seaports. France refused to recognize that there was any government at all in the United States. Even feeble Spain took advantage of the still more feeble United States and boldly tried to tempt western settlers to become Spanish subjects. Worst of all, the Barbary pirates of the Mediterranean began to prey upon American ships because they felt that the United States was too weak to protect the lives and the property of its own citizens. George

THE CONSTITUTION 

479 Washington clearly recognized the weakness of the Articles of Confederation when he remarked that "such a Union cannot long endure." 

BARBARY PIRATES ATTACKING A SHIP 

Under the Articles of Confederation, the United States government was very weak. Knowing this, the fierce Barbary pirates of the Mediterranean did not hesitate to attack American ships, capture their crews, and steal their cargoes.

Troubles within the United States. In addition to the many foreign troubles, the United States had many serious troubles at home. Although the people of the different states had fought side by side against Eng-

land and had echoed Patrick Henry in declaring that they were no longer Virginians or New Englanders or New Yorkers but Americans, they soon resumed their local prides and prejudices after the war was over. 

Colonial patriotism became state patriotism, and almost every one was much more interested in what happened to his state than in what happened to any other state or to the central government. 

State jealousies caused much trouble in the years immediately following the war. New York, New Hampshire, and Connecticut all claimed the territory that is now Vermont, and a bloody war was fought for its possession. Settlers from Connecticut who moved into the Wyoming valley of Pennsylvania were regarded as foreign intruders by the Pennsylvanians, who stirred up the Delaware Indians to massacre many of these newcomers. Virginia and Maryland quarreled bitterly over the trade rights of the Potomac river. New York laid heavy duties on goods shipped in from New Jersey and Connecticut. In return, New Jersey taxed a lighthouse built by New York on the New Jersey shore, and Connecticut refused to have further dealings with New York. Day by day, it became more evident that there must be some central government with authority to straighten out the many childish quarrels between the states.

The paper money which the various states issued was another real cause of trouble. Gold and silver coins were rare, so there was little metal money to back up the paper currency. Naturally, much of the paper

THE CONSTITUTION 

481 money fell rapidly in value and was soon almost worthless. None of it could be used to pay debts to foreign nations, and often the people of one state refused to accept the money of another. In general, the farther one traveled from home the less one's paper money was worth.

Shays' Rebellion. Massachusetts was the only state which did not suffer from paper-money troubles, for the Assembly of the state refused to issue any. This brought on troubles of another kind. With little gold or silver and no paper money, people had nothing which they could use to pay their debts and their taxes. Many lost everything they owned when their property was sold to pay what they owed. Many others were thrown into prison.

In western Massachusetts there lived a man named 

Daniel Shays, who had been a captain in the Revolution. Shays decided to put an end to the practice of imprisoning helpless people for debt. Raising an army of nearly 2,000 angry men, he marched to the courthouse at Worcester where trials for debt were going on. There he forced the court to stop the trials and to close its doors.

Shays' army next marched to Springfield, where there was a government arsenal containing guns, ammunition, and other supplies of arms. The rebels found that they were unable to force their way into the arsenal, and soon a body of militia, led by General Lincoln, appeared and drove them away. Shays himself fled to New Hampshire.

The Ordinance of 1787. In the midst of all this trouble abroad and at home, the Second Continental Congress did one fine and constructive bit of work. 

Some of the states, you remember, had surrendered their claims to the lands lying between the Appalachian mountains and the Mississippi river. The part of this land north of the Ohio river was now organized by Congress into the Northwest Territory. 

The Ordinance of 1787, the law by which the Northwest Territory was created, contained several important provisions. A governor and three judges were to be appointed to govern the territory until the population became great enough to justify dividing the land into states with regular assemblies. All citizens in the territory were to have complete religious and political freedom. "Schools and means of education" were to be provided. Slavery was forbidden, but all slaves who fled into the territory were to be returned to their owners.

Congress planned to sell sections of land in the Northwest Territory to people who wanted to live there. 

The money received was to be used in paying the debts of the United States. It was intended that sooner or later the entire section would be divided into either three, four, or five new states.

The Alexandria Convention. Virginia and Mary land, as we have learned, were constantly quarreling over the trade rights on the Potomac river. By 1785, the quarrel had become so bitter that it was decided that something must be done to end it before serious trouble

THE CONSTITUTION 

483 resulted between the two states. Accordingly, representatives from both Maryland and Virginia met at 

Alexandria, Virginia, in 1785. George Washington was one of the representatives who came to speak for Virginia.

As the meeting progressed, it became evident that other states were concerned with the questions being discussed. It was finally resolved to hold another meeting the next year at Annapolis, Maryland. Representatives from all of the states were to be invited, to see if something could be done to regulate the commerce of the United States as a whole. 

The Annapolis Convention. Although all of the states were asked to send representatives to the meeting at Annapolis, only five did so. This was discouraging. After a brief discussion, it was proposed that still another meeting be held the following year at Philadelphia. This time the purpose of the meeting was to revise and strengthen the Articles of Confederation. It was hoped that representatives from all of the states would be present at this meeting. 

The Federal Convention. By the time of the next meeting, in 1787, the people of the various states had begun to see the folly of interstate jealousy and to appreciate the weaknesses of the Articles of Confederation. All of the state legislatures except Rhode Island decided to send representatives to Philadelphia.

The representatives who met at the Federal Convention in Philadelphia were intelligent, farseeing men.

With only a few exceptions, all of the great leaders of

the country were among the fifty-five who attended the convention. George Washington and James Madison were two of the representatives from Virginia. Benjamin Franklin, James Wilson, Gouverneur Morris, and Robert Morris represented Pennsylvania. John 

Dickinson represented Delaware. Roger Sherman of 

Connecticut, Elbridge Gerry of Massachusetts, Charles 

Pinckney of South Carolina, and Alexander Hamilton of New York were also present. Five of the representatives-Washington, Franklin, Hamilton, Madison, and Wilson-took such an important part in the proceedings that they have been referred to as the "master minds."

The New Constitution. The Federal Convention went into session on May 25, 1787. George Washington was chosen as its president. None of the representatives had authority to do more than modify the Articles of Confederation, but very early in the discussions it became evident that the Articles were so weak and had so many defects that they could never form the basis for a strong, lasting government. They were, therefore, discarded entirely, and the members of the convention set about drafting a new Constitution, or plan of government.

From May until September, the Federal Convention worked in secret, behind closed doors. Many questions arose which had to be argued between the delegates before agreements could be reached. Some of the members went home before the work was finished because they did not approve of the powers which were being

THE CONSTITUTION 

485 given to the central government. But most of the representatives stayed until the end and worked hard to frame a plan of government which should prove successful for the country as a whole. To Gouverneur 

Morris of Pennsylvania fell the duty of writing the complete draft of the Constitution in its final form. 

The government of the United States, as provided by the new Constitution, was different from any government that the world had ever seen. As Abraham 

Lincoln expressed it many years later, it was a government "of the people, by the people, for the people." 

Though many powers were still left in the hands of the states, enough power was given to the central government to insure its control over the country and the respect of foreign nations. 

The Constitution provided for three branches of the federal government. One of these, the Legislative branch, was to be made up of the members of the Senate and the House of Representatives, who were to be elected by the states. Together, these two bodies were to be known as Congress. Congress was to have the power to make laws, to lay and collect taxes and duties, to regulate commerce, to coin money and regulate its value, to borrow money, to raise and support an army and a navy, to declare war, and to govern the territories of the United States. No law could become official until it had been passed by both the Senate and the House of 

Representatives. 

The Executive branch of the government was to include the President and the government officers under

him. To this branch was given the duty of enforcing the laws. Every bill passed by Congress was to come to the President for his signature. If he signed it, the bill would become a law. If he did not sign it, the bill was to be returned to Congress for a further vote. To become a law without the President's signature, the bill would have to be re-passed by a two-thirds vote in both the Senate and the House of Representatives. The 

President was also given the power to make treaties by and with the advice and consent of the Senate. 

The third branch of the government was the Judicial Department, consisting of the Supreme Court of the United States and lesser courts. In these courts, all law cases between states and all cases involving the Constitution of the United States were to be tried. The 

Supreme Court was to represent the highest law of the land.

Ratification by the States. Even after the Constitution was completed, it had to be ratified by nine of the thirteen states before it could be put into effect. It was by no means certain that nine states would be willing to ratify it, and an intensive campaign in its favor was carried out throughout all of the states. This campaign resulted in the formation of our first two political parties-the Federalists, who favored the adoption of the Constitution, and the Anti-Federalists, who opposed it.

In the midst of the campaign a little pamphlet appeared called The Federalist. In this pamphlet were articles urging the adoption of the Constitution and

THE CONSTITUTION 

487 pointing out the need of a strong central government. 

Though no names were signed to the articles in The Federalist, it was later discovered that Alexander 

Hamilton, James Madi- son, and John Jay were the authors of most of them. All three of these men were later to play very important parts in the history of the United 

States. 

On December 7, 1787, the Constitution was ratified by Delaware, the first state to vote upon it. 

Then Pennsylvania, New 

Jersey, Georgia, Connecticut, Massachusetts, Maryland, South Carolina, and New Hampshire voted to adopt it. New 

Hampshire's action was taken on June 21, 1788, and on that date the Constitution went into effect. 

ALEXANDER HAMILTON 

This brilliant American statesman played an important part in framing our Constitution and in securing its adoption by the states.

Virginia and New York soon followed New Hampshire's example, although not until after bitter struggles. This left only two states-North Carolina and 

Rhode Island-which had not fallen into line. In the convention called in North Carolina to consider the

question, the delegates failed to take a vote, and the matter was left unsettled until the following year, at which time North Carolina gave its approval. Rhode 

Island still held out, but at last was obliged to give in when Congress threatened to tax all goods sent from Rhode Island into any of the other states. This was in 1790.

The Bill of Rights. One of the chief reasons why many people were opposed to the new Constitution was the absence of a Bill of Rights, guaranteeing to the people the liberties and privileges which they had fought to gain. Although there were no such guarantees in the Constitution as it was adopted by the states, they were added in the form of ten amendments during the first two years in which the Constitution was in force. Among the rights guaranteed to the people of the United States were the right of trial by jury, the right of freedom of speech and freedom of the press, and the right to worship as they saw fit. Provisions against quartering soldiers in private homes in times of peace and against the use of Writs of Assistance and other general search warrants were also included. 

The people had suffered from these evils during the days of English rule, and they had no desire to repeat the experience under their own new government. 

The framers of the Constitution did their work brilliantly and thoroughly. The government which they devised in a few short months about a hundred and fifty years ago is the form of government under which we live today. It is called a government of checks and

THE CONSTITUTION 

489 

GEORGE WASHINGTON BECOMES OUR FIRST PRESIDENT 

On April 30, 1789, George Washington took the oath of office in New York City and became the first President of the United 

States. 

balances, for each of the three departments limits the powers of the others.

Washington Chosen President. When the Revo lutionary War ended, it was George Washington's hope that he would be allowed to retire to his home at Mount Vernon, Virginia, and live the rest of his life in peace and quiet. This the people would not permit him to do. 

So well had he led them in war that they were loud in their demands that he continue to lead them in peace.

Washington was unanimously elected the first President of the United States. John Adams was chosen as Vice President. On April 30, 1789, Washington solemnly took the oath of office on the balcony of 

Federal Hall in New York City. 

The critical period of American history was now at an end. The first President of the United States was in office, and our country was ready to assume its place among the nations of the world. 

The Second Continental Congress had no definite powers, but it assumed the government of the colonies during the Revolutionary War. 

A plan of government known as the Articles of Confederation was submitted to the states in 1777, but was not finally adopted until 1781. Maryland would not approve the Articles until the states which claimed land west of the Appalachian mountains should agree to give up their claims and turn the land over to the new national government. 

Under the Articles of Confederation, Congress had too little power and the states too much. Congress had no power to enforce its laws, no control over trade and commerce, no authority to lay taxes, and no power to regulate and control the paper money issued by the states. 

Under the Articles of Confederation, the United States had many difficulties with foreign governments.

THE CONSTITUTION 

491 England, France, Spain, and the Barbary pirates had little respect for the United States. 

During the period when the Articles of Confederation were in force, there were many troubles within the United States. Bitter quarrels arose between the states themselves, the paper money which the states issued fell in value, and in Massachusetts a former soldier named Daniel Shays led a rebellion to put an end to the imprisonment of debtors.

The Ordinance of 1787, passed by the Second Con tinental Congress, provided for the development of the territory north of the Ohio river, known as the North west Territory. 

In 1785 and 1786, meetings of representatives from some of the states were held at Alexandria, Virginia, and Annapolis, Maryland, to discuss the regulation of commerce. Little was accomplished at either meeting because so few states were represented. 

In 1787, the Federal Convention was held in Philadelphia for the purpose of changing and strengthening the federal government. Representatives from all of the states except Rhode Island were present. The 

Articles of Confederation were discarded, and a new Constitution was drawn up after much discussion. 

The new Constitution of the United States provided for the form of government under which we are still governed. It consisted of three branches-the Legislative, the Executive, and the Judicial.

After the Constitution was completed there was an intense campaign to have it adopted by the states. The

Federalists favored its adoption and the Anti-Federalists opposed it. 

By the end of 1788, the Constitution had been approved by eleven states. The other two states had adopted it by 1790.

Within two years after the adoption of the Constitution, ten amendments, known as the Bill of Rights, were added to it. These guaranteed to the people of the country many personal rights and privileges. 

On April 30, 1789, George Washington became the first President of the United States. 

A CHOOSING EXERCISE ON CONDITIONS IN THE UNITED STATES UNDER THE ARTICLES OF CONFEDERATION 

Read the following sentences aloud, using the word or words in parenthesis which make each statement correct. 

1. The Second Continental Congress, which met in (Philadelphia 

-New York), was made up of representatives from (twelvenine-all) of the thirteen colonies. Each colony had (two votes-one vote). 

2. The Second Continental Congress performed (no-several) important acts during the Revolution. 

3. In July, (1775—1776—1781), (William Penn-George Wash ington-Benjamin Franklin) submitted to the Second Continental Congress a plan of government known as the Articles of Confederation and Perpetual Union. It (was not-was) accepted.

A REASONING EXERCISE ON THE CONSTITUTION 

Following are given a number of statements concerning the United States Constitution. Some are true, and some are not true. 

Read each statement aloud, thinking about it carefully as you read.

Then tell, in your own words, why you believe the statement is true or is not true. 

1. If it had not been for the Alexandria and Annapolis conventions, we would still be living under the Articles of Confederation. 

2. The Federal Convention would have been more successful and more important if the men who attended it had improved the Articles of Confederation instead of drawing up a new Constitution. 

3. The Constitution which was written at the Federal Convention was not very good, for a number of changes have since been made in it. 

4. The people of the United States were better off after the 

Constitution was adopted than they had been under the 

Articles of Confederation. 

5. The fact that the Constitution was at last approved by all thirteen of the states proved definitely that the Anti-Federalists had been wrong in opposing it. 

6. Rhode Island acted wisely in ratifying the Constitution. 

7. Since "many are stronger than one," the thirteen states would have been more powerful if each had become separate and independent instead of all uniting under one government. 

8. Under the Constitution, each of the three departments of the 

United States government is somewhat limited in power by the other two departments. 

9. The addition of the Bill of Rights to the Constitution was not necessary.

CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 

Seventy-five years is a long time in the life of a man, but it is a very short time in the life of a nation. In its first seventy-five years the United 

States spread out from the Atlantic to the Pacific, and from Mexico and the Gulf of Mexico on the south to Canada on the north. How did it gain all this territory? And what drew people from many other lands to make their homes in the new young nation?

In Washington's Day. When George Washington became President of the United States, the land of the new nation extended from Maine's northern boundary south through Georgia; and from the Atlantic, west to the Mississippi river.

North Carolina, South Carolina, and Georgia had not yet given up their hold on their western lands and still claimed that their states reached from the ocean to the Mississippi. The land north of the Ohio river had been turned over to the government by Massachusetts, Connecticut, New York, and Virginia, and had become known as the Northwest Territory. 

North of the United States lay Canada, owned by the English. To the west was the vast stretch of Louisiana. To the south was Florida. Both Louisiana and 

Florida were owned by Spain. 495

ATLANTIC OCEAN THE UNITED STATES IN 1787 

Here we see the United States as it was soon after the Revo lutionary War. Find the Northwest Territory, formed of lands given up by Virginia, New York, Connecticut, and Massachusetts. What countries owned the lands bordering the United States?

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 497 How the West Was Settled. Back in the years between the French and Indian War and the Revolution, pioneers began exploring and settling west of the 

Appalachian mountains. There was Daniel Boone, a hunter who had always lived in wild country, who could walk through the woods as silently as a cat, knew which berries and nuts were safe to eat, and how to camp at night where he would not be discovered. He guided the people who settled Kentucky. There were James Robertson and John Sevier, educated men, who loved adventure and wanted to see settlements grow up in the western land that is now known as Tennessee. These men and other pioneers led small groups across the mountains and helped them to build up settlements in the very lands that had been the Indians' hunting grounds.

It is no wonder that the Indians resented the coming of the white men into their lands and that they attacked and burned the little backwoods towns and killed or carried off the settlers. Life was hard and dangerous for the pioneers, but they persevered. Others followed them, and before many years had passed the lands where they had settled became states. 

In spite of the wise laws made for the Northwest Territory, no large number of settlers bought the government's lands there in the first years following the Revolutionary War. This was mainly due to the constant danger of Indian attack. Then in 1811 William 

Henry Harrison, an American general, defeated the 

Indians. Their powerful leader, Tecumseh, fled to Can-

ada, and the Indians no longer dared attack the white men.

After the defeat of the Indians, many little pioneer settlements grew up north of the Ohio river in the lands that later became the states of Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Michigan, and Wisconsin.

Means of Travel. The early pioneers who moved into the western lands had hard traveling. If they went by land they had to make their way on foot or possibly on horseback. At first there were no roads, and wagons could not be drawn through the unbroken forests. 

Daniel Boone, it is true, opened a pathway through the 

Cumberland pass, known as "The Wilderness Road,' and in time other roads were opened into the lands to the west. These made travel easier, though bumping along over them in the first great lumbering wagons was far from comfortable. 

Many a man built himself a flatboat, which he launched on a stream or river near his old home. When the entire family and all their goods had been taken aboard and packed in as cozily as possible, the pioneer pushed off from the river's bank and drifted downstream into unknown country.

All might go well and the flatboat just float along for days. But sometimes there would be a shallow place in the river and the boat would run aground. Then what a time there would be to get the heavily loaded flatboat on its way once more! Sometimes the river's current would be so swift that the boat would be whirled along in constant danger of being overturned.

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 499 

HOW THE AMERICAN PIONEERS TRAVELED BY WATER 

Many built flatboats like the odd-looking affair in the middle of the picture. Flatboats could travel only downstream as they were carried along on the river's current. 

Flatboats were soon replaced by packet boats on the main rivers. These packet boats carried many people and much freight, but, best of all, they could go upstream as well as down because they were driven by sails and were not merely carried along on the river's current. However, when there was no wind to fill the sails a packet boat could not go at all, and when there was a wind it went very slowly against the current. 

It was in 1807 that Robert Fulton made his first steamboat, the Clermont, and tried it out on the Hudson river. The Clermont was a great success and before

long other steamboats came into use up and down the principal rivers of the country. 

The Purchase of Louisiana. Now let us recall what we have learned about Louisiana. About one hundred years before the battle of Yorktown, the

French explorer, La Salle, made his way down the Mississippi river to its mouth. Once there, he claimed for France the river and all the lands drained by it and gave to this great stretch of territory the name Louisiana. At that time the lands which made up Louisiana lay both to the east and to the west of the Mississippi. 

When the French and Indian War came to an end in 1763 and the Treaty of Paris was signed, France was forced to give to England, Canada, and all of Louisiana which lay east of the Mississippi with the exception of the city of New Orleans. So Louisiana was cut in two and the name Louisiana then applied to the western part only.

Soon it was discovered that even before the Treaty of Paris was signed France had turned over to Spain all of Louisiana west of the Mississippi and also New

Orleans in return for the help that Spain had given her during the war. And that was the way matters stood all during the Revolution.

During the years that followed the Revolution, American pioneers pushed on farther and farther west into the lands which had been the eastern part of Louisiana. These settlers worked hard to develop their land and before long they had products to send to market. With no railroads to carry their goods to the east,

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 501 

they shipped them to the Mississippi and down that broad river to its mouth. 

Then came the year 1802 and with it the news that France under Napoleon Bonaparte had overcome Spain in Europe, and that Spain had been forced to give 

Louisiana back to the French.

This was bad news indeed. With the powerful 

French holding the west bank of the Mississippi and the port of New Orleans they might close the river to American trade and, too, they might prove to be dangerous neighbors for the young United States nation. 

What was to be done?

At that time Thomas Jefferson was President of the United States. He sent two men to Napoleon to try to buy New Orleans and some land near the city. 

What Jefferson finally did buy in 1803 was not just New Orleans but the whole of Louisiana, for which he paid about $15,000,000. As Louisiana was larger than the entire United States at that time, our country after the purchase was more than doubled in size. 

No strong neighbor now threatened the United

States from the west; goods could be shipped up and down the Mississippi without fear of trouble; and surely there was plenty of land for the pioneers to explore, settle, and turn into good rich farmland. Jefferson had made a good bargain. 

Lewis and Clark Expedition. The year after he had bought Louisiana, Jefferson sent an expedition under Meriwether Lewis and William Clark to explore the new lands. The men set out in canoes from St.

Ewing Galloway, N. Y.

JOURNEYING TO THE WEST 

This "covered wagon" with its tentlike top is headed west. 

So traveled many of our early pioneers. 

Louis on the Mississippi, and followed the course of the Missouri river northwest for many, many miles and finally through a great gorge in the Rocky mountains. 

When they had journeyed for days up one of the Missouri's branches, they carried their canoes overland to the Snake river. Down the Snake they paddled till they reached the Columbia river which floated them west to the shores of the Pacific ocean. 

Oregon. By the time Lewis and Clark got back to St. Louis they had been gone nearly two and a half years, but through their explorations they had strengthened the claim the United States had already made to the wide northwestern lands.

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 503 

England, too, claimed the lands which were known as Oregon and for some years after Lewis and Clark's expedition both English and American settlers moved into the territory and made their homes there. 

Later, in 1846, a peaceful agreement was reached between the two countries by which England kept the part north of the 49th degree parallel of latitude and the United States the part south of that line. Why the 

49° parallel? Because in 1818 England and the United

States had adopted that line as the boundary between Canada and the United States from north of the Mississippi to the Rocky mountains. So, after the settlement of the Oregon ownership, the northern boundary of our country reached from the Atlantic to the Pacific. 

Florida. To the south of Georgia lay Florida, the western part of which extended along the northern coast of the Gulf of Mexico almost to the Mississippi. 

Florida was discovered in 1513 by Ponce de Leon who gave the land its name and claimed it for Spain. Spain held Florida until 1763, when she was obliged to turn it over to England because she had sided with the French during the French and Indian War. 

England held Florida from the close of the French and Indian War to the close of the Revolution. Then by the terms of the treaty which closed the war, Florida once more became a Spanish possession. 

Before very long the United States began to wish that they might own this southern land. There were two main reasons for wanting to own Florida. One was that there were good harbors along Florida's north-

west coast from which United States cotton could be shipped. The other reason was to stop the frequent raids across the border made by outlaw bands formed in Florida. These raids led to much trouble between the United States and Spain until at last in 1819 Spain agreed to give up to the United States the ownership of Florida. In return the United States agreed to pay $5,000,000 to the people of Florida for claims held against the Spanish government. So Florida was added to the United States and our country's eastern boundary reached clear to the southern tip of Ponce de Leon's flowery land, and our southern boundary extended along the Gulf of Mexico from the tip of Florida to the western line of Louisiana. 

Texas. When we purchased Florida, Spain still held the lands to the west of Louisiana and south of the Oregon Territory. Not long after, however, these 

Spanish lands broke away from Spain and became a new nation under the name of Mexico. The northeastern part of Mexico, called Texas, had been largely settled by people from the United States. As years went by, the people of Texas became very much opposed to the way they were being governed. At last, in 1836, led by Stephen F. Austin and Sam Houston, Texas revolted won its independence from Mexico, and asked to become part of the United States.

The many Americans who had moved into Texas. and many other people in the United States wanted to see Texas added to our country. Others did not agree. 

They feared that if we took Texas into the Union,

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 505 

trouble with Mexico would follow. So the matter drifted on until 1845 when Congress finally decided to accept Texas as part of the United States. 

California and New Mexico. Almost at once Mexico made trouble. Texas had claimed the Rio Grande river as her boundary and Mexico said the land along that river belonged to Mexico and not to Texas at all. 

The dispute became bitter. Soon war broke out between the United States and Mexico and some very fierce fighting took place. In the end Mexico was beaten. By the terms of the treaty of Guadalupe-Hidalgo, signed in 1848, the Rio Grande was recognized as the boundary of Texas, and, on the payment of certain sums of money, the United States gained from her defeated neighbor the parts of Mexico called California and 

New Mexico.

Within the territory gained from Mexico lived a group of people known as the Mormons. They had moved westward under a leader named Brigham Young in search of a place where they would be free to worship as they pleased. Many years later the chief Mormon settlement, Salt Lake City, became the capital of our state of Utah.

In 1853, Mexico sold us another piece of land south of New Mexico, and our country then had its present boundaries from the Atlantic to the Pacific and from 

Mexico north to Canada.

Growth of the United States. What was the United 

States to do with all this land? Who was to live in it?

It so happened that in the years our country was ex-
