THE UNITED STATES EXTENDS FROM SEA TO SEA 

Trace the country's growth by studying the dates, and note how each new section was gained.

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 507 

tending its boundaries to the west, conditions were becoming very bad in many countries of Europe. Some of these countries were overcrowded. In others, crops failed and food was scarce. The people of still other countries did not like the laws under which they were governed. These reasons, added to the spirit of adventure, tempted thousands to leave their homelands and sail to the United States.

From Ireland, Germany, Norway, Sweden, Den mark, and other countries they came. For the most part those from each country established settlements in the same section of their adopted land. The wide stretches of the United States offered space enough for all, and the government of the new nation promised the new settlers freedom to live according to their own ideas and to worship God according to their beliefs. 

As more and more people settled in our new lands they were divided into territories. A territory is land not lying in any state, but owned and governed by the United States. Then, gradually, one at a time, constitutions were drawn up for the different territories and they were taken into the United States as new states. 

So it was that seventy-five years after the thirteen little colonies along the Atlantic coast had won their freedom from England, the United States extended from the Atlantic to the Pacific and there were already -not thirteen-but thirty-two states in the United 

States of America.

When the thirteen English colonies in America became an independent nation, their lands extended from the Atlantic to the Mississippi. To the north lay 

Canada, owned by the English. Louisiana to the west and Florida to the south belonged to Spain. 

In 1803 President Thomas Jefferson purchased from France the vast territory named Louisiana, which Spain had by then turned over to France. 

Meriwether Lewis and William Clark explored the newly-purchased lands and so strengthened the United States claim to Oregon. England also claimed Oregon. 

In 1846 an agreement was reached which gave England the part north of the 49th degree of latitude and the United States the part south of that line. 

In 1819 the Spanish possession of Florida was given to the United States in return for the promise to pay $5,000,000's worth of Spanish debts. 

Soon the other Spanish lands on the North American continent revolted against Spain and formed the country of Mexico. Then in 1836 Texas broke away from Mexico and became part of the United States. 

Disputes about the boundaries of Texas led to war between the United States and Mexico. When Mexico was defeated she turned over to the United States, California and New Mexico. When Mexico later sold us another piece of land south of New Mexico our present boundaries were established. Into these widespread lands came settlers from many European countries. The new lands

EARLY DAYS IN THE UNITED STATES 509 

were first divided into territories and gradually the territories became states.

II TABLES EUROPEAN AND INTERCOLONIAL WARS, 1689-1763 

Four great wars were waged in Europe between 1689 and 1763. In each of them, France and 

England fought on opposite sides, and their colonies made war against each other in America. 

In the first three of these wars, neither side won a decisive victory, and the treaties which ended them gave no great advantage to either side. In the fourth war, England was victorious. The treaty which ended this war gave the English practically all of the French lands in eastern North America, and left England and Spain in possession of the continent.

THE IROQUOIS HISTORY SERIES By SOUTHWORTH AND SOUTHWORTH 

This series presents our history and its background from earliest times to the present. The text material is presented simply, vividly, and with a wealth of fascinating incident. Rich in illustrations and attractive, accurate maps. 

In this series, geography is correlated with history wherever this correlation makes for a clearer understanding of geography and history relationships. 

The workbooks provide filling-in, choosing, and other interesting types of test material, outline maps and map exercises, and self-scoring graphs. 

THE STORY OF LONG AGO. The myths of the Greeks and Norsemen, the story of early man, and Old World history to the fall of Rome.

THE STORY OF THE MIDDLE AGES. Our Old World background from the fall of Rome through the Middle Ages and the exploration period.

AMERICA'S OLD WORLD BACKGROUND. In one volume, the myths of the Greeks and Norsemen and the Old World background of American history from the time of early man through the period of exploration. 

A WORKBOOK ON AMERICA'S OLD WORLD BACKGROUND. For use with America's Old World Background, The Story of Long Ago and 

The Story of the Middle Ages, or any textbook covering this period.

THE THIRTEEN AMERICAN COLONIES. The story of the American 

Indians, a survey of the period of exploration, the history of each of the thirteen original colonies, and the Intercolonial Wars. 

A WORK BOOK ON THE THIRTEEN AMERICAN COLONIES. For use with The Thirteen American Colonies or any text covering the same period of history.

. Our country's history beginning with the discovery of America and continuing through the colonial period, the Intercolonial Wars, the Revolutionary War, the adoption of the Constitution, and the extension of the country to its present boundaries. 

AMERICAN HISTORY, COMPLETE EDITION. American history, be ginning with the period of exploration and continuing to the present day.

A WORK BOOK IN AMERICAN HISTORY. For use with American 

History, Complete, or any complete American history text.

THE AMERICAN WAY. A Democracy Reader by Southworth and South worth. A timely contribution to any Social Studies course in grades 6-12. It presents the speeches of great American patriots and statesmen from Patrick Henry's time to our own, and patriotic poems which will always be popular. 

Introductions by the authors link the selections and tell a connected story of the growth of democratic ideals and government in our country.

This book should be returned to the Library on or before the last date stamped below.

A fine is incurred by retaining it beyond the specified time. 

Please return promptly.
